## T: totorungu–tyre kicker

### totorungu
 
 <i>n.</i> a fresh-water fish

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The Maori bearers ... had sufficient energy to fish, catching nearly a hundred totorunga, the only New Zealand fresh water fish with scales ... <i>(Jan 19, 1841,Diary</i>) [NOTES: [1841 Jan 19 R.Taylor Diary] in 1966 Mead Richard Taylor 24]

### totowai
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [toutouwai](tot-tyr.md#toutouwai))

### totoweka
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [totoeka](ten-tot.md#totoeka))

### touch
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>touch</i> somebody's turn to buy a round of drinks; eg `Hey, Sid, isn't it your touch?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)

### touch noses
 
 <i>phr.</i> to hongi

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Duaterra immediately recognised [the chief], and ... behaved to him with particular respect, touching noses with him, the usual mode of salutation in this part of the world, when any honour is to be paid, and which he did not do with the other chief. The latter he contemptuously described as an ittee ittee rungateeda, or a chief possessing very little power. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/91</i>)

>  <b>ALEX McCRAE</b> On taking leave of these boys I touched noses with them. <i>(1820,Journal,12</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> We were received by them all sitting and after the salutation of touching noses, they eagerly enquired for what purpose we had come. <i>(1940,Rakiura,345</i>) [NOTES: 1823 June 1 Capt.J.R.Kent Mermaid log in 1940 Howard Rakiura 345]

>  An old man named Oree Pahbah ... saluted us, by touching noses (the New Zealand custom). <i>(1827,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 185]

>  An old man named OOree Pahbah came to us as we were walking up to the village, who saluted us by touching noses (the New Zealand custom). <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,68,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 68]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We had a very cordial reception, and as the chief was an old acquaintance of Mr W. they touched noses together, ... <i>(Apr 6, 1839,Journal,2/87</i>) [NOTES: [TS:ATB]]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> When they meet one another, or a European, after the first salutation, by touching noses, they do not remain standing upright, but squat down on their heels; ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/109</i>)

>  <b>T.S.GRACE</b> `Where?' (his back being towards me), and immediately jumping up he hongied (touched noses [?Ed.]) with me, and saluted me as `the face of Te Heuheu.' <i>(Aug 1877,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1877 Aug T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 264]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> Bill wondered uneasily how he would manage to keep working with Dumby [a Maori] ... But when Bill went back to the shed Dumby touched noses with him. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,47</i>)

>  <b>Monkhouse</b> ... however he at last ventured forward, they saluted by touching noses. <i>(Oct 6, 1769,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Oct 6 W.B. Monkhouse Journal in Cook Journals (1955) 1/567]

### touch of the stepmother's breath
 
 <i>phr.</i> a catchphrase said to indicate a nip in the air, a cold day [ca1964 Alistair Campbell Heard it in Wellington]

### touch of the tarbrush
 
 <i>phr.</i> (offensive) Maori ancestry [also dash or lick of the tarbrush]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> ` ... They're such nice people. Usually so quiet. ... You'd never dream there was a touch of the tar-brush.' <i>(1949,Brown Man's Burden,80,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,340</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> Suspect there might be a touch of the tarbrush there - just a smidgin, mind - ... <i>(1985,Shuriken,27</i>)

>  <b>ROSIER-JONES</b> Emma stiffens at the word `hori', but surely he's not referring to . . .[sic] `Emma has got Dally in in her y'know, Fred,' the manager says. `Coulda fooled me. Looks like the old tar-brush t'me.' <i>(1985,Cast Two Shadows,129</i>)

>  She told him that after Mr Beresford died ... his face turned black and [18] his stomach too. `Maybe it was the tar-brush coming out,' said Zip. <i>(Mar 1986,Landfall,157/17</i>)

>  Other [White Club] members feel perfectly free to talk to me about `bongas', `a touch of the tar brush', `the jungle coming out in him' and `bloody Maoris'. <i>(Apr 3, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,8</i>)

>  <b>MEIKLE</b> I can remember only a few Maori children at school and a few who were overtly `half-castes' or who had `a touch of the tarbrush' - labels commonly applied. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,128</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> .. their progeny, whose brown skins betrayed a touch of the tar-brush ... <i>(1935,Little Country, The,390,1937 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEIKLE in GIFKIN</b> I can remember only a few Maori children at school and a few who were overtly 'half-castes' or who had 'a touch of the tarbrush' - labels commonly applied. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,128</i>)

>  This dame, who was something more than a brunette, - in fact, her friends were wont to say that she had a touch of the tar-brush, - had long smarted under a sense of Mrs Chutney's pretensions to superiority, and she determined to assert herself. <i>(Aug 17, 1883,Bruce Herald,5</i>)

>  'So, Annie, where is it you're from in New Zealand. You've a lick of the tar brush like me eh?' <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,63</i>)

>  There are varying evidences of "the dash of the tarbrush" in Wellington. One sees a female glorification of Mongol ancestry[,] a negress with most of the coarseness eliminated, or an idealised Maori with most of the ancient characteristics blown away by the Wellington winds and metropolitan smartness. <i>(Jan 26, 1907,New Zealand Free Lance,12</i>)

### touch one's foblocker
 
 <i>phr.</i> to reach for or spend one's money; pay out (cf. touch one's kick etc.) [ca1951 Wellington (Foblocker, ? a portmanteau coinage from fob @n `small (money-) pocket in the waistband of trousers' (OED n2) and locker `a secure cupboard (for valuables etc.))]

### touch one's kick
 
 <i>phr.</i> to pay money

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>touch your kick</i> a modest loan, a `kick' being old word for sixpence; common request by Pedlar Thomas quoted NZ Oral History Unit Martinborough study. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> (Glossary) <i>Touch the kick</i>: To pay out money - put your hand in your pocket. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,169</i>)

### touch up
 
 <i>v.</i> to remind, to hurry up

>  Messrs Littlejohn and Sons are the parties you should make reference to, as I sub-lease from them that portion of the building, but not the clock so you had better touch them up about it. <i>(Jun 20, 1908,NZ Truth,157/4/7</i>)

### toucher
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> 10. `Why,' said he to me, `Old Toucher,/ You don't really meant to say,/ ... NOTES ... Verse 10: Toucher was a borrower, <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,55,1956 ed.</i>)

### touchie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a touch judge

>  Make friends with the touchies. ... And the touchies will love being on telly so much. <i>(Nov 25, 2005,NZ Herald,D11</i>)

### touchstone
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  TOUCHSTONE.- A black silicious stone used for testing the quality of gold. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,56</i>)

### tough
 
 <i>adj.</i> of wool

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Early in the season, especially if sheep have had hard treatment before shearing, some sheep become really `tough', and the wool seems glued to the skin. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,47</i>)

### tough ask
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) [esp. sport, used globally, esp. Austral.] (See [big ask](b-bla.md#big-ask),[hard ask](h-hea.md#hard-ask))

>  She's a tough ask, selecting a national soccer team. <i>(Jan 29, 2006,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),B4</i>)

### tough bickies/tough bikkies
 
 <i>phr.</i> in sense of `tough luck (on)'; a variant or euphemism of tough titty/ies [-] (See [cf. stiff bickies](../S#stiff-bickies))

>  The Budget used to be a time for mixed sweets - presents for some, tough bikkies for others. <i>(Jul 25, 1983,National Business Review,6</i>)

>  ...the staff have evolved a sensible self-protective psychology; they simply don't care. If individuals are treated unfairly, tough biccies. <i>(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> Tough biccies on me, I suppose, she said. <i>(1977,Of Ivory Accents,42</i>)

>  Mr Browlee said it was tough bickies, he had been given the floor and was therefore entiltled to be on his feet, and Dr Cullen wasn't. <i>(Apr 7, 2005,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### tough cheese
 
 <i>phr.</i> a var. of British slang <i>hard cheese</i>, ?with <i>tough titty</i>, hard luck.

>  <i>tough cheese</i>: hard lines <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### tough rag
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Tartan must be a `tough rag' after eighteen months' spell, including stud duty and dry-docking for repairs owing to an argument with a station fence, to account for the Randwick Plate in such a decisive manner. <i>(Nov 2, 1907,NZ Truth,124/2/6</i>)

### tough spin
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SARGESON</b> tough: difficult, demanding: <i>a tough spin, the going was tough, tough to work for. </i> <i>(1964,Collected Stories,304</i>)

### toughie
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a difficult sheep

>  I have seen a real `toughy' go from No.1 pen right down the catching pens to the bottom, as shearers threw it over the rails when no one was looking. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,48</i>)

### toughie-sticker
 
 <i>n.</i> a false tattoo

>  He's covered in tattoos and has an intimidating look about him, but he has the ruggedness to back it up. They're not just toughie-stickers, the joker has the minerals. <i>(Jul 15, 2005,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,16</i>)

### toumatou
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Discaria Toumatou Toumatou <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> DISCARIA TOUMATOU (<i>Raoul</i>.) ... The `TOUMATOU'.- ... The plant is very generally known throughout the Colony as `The Wild Irishman'. In the Middle Island, more particularly towards Otago, it is called `Native Thorn'. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,93</i>) [NOTES: ?an error on the Featons' part]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Discaria <i>australis</i>, Hook. ... Nat. name, `Toumatou', <i>Raoul. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,47</i>)

### toumatu gowrie
 
 <i>n.</i> tumatakuru, matagouri

>  These include euphorbia glauca, pseudopanax ferox (toothed lancewood), toumatu (matagouri), muehlenbackia astonii, pimelia aridula (sand daphne) and coprosma. <i>(Jul 6, 2000,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  The so-called roads were generally bridle tracks through the ordinary tussock and toumatu gowrie. <i>(Nov 2, 1920,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  I used to have to fix tumatu kuru scrub over the thatch of my.. small oat stacks to keep the pukeko from stripping them. <i>(Nov 2, 1920,Otago Witness,54</i>)

### tourist
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person who has opinions but shows ignorance about farming.

>  MAF is encouraging farmers to watch their image after complaints from townies and tourists over cattle being held in muddy sacrifice paddocks. <i>(Aug 1, 1996,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

>  A person who knows nothing about farming: a tourist. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### Tourist Bureau
 
 <i>n.</i> the Government Tourist Bureau

>  <b>SLATTER</b> So I went straight to the Tourist Bureau and was lucky enough to get a berth. [omit as encyclopedic: certainly much earlier] <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,16</i>)

### touristified
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Cr Allen says passengers could be carried in converted stock carriages like those that took lambs to the slaughter at the old Gear meatworks. An alternative would be "touristified" refrigerated trucks like those that carried carcasses to the wharves. <i>(Mar 9, 2001,Evening Post,18</i>)

### Tourists
 
 <i>n.</i> usu. with def art., a nickname for the first contingent, NZEF (cf. WAR BABIES)

>  <b>M & N ROSS</b> The men from the Antipodes have brought with them a humour and a slang of their own. Their definition of the various batches of volunteers that have come out to fight is worth quoting. The first contingent became known as `The Tourists'. They were out to see a bit of the world. Incidentally they would do any fighting that came along. And they did it. Then came `The Dinkums' - the true fighting men as they called themselves. <i>(1916,Light and Shade</i>)

### Tournament
 
 <i>n.</i> an inter-universities' sports tournament

>  <i>(1912,Auckland Capping Book,14</i>) [NOTES: [see cit WOODEN SPOON]]

>  There was a Tournament held in a Southern town:/ We sent our annual team to as usual to win renown,/ They kissed their mothers .../ And left by the Main Trunk Train. <i>(Jun 6, 1912,Auckland Univ. Coll.Students Assn Souv. Progr.,46</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> The newcomer was Te Rangitiroa [sic], Dr.Peter Buck, my friend of University Tournament days; ... <i>(1968,Seddons, The,188</i>)

### tout suiter
 
 <i>n.</i> (WW1 ?nonce)

>  <b>MILLER</b> These guns are known to be long range naval guns of high velocity. The boys call the shells `tout suiters' because they come with such a rush. <i>(Nov 27, 1917,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,184</i>)

### toutou
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> MIRO LONGPIPES \{MIRO AUSTRALIS\}. (NORTH ISLAND ROBIN.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Pitoitoi, Toutou, Toutouwai, and Totoara. <i>Ibid.</i> 122 \{1888 2nd edn 1/36\} MIRO ALBIFRONS. (SOUTH-ISLAND ROBIN.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Pitoitoi, Toutou, Toutouwai,and Totoara. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,121,{1888 2nd edn 1/33} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Toutou <i>(Miro longipes)</i> is a small grey and white bird, which some people have said is called the New Zealand robin. It is to be seen in the bush now and then, and seems tame, but we prefer to call another species <i>our</i> robin. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/224</i>)

### toutouwai
 
 <i>n.</i> the NZ robin [ORIGIN: Maori, aka totowai] (See [bush robin](bri-byo.md#bush-robin))

>  Above and to every side are riroriro (grey warbler), korimako (bellbird), tui, titipounamu (rifleman), miromiro (tomtit), kakariki (red-crowned parakeet), toutouwai (New Zealand robin) and even a pair of exquisite hihi, or stitchbird. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/16</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|ou t|ou wai; A bird so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,17</i>)

>  <i>Toutouwai</i> - This bird is nearly the size of the sparrow; ... its feathers are dark, tinged whit white about the breast and tail, ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),56</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>toutouwai</i> is also of the swallow species. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/301</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Toutouwai is a smaller slate coloured bird, has a shrill whistling note and is particularly fond of gazing (if I may so express it) at strangers, and if encouraged by a whistle will follow the person for some distance. [Glossary 120 North Island Robin ...] <i>(1844,NZ Journal,113,1956 ed.</i>)

>  Petroica longipes. Totowai. Robin. Common. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  The Popokatea (<i>Mohoua albicilla</i>) is represented by another species (<i>M. ochrocephala</i>) differing in colour but so closely allied to it that the natives apply the same name to both; the Toutouwai (<i>Petroica longipes</i>) ... is represented by (<i>Petroica albifrons</i>) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/111/5</i>)

>  The Popokatea (<i>Mohoua albicilla</i>) is represented by another species (<i>M. ochrocephala</i>) differing in colour but so closely allied to it that the natives apply the same name to both; the Toutouwai (<i>Petroica longipes</i>) ... is represented by (<i>Petroica albifrons</i>) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/111/5</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 31. PETROICA ALBIFRONS. Gml. Wood Robin. Totowai. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,12</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> This bird is the subject of an article - the Pitoitoi or Toutouwai of the natives and the `Robin' of ther colonists. <i>Ibid.</i> 121 MIRO LONGPIPES \{MIRO AUSTRALIS\}. (NORTH ISLAND ROBIN.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Pitoitoi, Toutou, Toutouwai, and Totoara. <i>Ibid.</i> 122 MIRO ALBIFRONS. (SOUTH-ISLAND ROBIN.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Pitoitoi, Toutou, Toutouwai,and Totoara. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,120,1888 2nd edn 1/33, 34, 36 ed.</i>)

>  I noticed the following:- Pigeon, tui, korimako, kaka, ruru, piwakawaka, toutouwai, kingfisher, duck and teal in the lakes, and pukeko in the swamp, and various sea birds. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  <i>toutouwai,</i> a bird (Miro longipes), the North Island form; the South Island form (Miro australis) bears the same name, the two forms differing only in the coloration of the breast and abdomen, which are white in the former, and in the latter the abdomen yellowish-white tinged with lemon-yellow on the breast. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>toutouwai</i>: New Zealand Robin. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> TOMTIT <i>Petroica australis Maori name</i>: Toutouwai. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,202</i>)

>  <i>258.a. Petroica australis longipes </i>(Lesson, 1828) <i>North Island Robin (Toutouwai) </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 68 <i>258.b. Petroica australis australis</i> (Sparrman, 1788) <i> South Island Robin (Toutouwai) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,68,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Robin Petroica australis </i>(SPARRMAN, 1788) ... <i>North Island Robin P.a. longpipes</i> (LESSON, 1828). <i>South Island Robin P.a. australis </i>(SPARRMAN, 1788). <i>Stewart Island Robin P.a. rakiura</i> FLEMING, 1950. OTHER NAMES: <i>Bush robin, toutouwai.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,282</i>)

>  This morning the robin or totowai <i>(Jan 1986,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1986 January Birdcall over National Radio [announcer]]

>  <b>R ICHARDS</b> <i>Miro Traversi</i> ... Found now only on Little Mangere Island ... Black Wood Robin. TOU-TOU-WAI, MIRO. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,71</i>)

>  A fine specimen of the blue-wattled crow (kokako), hopping from bough to bough, was observed at close quarters for five minutes, and two other rarely seen birds, the North Island robin (toutouwai, male and female), and the saddleback (tieke) were also seen. <i>(Jan 15, 1932,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  [caption] Left: North Island robin, toutouwai <i> Petroica australis longipes </i> with food in beak. <i>(Jul 2009,Open Space,32</i>)

### tow truck
 
 <i>n.</i> a truck designed to tow broken-down or damaged or illegally-parked vehicles [Jim writes: BritE = <i>breakdown van</i>. Not recorded in DNZE but cf <i>towie</i> - "tow-truck driver or operator". Not in NSOD. Collins tags it as <i>US and Canadian. Tow truck</i> is standard NZE.]

### tow truckie
 
 <i>n.</i> [also towie]

>  The tow truckies say it's common for car owners to storm into the yard exploding with unrepeatable criticism of the qualities of the towies in particular, and life, the universe and everything in general. <i>(Apr 7, 1983,Dominion,7</i>)

### towai
 
 <i>n.</i> a tall spreading forest tree of the North Island, related to the kamahi. [Maori towai[o-macron]]. also called tawhero; <i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>, kamahi (in the n. North Island <i>W. sylvicola,</i> tawhero) [TOWAI( or TAWHERO) <i>W. silvicola</i> up to 15m. n.NI to 38 lat.; <i>W. racemosa </i> kamahi takes over from about 37 lat. southwards (from Manukau south) and is the `red birch' of Westland]

>  <i>To&#769;.wa-i </i>(t&omacr.&#769;.wa.&ibreve. or t&omacr.&#769;.-w&imacr.),<i> n</i>. [Maori.] <i>(Bot.)</i> A New Zealand saxifragaceous tree (<i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>); - called also <i>birch. [Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> ... towha,another species of pine, not so large as the totara, small, narrow leaf, smooth bark, berry.[check that this is towai] <i>Ibid.</i> Nicholas NZ 2/245 The trees which the natives chiefly make subservient to their purposes, are [besides the pines cowrie, totarra, towha and zarida mentioned above] ... the henow, from which they extract a black dye, the towha, a tree resembling the sycamore, the <i>river river</i>, the grain of whose wood is similar to that of the beech, a species of cork-tree, called by these people vow, a large tree named eckoha, and another named kycata, a tall and beautiful tree, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/330 The<i> towha</i>, another species of pine, though not so large as the <i>totarra</i>, grew here [Waimate, Northland] at the same time to a considerable size, and the forest abounded with it. This tree has likewise a small and narrow leaf, but its bark is thin and quite smooth; it bears a berry which is eaten by the natives. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/329</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|owai; A shrub so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,217</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> A great variety of hard-wood grows at New Zealand, admirably adapted for the timbering of any sized ships; among them is the boride[e-acute], rattar[as-acute], taraide[ase-acute], mai[a-acute], totara[a1-acute], koi katoa[a2-acute], toa[a-acute] toa[a-acute], tani raha[as-acute], to wai[a-acute], reiva[a-acute] reiva[a-acute], tana[as-acute], and many others. <i>(1835,Extracts From a Journal,19,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Towai - a tree of the <i>Podocarpus</i> species, with a dark-brown bark, and a leaf similar to, and about the size of, the moss-rose. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),46</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other trees abounded in Pamaki [a's-acute], such as the kaikatea [a2-acute], towai, rewa-rewa, totara [a's-acute], puriri, rata [a's-acute], kahika [a2-acute], tipow, tanikaha [a2-acute], rimu, and various kinds of akkas, all of which will be found described in the Appendix (Note 7). <i>Ibid.</i> 2/396 The <i>Towai, (a<i> podocarpus</i>,) is but a small tree compared with those preceding. Its wood ... has a handsome deep red colour when polished. It grows to the heighth [sic] of twenty-five feet, and then is richly furnished with virent leaves. The wood is heavy and but little used hitherto. <i>(1838,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/109</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The towai is not remarkable for a tree, makes fair fencing. [<i>Glossary </i>120 ... <i>Weinmannia sylvicolaM</i>]. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,106,1956 ed.</i>)

>  The bark of the Towai, <i>Weinmannia silvicola</i>, does not appear to have been collected for tanning purposes ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/145</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  The Towai, and Tawhero, (<i>Weinmannia sylvicola</i>, and <i>W. racemosa</i>,) are small trees ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/46</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>BAINES</b> ... on either side [of the harbour] much of the land was covered with a growth of manuka (tea-tree), rewa-rewa, towai, karaka, and other smaller kinds of timber ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,144</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> The towai (<i>W. silvicola</i>), a closely allied tree, which is abundant in the north, ... would probably prove more durable [than tawhero]. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,20</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> WEINMANNIA SILVICOLA (<i>Banks & Sol.</i>) ... The `TOWAI'.- ... The wood is similar in quality to its ally<i> W. racemosa,</i> but is denser, and of finer grain; ... [136] This tree is sometimes called Tawhero, or Kamai, and is not unusually confounded with <i>W. racemosa</i>, the Tawhero or Kamai proper. In the same manner the tawhero is confused with this species by the uninitiated. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,135</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> In towai (<i>Weinmannia silvicola</i>) the trend is from one to five pairs of leaflets ... <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,38</i>)

>  It prefers stream banks and forest clearings and can often be found rubbing shoulders with a close relative called towai (Weinmannia silvicola) in a region south from Kaitaia to Whangarei. <i>(Oct 1, 2005,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  "The towai, or kamahi (Weinmannia racemosa), furnishes a splendid bark that is valuable for tanning cordage, fishing nets, &c., and when mixed with equal proportions of rimu (red pine) bark it is also valuable for tanning leather, and will ultimately be a great source of wealth to the colony ..." <i>(Jul 30, 1896,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  The bark used for tanning.. is not the bark of tawa.. but it is the bark of the towai - a tree that grows to the height of about twenty feet, and is somewhat scarce in most of the timbered districts. <i>(Feb 24, 1883,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [toetoe](ten-tot.md#toetoe))



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> From the high tawai-trees [[fn] Leiospermum racemosum.] a graceful moss hung down in long festoons. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/389 Towai - a timber tree <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/146</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Towai, <i>Col[enso]. Weinmannia racemosa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Towai, or Karmai (<i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>). A beautiful large tree, especially when in flower. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/37</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Towai. Weinmannia racemosa, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/41</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>, Forst. Towhai, Kamahi.--A large tree; trunk 2-4 feet in diameter, and 50 feet high. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,132</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori name for New Zealand tree, Weinmannia racemosa, </i>Forst., <i>N.O. Saxifrageae.</i> i.q. <i>Kamahi</i> in south of South Island, and <i>Tawhero</i> in North Island (Wellington). <i>(1898,Austral English,476</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>W[einmannia] racemosa, Linn. ... Towai; Kamahi.</i> Very closely allied to <i>W. Sylvicola</i> [Tawhero]. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,139</i>)

>  <i>(Dec 1958,NZ Timber Journal,73/2</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Towai .. .. towai .. .. <i>Weinmannia </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>towai n.</i> New Zealand tree, the bark of which is used for tanning. Called also <i>tawhero</i> and <i>kamahi</i> [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>towai ... n</i>. N.Z. tree, the bark of which is used for tanning. Called also<i> tawhero</i> and <i>kamahi</i> [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>towai n</i>. large NZ timber tree related to kamahi. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,814</i>) [NOTES: [.nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Paratrophis</i> milk tree

>  <b>HOOKER</b> The `Milk-tree' of the Nelson and Wellington colonists is described by Mr. Bidwill and Dr. Sinclair as producing a milk which is used with tea, and is equally suitable for the purpose with that of the cow; it is also drunk as it flows from the tree, and is good and sweet, but has a vegetable after-taste. - A large tree, 60 feet high, ... often extremely like <i>Carpodetus Serratus.</i> ... Nat. name `Towai', <i>Raoul. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 224</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Towai, <i>Raoul. Epicarpurus microphylla. [=Paratrophis] </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  Epicarpurus microphyllus Towai <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [1890 cit. S.I. confusion with Westland and Christchurch name of <i>red birch</i> for kamahi wood and ornamental foliage]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... towai ... It is used for ship building and is called by Englishmen `black birch'. [check towai, ??tawhai] <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/95</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> [Canterbury] [Near Castle Hill] there is but one patch of forest, the dull, sad green of the Towai (black birch). [fn] <i>Weinmannia racemosa. </i> <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,78 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1890 Feb 28 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 78]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Towai, <i>Hector. Cordyline indivisa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [cf 43 Towa berries; description of wood fits <i>W. racemosa</i> and not TAWA; but does kamahi have oil-bearing berries]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The red pines are the <i>Totara, Towa, Mahi</i>, and <i>Rata</i>, all of which are close-grained, and moderately hard. They are used principally for interior work, in house-building, and for furniture-making. They are all of a red colour, and when oiled and polished are very ornamental. ... All these red firs make good spars for ships, and are frequently used by vessels visiting the settlements. The Towa spars are considered the best for this purpose. The fore-yard of the <i>Tory</i>, a 400 ton ship, was made from a green <i>towa</i> spar obtained in Queen Charlotte's Sound, which stood many severe tests without sustaining any injury. ... The only fault of this wood is its weight, but in that respect it is less objectionable than the teak which is used for the masts of all Indian-built vessels. [43] ... The <i>Towa</i> and the <i>Kraka</i> berries also yield an useful oil, but not in such quantity as that first mentioned. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),42</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> They had simply decorated their hair ... with the pretty yellow flower of the towai, which hung in heavy clusters from that tree on the banks of the stream ... From their ears were appended the dried feathered skin of the towakawaka-bird [sic : a's-acute; ?error for <i>tiwakawaka</i>], whose body, when living, is scarcely larger than a walnut. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/113</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The ladies had decorated their glossy ringlets with oil, and inserted the pretty flowers of the <i> kaikatoa</i>[a2-acute] and<i> towai</i> in their beautiful tresses, which hung down on their backs in profusion. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/82</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i></i>

### towel up
 
 <i>v.</i> [ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream [mistaken meaning at the time] I got towelled up proper in the boxing finals.]

### towelling
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a beating

>  I reckon we could give the Aussies a bloody good towelling - it's on the cards judging by the World Cup form shown so far. <i>(Nov 14, 2003,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,8</i>)

### towelling hat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Dad would put on an orange towelling hat. When he wore it, we knew that summer had really begun. <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### towha
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [towai](tot-tyr.md#towai))

### towie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Not only do towies witness death and mayhem; many speak of having to watch their own backs and are aware of other tow truck drivers who have been badly injured or killed... The era of the cowboy towie - cruising the roads in hotted up V8s, chasing and pouncing on a crash to hook, and fighting off other towies - has passed. <i>(Feb 6, 1999,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  If the general confusion worsens, and anarchy prevails, he said that towies would simply `sit back and let Rafferty's Rules take over'. <i>(Feb 28, 1983,Salient,3</i>)

>  The tow truckies say it's common for car owners to storm into the yard exploding with unrepeatable criticism of the qualities of the towies in particular, and life, the universe and everything in general. <i>(Apr 7, 1983,Dominion,7</i>)

>  There is a great deal of yahooing and complaining; obscenities fall as thickly as the rain and echo from truck to truck as the towies climb into their cabs and roar away. <i>(May 19, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  The towies cart away the pieces of the cars and the ambulances the pieces of the people. <i>(Sep 1986,Metro,70</i>)

>  Lastly, a huge thank you to police and St John Ambulance, our 'towie' and the wonderful service at Whakatane Hospital. <i>(Oct 5, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

>  Bruce Hopkins who, as the bewildered policeman in last year's <i>I'll Make You Happy</i> succeeded in making gormlessnesss endearing, does it again with Rhys the towie. <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  The problem is not the towies, it's the motorists who keep them in a job. <i>(Mar 6, 2002,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  [Hdg] SH1: A towie's perspective. <i>(Aug 2, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Towies work day and night in all weathers, cleaning up after motorway accidents, towing illegal parkers, pulling cars out of ditches and rivers, and patrolling the clearways to keep rush hour traffic flowing. <i>(Sep 18, 2000,Evening Post,TV Week : 5</i>)

>  There is also a cop.., doctors.., a towie.. and a neighbourhood curmudgeon. <i>(Jul 21, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  Just occasionally someone representing an unpopular cause or group, like.. the chairman of the towies federation will spend an hour putting their case and answering to the public directly. <i>(Feb 1993,Metro,62</i>)

>  The towies say they are the ones being penalised, <i>(Jan 17, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,1</i>)

>  Money hungry towies .. I called their number and I was told they had my car and that it would cost me $180 to retrieve it! <i>(Aug 8, 2007,Capital Times,4</i>)

### towitowi
 
 <i>n.</i> toetoe [sometimes towai, towai grass] (See [toetoe](ten-tot.md#toetoe))



 
 <i>n.</i> toetoe [towi towi, toti toti, towai, towai grass]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),92</i>) [NOTES: [towai grass: see TOE GRASS]]

>  Islands now began to appear, generally covered with flax and <i>towi towi</i>, a species of reed with long drooping leaves. (v) ?erroneous forms, misprints, mistransliterations <i>(1847,Oamaru Mail</i>)

>  Islands now began to appear, generally covered with flax and <i>towi towi</i>, a species of reed with long drooping leaves. (v) ?erroneous forms, misprints, mistransliterations <i>(1959,Early Travellers,128</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes for Journal in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 128]

>  <b>MOORE</b> You can get the longest stalk of Toti Toti [sic] and strip the feathers off. <i>(1937,Reminiscences</i>)

>  We now ascended the vale, whose bed was well-furnished with several varieties of grass, and clumps of towi-towi, and would form a good cattle-run. <i>(Nov 13, 1847,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

### towmotor
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... yellow fork-lift trucks, towmotors they call them, with sides striated from squeezing up the narrow alleys, racing about ... pushing and lifting and squeezing the cargo into position and puffing hot fumes from their exhausts. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,22</i>)

### town
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a Maori village

>  <b>HOVELL</b> ``At 10 a.m. sent a party of men on shore to destroy the town and the canoes. At noon the people returned with two pigs, having destroyed the principal part of the town and canoes with fire. At 2 p.m. the people went on shore again and set fire to the remainder part of the town, canoes, huts, and all their food, and returned with one large pig and two small ones. <i>(Aug 31, 1815,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: 1815 Aug 31 Captain Hovell quoted in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 89]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> The next day (24th) we landed the horses and cattle, and fixed upon a place for the present residence of the settlers, and began to clear away the rubbish and prepare for erecting the houses for their reception on a piece of ground adjoining to the native town pitched upon by Duaterra and the chiefs of the place. <i>(1814,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,91,1932 ed.</i>)

### town acre
 
 <i>n.</i> an allotment of land in a prospective town to a New Zealand Company settler. [AND 1838]; the acre-section of town land allocated by the NZ Company for every twenty acres of uncleared `suburban' land purchased [see also town section] (See [cf. country acre](con-cyp.md#country-acre))

>  Each "land purchaser" was entitled to a "town" acre, a 50-acre section of "suburban" land, and a 150-acre "rural" section. <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,143</i>) [NOTES: Pagination from 1988 edition]

>  `These doubly-selected lands will be divided into 1100 sections, each section comprising one town-acre, and 100 country-acres. 110 sections will be reserved by the Company, ... <i>(1852,GBPP,1852, xxv (570) /19</i>) [NOTES: 1839 June 1 Terms of Sale of the New Zealand Land Company; Great Britain, Parliamentary Papers, 1852, xxxv. 570, p.18 in 1971 Speeches and Documents. 19]

>  <b>1/154</b> (et pass.) Extremely well fitted for a system of jobbing in town acres. <i>(1840,NZ Journal</i>)

>  The 27th had been the day appointed for the holders of land [in Wellington] under the Company to choose their Town Acres and during the previous week the Plan had been exhibited, ... <i>(Jul 27, 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,231,1966 ed.</i>)

>  We are going to select the town acres. <i>(1840,Letter,52</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Revans to Chapman (MS) 52]

>  I will buy for you a town acre. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/32/94</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> ... we were accompanied by two gentlemen ... who had not hitherto seen some town acres belonging to absentees, for which they were agents. <i>(1843,Twelve Months in Wellington,10</i>)

>  <b>PO</b> ... we find [the New Zealand Company] planting rival settlements one after the other ..., without reference to the wants or wishes of the colonists, but with the goood commercial purpose of getting the highest possible price for the land, by the sale of those delusive baits, <i>`town-acres'. </i> <i>(1848,Sketches in NZ,198,1849 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> ... we find [the New Zealand Company] planting rival settlements one after the other ..., without reference to the wants or wishes of the colonists, but with the goood commercial purpose of getting the highest possible price for the land, by the sale of those delusive baits, <i>`town-acres'. </i> <i>(1848,Sketches in NZ,198,1849 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> cf Town-section [In Wellington] ... transfers [have] been effected ..., in quantities varying from ten or twelve sections to the smallest subdivision of a valuable town acre. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,175</i>)

>  <b>DILLON</b> Of this, one town acre [in Nelson] has a house and garden rented by Major Richmond ... <i>(Dec 29, 1850,Dillon Letters,141,1954 ed.</i>)

>  (Canterbury) `The immediate choosing of the town acre sections has been a most important and useful measure.' Section has remained the normal New Zealand word for a city building plot and tutu, usually pronounced and sometimes spelt `toot', is a poisonous shrub. <i>(Jan 11, 1851,Lyttelton Times</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Jan 11 Lyttelton Times in 1966 Turner 165]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> He is jealous of Clifford and the Squatters - is addicted to Landscrip, Town Acres and Rural Sections, and therefore I can see evidently has a leaning in favor [sic] of cheap land. ... <i>(1853,Journal,1/188,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> Because most of his men [of the Number 1 Company of the Fourth Waikato Militia Regiment] received their town acre grants in what is now the suburb of Whitiora, that locality was once called Number One . <i>(1964,Settlers in Depression,6</i>)

>  Messrs. J. H. Bethune & Co., land and estate agents, report that, acting in conjunction with Mr. Thomas Ward, they have negotiated the sale by private contract of the splendid freehold property, containing two acres, one rood, one perch (town acre No. 449, and parts of town acres Nos. 448 and 447) owned by the late Captain Chas. Sharp. <i>(Apr 16, 1898,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  AUCTION SALE. 3 TOWN ACRES. <i>(Jan 30, 1924,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  TARANAKI STREET, WELLINGTON. Town Acres 243 and 244. <i>(Jun 25, 1935,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  Each order represented one town acre and a hundred acres of country lands which they were to select on arrival, and this selection became the first important business of the new colony. <i>(Jan 5, 1940,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Francis Joseph Wardell's land grants of a Town Acre in Cambridge and 200 acres of swamp in Whangamarino were useless as, when the war ended, there was a recession with no money or jobs. <i>(2009,NZ Memories,77/15</i>)

>  With respect to the town acres, however, it seems indispensable that the whole should be surveyed and mapped before any choice is allowed, and that the allotment of the whole should place at one time. <i>(Aug 21, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  There is a conveniently arranged Six-roomed Dwelling House on Town Acre No. 498, in excellent state of repair, with Outhouses, Garden, &c, &c. <i>(Apr 13, 1867,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  ELIGIBLE CORNER BUILDING SITE, having a frontage to Adelaide-road of 33ft 8in, and a frontage to Alfred-street of 90ft 10in, containing an area of 11.20 perches, being part Town Acre No. 741. <i>(Mar 5, 1906,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The following is a complete list of the properties disposed of by Messrs. J. H. Bethune and Co., at their sale yesterday .. lot 3, three town acres, Nos. 360, 361, and 362, Austin-street, GBP2400, Mr. J. M. Taylor ... <i>(Jun 6, 1878,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  FREEHOLD TOWN ACRE No. 29 on the plan of the City, and fronting EPUNI-STREET, Polhill's Gully. <i>(Sep 17, 1883,Evening Post,3</i>)

### town acre man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person who owned land in town under the town acre system and did not farm his allotted rural land seriously.

>  <b>HASTIE</b> One particular settler owned a lot of horses and had very little land to support them. He called this gentleman one of the 'loafing society' and the 'town acre man'. <i>(1864,BLANK ENTRY,5</i>) [NOTES: in Hastie (1986) Hastie History]

### town allotment
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  On the expiration of three years ..., each settler ... will be entitled to a Crown Grant of the town allotment, and farm section allotted to him, .. <i>(1864,NZ Gazette</i>) [NOTES: c1863-64 NZ Gazette in 1879 Featon Waikato War 52 (15).]

>  PIAKO SECTIONS, TOWN ALLOTMENTS. <i>(May 27, 1914,Thames Star,4</i>)

>  Call and inspect our list of other farms. Houses and town allotments. <i>(Mar 15, 1920,Ohinemuri Gazette,3</i>)

>  Mr. G. will explain his views more fully in a subsequent advertisement; meantime he will be happy to receive offers either to lease or sell him a town allotment. <i>(May 9, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  TO LET OR SELL, TWO TOWN ALLOTMENTS, situated in Princes Street, and one ALLOTMENT on the Road leading to the Half-Way Bush. <i>(Oct 18, 1851,Otago Witness,1</i>)
