### town belt
 
 <i>n.</i> OED : N.Z., a belt of public land reserved chiefly for recreational purposes in or round a town; a belt of reserve land around or in a town or city, vested in the town authority and reserved for public, scenic or recreational purposes; also THE BELT

>  This is a victory for conservation and the Friends of the Town Belt, who have lobbied hard to protect the town belt. <i>(Dec 7, 2000,City Voice,12</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> This town-belt, as it is called, is pretty much in a state of nature. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,23</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> `Does she live near the Town Belt?' `Yes.' `Why, then, ... My people live within cooee - quite near to her.' <i>(1881,White Hood and Blue Cap,59</i>)

>  Our common Tutu (<i>Coriaria ruscifolia</i>) is familiar to everyone who rambles on our Town Belt. <i>(Mar 1881,NZ Journal of Science,371</i>)

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> The native bush which covers a large portion of the [Dunedin] `town belt' is in itself a heritage to be highly prized, ... <i>(1889,Stories of NZ Life,61</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... the Town Belt <i>(1890,Picturesque Dunedin,16</i>)

>  The whole is admirably set off by Hagley Park (400 acres in extent), the Domain and Botanical Gardens (79 acres), Lancaster Park, the Town Belts, and other public and private gardens and plantations. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,55</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The Town Belt of Dunedin consists of a remnant of such a forest; ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,47</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The best houses [in Dunedin] stood up on the hills, set back in the dark native green of the Town Belt; ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,40</i>)

>  <b>VOM HAAST</b> On May 13 [1867], the City Council received a report from a committee on planting, recommending planting the Belts in the following manner. A footpath, 10 ft. wide on each side of the Belt, two roadways running parallel to the footpaths, each half a chain in width, leaving 46 ft. clear in the middle of the Belt, to be planted with trees, so as to allow a 10 ft. grass-walk down the centre of each block between the intersecting streets. Each block was to be planted with one variety of tree. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,490</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Ellen's house was up on Maori Hill and every night I took her out I used to see her safely home, ... and come down through the Town Belt ... <i>(1959,No Remittance,84</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Lying awake on a bench in the town belt,/ Alone,. . . <i>(1979,Collected Poems,256</i>) [NOTES: (1961-62)]

>  <b>ARMFELT</b> The [Dunedin] house was large ..., and the situation - just above the Town Belt - beautiful. <i>(1971,Catching Up,243</i>)

>  ... he was off this high summer evening along the road that led to the Town Belt - that was to say a back-to-nature acre or so with a name that derived from many decades previously, when the city fathers as they used to be called had wisely decided that not every square foot of the territory they administered should be privately owned, either to be lived on or for commercial purposes. <i>(1987,Islands,7/92</i>) [NOTES: 1974 Sargeson (Brixton) in Islands 7/92]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... he was off this high summer evening along the road that led to the Town belt. <i>(1976,Sunset Village,30</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [Karori] district begins immediately outside the boundary of the Town-belt, ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,97</i>)

>  We afterwards went on to the Town Belt and Riccarton. <i>(Jan 3, 1851,Journal of Edward Ward,98,1951 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> What, cut up the Town Belt which we had engaged and were legally bound to keep open? <i>(1853,Journal,1/256,1980 ed.</i>)

>  Extension to house Cook Island nest [heading] A hall is to be extended on Wellington's town belt land so a Cook Islands language nest can be established. <i>(Oct 7, 1988,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Took a house for the Pattissons. South town belt. ... <i>9th.</i> Returned to Ch.Ch. cf OEDS <i>Green Belt</i> [Belt sup. n1 5a] An officially designated belt of open coutryside in which all development is severely restricted, usually enclosing a built-up area and designed to check its further growth. 1932-1961 <i>(Sep 8, 1877,Chudleigh Diary,262,1950 ed.</i>)

>  [Hdg] Town belt saved by house. Part of Wellington's town belt - where some Wellington Hospital buildings stand on reserve land - are now safe, following a decision of parliament this week... "This ia a victory for conservation and the Friends of the Town Belt, who have lobbied hard to protect the town belt,"... <i>(Dec 7, 2000,City Voice,12</i>)

>  The Town Belt was originally 1500 acres in extent. It is now only about 1100 acres (430 hectares). But this is land that was supposed to be "<i>reserved for the enjoyment of the public and not to be built upon</i>"... A need arose, an itch had to be scratched, and the Town Belt was the best/cheapest/least politically-damaging option available. <i>(Jan 7, 2001,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  The Town Belt is so fundamental to our vision of Wellington and our feelings for it that there is a tendency for us to take it for granted. <i>(Jan 24, 2001,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  Two blocks on the North town belt fenced with wire, trenched, and planted; and the whole of the plantations on the East and North town belts kept in order. <i>(Dec 14, 1869,Star, The,2</i>)

>  A recommendation by the reserves committee that a request that a proposed children's play area on the Town Belt at Owen Street be leased for the formation of bowling greens be not approved, but that the applicants be offered a nearby area at present used by garden plot holders, was referred back to the committee by the City Council last night at the request of the chairman of the committee, Councillor R. L. Macalister. <i>(Jun 14, 1945,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Most Wellingtonians would be surprised at the history of the city's Town Belt, but the mystery will be unravelled at an exhibition opening at the Museum of Wellington City and Sea tomorrow... The original Town Belt Deed of 1873, which transferred the Town Belt in trust to the people of Wellington from the Provincial Government and is still an active document, will be on display. <i>(Jun 25, 2001,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  "It is indeed desirable that the whole of the town inland should be separated from the country sections by a broad belt of land which you will declare that the Company intends to be public property on the condition that no buildings ever be built on it." New Zealand Company Secretary John Ward to Surveyor General William Mein Smith, August 1839. And so the idea of Wellington's fantastic Town Belt was born... The 425 ha Town Belt, including the beloved Botanic Garden - is indeed public property - and it's admniistered by the City Council on behalf of Wellingtonians. <i>(Aug 2001,Absolutely Positively Wellington,1</i>)

>  [The Crown] acquired 1,500 acres of the Wellington town belt as well as Somes and Ward Island. <i>(Jun 2003,Wel-com (Wellington),13</i>)

>  2 Allotments of Town Acre No. 776, adjoining Sellars' Dairy, commanding a run of the Town Belt. <i>(May 12, 1843,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,2</i>)

>  A comprehensive view of Wallace Street, (Town Belt area), in the vicinity of which three women were attacked by a manaic [sic] during previous assaults. <i>(May 2, 1929,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  [Hdg] 'Town Belt' land ours - Capital Coast Health. Capital Coast health has conceded that exclusive real estate it plans to sell in Kelburn was originally part of the Town Belt but says it is now the legal owner... He said the Government took ownership of the land when it assumed control of the New Zealand Company's assets, and the properties were not part of that finally vested to Wellington City Council as Town Belt in the 1870s. <i>(May 2, 2001,Dominion,16</i>)

>  ISBISTER - May 22, at Fulham, South Town belt, the wife of J. J. Isbister, of a daughter. <i>(May 25, 1893,Star, The,2</i>)

>  DEATH. GLEESON. - On July 16th, at her residence Town Belt, LOUISE, beloved wife of Charles Gleeson, Native of Okarito. <i>(Jul 17, 1915,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  The proposed tramway was intended to be constructed on portions of the Town Belt which were vested in the corporation. <i>(Sep 16, 1924,Otago Witness,60</i>)

### town bike
 
 <i>n.</i> the local (small-town) prostitute; one who is free with sexual favours (sc. everybody has a ride) [ca1938]

>  <b>GEE</b> I was having my first real affair with a girl of fifteen called Melva Butter. Charlie said she was the town bike (he was sour at missing his turn) but she had her own sort of morality: ... she took on only one boy at a time. <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,55</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>town bike:</i> This is teenage slang for the most ridden members of their female compatriots. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,105</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> ... Neddy's girlfriend in Te Tarehi wasn't worth it. He said he'd met someone who knew her: she was flat-chested and the town bike. <i>(1982,Master of Big Jingles,112</i>)

>  Ellen was the town bike. <i>(Apr 1992,Charivari (Auckland),30</i>)

### town bike of politics
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Politics) Peter Dunne of the United Future Party, who is willing to join with any party under MMP.

>  Dunne is the town bike of politics. He's available to anyone who will have him ... <i>(Sep 19, 2008,Dominion Post,B5</i>) [NOTES: Bob Jones column]

### town board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THATCHER</b> And the booth they visited for prog,/ And sometimes took a nip of grog;/ There was Grieve, one of your famed Town Board,/ McIvor too his orders roared/ <i>(1864,Invercargill Minstrel,6</i>) [NOTES: (The Caledonian Gathering)]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> He worked for the town board and in the dry weather he'd have to drive about the streets on the water-cart. The butcher would say no, it was the fair dinkum truth. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,20,1945 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1945 Sargeson in 1977 Listener Short Stories 20]

>  <b>GEE</b> I was having my first real affair with a girl of fifteen called Melva Butter. Charlie said she was the town bike (he was sour at missing his turn) but she had her own sort of morality: ... she took on only one boy at a time. <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,55</i>)

>  It was decided at the meeting of the Johnsonville Town Board last evening to ask the Wellington City Council whether it would consider the amalgamation of Johnsonville with the city, and on what basis. <i>(Jun 15, 1937,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Manaia and Opunake Town Boards wrote regarding the reduction of publicans' licenses. <i>(Jul 22, 1909,Hawera & Normanby Star,6</i>)

>  NOTICE is hereby given that the Balance-Sheet for the year ending 31st March, 1915, is now open to inspection at the Town Board Offices, Upper Hutt. <i>(Jun 8, 1915,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The Council met on the 3rd inst., when the following Bills were read a first time:- Excess of Expenditure Bill - Dunedin Town Town Board Bill - Immigration Appropriation Debentures Bill - Dunedin Roads and Streets Bill. <i>(Jan 6, 1855,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  The demand for a new school for Cobden is being endorsed by the Cobden Town Board. <i>(Dec 24, 1920,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  TOWN BOARD OF NEW PLYMOUTH ENDOWMENT ORDINANCE, 1871. <i>(Mar 4, 1871,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  A letter was received from the Clark to the Gore Town Board, stating that Mr Driver, M.H.R., was about to introduce a bill into Parliament to amend the Town Districts Bill. <i>(Jul 1, 1882,Otago Witness,21</i>)

>  From the chairman Stratford Town Board inviting co-operation in obtaining similar powers for Town Boards as exists for Road Boards in the matter of bank overdrafts. - Approved. <i>(Aug 14, 1896,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

### town day
 
 <i>n.</i> the day that farmers travel to town for supplies, usually on a stock sale day.

>  <b>TURNER</b> [In winter] a farmer does not have to be mindful of <i>cow-time </i>or milking time on<i> town day</i> (or <i>sale day</i>, equivalent to English `market day'). In winter, one or two<i> house cows </i>might be milked by <i>hand. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,150</i>)

### town district
 
 <i>n.</i> [variously a specific classification with ref to local government or more general description. Note Town Districts Bill &/or Town District bill, early 1900's, but term used before that.]

>  Not a great deal of change has occurred in the population of Springs and Ellesmere counties and the Leeston and Southbridge town districts during the 10 years since the 1926 census. <i>(Apr 21, 1936,Ellesmere Guardian,4</i>)

>  Leeston town district: 609; last census 566; increase 43. <i>(Oct 23, 1945,Ellesmere Guardian,3</i>)

### town house
 
 <i>n.</i> one of a group of similar houses especially but not essentially in the central part of town (gen. Engl. sense a house in town) [cf British use and Canterbury use eg of Runholder Charles H.Greenstreet He had a town house in the corner of Mays[???] Rd. Papanui.]

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> Mr Silva [the owner] went back to his town house and ... there were only Charlie and his wife and I on the station. <i>(1959,Stone, The,50</i>) [NOTES: (The Stone)]

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> So a moving van was eventually sent from ... Dick's townhouse ... <i>(1977,Of Ivory Accents,8</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> She's moved into one of those town houses next door. <i>(1980,Murder or Three, A,79</i>)

>  <b>BAUER</b> ... in New Zealand a <i>town house</i> is a semi-detached house with a very small garden, and not just any house in a town - this can be contrasted with the traditional British English sense of <i>town house</i> with its implications of secondary residence. <i>(1983,English Word-formation,58</i>)

>  ... the things just below [the crane] look good too: the toy-town city, with its shops and vehicles, the flash townhouses by the beach, and especially the big ... hotel right next door. <i>(Jan 1987,Metro,126</i>)

>  LOWER HUTT: Central. $165,000. 3 br townhouse to be built of low maint materials on sunny section. <i>(Jan 7, 1987,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  <i>Town house</i> is in, and uneasily acceptable as a `residence in town esp. of person with house in the country' although `in the country' could be better phrased; but `house in (esp. terraced) group in town' is not apt, and increasingly the style for this type of dwelling is <i>townhouse.</i> That is, `My town house is a townhouse', correctly distinguishes, on the page, and will be distinguished in speech; tow stresses for the one, and one stress (TOWNhouse) for the other. There is as well a question of nuance to be noticed. A <i>townhouse</i> is emphatically upmarket from a <i>home unit.</i> <i>(1987,Te Reo,30/132</i>) [NOTES: Smithyman Review NZPOD]

>  *Modern 3 bedroomed Townhouse in Papakowhai, available from the beginning of October - short or long term. <i>(Sep 16, 1988,Victoria Univ. Weekly Staff Circular,902/5</i>)

>  L. Bauer noted in April in UK town-house being used in a sense similar to the usual NZ sense of compact executive homes on very small sections <i>(1988,Personal Communication</i>)

>  This in the context of a <i>Metro</i> piece that reverently treats his own decision to replace the family home with a block of townhouses as a shining example of the new entrepreneurial spirit. <i>(Jun 5, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### town lot
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  The memorial [from Mr.Sinclair, Wellington] states that certain town lots ... had been advertised for sale by auction, but that several allotments, comprising some of the most valuable lands in the township, ... had been reserved from such sale ...; that these sections were to be paid for at the average price of half the town sections ... <i>(Sep 10, 1841,GBPP,(No.569) 130</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Sep 10 Villiers and Lefevre : Colonial Land and Emigration Office in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 130]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> It was at a very early stage of the existence of the embryo capital, when we were all squatters, and the survey lines of the town were only half cut, and when we were all helping each other to do nothing until the first sale of town lots should come off, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk4/12/342</i>)

>  That, with the foregoing exceptions, no lands be reserved as sites of towns, and that in all cases in which such reserves may have been already made, the lands comprised in them shall be sold by auction to the highest bigger, should any portion of them have been disposed of as town allotments, but, if none have been so disposed of, shall cease to be reserved as town lots, and shall be open to sale at the general uniform price. <i>(May 1, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  Sale of Paeroa town lots at Paeroa. <i>(Aug 27, 1909,Ohinemuri Gazette,2</i>)

>  Auction sale of town lots at Paeroa. <i>(Nov 5, 1913,Ohinemuri Gazette,2</i>)

>  WANTED, a Conveyancer to convey town lot at Waikanae. <i>(Oct 10, 1928,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Until the town lots were surveyed, he lived on an island in the harbour, Brown's Island, and kept pigs there in readiness for selling to the townspeople once the town site was decided upon. <i>(May 14, 1935,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Thirteen town lots, four suburban, and two country, were purchased, in some instances, at an higher than the upset price. <i>(Jan 26, 1850,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  Saturday's sale brought forward 70 entries, principally made up of dealers' and town lots. <i>(May 1, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  Te Ngakau has been going round the Waikato townships examining what allotments belong to the Government, in view of Sir G. Grey's proposals to give town lots to the Kingites. <i>(Jun 14, 1878,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  "No town lots have been offered on Doubtless Bay; no town or village lots have been offered at GBP2 per acre in that vicinity, nor have any town lots whatever been sold for GBP2 an acre continuous to any town lot bringing GBP30 an acre." <i>(Jun 24, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### town Maori
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BROWNING</b> `Blay stuck-up bish, blay pakeha ... Her'n that other one, o' there. Blay town Maori things she's good, eh?' <i>(1976,Watch Your Language,28</i>)

### town milk
 
 <i>n.</i> [milk produced or delivered in town, cf town milk produced in the country for 'town supply'.]

>  In New Zealand about 85 per cent. of all town milk sold in urban areas is pasteurised. ... Too much 'billy' milk is still sold. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,306</i>)

>  Town milk supply is.. a question of Quotas. <i>(Aug 1959,NZ Dairy Exporter,41</i>)

>  [Hdg] Price-Determining Formula for Town Milk <i>(Sep 1959,NZ Dairy Exporter,19</i>)

>  WANTED, a Man capable of conducting a Town Milk Business. <i>(Jul 2, 1878,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Our Borough Council is in favour of Government inspection of the sources of the town milk supply. <i>(Apr 13, 1899,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Ideal place for retired farmer, contractor, carrier or small town milk run. <i>(Jun 20, 1922,Otautau Standard,4</i>)

>  The country milk is a richer and more creamy fluid than the town milk, and we may fairly estimate that town buyers get for their shilling the equivalent of fourpennyworth of country milk. <i>(Sep 6, 1906,Poverty Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  This shows that town milk is much stronger on the water, and has far less butter and milk sugar than that given by a country cow. <i>(Mar 16, 1881,North Otago Times,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) (in attrib. use) [of farms, farmers and herds] providing milk all year round for town consumption. [aka townmilk, town-milk]

>  The majority of town milk suppliers do not breed their own replacements, but rely on buying close-up cows at dispersal sales. <i>(Oct 9, 1952,NZ Farmer,22</i>)

>  As might be expected, there's a very ready demand in the Christchurch town milk herds for the Macartney culls. <i>(Mar 1, 1955,NZ Dairy Exporter,6</i>)

>  Townmilk farmers on the plain generally consider it's easier to produce milk in winter than it is in summer in our climate. <i>(Jan 1977,NZ Dairy Exporter,2</i>)

>  The town-milk industry is composed of three fairly clearly defined sections, production, treatment, and distribution, all of which seek to serve the interests of the housewife. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,303</i>)

>  This would bring the average price paid to cheese suppliers for the 1934-35 season to 10.6953d per lb butter-fat, excluding town milk sales. <i>(Jan 23, 1936,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  A request for an increase in the price of milk for the 1945-46 season was placed before the Minister of Marketing (Mr. Roberts) and the Minister of Health (Mr. Nordmeyer) by a deputation from the Town Milk Producers' Federation. <i>(Jun 11, 1945,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Assets. - Freehold and leasehold premises and plant GBP11,501 16s 9d, additions to buildings GBP178 2s 6d, additions to plant GBP195 6s 3d, paid on Newstead property GBP169 1s 11d, town milk supply, capital (GBP642 7s 9d, less depreciation GBP30) GBP612 7s 9d, stocks on hand GBP389 4s 1d .. total GBP16,843 13s. <i>(Apr 15, 1915,Poverty Bay Herald,7</i>)

### town quarter-acre
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [`Terms of Purchase of Land in the Settlement of Otago' agreed on by the `Otago Association' and the Company] 16 In exchange for each sum of 120l. 10s. ... each Purchaser to receive ... three separate Land-Orders, namely, for the Town quarter-acre, the ten acres of Suburban Land, and the fifty acres of Rural Land, respectively. These lands to be severally selected according to priority of choice, to be determined by Ballot. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,317</i>)

### Town Ranger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Letter from Mr. Baker Polhill, soliciting the appointment of Town Ranger of Public Reserves ... <i>(Oct 18, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,2</i>)

### town reserve
 
 <i>n.</i> land set aside as the site of a town [1839[??=NZ paper town]]]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> A large space has been set apart as what is called a Town Reserve near Lyttelton. <i>(1853,Journal,1/139,1980 ed.</i>)

>  In 1900, after a long search, the Council obtained statutory authority to acquire a site of six acres in Salamanca road, part of a town reserve made available by arrangement with the City Council and the Board of Governors of Wellington College. <i>(Apr 9, 1924,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  For summer bedding in the gardens and town reserves 63,000 plants have been tended in the last year, and for the spring beds which, in November, will have run their span, there were 5000 tulips, 3000 hyacinths, 5000 ranunculi, and 5000 anemones. <i>(Sep 11, 1940,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  SUGGESTIONS FOR RESOURCES EQUAL TO DEFICIT FROM LOSS OF SUBSIDY.- 1st. The Government to compensate us for loss of subsidy by granting the licenses as annexed: Public houses L150; liquor, L100; .. 2nd. To relieve the Corporation of their debt incurred under the impression that the subsidy would be continued. This, with the Crown Grants for Oamaru Town Reserves, would enable the Coproration to contunue such works and improvements as were needed. <i>(Feb 23, 1872,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  The Town Council asked the Minister of Justice to authorise prison labor to be employed free of charge to carry out certain municipal works, and in this way compensate the Corporation for loss sustained through the Railway Department having taken one of the town reserves, the effect of which had been to render the public baths useless. <i>(Sep 21, 1888,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  Without in any way disparaging Victoria Park, it cannot for a moment be contended that that park will ever be anything like so popular as the town reserve, which, from its proximity to town and natural advantages, must ever claim pride of position. <i>(Jun 2, 1896,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  The City Council have decided to erect a building immediately, on a town reserve, which could be used as a hospital in the event of the plague visiting the city. <i>(Apr 19, 1900,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  TREE-PLANTING ON TOWN RESERVES. <i>(Apr 25, 1914,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Only a small portion of this road requires to be made, and that is chiefly before the country sections are reached. The first part that must be made practicable is within the town, and is the termination of the Tinakori-road; the other part passes through the town reserve. <i>(Oct 13, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  ALL persons occupying Town Land under Business Licenses are reminded that the land included within the Town Reserve of Hokitika will shortly be withdrawn from the goldfields, and the sections either sold to the present occupiers, or put up to public auction. <i>(Feb 15, 1866,West Coast Times,4</i>)

### town section
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a section of land (often an acre) within a planned town [See also town acre; rural section]

>  <b>GIFKIN</b> The name 'the Quarter Acres' for an area of town sections allotted to Pakeha after the battles of Gate Pa and Te Ranga (1864) was commonly used. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,130</i>) [NOTES: Article by Meikle]

>  In June 1839, the New Zealand Land Company, issued proposals for the sale of nine-tenths of a township of 110,000 acres, in lots of 101 acres, each comprising 100 acres of country land and one town section. <i>(Sep 24, 1840,GBPP,1841 (No.311) 123</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Sep 24 Port Nicholson Deputies to Gipps in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 123]

>  By the Government advertisement for the sale of town lands at Auckland, it was stated that sections ... would be exposed to sale by auction, but we have since learned that several allotments, comprising some of the most valuable lands in the township, have been reserved from such sale ...; and that these sections are to be paid for, not according to the price ... of the less valuable lots in their vicinity ... but at the average price of half the town sections ... <i>(Feb 10, 1841,GBPP,1842 (No.569) 131</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Feb 10 D.Sinclair and others, Wellington to Sir George Gipps in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 131]

>  The memorial [from Mr.Sinclair, Wellington] states that certain town lots ... had been advertised for sale by auction, but that several allotments, comprising some of the most valuable lands in the township, ... had been reserved from such sale ...; that these sections were to be paid for at the average price of half the town sections ... <i>(Sep 10, 1841,GBPP,1842 (No.569) 130</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Sep 10 Villiers and Lefevre : Colonial Land and Emigration Office in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 130]

>  We have had a selection of town sections of land, by the different proprietors and there agents, and they are now being leased - I mean the sections of absentee proprietors, and such others as have them to dispose of - and this at high remuneration. The town section measures a quarter of an acre, and costs in London 10s; ... <i>(May 2, 1848,Letters from Otago,10,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [spg town-section] cf Town-acre One Town-section [one acre] was attached to each rural section [100 acres]. <i>Ibid.</i> 284 Two hundred and fifty Town-sections [at New Plymouth], with their orders of choice ..., each with a Rural section of 50 acres attached ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,175</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> He wants to take some of the Church Lands at Kaiapoi, a rural section and a Town Section. <i>(1853,Journal,1/336,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> The 21st of April [1848] was fixed for the formal selection of their own town sections by the land purchasers, ... Mr.David Garrick ... was the first private individual to select in Dunedin, and ... he chose what is now probably the most valuable site - that quarter acre upon which now stands the Bank of New Zealand ... Upon this he erected his frame house brought out on the <i>John Wicliffe, ... </i> <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,99</i>)

>  The immediate choosing of a town acre section has been a most important and useful measure. <i>(Jan 11, 1851,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  APPLICATION having been made to me for the issue of a New Certificate of Title in the name of OWEN BECKWITH SNELLING, of Kaukapakapa, Farmer, for 9 acres and 3 roods, more or less, being Town Section 101 and Suburban Sections 23 and 26 .. I hereby give notice that I will issue the New Certificate of Title as requested after fourteen days from the date of the "Gazette" containing this notice. <i>(May 1, 1930,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  ALL THAT piece of land situated in the City of Wellington containing Ten and Two-Tenths Perches (10.2P.) being the same a little more of less being part of Town Section 967 <i>(Dec 19, 1945,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  The following town sections were sold by Mr Osborne, on behalf of the Government, at the Waste Lands Office on Wednesday. <i>(Apr 3, 1874,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  The long looked for sale of the Paeroa town sections took place in the Criterion Theatre yesterday. <i>(Dec 4, 1908,Ohinemuri Gazette,2</i>)

>  TO BUILDERS, WHARFINGERS, AND OTHER CAPITALISTS PROCEEDING TO NEW ZEALAND. TO BE SOLD or LET on Building Leases a Town and Country Section, being the second in the order of choice in the first British settlement in New Zealand. <i>(Aug 21, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,1</i>)

>  Any one having a town section of land (an early choice) to dispose of, will meet with a purchaser, by applying to F. Bradey, on the Beach. <i>(Apr 18, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  There was not much demand for Manaia town sections, but suburban were keenly competed for, and brought a good deal more than the upset price. <i>(Feb 26, 1881,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  Messrs Friedlander Bros. and D. Thomas sold to-day by auction 29 sections of Cameron's Springfield estate, a town section realising GBP20 per acre. <i>(Apr 23, 1902,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  MORRISON, SCLANDERS, AND CO. have for SALE - Part TOWN SECTIONS 437, 439, and 442, with frontage to Hardy-street, Collingwood-street, Selwyn-place, Morrison-street, and Hope-street. <i>(Sep 28, 1867,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,1</i>)

>  This demand for big rents is based on the mad speculation which has been allowed to go on in town sections, always forcing the prices up and consequently the rents. <i>(Nov 27, 1918,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  THE undermentioned Town Sections will be sold by auction, at the Land Office, Invercargill, at noon, on Monday, the 31st day of July current. <i>(Jul 22, 1876,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  TENDERS FOR PURCHASE OF HOTEL AND TOWN SECTIONS. <i>(Nov 29, 1920,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

### town supplier
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who provides milk all-year-round for local consumption.

>  While the farming methods do not differ greatly from those adopted on an ordinary dairy farm, the town supplier is confronted with special problems incidental to an all-the-year-round supply of milk. <i>(Aug 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,157</i>)

### town supply
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the provision of fresh whole milk by farms to local milk treatment stations [requiring a proportion of autumn-calvers for milking to continue in the winter months] or the farm that provides such milk. [aka town-supply]

>  But between the prices given at a bush creamery and for milk sold for town supply there is a wide margin. <i>(Jul 1899,NZ Farmer,238</i>)

>  Handy suburban farm. Milking for town supply. <i>(Nov 1, 1920,Hawera Star,03</i>)

>  This..is the opinion of a well-known farmer who has had over 20 years' experience in catering for town supply on his farm at Albany about 10 miles north of Auckland. <i>(Aug 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,155</i>)

>  That town supply has in the main proved profitable over a period of years is well demonstrated by the fact that in addition to providing a good living for the occupier ... <i>(Aug 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,159</i>)

>  A herringbone milking shed without walls and with a round - or partly round - yard has been built at Te Kuiti by Mr John Ormsby for his town supply herd. <i>(Sep 4, 1961,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

>  ... Mr Steve Algie, a town supply dairyman on the low-lying Taieri Plain, has provided a partly-covered loafing yard to keep his milkers off the pastures ... <i>(Sep 28, 1961,NZ Farmer,46</i>)

>  A 90-acre town supply dairy farm with 35 cows ... [town supply herd, town supply farm, town supply farmer] <i>(Aug 2002,Growing Today,7</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> A single bird spent the cold winter of 1963, ... near Christchurch alternating between two herds of Friesian-Holsteins `on town supply'. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,79</i>)

>  A producer stated that long before butter rationing came into force producers were regarding the town supply with disfavour, and the latest restriction, on cream selling was the last straw. <i>(Nov 15, 1943,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  The milk was sold to a retailer for the town supply. <i>(Mar 29, 1926,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Such things may have taken place before inspection of our town supply; but for such an insinuation to be levelled at our milk vendors I consider is unjust and uncalled for. <i>(Apr 29, 1905,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

### town-supply
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of a farm) devoted to the production of milk for town drinking as opposed to butter and cheese production [town supply] (See [town milk](tot-tyr.md#town-milk))

>  [caption] Tanker collecting milk from a town-supply farm at Te Awamutu... <i>(1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,93</i>) [NOTES: A town-supply farmer undertakes to supply a fixed quota of milk all year round.]

>  [Recolonisation] turned New Zealand into "a town-supply district of London" while "London became the cultural capital of New Zealand." <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,12</i>)

### towner
 
 <i>n.</i> a rare and obs variant of townie

>  ... the `towners' piled up a score of 13 points. <i>(Jun 2, 1906,NZ Truth,50/2/8</i>)

### towneyite
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> Or when we were playing around having a fight down the back, someone would say, `Go away, nigger.' ... Then there'd be a fight. Same thing when the towneyites - the kids from up Kaikoura - came down here. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,98 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Marama, Kaikoura)]

### townie
 
 <i>n.</i> a person bred to the town and ignorant of or awkward in country ways

>  Dr. Pigeon and his young wife are not fitted for this island. ... She is a little towny, wee bit thing, quite lost in the country. <i>(Sep 15, 1904,Chudleigh Diary,424,1950 ed.</i>)

>  these poor Towneys. <i>(Jul 5, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,252</i>)

>  <i>Townie</i>.- (1) Two men from the same part of England speak of each other as `a to<i> t</i>. of mine'. (C.) (2) I have heard people from town derided as townies but perhaps it is a schoolboy word. <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Should the wife be a confirmed `townie' the sheepman must get work nearer the fleshpots. [cp ibid. 29 Most `town fellers' he would pity as being too smart.] <i>(1945,High Country,28</i>) [NOTES: 1945 Pascoe High Country in NZ Geographer 28]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> A towny travels for the good of his health. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,3</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In the eyes of many a farmer the `townies' were mostly parasites who, sheltering behind the tariffs, lived by selling him dear and inferior goods. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,200</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> Lucas [the head shepherd] thought of him as a townie. <i>(1970,Tat,96</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> When I took a sitting shot [at a pigeon] ... Miles said he didn't believe there was anyone except a townie who could be so dirty ... [sic] <i>(1973,Once Is Enough,64</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Townie.</i> Person from town <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,98</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> The other blokes at school were always slinging off at him because he was a dunce and a townie and a holy roller. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,45,1988 ed.</i>)

>  We came in as townies, and had never even seen a possum. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/89</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> Country children wore proper boots all the time, summer and winter, whereas townies - at least in the Auckland of my childhood - made it a point of honour to go barefoot, even on the roughly metalled streets of Takapuna, and our feet were always covered in stone bruises to prove it. Ibid. 90 'That's what I think of bloody townies.' 'Why?' I asked. 'Cos they don't bloody smoke,' he said. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,87</i>)

>  It is mostly townies like us who have fuelled New Zealand's smallfarming revolution. <i>(Jan 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 45/40</i>)

>  A hopeless townie like me wouldn't last five minutes in Te Kuata's rugged world. <i>(Nov 21, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  It's easy to bag the creative townies behind the funny ads when they are just reflecting an image, Mr Ryan says. <i>(Feb 25, 2000,Dominion,16</i>)

>  Being a good townie I must have missed something <i>(Jul 19, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Native trees and other features of interest on the track are numbered in black paint, and the leaflet gives you enough information at least to impress a visiting townie. <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  Mr Kidd says the ignorance of some townies amazes him. <i>(Sep 9, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  Brought up a townie, his dream was always to be a sheep and beef farmer and he had to teach himself basic skills, at one stage single-handedly building a house with only a book on carpentry as his guide. <i>(Jul 1, 2004,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  <b>FARRELL</b> At their first encounter Peg and Jack and Kath had decided that their cousin was a sook, a townie and a sook. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,24</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but widely and often derisively in NZ] a person inexperienced and/or incompetent in rural matters.

>  It is essential that many preconceived ideas about the idyllic existence of 'townies' or conversely 'farmers' must be scrapped ... <i>(Jan 1, 1941,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

>  <b>MARRIOTT</b> But when a townie is suddenly transplanted from the city to a real backblocks farm, in the middle of the worst slump in history as I was, there are likely to ensue all kinds of episodes, hair-raising as well as hilarious. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,9</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS & TURNER</b> When a 'townie' comes to the high country the pranksters are ready and waiting to conduct proper initiations. <i>(1983,NZ High Country Four Seasons,17</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> He was not really a farmer, just a townie gone bush, and did not fully understand the time-lag involved in making little pigs. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,129</i>)

>  <b>HARRISON</b> Cups fell off everywhere as the townies tried frantically to put cups on teats with unaccustomed fingers. <i>(1986,She's a Fair Cow,20</i>)

>  <b>BARNES</b> She used to cope with what a townie could only imagine in her worst dreams - feeding station staff with provisions coming in only twice a year. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),20</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Unfortunately the orchard was across the paddock where the sows were kept and June and Joyce being 'townies' were scared of being in the same paddock as the sows. <i>(1990,Bryans, The: At Home on the Farm,27</i>)

>  Uncontrolled, thousands of romantic townies would canker the nation's farming backbone. <i>(May 12, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  Paul van Beers, competing in the fencing heats at Mystery Creek, was a townie the first time he picked up a strainer and went fencing more than 20 years ago. <i>(Jun 15, 2001,Dominion,14</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of an animal or person] inadequate or inexperienced in rural matters. (See [townie dog](tot-tyr.md#townie-dog))

>  <b>STACEY</b> I could have shown my new townie friends how to skin a rabbit or an eel, clean a fish or work a dog ... <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,34</i>)

>  <b>BLAKE</b> There was a delightful array of typical townie-type dogs waiting in the pound .. one look was enough to convince me that no self-respecting possum hunter would risk his reputation being seen dead with any one of them. <i>(1999,Trappers, Dogs 'n' Deer,41</i>)

>  <b>BLAKE</b> The SF90 bush was such that these townie shooters, completely unfamiliar with their surroundings, were always getting themselves lost. <i>(1999,Trappers, Dogs 'n' Deer,235</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Let's clear up a townie misunderstanding. Sheep will eat tussock, but only in dire circumstances .. A young townie relative would learn to pick up, starting on the morrow. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,83</i>)

### townie dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog that is not capable of long hours of hill or hunting work.

>  <b>STACEY</b> I must admit it gave me a certain amount of satisfaction to see the townie dogs go howling home, snapping at their fat backs where the whips [drovers' stock whips] cut in. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,43</i>)

>  <b>BLAKE</b> Corletts were running a few dry cows on the flats, and Roy being a townie dog had more than likely never seen a cattle beast before. <i>(1999,Trappers, Dogs 'n' Deer,70</i>)

### townified
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of a person or community] with few rural characteristics or allegiances.

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Invercargill became townified <i>(1976,On The Edge of the Bush: Women in Early Southland,65</i>)

### Towns' skink
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) an endangered skink species resident only on Mokohinau, Hen and Chicken, and the Great and Little Barrier island groups. [<i>Oligosoma townsi</i> aka pre 2009 <i>Cyclodina townsi</i> aka mokohinau skink]

>  New lizards found off the Northland coast include... the Mokohinau skink.. from the Mokohinau islands and Hen and Chickens Islands in Hauraki Gulf. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/18</i>)

### township
 
 <i>n.</i> a paper town

>  The memorial [from Mr.Sinclair, Wellington] states that certain town lots ... had been advertised for sale by auction, but that several allotments, comprising some of the most valuable lands in the township, ... had been reserved from such sale ...; that these sections were to be paid for at the average price of half the town sections ... <i>(Sep 10, 1841,GBPP,(No.569) 130</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Sep 10 Villiers and Lefevre : Colonial Land and Emigration Office in GBPP 1842]

>  A township can, I apprehend, have no meaning other than a continuous block of land; ... <i>(Oct 6, 1840,GBPP,(No.311) 122</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Oct 6 Gipps to Lord John Russell in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 122]

>  Shares to the amount of 100,000<i>l</i>, to be paid up almost immediately, have been suscribed for; and, what is still more remarkable, the sum of 100,000<i>l</i>. was paid within five weeks, for as many acres of land, in a township the site of which is not yet determined. <i>(Aug 21, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,4</i>)

>  The survey of Kawhia township is completed. <i>(Mar 5, 1883,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  ALTHOUGH over two years have passed since the Premier visited Pipiriki and took over a site for a township from Kemp and Topia Turoa, which was to be surveyed and disposed of as soon as possible by the Government as trustees for the native owners, not one single allotment has been dealt with. <i>(Jun 2, 1897,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a small town,a village; occasionally in early NZ usage (or of British planners' usage) the paper or planned site for a town; but quckly developing the sense village, small town, and becoming the usual word in NZ for what in Britain (and in the usage of many early writers) would be called a village or hamlet (cp VILLAGE) [ca1960 the Main Highway signs in diminishing progression of size include: City, Borough, Township, Settlement, Settled or Populated Area, Beware Stock [check AAand/or MWD]] (See [native township](n-new.md#native-township),[Maori township](m-mar.md#maori-township))

>  <b>COLLINS</b> <i>(1802,Account of the English Colony in NSW, An,2/7</i>)

>  In June 1839, the New Zealand Land Company, issued proposals for the sale of nine-tenths of a township of 110,000 acres, in lots of 101 acres, each comprising 100 acres of country land and one town section. <i>(Sep 24, 1840,GBPP,(No.311) 123</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Sep 24 Port Nicholson Deputies to Gipps in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 123]

>  <b>CAPT SMITH</b> Port Ashley ... is quite unfit for settlement ... as there [330] is no fit place for a Township. <i>(Nov 1842,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Nov Capt. Smith's report in 1848 Wakefield Handbook for NZ 329-330]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> ... while the additional circumstance of Auckland possessing a tolerable harbour, would also have always caused its pre-eminence over the adjoining township [at Thames]. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),21</i>)

>  <b>MAJORIBANKS</b> It is fortunate that it [the Hutt] was abandoned as a township, as the water on one occasion, rose three or four feet in several of the huts, ... <i>(1845,Travels in NZ,62</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> This is now the site of a township, called Dunedin, founded by a Scotch company. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,174</i>)

>  ... Ordinance to abate the Dog nuisance, the boundaries of the township of Akaroa in the Middle Island of New Zealand, shall be hereafter known as follows:- ... <i>(Nov 19, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/28/169</i>) [NOTES: [compare VILLAGE used about the same date in NZGG] 1852 Nov 19 NZGG (Governor Grey's Proclamation) V/28/169]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> The notion of the new Township spreads like wild-fire. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/130 The general opinion is that the contiguity of this native Pah is no objection to the new Township. <i>(Feb 4, 1853,Journal,1/126,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>JOHN GROVES</b> ... we are in the centre from Wellington to a township called Ahuriri. For all travellers ... are often obliged to sleep under the flaxbush. <i>(Nov 17, 1855,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Nov 17 John Groves in 1971 Manson I Take up My Pen 31]

>  <b>PAUL</b> Malvern Hills, where Mr. Cass thinks there is a site suited for a township. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>WILLIS GANN & CO</b> The sites of new towns and townships [are] rising or filling up in the Colony. <i>(1864,Handbook of New Zealand,37</i>)

>  <b>WILLIS GANN & CO</b> The sites of new towns and townships [are] rising or filling up in the Colony. <i>(1864,Handbook of New Zealand,37</i>)

>  <b>BURGESS</b> While we were at the township [in Otago, c1862 March], one of our acquaintances came to me and told me there was a man in a disreputable shanty, that had plenty of money and would I come and put him up? Now the Wetherstone diggings were very quiet, so that I objected to men being put up and let go to alarm the place - for I anticipated robbing this banker sooner or later, and that would do me. <i>(1866,Confessions,76,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> (F. Robinson) Mr Duncan's [property] comes between me and the Township. <i>(1866,Papers,26/137</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> About this time, though almost a `new chum' myself, I was much amused by a trio who arrived in the township and put up opposite my `shanty'. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,17</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> We reached the township of Maori Point ... consisting of ... a few `frame tents' ... <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,134</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> The village, or township as they call it in this part of the world, has an unpronounceable Maori[?] name. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,181</i>)

>  <i>(1895,NZ Reader,97</i>) [NOTES: 1882 in 1895 NZ Reader 97]

>  <b>PAYTON</b> Every little township has its public park (so called). <i>(1888,Round about NZ,15</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> ... cities and Townships (colonial for towns) <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,23</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> I have to go to the village - I beg its pardon, the <i>township. </i> <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,548</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... township <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,42</i>)

>  <b>ARTHUR</b> Townships are the smaller colonial towns, and vary in size from the dimension of a moderate English village to about a fifth-rate English country town. <i>(1894,Kangaroo and Kauri,89</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> a village, a possible future town. In the United States, the word has a definite meaning - a district, subordinate to a country, the inhabitants having power to regulate their local affairs; in Australia, the word has no such definite meaning. It may be large or small, and sometimes consists of little more than the post-office, the public-house, and the general store or shop. <i>(1898,Austral English,476</i>)

>  <i>Town&#769;.ship</i> (toun&#769;.sh&ibreve.p), <i>n. [Add.] 4.</i> In Australia, a small town; a village. Also, the space surveyed for the site of a town. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Townships are formed here and there as settlement proceeds; - at first a store and post office, a cottage or a blacksmith's shop perhaps; then a hotel, another store, and again another hotel. <i>Ibid.</i> 20 Sanguine in their anticipations, and bountifully provident for the wants of posterity, the promoters of these bush townships - whether it were the Government, private companies or individuals, or a supplementing of the former by the latter - made the boundaries of them wide enough for all possible future requirements, and gave ample scope for prospective development. This was very commendable and necessary in some instances, but where a township, as sometimes happened, did not `go off' at the sale, or if it did go off, was never subsequently embellished with a building of any kind, the effect had something of the ludicrous about it - for while settlement went on around the `township,' and the land was cleared and occupied, the `town' itself remained a solid block of standing bush, and the absentee owners of its acre or quarter-acre sections were only reminded of their possessions by the persistent recurrence of the notices to pay rates <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,3</i>)

>  <b>BAYLY</b> ... a so-called `township'. <i>(1905,NZ Courtship, A,8</i>)

>  <b>HULL</b> Generally speaking, a township [in North Auckland] consists of a `pub', a store, half-a-dozen scattered homesteads, and about thirty-nine million Maori bugs. The Maori bug is about half an inch by an inch, but his influence extends over about half an acre. <i>(1907,College Songs,46</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> He had ridden over to the township with four of the boys to help hunt down the young fellow who'd murdered Mr Williamson. ... Willie Cox said they found him in the barn, shot bang through the head, the young English `johnny' who'd been on the station learning farming - <i>(1913,Stories,134,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> The bay was now a township. It had its own post office, its little public school, its town hall, its football field. <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,198,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1933,Early Canterbury Runs,100</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>township </i> <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> During our short history villages have come and gone, have flourished and then decayed, and some once again flourished. There is a story behind all these happenings. These many widely-scattered urban oases in the rural desert have been known for all their short lives as `townships'. A very ugly name coming down to us from the old digging days. Village' or just `town' are now gaining ground over the ugly old name, and rightly so. You could scarcely talk of beautifying a township, but could get quite enthusiastic over improving a village. <i>(1954,Te Raka,19</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> There'd been a few more drinks with Ron Strachan, who ... was in the township for his annual booze-up. <i>(1956,Sullen Bell,84</i>)

>  <i>A small town. </i>- <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),418</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> (Not for a Seagull) I remember I was jiving with a girl ... who worked in a milkbar in the township. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,161</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The back country runs have quite a vocabulary of their own ... terms like <i>cow paddock</i> and <i>horse paddock </i>and <i>tea-oh </i>and <i>township</i> which, like most of these terms, took on their special use in Australia. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <i>township n</i> in Australia any small town, village or settlement. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>township n</i> . in Australia, any small town, village, or settlement. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>ARMFELT</b> Down to Elgin: sprawling, squat, bungaloid: not even large enough to be a `township': just the all tar-sealed `main drag', quite wide, with crooked telegraph posts on both sides. <i>(1971,Catching Up,20</i>)

>  <i>township n. </i>small town or settlement.<i> (NZ & Austral.) ... NZ & Austral. hist.</i> tract of surveyed land, site for a town <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,814</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1239]]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> The fact is that when the Gladstone scheme was abandoned, Simeon and I agreed that the opportunity should not be lost of realising a Township Scheme upon the same site for the benefit of the Church. <i>(Feb 4, 1853,Journal,1/424,1980 ed.</i>)

>  He never omits to call at the township post office which usually comprises the tuckershop as well ... At the way-back township of Mataroa ... <i>(Jan 6, 1906,NZ Truth,29/8/4</i>)

>  Clarke's is not a township; it is simplv a roadside hotel. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  The subscriptions will be confined, in a great measure, to the permanent endowment of the church, by the purchase of one or more sections of land in the principal township of the colony, to the erection of the necessary buildings, including a house for the clergyman, and to the providing for him a suitable outfit. <i>(Aug 21, 1839,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,7</i>)

>  FOR SALE, Several quarter-acre sections in the Government Township, Timaru, and A few sections in Rhodes' Township. <i>(Jun 11, 1864,Timaru Herald,1</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> [quoting Land Regulations] All reserves for townships or agricultural establishments shall have reserves marked off of one-twentieth of all bush-land within the limit of such block, or if there be no bush within such block, then a sufficient reserve of bush-land outside the block, ... shall be made. <i>(1858,Handbook Wellington,13</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... the Counties act [passed in 1856], a precursor of the counties, hundreds, and townships of Old England; ... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/242</i>)

>  <b>WILLOX</b> ... different districts, townships and settlements. <i>(1860,Handbook of New Zealand,52</i>) [NOTES: [quoting Land Regulation terms]]

>  [The runanga] will bear the same relation to other political machinery ... as the township in the United States of America bears to the county and state organization, and ministers suggest the name of township as the most suitable English term for the community that will be represented by a single runanga. It will correspond with the division of hundreds proposed by his Excellency. [Grey's annotation] I should have preferred the term `hundred'. It is already in use here. The natives will make a strange word out of `township'. <i>(Nov 1, 1861,Memorandum of Ministers</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Nov 1 Memorandum by Ministers on Governor Grey's Proposed Institutions for the Native Race in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 112e]

>  It [the new bridge] will cross the Waipa at Whatiwhatihoe, and literally joins together the European and Maori townships, bringing Finch's hotel in a line with the native township, a facility which, judging by last night's proceedings, will be largely taken advantage of. <i>(May 1, 1882,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Manutuke, a Maori township about fifteen miles from Gisborne, suffered a heavy loss within the past few days in the deaths of two tribal leaders, the second occurring under unusual circumstances. <i>(Apr 12, 1932,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The land in the neighbourhood of Korarika was rapidly advancing in value. Our correspondent states, that on the 24th ultimo, 40 acres situated at a little more than a mile from that township, were sold in nine separate lots, for in all GBP241; and on the very present day, the buyer of one of these these [sic] lots realised a premium of 70 per cent by his purchase. <i>(Jun 6, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,4</i>)

>  The farmers and citizens in the township grew anxious. <i>(Sep 28, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,62</i>)

### towser
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A better-hearted old towser never cracked a stock-whip. <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

 <i>n.</i> ? a rabbit poison {ref. early 20C)

 <i>n.</i>

### toy
 
 <i>n.</i> an inexperienced or inexpert tagger (of graffiti)

>  Leaving the ranks of bored adolescents getting quick thrills from quick tags (toys, as the amateurs are called), she has gone on to the more esteemed throwup or bomb (a tag in fat letters with the outline coloured in), then the dub (a throwup with an outer line and bubbles), and finally the piece, short for masterpiece (a dub with filled in letters and a painted background). <i>(Jan 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

 <i>n.</i> territorial soldier

### track
 
 <i>v.</i> to smuggle contraband into and out of prison

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> TRACK (v) Smuggle contraband in and out of prison. Possibly a corruption of TRUCK, Standard English for dubious trafficking in goods, since the early 16th century. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,255</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ since 1839 (as elswhere), a rough path or way usually, originally, trodden clear; a rough unsurfaced road [also bush track TRACK is the general use in NZ among outdoors people (trampers, etc); also for regular paths trodden by animals, e.g. <i>sheep tracks, goat tracks; trail</i> is occasionally used, ?esp. of deer cf also MAORI TRACK [AND seems to suggest a much wider spec. use in Aust.]] (See [also bush track](bri-byo.md#bush-track))

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Track.</i>- Pack<i> t</i>., etc. Originally an unformed, unfenced way, but Leach's <i>T</i>., Shand's <i>T.,</i> etc., perpetuate the name. The county councils will probably alter these in time. The Bealey <i>T.</i> (to Hororata) is oftener than not called the Bealey road. `We came upon a very bad track-- that is the name by which all roads are called, and they don't deserve a better.'--S.L., p.76. <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>ODELL</b> They will no doubt be ultimately opened up by the formation of tracks and the erection of huts, and will then allow of the fullest exploitation of the winter sports attractions of the Park. <i>Ibid.</i> 25 On tracks which are little used, for instance the Upper Edwards Bush Track, much assistance could be given to subsequent parties if travellers were to renew the blaze marks so that the `blazes' attracted the attention from a distance of those unfamiliar with the district. <i>(1935,Handbook Arthur Pass National Park,20</i>)

>  In the bush or in the open do you tramp on a <i>track</i>, a <i>trail</i>, or a <i>path</i>? Not on a path one may be sure, seldom if ever on a trail, almost always on a track, like a proper New Zealander... <i>Track</i> is undoubtedly the established word in New Zealand. It is the only one occurring on maps or applied to named routes, such as <i>Hongi's track</i>, the <i>Milford track</i>, the <i>Pahiatua Bush Track Road</i>. ..[25] It is interesting to compare the use of <i>track</i> and<i> trail</i> in <i>The Tramper</i>. Through the first volumes <i>track</i> is always used when referring to an actual track, and <i>trail</i> occurs rarely and then only in the expression <i>blazed trail</i> or more or less figuratively - `to fare forth on the trail'. In 1935 there is the first definite use of <i>trail</i> as a synonym for <i>track</i> in a reference to the `Handbook of Arthur's Pass National Park'. It is significant that it occurs in reference to Arthur's Pass for trail seems to have come into use in Canterbury in this sense before it reached Wellington. It occurs once or twice in the first volume of the <i>Canterbury Mountaineer</i>, and there is the <i>B.B. Trail </i>in the Arthur's Pass Hand book of 1935. From 1939 till 1940 in <i>The Tramper, trail </i>is used moderately frequently for an actual track, e.g. `an old Maori trail', `a miner's trail', but only by the one writer, a Wellington member of the Canterbury Mountaineering Club. <i>Trail</i> shows no sign of ousting <i>track. </i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,24</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Beyond Top Hut the track is benched for maybe 20 minutes before large poles take over to mark the easy, well-worn path across the tops. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,65</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> We did not stay long at Whanganui, and returning made a slight diversion from the usual track to visit Otaki, which is considered the finest native town in the country. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,27</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... first it was necessary to build on a flat (as the valleys here are called), not too far off the main track, on account of having to make the road to it ourselves; ... and a good creek, or brook, which would not go dry in the summer, close at hand. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,63,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> After we had passed our own boundary fence we came upon a very bad track - this is the name by which all the roads are called, and they do not deserve a better. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,76,1874 ed.</i>)

>  We started ... for the track through the Bush to Lake Brunner. You must not mistake the word `track' to mean a road, for we had to scramble and crawl ... for seven miles. <i>(Jan 20, 1890,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1890 Jan 20 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 50]

>  "I don't think that is always the case here and until that is fully sorted out and the tracks brought up to standard, we'll obviously struggle." <i>(Dec 22, 2002,Sunday Star Times</i>)

>  Soon after parting from our guide the path became clear enough, at first passing through a grove of tawa; when at fault, a few broken branches shewed us the track again. <i>(Mar 13, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  I have had this track repaired of slips, and broken cu[l]verts, and cleared it throughout of underbush and fern, as it was entirely grown over, but is now in good order. <i>(Sep 15, 1888,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  The damage principally complained of is that done by the gale of April 13th last. Although the timber was not cut off the track, tracks were cleared around the trees and horses and cattle could be taken through. <i>(Sep 7, 1895,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  Mr Dale to move, "That this Council allow the Kanieri Road Board to collect all rates in the district, for the purpose of keeping in repair all tracks, dray roads, bridges, and culverts constructed by the Board, and such other works as the Board thinks fit; and that James Webster be appointed rate collector by this Council for the district." <i>(Apr 9, 1878,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  The track from Milford to Glade House is sound and in good order and makes excellent walking, while the accomodation is entirely satisfactory, so that any compaints from tourists are not likely to be heard again. <i>(Feb 3, 1909,Bush Advocate,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [(contrast trail)](../(#(contrast-trail)))

>  In recent years there seems to have been a tendency to use both <i>track</i> and<i> trail</i> and to differentiate between them. A trail seems to be something narrow and perhaps rather hard to follow - a way marked only by blazes or worn by animals, usually deer. A track seems to be something broader, cut or formed by man. ... A well-known older Club member made this distinction when, on hearing him speak of a deer trail, I asked him what he thought to be the difference between <i>track</i> and <i>trail</i>. I remarked that I thought it a very recent distinction, one not occurring in the Club until about 1957. He disagreed, believing it to be one he had always known. Yet, on looking through <i>The Tramper, </i> I could not find <i>deer trail</i> used until 1944, but <i>deer track</i> occurs from the first number in 1928 onwards, and he himself had used it in 1940. It remains to be seen whether this distinction will become established. In the meantime<i> deer track </i>still seems to be used far more than <i>deer trail.</i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,25</i>) [NOTES: 1958 Sep Ruth Mason `Track and Trail' in Tararua 25]

>  We followed deer tracks zigzagging up through snow totara and <i>Dracophyllum,</i> then plugged up through soft snow to Brodrick Pass (5,308ft.), taking nearly two hours from camp. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,49</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> There's a whacking great suspension bridge across the river, but get to the other side and there's nothing but a few pig tracks through the scrub. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,62</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a man-made and maintained trail

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> ... most mornings found Dad and Arthur out with slashers, opening up tracks ... <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,47</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,29</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> Beside the track the last of the flax bushes ... clapped encouragement. <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,232</i>)

>  DOC contractors cleared vegetation and remarked sections of track along 16 km of track in the western Tararuas. <i>(May 2004,Footnotes,No. 21/2</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to pull (up-stream) by rope [<i>pull</i> = row]

>  [We] took oars and a rope to the boat. ... After more hard work we succeeded in tracking her two miles up stream. Tracking is pulling by a rope fastened to the bows. <i>(Dec 29, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,209,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> `We'd best put him in the boat ... and track it up as far as the hotel.' ... Taking one of the oars, and placing the one end against the boat's prow, ... so as to keep the boat out from the bank, the boatman took the rope over his own shoulder, and began to slowly drag the boat up the stream. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,253</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to follow land contours

>  <b>LEVY</b> On steep hillsides stock will tend to track and contour the land, and the ultimate surface formation of well-stocked country will be a series of broad or narrow terraces with steep slopes between them upon which stock seldom tread. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,254,1970 ed.</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> ??

 <i>v.</i>

### track-cutter
 
 <i>n.</i> [track cutter, trackcutter]

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> A shot distance below them he came to a gametrail so pronounced and so well defined that a party of Forest Service trackcutters, using grubber, slasher and spade, might have fashioned it out of a steep hillface. <i>(1976,Razorback,76</i>)

>  Even then he returned to make sure we were leaving, and finally departed in a glorious gallop which was the envy of four track-cutters toiling up the ridge to Hector. <i>(Jan 4, 1932,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  As regards Big Bay, the presence of a surveyor and track-cutters will enable the place to be fairly prospected for the first time. <i>(Oct 1, 1886,Otago Daily Times,4</i>)

>  The Okarita paper says:- Several of the track cutters have arrived in town during the week, having completed their contracts, or done so at least as far as their instructions serve. <i>(Sep 3, 1867,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  About 4 p.m. the track cutters reached a SWIFT RIVER coming in on the left, rushing down a steep rocky face, then over a rough bed. <i>(Apr 9, 1896,Otago Witness,51</i>)

>  As we are given to understand that Dr Bell's field work is to be commenced on the Coast, he should be proceeded by poineers in the way of track cutters. <i>(Mar 27, 1905,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

### track-cutting
 
 <i>n.</i> [track cutting]

>  <b>PRINCE</b> The writer started from Quinten McKinnon's (track cutting) camp, now called Trackton ... <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,5</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> Besides coal surveys and track-cutting on the West Coast, the Nelson Provincial Government endeavoured to shorten the inland route from the capital to the middle valley of the Buller, <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,75,1962 ed.</i>)

>  The deepest parts of the forest have not yet been pierced by the track-maker, though this work will probably be undertaken in the near future. <i>(Feb 7, 1914,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The subsidy of 30s granted in such cases by the Unemployment Board will be increased to 35s a week while the men are engaged in track cutting. <i>(Jun 2, 1933,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Tentative selection of homestead sites has been made and road and track cutting has been mapped out. <i>(Jan 17, 1945,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  This man piloted the surveyors and track-makers of Gippsland through the ranges, and marked out the way for them to follow through country that could not have been faced without the assistance of great skill and courage. <i>(Jul 23, 1902,Otago Witness,37</i>)

>  As I now had done all I promised and was anxious to get home as soon as I could, and there being three days track cutting to do on the way back, and also there being chances of rain, I thought by coming down the river and getting the steamer about the 15th or 16th, I would be as soon home as going overland, especially as I had to come to Nelson. <i>(Oct 23, 1863,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  Farther north, in the Levin hinterland, track-cutting has recently been instituted on a moderately extensive scale, and the present article deals briefly with the progress to date. <i>(Dec 5, 1929,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  Mining affairs in the district are by no means of discouraging nature, and if more attention was paid by the Government to that indispensable desideratum of track cutting, to allow miners to penetrate into the interior, so from them alone must fresh discoveries emanate - no new ground having for some time been opened - everything would be more prosperous. <i>(Aug 20, 1870,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  However, he felt confident, and when he found that track cutting had to be done he took the party up the hill. <i>(Apr 5, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,7</i>)

>  Of what use is it for men, who will dare almost anything for the gold, to rush about the country and find payble ground if the Government is not also prepared to venture a few pounds in track cutting? <i>(Oct 31, 1863,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  WANTED - Man for track cutting, wages 8s per day. <i>(Dec 10, 1904,Bush Advocate,1</i>)

>  The first 18 days were spent in track cutting and prospecting in a north-eastern direction from Port's hut. <i>(Mar 12, 1896,Otago Witness,17</i>)

### tracking
 
 <i>n.</i> track or the railing from which curtains or drapes are suspended [Jim writes: In relation to curtains or drapes there is no entry for these words in any dictionary I have seen., <i>Track</i> may be covered, though imprecisely, under one of the general definitions but <i>tracking</i> is not. Both words are in standard use in NZ. They seem likely to be international English but may be worth checking out.]



 
 <i>n.</i> [(whaling)]

>  [We] took oars and a rope to the boat. ... After more hard work we succeeded in tracking her two miles up stream. Tracking is pulling by a rope fastened to the bows. <i>(Dec 29, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,209,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> Up to here I went in a whale boat, which was partly pulled, partly poled, and the rest of the distance dragged up by the crew - they up to their waists in water, with a rope over their shoulders. This was termed `tracking'. <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,133</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEVY</b> In areas where much timber remained unburnt, tracking of the burn was necessary to enable stock to graze all parts of it freely, because the ability of stock to graze and tread the burn largely determined whether the succession was to grass or back to forest. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,246,1970 ed.</i>)

### trackmaker
 
 <i>n.</i> [track maker, trackmaker]

>  Then in April 1864 a note was brought by a track-maker and left at the store for Moffat ... <i>(1976,Difficult Country,34</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Roy Mytton, trackmaker, musterer, Mount Arthur and Cobb Valley, Motueka, August 1979. <i>Ibid.</i> 117 On trackmaking over the hills and far away, with precious few witnesses, with payment being made by the distance covered, usually so much per chain, the sceptical are inclined to remark: `If there's only the trackmaker and the overseer there, just the two of them present on the job, ...' <i>Ibid.</i> 122 The rare perspiring trackmaker and the flitting packman were Chaffey's passing neighbours, who'd drop in and leave stores and mail from time to time. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,7</i>)

>  This man piloted the surveyors and track-makers of Gippsland through the ranges, and marked out the way for them to follow through country that could not have been faced without the assistance of great skill and courage. <i>(Jul 23, 1902,Otago Witness,37</i>)

>  The deepest parts of the forest have not yet been pierced by the track-maker, though this work will probably be undertaken in the near future. <i>(Feb 7, 1914,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  .. and Satruday night was spent in the tents of the surveyors and track-makers a little above the Orongorongo bed. <i>(Oct 25, 1921,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Besides the amateur effort, quite a good story could be told of the work of the professional track-maker and hut-builder associated with the club. <i>(May 10, 1930,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Another jolly evening, the Abbot's stew was consumed to the last drop, the track makers, three in number, had joined the party before tea and they took part in the enjoyments of the evening. <i>(Apr 27, 1907,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

### trackmaking
 
 <i>n.</i> [track making, track-making]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> When fifteen-year-old Les Waghorn, up in the mountains on trackmaking, called in with the mail, she always gave him a little packet, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 117 On trackmaking over the hills and far away, with precious few witnesses, with payment being made by the distance covered, usually so much per chain, the sceptical are inclined to remark: `If there's only the trackmaker and the overseer there, just the two of them present on the job, ...' <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,28</i>)

>  Local committees in Greytown and Otaki have struggled valiantly with the work of track-making and hut-building, but the burden of maintenance and extension of this accommodation requires assistance from Wellington. <i>(Jul 8, 1919,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  In reporting to the executive on the progress of the track-making work on the Otaki side, the two members spoke in glowing terms of the excellent work done by the track-making party. <i>(Feb 11, 1921,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Excellent work, says Mr. F. W. Vosseler, who has just returned from a trip into the country at the back of the Ohau River, has been done in track-making by the Palmerston North Tramping Club. <i>(Dec 27, 1932,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Another in the Tararuas was well advanced when war forced the suspension of track-making. <i>(Dec 28, 1944,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The Board must recollect that the summer is passing away and the time is flying when branch road and track making could be done cheaper and more efficiently than in the rainy season and short days. <i>(Nov 26, 1874,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The most of the river claims at the top end are worked out, and the parties are setting in to the terraces on the banks, some of them are doing fairly well, but the body of wash is small, and will run out very soon, and unless the Government assist the miners in track making, to open up the country further ahead, there will not be much encouragement for men to come here, or for those that are here to stay. <i>(Dec 6, 1892,Bruce Herald,2</i>)

>  <i>Track making</i> is here understood to mean clearing off the timber <i>only</i> - no formation. <i>(Mar 6, 1880,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  Aware, however, of the known procrastination and apathy of the Government in all matters relating to the real advancement of Westland, but particularly in track-making, they shake their heads doubtingly, and fear that the Waimea district will fare no better than the rest. <i>(Aug 27, 1866,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  There is a lot of road work and track making going on, in fact more than at any period since the days of Clark and Ryan. <i>(Jan 27, 1903,West Coast Times,3</i>)

### tracks
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Action terms related to the movements of the marbles are, Throughs and No Throughs, Tracks and Clears, Stops and Rolls, Lay-Up, Manyies, Kiss, Ons, Gees, Haystacks, Stays, Spiders Can't Cross a River etc. Action Terms related to taking an opponent's marbles are Fires, Double-Hits and Forces. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/769</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs, finking [sic, ?funking], sips, spanning, placing; and from Dunedin School in 1949: knee high, manyies, tracks, screws, nuts, sleets, kills, forces, dig, dubs, cheats. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)
