### trail
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [(contrast track)](../(#(contrast-track)))

>  <b>BEST</b> ... bush trails [alo uses anent, `hapless', `wend his way' etc] <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/159</i>)

>  (Mikonui) ... a foot-track, which branching off near Frew Creek from the Whitcombe Pass trail, leads to Frew Saddle ... <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.6 (N.S.) 21</i>) [NOTES: 1908 Morgan NZ Geol. Survey Bull. No.6 (N.S.) 21]

>  <b>HYDE</b> There was a dark trail, ... and a stream came with edgings of fine malachite ferns, ... Purple berries lay fallen, rich as damsons but not as big, and good only for the fantails ... <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,126,1970 ed.</i>)

>  In the bush or in the open do you tramp on a <i>track, trail</i>, or a <i>path</i>? Not on a path one may be sure, seldom if ever on a trail, almost always on a track, like a proper New Zealander. ... I well remember the shock of astonishment when in 1937 I first saw <i>trail </i>used in New Zealand. The word was on a rough signpost pointing out the beginning of White's Track at Piha. I am afraid I thought somewhat scornfully, `Hm, some townie who has never been out of the city or set foot off a pavement has got all his ideas of New Zealand outdoor life from reading books about North America.'...[25] It is interesting to compare the use of <i>track</i> and <i>trail</i> in <i>The Tramper</i>. Through the first volumes <i>track</i> is always used when referring to an actual track, and <i>trail </i>occurs rarely and then only in the expression <i>blazed trail</i> or more or less figuratively - `to fare forth on the trail'. In 1935 there is the first definite use of<i> trail</i> as a synonym for <i>track</i> in a reference to the `Handbook of Arthur's Pass National Park'. It is significant that it occurs in reference to Arhtur's Pass for trail seems to have come into use in Canterbury in this sense before it reached Wellington. It occurs once or twice in the first volume of the <i>Canterbury Mountaineer</i>, and there is the <i>B.B. Trail</i> in the Arthur's Pass Hand book of 1935. From 1939 till 1940 in <i>The Tramper, trail</i> is used moderately frequently for an actual track, e.g. `an old Maori trail', `a miner's trail', but only by the one writer, a Wellington member of the Canterbury Mountaineering Club. <i>Trail</i> shows no sign of ousting <i>track. </i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,24</i>) [NOTES: 1958 Sep Ruth Mason `Track and Trail' in Tararua 24]

>  In recent years there seems to have been a tendency to use both <i>track</i> and <i>trail</i> and to differentiate between them. A trail seems to be something narrow and perhaps rather hard to follow - a way marked only by blazes or worn by animals, usually deer. A track seems to be something broader, cut or formed by man. ... A well-known older Club member made this distinction when, on hearing him speak of a deer trail, I asked him what he thought to be the difference between<i> track </i>and <i>trail.</i> I remarked that I thought it a very recent distinction, one not occurring in the Club until about 1957. He disagreed, believing it to be one he had always known. Yet, on looking through <i>The Tramper</i>, I could not find <i>deer trail</i> used until 1944, but <i>deer track</i> occurs from the first number in 1928 onwards, and he himself had used it in 1940. It remains to be seen whether this distinction will become established. In the meantime <i>deer track</i> still seems to be used far more than <i>deer trail.</i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,25</i>) [NOTES: 1958 Sep Ruth Mason `Track and Trail' (deer shooters')]

>  From the top we had seen many deer trails on the other bank, but there were none on our side, so we fought our way down the bank ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,35</i>)

>  <i>(1959,Tararua,xiii/50</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,29, 161</i>)

>  There's never a trail like the home trail, / At the end of a toilsome day. <i>(May 7, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ?a trace

>  The pioneer prospectors had discovered `trails of gold' in the creeks on the western side of the valley ... <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,(NS)No.15/8</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Waihi-Tairua)]



 
 <i>n.</i> ?the `innards' of a small bird

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> They [Maoris] cook a pigeon by covering it with plastic clay, ... When cooked, they crack the clay, which retains all the feathers - and the gravy. The `trail' shrinks into a small ball, and is easily removed. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,37,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1872 in c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 37]

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>trammie:</i> tram conductor <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <i>trammie n.</i> tram-driver or conductor. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Established diminutives are ... <i>possie</i> (position or place), ...<i> jelly</i> (gelignite), <i>trammie</i> (tramway worker), ... and <i>shrewdie</i> (a shrewd person). <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,118</i>)

>  <i>trammie ... n.</i> tram-driver or conductor. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  In fact, the [Christchurch] trammies, justly wild, made up their minds to strike. <i>(Apr 7, 1906,NZ Truth,42/5/3</i>)

>  [Hdg] A Trammie's Reminiscences. In July 1942 I joined the trams. <i>(Aug 2003,NZ Memories,No. 43/45</i>)

>  When He opens wide the portals / And you're stepping softly through. / Will you ask the lord to guide you / To those trammies once you knew. <i>(Oct 2003,NZ Memories,39</i>)

### Trammies
 
 <i>n.</i> [Tramway Players rugby team]

>  Now, the Trammies ask, how could they possibly rebut evidence they were not allowed to hear? <i>(Jul 24, 1930,NZ Truth,16</i>)

### tramming
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining context, OED 1983, not clear that this is a New Zealandism.]

>  It is purposed shortly to make use of this connection for trucking purposes, thus avoiding the present low level tramming through the mine of drainage water. <i>(Jan 10, 1916,Grey River Argus,8</i>)

>  TENDERS are invited for Cutting, Logging, and Tramming, for the Cheltenham Sawmill for the ensuing twelve months. <i>(Jan 3, 1888,Feilding Star,4</i>)

>  The Geldenhuis was the first deep level worked, and the stopes that caved in were worked some years back, and the levels were used more for tramming than anything else. <i>(May 21, 1906,Thames Star,1</i>)

### tramming carriage
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He was killed whilst removing the log from the tramming carriage, the log rolling on him, and crushing his neck and face. <i>(Nov 21, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### tramp
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>trip</i> or <i>tramping trip</i> is NZ trampers' common usage, where British usage might prefer `walking expedition; hike' (OED 1859) for something similar

>  <b>WALKER</b> on the tramp I have a better chance of getting these things than Bill as I am nearly always on the tramp and he is in the store always. <i>(Apr 5, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>) [NOTES: (Central Otago)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> [? walking trip] A good breakfast, and off we start on our tramp. ... On the hill side sometimes as high as 1000 feet up, we see numerous races or flumes as they are called used by diggers to take water to sluice the gold out of the stuff the gold is found in. <i>(Jan 30, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Jan 30 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 158]

>  <b>COWAN</b> Led by Julian with his slash-hook ... - bushmanlike figure enough was `Wirihana' for he ... travelled bare-legged in Maori fashion all that week's tramp - <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,9</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> During our tramp he told me much about his people and the Maori wars. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,156,1933 ed.</i>)

>  Words that concern walking and moving about might be expected to be of interest to a tramper. First there is the word <i>tramp</i> itself. Though the Club in 1919 was the first in New Zealand and probably the first in the world to take the name of <i>tramping club</i>, and by its usage and activities has played a considerable part in establishing the term, the meaning we give to <i>tramp</i> was not of New Zealand origin, for it was used colloquially in England as early as 1908 for `to go on foot, to go on a walking expedition'. The nature of the country into which the expeditions are made may often be very different from that [29] where the term originated, but the essential idea is the same. ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,28</i>) [NOTES: 1958 Sep Ruth Mason `Track and Trail' in Tararua 28-29]

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> He was passionately fond of long tramps with his breach [sic] loader in search of sport, and ... he and Willam Steuart soon became fast friends. They tramped the creeks and over the ridges, carrying back their game, ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,10</i>)

>  <i>(Aug 3, 1966,Weekly News,7/4</i>)

>  We were a small railway party and had completed the tramp of nine miles through the bush to a mountain hut. <i>(1926,NZ Railways Magazine,1(6) : 9</i>)

>  We were rested sufficiently to commence the last portion of our tramp the following morning. <i>(1929,NZ Railways Magazine,4(7) : 45</i>)

>  There is absolutely no need to make yourself a hideous spectacle merely because you are going for a tramp. <i>(1929,NZ Railways Magazine,4(8) : 53</i>)

>  ... it is very hard work humping your blankets and tucker. ...[sic] On the tramp along the Buller ... you must reckon on being wet through half your time. <i>(May 12, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/111,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond, Nelson)]

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> But at first [women] were excluded from the more adventurous expeditions that appeared on the club schedule the following year headed 'Reasonably Strenuous Tramps - Gentlemen Only.' <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,129</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a trampoline [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  <b>PROFESSOR S JOHNSTON</b> A notice outside a Mangere motel: `Children's Tramp' <i>(Jun 1987,Personal Communication</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to travel on foot in unroaded country usually for recreation and esp. as a member of a TRAMPING CLUB; to hike; to bush-walk

>  The thought of again tramping that thirteen miles with heavy packs was not pleasing. <i>(1929,NZ Railways Magazine,4(7) : 43</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... some strange pakehas would come and take the land while I was tramping about.) <i>(Mar 27, 1840,Diary,1839 ed.</i>)

>  ... encumbered only with a blanket ... they have tramped from diggings to diggings, from rush to rush - free and unfettered. <i>(1866,NZ & Australian Songster,9,1970 ed.</i>)

>  I'll have to tramp it [sc the distance].] <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,2/591</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> He was passionately fond of long tramps with his breach [sic] loader in search of sport, and ... he and Willam Steuart soon became fast friends. They tramped the creeks and over the ridges, carrying back their game, ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,10</i>) [NOTES: transitional' usage]

>  <b>GILKISON</b> Along the road you can `hike', but over the winding trails and trackless hills you can really tramp. <i>(1940,Peaks, Packs & Mountain Tracks,80</i>)

>  <b>GILKISON</b> Along the road you can `hike', but over the winding trails and trackless hills you can really tramp. <i>(1940,Peaks, Packs & Mountain Tracks,80</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>to tramp</i>: to hike or walk <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Tramping gear instead of business clothes. ... Had a tramping pack ready just in case. ... He was always going off on tramping trips, seems like.' `He didn't tramp all the way back to Wellington!' <i>(1980,Murder or Three, A,155</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Peter was tramping Te Anau, having tramped Britain and Borneo. ... I asked if he had met a gaunt tramper called Maurice walking round the mountains. <i>(1985,G'day Country,170</i>)

>  A Man tramped into the Pines. <i>(Jun 19, 1914,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,8</i>) [NOTES: capital for pine is correct - caption.]

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> I have tramped and climbed in the Southern Alps for over forty years, and yet the Tararua retains its magic for me. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,29</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) go fast

>  ca1938 Havelock e.g. His new car can really tramp. In the half mile he was tramping along well ahead of the rest and eventually won by a big margin. <i>(May 23, 1969,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12/1</i>)

>  It was a souped-up Ford. They were tramping, in anybody's language. <i>(May 23, 1969,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a tramping route

### tramp metal
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... Uranium is soluble, even more so than copper, which is commonly known as `tramp metal', and consequently uranium salts have the ability to move through rocks provided they are porous. <i>(1957,NZ Geog. Society Rec.,24/15</i>)

>  ... uranium is what the old miners used to call `tramp metal' ... What they meant was that it reacts readily with the weak acids in the ground water and the salts of uranium resulting from this reaction are very soluble moving through the space in porous rocks, or the cracks in denser rocks for miles from where they originated. <i>(Sep 10, 1958,Weekly News,4</i>)

### tramper
 
 <i>n.</i> one who travels on foot over rough country for recreation; a hiker; a bush-walker

>  <b>ODELL</b> ... the track to Aicken through the bush was not cut originally to assist trampers and mountaineers, but was made for the purpose of providing a way for a pipe line used during the constructional work on the tunnel. <i>Ibid.</i> 59 The place is a strategic base for mountaineers who wish to conquer the peaks at the head of the Waimakariri, and for trampers on the `Three Pass Trip' (see Harman Pass) it makes a convenient resting place. <i>(1935,Handbook Arthur Pass National Park,27</i>)

>  ... the track to Aicken through the bush was not cut originally to assist trampers and mountaineers, but was made for the purpose of providing a way for a pipe line used during the constructional work on the tunnel. <i>Ibid.</i> 59 The place is a strategic base for mountaineers who wish to conquer the peaks at the head of the Waimakariri, and for trampers on the `Three Pass Trip' (see Harman Pass) it makes a convenient resting place. <i>(1935,Handbook Arthur Pass National Park,27</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ??a tramp; one on the tramp or swag Sometimes Maraekakaho [station] entertained ... twenty-two swaggers in a night. Most trampers looking for a job ... were bound for the patriarchal Maclean estate. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,145</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The homestead was full of people : two parties of deerstalkers, one party of trampers - the word `hiker' had not then arrived - and a gentleman of the road. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,55</i>)

>  The term <i>tramper</i> likewise has an English origin, as `one who tramps or travels on foot, a pedestrian', but it may specifically, and less respectably, be a tramp or vagrant. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,29</i>) [NOTES: 1958 Sep Ruth Mason `Track and Trail' in Tararua 29]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,60,. 70</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> ... today fit trampers can journey from the pass to the farmland in Koiterangi in two days ... <i>(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,14</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1965,Memoirs of a Peon,33</i>)

>  <b>MEAD</b> [title page] <i>(1966,Richard Taylor</i>) [NOTES: 1966 Richard Taylor - Missionary Tramper by A.D.Mead/ A.H. & A.W.Reed-]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>Tramping</i> and <i>tramper</i> were used colloquially in England as early as 1908 but the word seems not to have been used in the title of a tramping club until 1919 when it was used in New Zealand. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,160</i>)

>  <i>(Jul 24, 1967,NZ Woman's Weekly,8/2</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I had on my shoulders my few items of clothing ... all packed into a sugar bag transformed by a loop of light rope into that handy pack for trampers which the Maoris called <i>pikau. </i> <i>(1977,More Than Enough,25</i>)

>  <b>ALLAN</b> And a wharf where trampers were gathering attracted by the <i>Titan's</i> hooter. <i>(1981,See Our Island,49</i>)

>  <i>tramper n.</i> N.Z. a person who tramps, or walks long distances, in the bush. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1243</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 817; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 509]]

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Trampers who walk the four-mile bush track from Halfmoon Bay ...come out on the open green patch ... <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,31</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Who were the first trampers? The first person who carried a swag and did it for pleasure and not for its own sake? What about the Maori? Did they go tramping as such or were they out for food, or greenstone, or other Maori? And what about the gold-digger and the musterer? Surely their trades came first and if they enjoyed the walk it was likely that it was because they'd found some good pasture or a `bit of the colour'. <i>(1988,Hills, The,5</i>)

>  I'm not the girl you'd find at a nightclub at 4am, but I'm not a tramper either. I could never go camping with this hair cut. <i>(Feb 6, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  Here are a few suggestions for the wardrobe of the girl tramper. <i>(1929,NZ Railways Magazine,4980 ; 53</i>)

>  WANTED to Buy, Down Sleeping Bag, suitable for tramper. <i>(Nov 24, 1945,Evening Post,4</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

>  TRAMPING TRAGEDY. <i>(Jun 20, 1928,Evening Post,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> hiking

>  <i>tramping n. N.Z. 1. </i>the leisure activity of walking in the bush. 2. <i>(as modifier): tramping boots. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1243</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 817; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 509]]

### Tramping Club
 
 <i>n.</i> an organisation whose members engage in back-country expeditions on foot. [incl attrib; see Hills and Valleys; letter D.Close]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,74</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>Tramping</i> and <i>tramper</i> were used colloquially in England as early as 1908 but the word seems not to have been used in the title of a tramping club until 1919 when it was used in New Zealand. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,160</i>)

>  This is the story of the Victoria University tramping club's first fifty years. <i>(1971,VUWTC'71,5</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> By 1900 these [bucking a rider off one's back] games had faded out of regular school playground play. But out of school, we can assume that these games were still played occasionally. For example, a version of Buck Buck ... under the name Jumbucks is played today by one of New Zealand's tramping clubs (hiking clubs) in its haunts near the snowline. It is said that the hobnailed boots of the players contribute strongly to the hazards of the game. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,194</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> The first tramping club was formed in 1919 and many more followed in the twenties. A hardy lot they must have been. Heavy leather boots studded with hobnails or clinkers on the soles, `kidney-crusher' or A-frame packs, and canvas or cotton tents ... <i>(1988,Hills, The,6</i>)

>  <b>OWEN</b> I finally had enough sense to join the Tramping Club which was greatly despised by the rest of the University. <i>(1998,Snapshots of the Century,24</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> After the band came the Tem Boy Scouts ... and the members of the Tem Tramping Club, and the members of the Tem branch of the Ancient Order of Buffaloes. <i>(1935,Little Country, The,319,1937 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ODELL</b> In February, 1927, a party of Tramping Club members went up to Camp Corner, .. <i>(1935,Handbook Arthur Pass National Park,59</i>)

>  River crossing is an important skill for tramping club members - it used to be the most popular way of trampers dying. <i>(Jan 2, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  DUTY OF TRAMPING CLUBS. <i>(Jun 16, 1926,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>GREIG</b> The public meeting held on July 3, 1919, well attended by leading citizens, resulted in the foundation of the Tararua Tramping Club. <i>(1946,Tararua Story,13</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The Tararua Tramping Club, which was founded in 1919, is the largest [tramping club]. With the Tararua Ranges so readily accessible from Wellington it was in fact the first home of tramp ing. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,II/597</i>)

>  The Tararua Tramping Club held its adjourned meeting last week when a draft constitution and set of rules were submitted and adopted. <i>(Aug 5, 1919,Evening Post,10</i>) [NOTES: Couldn't find similar report in Dominion. Both reports of the first meeting call it simply the Tararua Club and do not use the word "tramping" in the report. I think this is probably the genuine first occurrence of the combination in print.]

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Tramping gear instead of business clothes. ... Had a tramping pack ready just in case. ... He was always going off on tramping trips, seems like.' `He didn't tramp all the way back to Wellington!' <i>(1980,Murder or Three, A,155</i>)

### tramping hut
 
 <i>n.</i> a back-country shelter for members of tramping clubs and others

>  Tramping huts and bivvies are dotted around the back country of New Zealand like nowhere else on earth... Many huts are a legacy of the early pioneering days of exploration and endeavour in the rugged and unforgiving high country. <i>(Nov 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 54/88</i>)

### trampoline
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> I was down there with Old Ashmore's lot, doing the trampoline, and so on; but things was very quizby. Everything went crooked somehow, and the gorger had a hawful cramp in his kick. Well, ramp was the word, and vamose we did, the lot on us; some here, some there. I was reg'lar beached, and I tell you when a cove 'as to spout his smish it's about time to look out. However, I got a job at a livery stable, but although that kep' me in grub, and I slep' in a spare stall, the billet was very multy, and I kep' on the kivvy for something better. [fn] 1. [trampoline] Vaulter. 2. [quizby] Bad. 3. [crooked] Wrong. 4. [cramp in his kick] Emptiness in his pockets. 5. [ramp] Clear out. 6. [vamose] Go. 7. [beached] Hard up. 8. [smish] Shirt. 9. [multy] No good. 10. [kivvy] Look out (qui vive.) <i>(1879,Spangles and Sawdust,5</i>)

### tramway
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also tram](tot-tyr.md#tram))

>  A poll is about to be taken for a loan of GBP30,000 to construct a tramway between Gisborne and the Patutahi quarry, and as it has been shewn in the orignal [sic] prospectus of the 11th March last that the financial success of the proposed tramway depends on the ratification of a tentative agreement entered into by the late members of the Borough Council ... <i>(May 18, 1909,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  <b>HINGSTON</b> [Hokitika] has a wooden road also, in the shape of a tramway, ... on which cars go to the diggings fourteen miles away. <i>(1879,Australian Abroad, The,293</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> The country north of Auckland ... seldom admits of a tramway being laid down to carry the logs on. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,112</i>)

>  The one is the erection ... of a steam saw-mill; the other, the formation of a tramway leading from the forest ... to the mill. <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/487</i>)

>  In the south, rough tramways are laid down in the bush, and the logs hauled on low carriages to the mills by horse- or bullock-teams. here the great round baulks of timber are `broken down' being cut lengthwise (by saws moving up and down vertically) into `flitches', which are passed over to the circular-saws to be ripped into boards and scantling. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,158</i>) [NOTES: (sawmillers')]

>  But before this [treefelling] has been done rolling-roads have been formed, or tramways laid, involving the vast destruction of arboreal growth ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/16</i>)

>  ... an old tramway-track runs through a saddle in the coast range ... <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/536</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> TRAMWAY: Bush wooden rails on which log trucks, originally pulled by horses, travel. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,189</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 189]

>  They first `blaze' or mark a course from the fallen tree to the nearest stream or tramway. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,5/374</i>)

>  <b>JEWELL</b> <i>Tram [way]:</i> A light railway built for the transportation of logs and/or sawn timber. <i>(1948,Accounting in the Timber Industry,128</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The term `tramway' could be applied in New Zealand from 1860 onwards to lines constructed to carry timber from bush to sawmill. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/444</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The previous night he and two others had been driving a tramway downhill taking out the coal and timbering up as they went. <i>(1980,Canterbury Tale, A,128</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> Well, no, sir. It's an aerial tramway. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,159</i>) [NOTES: (coalminers')]

>  The logs were transported to the mill in the case of the Waitanui by a wooden-railed tramway; in the case of the Tamaki they were `driven.' <i>(1913,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.16 (NS) 19</i>) [NOTES: (Henderson and Bartrum, Aroha and Hauraki)]

>  A section of a wooden rail line buried between a landslip since 1938 has been uncovered during efforts to restore a historic log hauler tramway near Otaki Forks. <i>(May 2004,Footnotes,No. 21/14</i>)

>  Cr. Phillips.. moved that any saw mill proprietor be allowed to lay a tram way along the public road, and in reference to junkers, he moved that the use of them be allowed. <i>(Feb 16, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  A tramway could be made, but it would have to be carried about ten miles, and it would not pay to convey the timber by that means. <i>(May 3, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,11</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> First, a broad clearing leads from the interior of the forest, generally straight down the steep mountain slopes, and forms a kind of a road along which the logs are rolled, down to the head of a regular tramway. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),147</i>)

>  The centralization of New Zealand traffic at Manakau [sic] would afford a reasonable prospect for the introduction of steam to Sydney. It would reduce the voyage one-third, and few persons would either adventure by sailing vessels through Cook's Strait or round the North Cape; whilst the produce of our own East Coast, would easily find its way to Onehunga, by the tramway which would assuredly traverse <i>our</i> Epsom downs. <i>(Jan 24, 1849,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  To complete the advantages derivable from steam communication upon the Waikato and Manukau, a tramway (preferable perhaps for the present to a canal) at the Awaroa portage would be requisite. <i>(Apr 17, 1855,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The tramway on which the logs are taken into the mill comes down some steep hills. <i>(Aug 5, 1924,Otago Witness,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  My first lot came from the Nihotupu water pipeline tramway in the Waitakeres. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Memories,No. 30/49</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Trans
 
 <i>n.</i> the Transactions of the Royal Society of New Zealand (earlier, New Zealand Institute) abbrev. in DNZE to <i>TrRS</i> and <i>TrNZI </i> [ORIGIN: abbreviation, ca1945 at least (R. Mason, and others) A common abbreviation used by a good many, but certainly not all, who have occasion to use this series.]

### trans-Tasman
 
 <i>adj.</i> a) travelling or extending across the Tasman Sea: `trans-Tasman travel'. b) held or holding between New Zealand and Australia: `a trans-Tasman pact'. [trans Tasman, transtasman]

>  Fishguard is a recent case in point, and perhaps when it is a question of saving a few hours in the trans-Tasman run, and improving the connection between Australia and New Zealand, Point Elizabeth will have its day. <i>(Sep 10, 1910,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  <i>(Oct 15, 1963,NZ Truth</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>trans-Tasman:</i> The body of water separating New Zealand and Australia is called the Tasman Sea. ... [106] beware of thinking of Australia and New Zealand as one lump. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,105</i>)

>  <i>trans-Tasman a.</i> (of yacht race etc.) traversing the Tasman Sea; (of series of sporting contests etc.) held in both Australia and NZ (<i>Auckland won the Trans-Tasman Cup from Sydney</i>). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,819</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <i>Transtasman rationalisation</i> Union Shipping has joined forces with the New Zealand Line and the Australian National Line in a rationalisation of transtasman shipping. <i>(Sep 8, 1987,Evening Post,40</i>)

>  INWARD MAILS DUE WELLINGTON. BY AIR. Australia, by trans-Tasman service: Every Monday, Wednesday, Thursday, and Friday. <i>(Dec 6, 1945,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  A theatrical fiend of mine predicted one day this week that the Maori League players who went across to Australia some weeks ago have proved to be mighty poor advance agents for the Maori Rugby men who travelled trans-Tasman last week. <i>(Jun 24, 1922,NZ Truth,8</i>)

### trans-Tassie
 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) trans-Tasman; Australian

>  cardwatch..assesses all the cards in New Zealand from one to five stars based on whether you are a "transactor" or a "debtor". <i>(Dec 20, 2005,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

The current cost estimate for Transmission Gully is $1.09 billion compared to the current cost estimate of $730 milllion for the Coastal Route. <i>(Oct 2005,Greater Wellington Regional Council,00,Western Corridor Transportation Study ed.</i>)

### TranzAlpine
 
 <i>n.</i> a passenger train between Christchurch and Greymouth

>  The Tranzalpine train which passes through this surfeit of beauty is a bit of a bone-shaker despite being rated among the six top journeys in the world. <i>(Aug 30, 1999,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  It's not quite the Orient Express but the Tranzalpine is up-market by New Zealand Railway standards... Seemingly existing in a time all of its own, the Tranzalpine clickety-clacks through the rapidly yuppifying Moana.. above Lake Brunner. <i>(Feb 1989,North & South,18</i>)

>  Twice a day the Trans Alpine train passes through the world. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Geographic,52/36</i>)

### TranzAlpine Express
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CHAPMAN & HURST</b> At the end of 1987 a new concept in long distance passenger trains came with the upgrading of the Christchurch-Greymouth Express which was renamed the 'Tranz-Alpine Express' <i>(1990,Radio NZ Sporting Annual,64</i>)

>  My visit to Greymouth was the second I had made in a fortnight; the first was by the Tranz Alpine Express to visit the Coast in between rugby tests... <i>(Aug 2004,Photographer's Mail, The,13 (8)/8</i>)

>  The Tranz Alpine Express gathered speed and the click-clack of the wheels, the gentle swaying and the warm sun on my face had me thinking ... Railways invested $150,000 in the Christchurch-Greymouth train and renamed it the Tranz Alpine. <i>(Nov 12, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),89</i>)

>  Cheaper projects such as the new Tranz-Alpine Express - Christchurch to Greymouth and back, daily - courageously ignore the fact that New Zealand has the population of one medium-sized city spread over a landscape larger than Great Britain... From Springfield the Tranz-Alpine Express jistifies its name, all but hovering over the upper Waimakariri River as it climbs into a magical world of high mountains spectacularly snow-clad in winter, spectacularly barren in summer. <i>(Feb 13, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

### trap
 
 <i>n.</i> a four-wheeled, horse-drawn carriage [OED from 1807]

>  <b>HOOD</b> Cabmen or drivers of `traps' as they term their hideous street vehicles in Wellington. <i>(1875,Chowbokiana,9</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Trap</i>. 3. A smallish, sprung carriage; in Britain, esp. an gig, but in Australia and New Zealand a four-wheeled carriage; from ca. 1805: coll. , by 1900, S.E. Perhaps <i>ex rattle trap. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,906</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a policeman, a cop [Also Aust. AND from 1812. (esp. in pl, THE TRAPS -->very frequent in early <i>Truth</i> 1905 to c1914: e.g. 1905 Aug 12 Truth 8/1/6; and see esp. the citation 1905 of JOHN DUNN]

>  <b>tha</b> The traps I twigged, all got up very nobby,/ By Mr Weldon their commanding bobby./ And Sergeant Chapman, too, I noticed there,/ Togged up just like a trooper I declare;/ <i>(1864,Invercargill Minstrel,3</i>) [NOTES: (The Sessions)]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> Mine [police] are regular traps,/ None of your blue shirt identities./ <i>(1865,Otago Songster,19</i>)

>  I'm a Dunedin peeler,/ .../ Trap, trap, trap, trap! they calls me trap,/ But that I never mind. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,3/62</i>) [NOTES: Trap, Trap]

>  (Some of the Traps collar him, and walk him off to the chokie.) <i>(Aug 5, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Apparently Black pulled a doublecross on McRae, and when the traps came through - 'traps' being what the police were called at the time - Black did nothing about it. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,65</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> trap rock [OED from 1813, seems universal, no obvious NZ difference]

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> On the summits of the [Otago] hills the surface is almost covered with large blocks of stone (a species of trap rock); ... <i>(Apr 24, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Apr 24 and 25 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 211]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> The bed of the Kapanga, in which the gold was found, consists of rocks of quartz, trap, and a bluish granite containing iron pyrites... In the detritus on the sides of the mountains, and in the beds of the stream, quartz occurs in detached blocks of various sizes, mixed with fragments of slate, granite and trap; but regular dykes of quartz are not generally met with, although they are reported to exist. <i>(1853,Auckland,91</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> trapdoor to the cells; or fig.

>  [Headline] Kathleen Mavourneen Stakes [sub] Sheehan a Sure Starter ... Sheehan did not exhibit any intention of worrying, and when sentenced to twelve months and labelled as an habitual criminal, which at his age (63) means life, he passed down the trap feeling apparently relieved. <i>(Jun 4, 1910,NZ Truth,258/7/5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [running trap - plumbing and other uses, OED from 1880, doubt any NZ specificity]

>  <i>Trap, running</i>- U-shaped trap with inlet and outlest at the same level. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,120</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [petrol trap; not in OED]

>  <i>Trap, petrol</i>- Trap installed in the drainage system to prevent ingress of petrol into the drainage system and fitted with vent pipes to carry off the petrol vapour <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,120</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [oil trap OED 1863 re coal oil, 1927 re machinery]

>  <i>Trap, oil</i>- Trap to prevent oil entering the drainage system. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,120</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [trap-screw; ubiquitous on google but no OED mention]

>  <i>Trap-screw</i>- Screwed plug giving access to a trap for cleaning purposes. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,120</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [anti-siphonage trap; 1989 Charles Ransom notes: Trap, antisiphonage - Not found elsewhere in this form, but Webster II has <i>antisiphon</i> as an adjective designating such a trap.]

>  <i>Trap, anti-siphonage (resealing trap)-</i> Trap designed to prevent siphonage unsealing the trap. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,120</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [deep seal trap - plumbing not sealing!]

>  <i>Trap, deep seal</i>- Trap having a water seal of 75 mm or more. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,120</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [interceptor trap]

>  <i>Trap, interceptor</i>- Trap situated on a drain at some point between a sewer and the lowest inlet to the drain to prevent the passage of air or gases from the sewer to the drain. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,120</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [neutralizing / neutralising trap]

>  <i>Trap, neutralizing</i>- Trap containing crushed limestone or caustic alkali to neutralize acid waste. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,120</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [offal trap]

>  <i>Trap, offal</i>- Trap for preventing waste matter or refuse entering the drainage system. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,120</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Clematis</i> spp. other than <i>C. vitalba</i> [1938 Godwits Fly HYDE Page 39 Edition 1970 Quote ... on scrubby bushes grew the pale-starred clematis, traveller's joy.(which species?)]

### treadin
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a narrow graduated metal fencepost used in temporary fencing, similar to a standard or waratah.

>  .. 2 station saddles, elastrators, Gallagher tredin standards ... <i>(Nov 10, 1993,Napier Daily Telegraph,21</i>)

>  There are a number of treadins or temporary posts available. Treadins should be spaced at 10-12m. <i>(2000,Lifestyle Farmer,Autumn 66</i>)

>  Use pigtails for cattle and treadins for sheep. <i>(2000,Gallagher Power Fence Manual,29</i>)

>  He has designed a new hotwire tread-in with the pigstail curl at the top extended into a simple handle. [tredin; tread-in] <i>(2002,Lifestyle Farmer,Aug/Sept 11</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>treadly</i>: a bicycle [1981 Rangiora High School (Goldie Brown Collection)]

 <i>phr.</i> a trio of financial politicians (Roger Douglas, Richard Prebble, David Caygill) in the Lange-led fourth Labour government

### Treaty
 
 <i>adj.</i> [pertaining to the Treaty of Waitangi]

>  <b>Lambert</b> The wealth of its carving ... will compare favourably with those of the Waitangi meeting-house, contemplated to memorialize the recent Treaty centenary celebrations. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,138</i>)

>  Winston has won the ballot, something of a coup so close to an election, though his version does little more than delete the offensive phrase "treaty principles" from 27 acts. <i>(Feb 20, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  He then laid into "the elements within our leadership" who, he says, are prepared to sell Treaty rights for cash - "mixing the kai with the tapu", as he put it, "having their hands in the hangi". <i>(Jan 14, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  The imposition of the so-called "Treaty process" upon New Zealanders, by an unholy alliance of left-wing social engineers and Maori activists, was actually a covert declaration of war against the better part of all things European in this country. <i>(Aug 2, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  `Pakeha' is a word that seems to be bugging many New Zealanders. Perhaps this is the reason why the Royal Commission on Social Policy in its booklet dealing with the Treaty of Waitangi uses the word `Tauiwi' instead of `Pakeha'. For example a section of the booklet is headed `Partnership between Maori and Tauiwi'. Tauiwi, according to the booklet's glossary, means `later settlers from another country'. A Dictionary of the Maori Language ... gives `strange tribe, foreign race' as the meaning of Tauiwi. As a New Zealander born and bred in this country I was disturbed at the unceremonious use of a word which seems to make me some sort of foreigner in my own land. ... On phoning the commission's office I was informed that despite considerable unease over the use of Tauiwi in the treaty booklet staff were told that it had to be used. I was also informed that the use of Tauiwi instead of Pakeha had been discontinued <i>(Jun 6, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,11</i>)

>  Sir Paul Reeves refers to Aotearoa as a `Treaty driven' society. ... To use the phrase `treaty driven' is to raise questions about who drives, who rides, ... <i>(Mar 1988,Human Rights Commission Focus</i>) [NOTES: (H. Kaa)]

>  She sees the Treaty process as a disincentive, however, because it has created an expectation in some quarters that really big benefits are on the way... The Treaty process has reinforced the iwi model, perhaps because iwi are nice, neat compartmentable boxes into which you can put things, such as apologies and reparations, whereas urban Maori are a comparatively ill-defined lot. <i>(Mar 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (with def. art.) the Treaty of Waitangi [also Tiriti also attrib. uses below] (See [also Tiriti](ten-tot.md#tiriti))

>  <b>MATHEW</b> Accordingly, [we] sailed down to the place of Rendezvous where the ceremony of signing the Treaty (as it was called) was soon performed, ... <i>(Mar 4, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar 4 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 70]

>  <b>STACK</b> I beg to apologize for the very soiled state of the treaty, but the native habits are so filthy it could hardly be avoided. <i>(May 23, 1840,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1840 May 23 James Stack to Willoughby Shortland, Acting Col.Secretary in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 104]

>  <b>HOBSON</b> The treaty which forms the base of all my proceedings was signed at Waitangi on the 6th February 1840, by 52 chiefs, 26 of whom were of the confederation, and formed a majority of those who signed the Declaration of Independence. This [=?, or copy of treaty] instrument I consider to be <i>de facto</i> the treaty, and all signatures that are subsequently obtained are merely testimonials of adherence to the original document. <i>(Apr 25, 1840,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 25 Hobson to Bunbury in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 17]

>  Observe what the Treaty was that was framed under those instructions to Captain Hobson: ... [quoting Hobson] When I had finished reading the Treaty, I invited the chiefs to ask explanations on any points they did not comprehend, and to make any observations or remarks on it they pleased. <i>(Jul 1845,House of Commons: Parliamentary Debates (UK),Vol. LXXXI col. 947</i>) [NOTES: 1845 July Sir Robert Peel in House of Commons: Parliamentary Debates (G.B.) Vol. LXXXI col. 947 in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents4 14A page 7]

>  We are having this function next anniversary of the signing of the Treaty to [87] acknowledge the Bledisloe gift. <i>(Jun 11, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/86</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> `Don't let the <i>pakehas</i> brainwash you. ... All the time we're here I feel guilty because I'm not up north campaigning against the Treaty.' <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,49</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> the rough copy of the treaty was sent to me to get copied.... I sat up late copying the treaty on parchment and I kept the original draft for my pains. <i>(Feb 5, 1840,Journal,2/189</i>)

>  ... it will be found of interest to give in regular order the printing that was done after the treaty was signed: - Feb. 17 Captain Hobson, R.N.: Compositing and printing 200 copies of treaty, foolscap folio, [pounds]1 10s. 6d.; 41/2 quires of foolscap paper for above, at 2s., 9s. [from Colenso `The Signing ...'] <i>(Feb 17, 1840,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 17 in 1901 TrNZI 33/423 (Hill Printing)]

>  On the morning of the 14th ... two tribes ... requested that their names might be withdrawn from the treaty <i>(1841,GBPP,(No.311) 11</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 17 Hobson Report in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 11]

>  T is for Treaty; unusually confused term, used both to describe the founding document of a nation and as a codename for a runaway gravy train. <i>(Jul 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  But his trick is to have found a formulation of words that gives people permission to feel uncomfortable about immigration and the Treaty, without feeling guilty. <i>(Jul 22, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Among the chiefs present was the son of Te Heu Heu, that fine barbarian who was the last of the old heathen warriors, who could neither be coerced or persuaded to sign the Treaty. <i>(Mar 17, 1908,Feilding Star,4</i>)

>  However, even this is being questioned, as we see the Treaty being used in ways that were not intended. Using the Treaty to get out of paying dog registration fees on the grounds that they are a taonga, and claiming preferential health treatment are two examples. <i>(Feb 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  I think National's equivalents are the action and courage around Treaty issues. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

### Treaty industry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  They did not foresee that one day Pakeha would set up a Treaty industry. <i>(Dec 17, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  ACT was bounding away on its cynical promises to end the "treaty industry" and lock up criminals for even longer terms than National has imposed this past nine years. <i>(Dec 1999,North & South,170</i>)

>  This widening gap between the lives of claimants and those farming the Treaty industry is a recipe for social disaster... Sooner rather than later ministers will be forced to take a grip of the Treaty industry or it will grip them. <i>(May 1, 2002,Dominion,10</i>)

>  "This change has been brought about by the Treaty industry that the Government has fostered and funded through the Waitangi Tribunal, the Crown Forestry Rental Trust and the Fisheries Commission." <i>(Feb 8, 2003,Dominion Post,A15</i>)

>  The treay industry is awash with cash. <i>(Feb 6, 2002,Dominion,12</i>)

>  [The government] has also vetoed the Treaty industry's attempt to have listed under the definition of "historic heritage" councils have to preserve in approving developments, non-definable terms such as "cultural landscapes", "ancestral landscapes" and "spiritual resources". <i>(May 25, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  During the 1990s, the Treaty industry continued to breathe strange reasoning into that slender 1840 document, which, like the 1985 act, had been hastily cobbled together. <i>(May 27, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  For our sins, and because of our craven readiness to go along with the excesses of the Treaty industry, we have to tolerate offering jobs to Maori-only candidates. <i>(Jun 16, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C12</i>)

### Treaty of Waitangi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Chatham Island Morioris have joined the chorus of discontent at the Government's selection of the Treaty of Waitangi Fisheries Commission. <i>(Jun 1, 1993,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Though by no means faultless, the measure [1846 Constitution Act] seems calculated to be a fair opening to the true history of our great Southern Colony, after the preliminary chapters of tragi-comic blundering. It sweeps away the whole system of official machinery and self-impeding sophistry established by Lord Stanley - all the `Treaty of Waitangi' nonsense, and all the past Downing-Street plans for hindering the settlement of the islands.' Henceforward, echoed the Colonial Gazette, `the humbug treaty of Waitangi' would be `very properly laid on the shelf'. <i>(Jan 2, 1847,Wellington Spectator</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Jan 2 Wellington Spectator in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 149]

>  A Parliamentary committee was thwarted only by the early election from sparking a radical and long-awaited overhaul of the Treaty of Waitangi industry. <i>(Jun 21, 2002,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Race relations in New Zealand will never be harmonious while we have the Treaty of Waitangi industry promoting racial discrimination. <i>(Oct 29, 1992,Evening Post,6</i>)

### Treaty trout
 
 <i>n.</i> (humorous, for trout illegally taken)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> The sport turned out to be spearing trout. ... Seeing Henry's wonderment, Moses gave him a sly dig in the ribs. `These the treaty trout,' he grinned. `Waitangi, you know, the Maori own all the fish for ever. God bless the Queen!' <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,22,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> `treaty trout'. A reference to the widely-held belief in a clause of the Treaty of Waitangi, signed in 1840 between Governor Hobson and some of the Maori chiefs, which ostensibly guarantees to Maoris the right to hunt and fish for as long as they wish in the country of which they gave sovereignty to Queen Victoria, the queen Moses blesses. Recent historical research shows that the supposed clause is not present in the Maori text that was signed, though it occurs in some of the variant English versions. ... In any case Maoris have not been exempt from laws restricting fishing and hunting. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,136 [note 11 to p.22]</i>)

### treatyism
 
 <i>n.</i> an excessive reliance on, or application of, the principles of the Treaty of Waitangi.

>  It is seen that Justice Elias is an admitted judicial activist, a supporter of treatyism, a de fact race separatist... Despite all this, Justice Elias is recruited as Chief Justice in the apparent expectation she will help Parliament ablate, if not suppress, public opposition to treatyism... Even Albert Einstein, brilliant in one orbit, was a dunce in another, as Justice Elias seems over treatyism. <i>(Apr 16, 1999,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  In his eyes, Dr Prasad committed the unpardonable sin of challenging the all-pervasive advance of Treatyism, with its implications of preferential treatment for Maori. <i>(Nov 7, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

### treatyology
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Perhaps realising this topic could be seen as disloyal, English revised and delivered a 2002 speech on the :"Treatyology" of relations instead... <i>(Oct 29, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  English's Robert Chapman lecture last night, which Upton was originally asked to deliver, focussed on what he calls "treatyology" and the challenge of ethnic diversity, which he illustrated by reference to his "Samoan-Italian-Pakeha" son's school class in which there are only three obviously "European" names. ... English's "treatyology" is "public doctrine and public policy that ... asserts that the Treaty of Waitangi underpins a contemporary constitutional partnership between Maori and the Crown or its agents and that this partnership will continue to develop towards a bifurcated state". <i>(Oct 4, 2005,NZ Herald,A13</i>)

>  "Labour can fiddle all they like, but treatyology and low expectations are rife in the education sector ..." <i>(Dec 17, 2004,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### tree avalanche
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I find that the drowned trees had probably come from what the Fiordlanders call a tree avalanche. Because Fiordland was so thoroughly gouged by glaciers, there is virtually no topsoil on the steeper valley sides. Even the fully-grown forest has only the precarious hold of a woven skin of roots that stretch over rock. This net has a weak grip and if one section tears free it can peel the forest from the cliff-faces in great strips. The fall of just one tree can trigger a tree avalanche. <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,92</i>)

### tree fern
 
 <i>n.</i> a large fern having a trunk-like stem with fronds at the top. See also ponga, mamaku, wheki (See [also fern tree](f69-fin.md#fern-tree))

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> High tree-ferns [fn <i>Cyathea medullaris</i> and <i>dealbata</i>with the cabbage-palm [fn <i>Areca sapida</i>], strike the eye as the most beautiful forms of New Zealand vegetation. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/27</i>)

>  <b>MANSON</b> The allotments were made to consist of 100 acres each, and these are what we call `sections'. ... Papa has taken a great deal of pains with it, indeed I think he has too much taste for a Colonist, for he cannot bear to see the beautiful trees and tree-ferns cut down. <i>(1971,I Take Up My Pen,25</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Mary Swainson in 1971 Manson I Take Up My Pen 25]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Amongst the most remarkable of these are the lofty tree-ferns of New Zealand, the <i>Cyathea dealbata</i>, which attains a height of forty fee, the <i>Cyathea medullaris</i>, the <i>Dicksonia squamosa</i>, and the <i>Mahrattia elegans.</i> <i>(1866,Polynesia,47</i>) [NOTES: [? f 1860 Bennett Gatherings of a Naturalist]]

>  Some people speak and write of them as `tree ferns' ... this is of course loose and misleading. There are fern-trees on my section up to fifty feet in height, and at least one of them - the wheki variety, is a hard-bitten fellow ... The unobservant are apt to lump all fern-trees together, as pongas, or, in the vernacular, `bungers'. <i>(Mar 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Everywhere are to be seen Tree-ferns, (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i> and <i>dealbata</i>) some of them with stems forty to sixty feet high, ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,144</i>)

>  ... in many places the undergrowth consisted of young tree [84]ferns and cabbage plants. many of the ferns attain a great height but the palms more rarely, probably for their being cut down at a certain age for the sake of the heart of the young stem which is very good to eat either raw or cooked, being something between the heart of a cabbage and a cocoanut. <i>(Apr 3, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/83</i>) [NOTES: (H.R. Richmond)]

>  Tree Fern .....<i> O.</i> Filices; <i>G.</i> Cyathaea; <i>S.</i> C. dealbata or Silverfern and C. medullaris. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/37 The stately tree-fern leaned aside/ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/336</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> ... it is again ferns ... that meet the eye, magnificent Tree ferns, their trunks as if coated with scales, and with neatly shaped crowns (<i>Dicksonia</i> and <i>Cyathea</i>). <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),133</i>)

>  ... and an undergrowth which comprises many species of ferns, including tree ferns [fn .<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>.]. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,186</i>)

>  I have [a kakariki nest] made entirely of feathers, moss and the downy scales of tree ferns (<i>Dicksonia</i>); ... <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,480</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  Tree Fern. Cyathea dealbata, Swartz ... Tree Fern. Cyathea medullaris, Swartz ... Tree Fern. Cyathea Smithii, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/51</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  While botanising in the high and dry woods near Matamau, I came upon a fine tree fern (Cyathea dealbata). <i>(Aug 27, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,34</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> The Tree Fern is a very striking object in the woods, it grows from 20 to 40 feet in hight [sic] and from four to eight inches in diameter in its stem, which is of a spongy brittle nature. <i>(1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 356]

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> ... lofty trees ..., the graceful tree-fern and a sort of tufted reed-like grass tree of very bright vivid green; the ground covered in many places by a most delicate carpet of a peculiarly beautiful creeping fern. <i>(Apr 7, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 7 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 106]

>  Another small portion lying along the banks of fresh water creeks, covered with evergreens and tree ferns, affords a rich, pliable clay, ... <i>(1843,Report of Auckland Agricultural Society</i>) [NOTES: 1843 First Report of the Committee of the Auckland Agricultural Society in 1853 Swainson Auckland 21]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... there is an undergrowth ... composed of the <i>nikau</i> palm (areca sapida), and the beautiful tree-fern, which ... has been aptly styled the king of ferns. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/245</i>)

>  <b>ARNOLD</b> Then there is the cabbage-tree and the beautiful tree-fern. <i>(Apr 26, 1848,Letters</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Apr 26 Arnold Letters (1966) 41]

>  [see cit BUSH attrib] <i>(Apr 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/79</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> The traveller ... on arriving in New Zealand, finds himself surrounded by a vegetation that is almost wholly new to him; with little that is at first sight striking except the Tree-fern and <i>Cordyline</i> of the northern parts, and nothing familiar, except possibly the Mangrove; ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants xxvii</i>)

>  <i>tree-fern</i> large fern with upright woody stem <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,821</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1249; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 12]]

>  <b>POTTS</b> We now pass under the Tree-ferns; the tribe Cyathea is nobly represented by some fine species ... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,55</i>)

>  <b>Prince</b> The Beech Hut is built of slabs with a tree-fern roof, and contains eight sleeping berths ... <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cyathea medullaris</i>, mamaku

>  (And) <i>Cyathea medullaris</i> black tree fern <i>(1925,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The great uncoiling fronts [of the mamuku] are thickly clothed with dark brown scales ... It is these scales which gave the name black tree-fern in contradistinction to the name silver tree-fern given to the ponga (Cyathea dealbata) with its silvery-undersided fronds. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,273</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Black treefern .. mamaku <i>Cyathea medullaris</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>black tree fern [=] mamaku [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,5</i>)

>  <b>CROOKES</b> ... we can be sure that the beauty-loving soul of the ancient Maori delighted whole-heartedly in these lovely inhabitants of the New Zealand bush. However, his interest in the different tree ferns was certainly not purely aesthetic. Take, for instance, the mamaku, or `black tree fern' (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>). This is one of the largest and most handsome of all our tree ferns. It can be distinguished by the black stalks of the fronds and the leaf scars that form a definite pattern all round the slender black stem. <i>(1926,Plant Life in Maoriland,10</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> [311] <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i> (The Tree Fuchsia) ... Maori name Kotukutuku or Kohutuhutu, and of the fruit, Konini. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,294,1940 ed.</i>)

Very often called konini, but konini is the berry of kohutuhutu, edible if rather insipid. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BURTON</b> Other berries popular as delicacies were the fleshy, white, pineapple-shaped bracts and berries of the kiekie (<i>Freycinetia banksii</i>), the ripe, black berries of the makomako or wineberry (<i>Aristotelia serrata</i>) and the small, sweet berries (known as konini) of the kotukutuku or tree fuchsia (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>). <i>(1980,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i> TREE FUCHSIA ... Kootukutuku <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,70</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> The flowers of the tree fuchsia (<i>Fuchsia excortica</i>) are mostly produced towards the ends of woody branches... <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,35</i>)

>  Our native tree fuchsia reputedly bears the tastiest fruit, being likened to blueberries or gooseberries. <i>(Jan 12, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> A wide variety of plants and trees crowded the track as I made my way up to Harpers Pass. Mountain holly, the Mount Cook lily, tree fuchsia, the turpentine shrub, mountain pine. <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,191</i>)

### tree grass
 
 <i>n.</i> ? tussock; ? toetoe

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> Beyond the bush land, that which is called grass land chiefly produces a very cutting wiry species, very similar to that which in swamps we call tree grass, but without the stem or trunk on which the former is elevated above the water surface; these plumes of grass on this dry land in burning diffuse a fragrant and aromatic odour, ... <i>(May 13, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 13 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 220]

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> ... the prevailing species of trees are remo [rimu], totara, and tall manuka, all indicative of inferior soil.... The bush-like woods on the east side are of white birch, likewise no recommendation of the soil, but the chief objection to the district is, that the prairie soil affords scarcely any food for cattle ... it grows only the wiry tree grass [tussock] ... and <i>Phormium tenax</i> [flax], no aniseed, perennial groundsel, or milk thistle, and very little couch grass. <i>? Carex secta (Niggerhead) </i> ... [Andersen notes:] Tuckett saw a grass, `a very cutting wiry species, very similar to that which in swamps we call tree grass, but without the stem or trunk on whic h the former is elevated above the water surface; these plumes of grass on this dry land in burning diffuse a fragrant and aromatic odour, but as food for cattle they are valueless.' HCE, p. 220. - Dr. D. Munro called this the grass-tree, speaking of `grass-tree swamps.' HCE, p. 245. <i>(Aug 16, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Aug 16 Tuckett's Diary in 1898 Hocken contributions 223-224]

### tree lucerne
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Chamaecytisus palmensis</i>. A small quick-growing tree, native to the Canary islands but naturalised in NZ. [Jim writes: Both <i>tree lucerne</i> and <i> tagasaste</i> are recorded in Macquarie 1981 (there classified as <i>Ch. prolifer</i>) but not in any other dictionary I have seen. It is interesting that there is no reference to this tree in Guthrie-Smith's <i>Tutira</i>. Its naturalisation at least in Hawke's Bay is thus likely to be later than 1939.] (See [tagasaste](t-ten.md#tagasaste))

>  A search party was formed, gumboots scuffing the gravel of the driveway, past the ute and the well, past the Californian lilacs and the tree lucernes, until we came to a clearing, and there was the gum tree on its side. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  On these south slopes, you can see the native plants popping up through the gorse and tree lucerne. <i>(Jan 5, 2006,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Three-quarters deciduous, one-quarter evergreen with lots of tree lucerne stuffed in the gaps as minders. <i>(Jun 30, 2002,NZ Catholic,17</i>)

>  <i> lucerne, tree [s] Cytisus proliferus </i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 80 tree lucerne [s] Cytisus proliferus </i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,45</i>)

>  W3 Mainly Aust (1933 Aust) =TAGASTE, a shrub (Cytisus proliferus) of the Canary Islands that yields cattle fodder. <i>(Sep 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,208/1</i>)

The tree-manuka is kanuka, with smaller leaves and smaller flowers than the scrub-manuka, and it is the kanuka that is usually sold for firewood. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### tree tomato
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tree Tomato Pie. - Skin the tomatoes by pouring boiling water over them. <i>(Jul 27, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,59</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [These were thought to be first mentioned in NZ in the 1891-92 Seed Catalogue of D Hays and Sons, Nurserymen of Parnell, but see quote 1887 Auckland Weekly] (See [also tamarillo](t-ten.md#tamarillo))

>  It appears that from Kew, during the last three years, hundreds of packets of seed of the tree tomato have been distributed to various people in the colonies, very favourable accounts having been received of the introduction of the fruit into Southern India, Ceylon and other places. <i>(May 21, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,28</i>)

>  The tree tomato likes a rich, well-drained soil. <i>(1921,Brett's Gardening Guide,201</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition]

>  Tree tomatoes sold at 3s 6d to 6s per case. <i>(Mar 30, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,46</i>)

>  On February 1, 1967, the name tamarillo officially replaced tree tomato. <i>(Feb 1967,Orchardist NZ,23</i>)

>  The tamarillo or tree tomato, <i>Cyphomandra betacea</i> Sendt.. is a native of Brazil and Peru. <i>(Jun 1970,Orchardist NZ,149</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> For household use we had plum,lemon, grapefruit, peach and nectarine trees and a small grove of tree tomatoes (tamarillos today). <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,83</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The origin of the old name [sc. of tamarillo], tree tomato, is fairly obvious since it does indeed look like an elongated tomato growing on a tree, but although the tomato and tamarillo belong to the same family, the Solonacaea, there is no similarity between the flavours. <i>(1985,Delectable Fruits Cookery,146</i>)

>  Tree-tomato (<i>Cyphomandra betacea</i>) . This plant is a native of tropical America, but has been introduced into Jamaica, India, and other countries, including the Australasian Colonies .A quantity of fruit was this summer sold by Wellington fruiterers under the name of "Queensland tomato," and quite took the fancy of buyers. <i>(1894,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,II/79</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  Tree Tomato Chutney. Put 2 dozen tree tomatoes in boiling water to remove skins. ... <i>(1936,Cookery Book of NZ Women's Institutes,168</i>)

>  Other fruits [grown at Kerikeri], of which there are fairly extensive plantings, are Chinese gooseberries and tree tomatoes. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,10</i>) [NOTES: 1948 Our Own Country (from Korero, AEWS magazine) 10]

>  <i>(May 8, 1959,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22/3</i>)

>  <i>(1962,Short Stories (various authors),359</i>) [NOTES: 1962 Duckworth in 1966 Short Stories 359]

>  <b>TURNER</b> Among cultivated plants, <i>silver beet, tree tomato</i> and <i>Chinese gooseberry </i>seem to be commoner in New Zealand than in other English-speaking countries. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,172</i>)

>  The currency of <i>tree tomato</i> (p. 263) might also owe something to New Zealand, where the tree is well known and has always been called <i>tree tomato</i> (until in 1967 the growers decided to change the name to <i>tamarillo</i>, thought to be a better name for publicity). <i>(1968,Anglia,86/359</i>) [NOTES: G.W.Turner review Pitcairnese Language in Anglia 86/359]

>  <b>GEE</b> For household use we had (...) a small grove of tree tomatoes (tamarillos today). <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,83</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Supplies of tropical fruits were curtailed because of the Japanese threats to the sea lanes, and tamarillo (or tree tomato as they were then known) growing began in earnest. [WWII] <i>Ibid.</i> 141 New Zealanders always used to know the tamarillo by the rather uninspired name of `tree tomato', but after the fruit became a big export business in the 1960s, the growers decided to change the name to the far more appealing `tamarillo', in 1967. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,31</i>)

>  <i>tree-tomato</i> tamarillo. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,821</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 512]]

>  Although it sounds Spanish, the name 'tamarillo' is another New Zealand invention. The former name 'tree tomato', was considered inappropriate for use overseas where people buying the fruits for the first time might expect them to taste like tomatoes. In 1967, orchardist Bill Thomson took the Maori word 'tama' added 'tillo' (taken from tomatillo, a complete unrelated Mexican fruit) and then (just to make the sound more mellifluous) he replaced 't' with an 'r'. Thus, 'tamarillo' was born. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,58</i>)

>  <b>CRICHTON</b> Tree Tomato. - The plant known under this name is Solanum betaceum (Cyphomandra betacea), a tall evergreen shrub or small tree, which sometimes attains a height of twenty feet. It is indigenous to Central America. <i>(1893,Australasian Fruit Culturalist, The,II/423</i>)

>  MESSRS GREEN AND CO., having received instructions, will sell by Public Auction at their Yards 5000 TREES. Consisting of .. Pears, Guavas, Tree Tomatoes ... <i>(Aug 1, 1900,Marlborough Express,3</i>)

>  The tree tomato is much better known commercially than the egg-plant, but this is probably owing to more having been put upon the market. <i>(Dec 31, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,8</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### treedozer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bulldozer specifically used to knock down trees and push root, trunk and branches into a stack.

>  <b>FERRIS</b> The roughing out work was done with either a bulldozer or a treedozer. The treedozer had the advantage of leaving a windrow on either side of the blade as it passes through the ground surface in the roughing out operation. <i>(1940,Cavalcade of Farming 1840-1940,70</i>)

>  <b>FERRIS</b> When the treedozer was used for 'roughing out,' windrows were left throughout the paddock containing stumps, bush, debris and some soil. <i>(1949,Cavalcade of Farming 1840-1940,71</i>)

>  The dozer pushes everything - sawn trees and those it overturns itself, plus all the undergrowth - into windrows. [dozer] <i>(Mar 9, 1967,NZ Farmer,8</i>)

### trefoil
 
 <i>n.</i> [?should TREFOIL go to GRASS; or a separate CLOVER (as TUSSOCK) entry: prob. most to be omitted as gen English]

>  trefoil [=] black medick: clustered clover: haresfoot refoil: hop trefoil: lesser suckling clover: oxalis: striated clover: suckling clover [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,80</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> stone clover, pussy tail

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> HARESFOOT TREFOIL (<i>Trifolium arvense</i>), also occasionally called stone clover and pussy tail, is a weed of light dry gravelly soils and roadsides over both islands. It is an annual with greyish, downy, very inconspicuous leaves, ... and the hairy flower heads, feeling something like a hare's foot, are very characteristic. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,108</i>)

>  <i>haresfoot trefoil [s] Trifolium arvense</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,36</i>)

>  <i>trefoil, haresfoot</i> [s] <i>Trifolium arvense</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,80</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> trefoil, English [=] black medick</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,80</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUTTON & HECTOR</b> The Arara of the Maoris, or the Trevally or Cavalli of the fishermen. <i>(1872,Fishes,110</i>)

>  <i>hairy birdsfoot trefoil [=] lotus hispidus </i>[s] <i>Ibid.</i> 80 trefoil, hairy birdsfoot [=] lotus hispidus [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,35</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>hop trefoil</i> [s] <i>Trifolium campestre </i>Schreb.<i>Ibid.</i> 80 <i>trefoil, hop </i>[s] <i>Trifolium campestre</i> Schreb <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,38</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>trefoil, lesser [=] lesser suckling clover</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,80</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> suckling clover

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SUCKLING CLOVER (<i>Trifolium dubium</i>) was formerly named <i>T. minus</i>. Alternative common names are yellow suckling clover and little trefoil. it is common in pastures throughout all the sown grass lands and even where no sowing has taken place. Its seeds pass unharmed through sheep, and are therefore scattered wherever sheep can travel. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,105,1967 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> But the ... the bird's-foot trefoil, ... and the foxglove, to mention only a few British plants, are now wild in various places. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,135</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> <i>Birdsfoot trefoil.</i> - Under this heading can be considered<i> Lotus major, Lotus corniculatus, Lotus hispidus,</i> and <i>Lotus augustissumus. </i> <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,105</i>)

>  <i>birdsfoot trefoil, [=] [lotus </i>sp(p) [s]] ... <i>birdsfoot trefoil, hairy [=] lotus hispidus</i> [s] <i>Ibid.</i> 80 <i>trefoil, birdsfoot [=] [lotus</i> spp. [s] ] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,5</i>)

### Trekka
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand's only locally designed and mass-produced motor vehicle

>  "I took a Trekka down to the phonebox - I ran out of petrol - to send a telegram on behalf of the Mt Eden Committee."... The Trekka became known as "the poor man's Land Rover", and many an unfortunate motorist made the mistake of treating their Trekka as if it were a Land Rover, and came to grief when attempting to tackle rough terrain... Even so, the maker found it necessary to suggest to new Trekka owners that "if the engine fails to start ... a push start may be sufficient." <i>(Dec 31, 2001,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Making the boxy Trekka the centre of New Zealand's contribution to the 2003 Venice Biennale bemused New Zealanders, as well as those who visited. ... The Trekka is an extinct fossil that led nowhere, not even to a kiwi or tuatara, which if clunky, have at least survived millions of years. <i>(Nov 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  You are right, Brian Easton, the Trekka was but a fleeting moment in our manufacturing history ... but the Trekka existed. <i>(Nov 20, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  Four "arts ambassadors" from public galleries will spend six weeks working as attendants at the New Zealand exhibition, <i>This is the Trekka</i>, at the 50th Venice Biennale of Art. <i>(May 27, 2003,Dominion Post,A10</i>)

>  Yes, yes, that ruffled bikini looks fetching, and you've got to love those surfboards, but the star of the picture is the Trekka - a forgotten New Zealand design classic... It's a brilliant story, New Zealand's only locally designed and mass-produced motor vehicle, the Trekka, was the brainchild of Noel Turner, a millionaire who supported Labour. The first Trekka rolled off his assembly line in Otahuhu ion 1966. It came in two colours... It had a four cylinder engine, and took 11 gallons in the tank... Turner's formula was a Skoda mechanical kit, bought at basement prices from Czechoslovakia, bolted to a simple boxy body. <i>(Apr 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  Stevenson has transformed the church into an Iron Curtain-style trade fair exhibition, taking its cue from the Trekka, the only New Zealand-designed and mass-produced motor vehicle, which was based on mechanical components from then-communist Czechoslovakia... The Trekka was the ultimate product of a mnyriad of import restrictions in the 1960s, designed to foster motor vehicle assembly in New Zealand. It was loved by those who admired it's [sic] cheap no-nonsense style as a Land Rover lookalike... "The Trekka is a way of repsenting the idea of New Zealand as an industrialised state, an example of what New Zealand in those days could become"... <i>(Jun 22, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),26</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Trentham stoop
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CLEVELAND</b> Used 1939-1945 by Army servicemen to describe a special posture adopted by the people trained at the Central Military District school of instruction by the permanent staff instructors, and especially noticeable in the attitude adopted when making a salute or when marching at attention, as the buttocks were thrust back and the arms swung, or hung, stiffly and slightly forward as the situation demanded. <i>(1945,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: WW2]

### Trents
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf Dinkums](dig-dou.md#dinkums),[Dinks](../D#dinks))

>  We have about 1400 men on board [the troopship] all belonging to the Trents ... <i>(Oct 14, 1915,Great Adventure,76,1988 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Gray)]

>  Sir, - `The Gosling' ... warbles a satirical ditty on the Dinkums. (Applause.) Now listen. As an `Extra Unit' we hit Trentham like any mudlarks in the early winter of 1915. We were dubbed, inter alia:- The Trentham Battalion, Trentham Regiment, `Trents,' Lord Liverpool's Own, Earl of Liverpool's Own, England's Last 'ope, Fulton's Blind Half Hundred, The Portuguese Army, Rifle Regiment, N.Z. Rifle Brigade, The Dinkums, and The Dinks. Amateur army evolution? Sure! <i>(Jul 1, 1918,Quick March,26</i>)

>  The new-born battalions, popularly known as "The Trents," were drawn up on the parade ground 2200 strong in the form of a hollow square, and presented a brave showing, the whole of the men, with the exception of a very small percentage, being well matured and of excellent physique. <i>(Jun 2, 1915,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> The Rifle Brigade, or Trents as they were affectionately called, for they were trained at Trentham. unaltered, and behind it the open tray, like a lorry. <i>Ibid.</i> 267 With the formation of the Rifle Brigade, or the Trents, as they were affectionately called, for they were trained at Trentham, the shortage of men became acute. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,266</i>)
