### Trev
 
 <i>n.</i> a typical New Zealander, one of Fred Dagg's sons and often the butt of his remarks

>  "She'll be right, Trev - kick it in the guts," he hollered from a back paddock somewhere near Taihape, decked out in his iconic black singlet and gumboots. <i>(Dec 20, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

### trevally
 
 <i>n.</i> SI name for (blue) warehou [aka trevalli]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> [quoting Hector <i>op. cit</i>.] Trevalli or cavalli of the fishermen ... In Auckland it is sometimes called yellow-tail, but this name appears to be also used for the king-fish. The fish known aa trevalli in the Dunedin market is a different fish, allied to the warehou. [Neptonemus brama] <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,99</i>)

>  <i>Seriolella brama</i> Gunther. The warehou is commonly sold in Dunedin as `trevally', but the latter name belongs to <i>Caranx platessa</i> Cuv. & Val. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/230</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  SERIOLELLA BRAMA (Gunther[u-dieresis]). <i>Warehou.</i> Commonly sold as `trevally' in Christchurch and Dunedin. Rare at Bay of Plenty and Auckland. ... Distribution: A southern species; rare in the extreme north. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/120</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <i>Seriolella brama</i>: Warehou (called trevally in Otago). <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> WAREHOU <i>Seriolella brama</i>(Gunther) In Otago, commercial and sport fishermen, auctioneers, retailers, consumers of fish and readers generally have for many years been giving the name Trevally (Usacaranx lutescens) to small Warehou up to twelve inches in length. It is to be hoped that this misnomer will continue to be replaced by the proper name of Warehou. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,218</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Warehou is a southern fish becoming less plentiful in the North Island. It is known as the Trevally, and sold under this name, in Dunedin and Christchurch. It is an excellent food fish, and is always in good demand. It is also found in Australia where it is known as the `Whario' a corruption of the Maori name Wharehou, and in Tasmania it is sold as `Snogtail' Trevally. <i>Ibid.</i> 170 Warehou Seriolella brama Trevally; Sea Bream <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,65</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> It should be noted that the name sometimes varies according to district. Thus in Otago small-sized warehou (fish) are called trevally, and in the South Island generally the European name groper is preferred to that of the Maori hapuku. (Ed.) <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> WAREHOU: ... <i>Other names: Seriolella brama</i>; trevally <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,222</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> WAREHOU [<i>Seriolella brama</i>] ... In southern waters the name trevally is sometimes used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,44</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>WAREHOU (a) Scientific name: <i>Seriolella brama</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Sea Bream; Bream. (c) Other common names: None known, except that in southern waters this species is sometimes called Trevally. (d) Principal localities where taken: Cook Strait; Nelson; Greymouth. ... The Spotted Warehou, <i>Seriolella punctata</i>, taken in very small quantities, is usually counted in with the warehou catch. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,50</i>)

>  The paper also referred to the methods of preserving fish, and gave statistics which showed that during the year groper had been in the market 150 days .. trevalli, 124 days .. and there were 22 days during the year upon which there were no fish in the market. <i>(Aug 18, 1877,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Some fine hauls of fish have been made off the Breakwater during the past two or three days, the takes consisting chiefly of mullet, cod, kawai, and trevally, the two latter being particularly plentiful, while conger eels, skate, and ling of very large size have been occasionally landed. <i>(Jan 7, 1882,Timaru Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Native or Common Name] Arara, Trevally .. [Scientific Name] <i>Chilodactylus macropteus </i>[this = tarakihi] <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago) is Thomson's<i> C. macropterus</i> (=tarakihi) an error here??]



 
 <i>n.</i> [unspecified] (See [also trevalli](../A#also-trevalli))

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Longirostrum platessa</i> Cuv. & Val. TREVALLY; ARAARA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,33,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Usacaranx lutescens: </i>Trevally. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> <i>Caranx Platessa </i>... Trevally <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/233</i>)

>  Trevally. <i>Caranx platessa</i> ... Small quantities of trevally appear for sale on several days during each of the summer months. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/271</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  CARANX PLATESSA Cuvier and Valenciennes. <i>Trevally; Araara</i>. A school fish taken when trawling from 15 to 20 fathoms. ... Distribution: Rare south of Banks Peninsula. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/117</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  The Trumpeter is rare, but its close relative the Moki (<i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i>) is secured in good numbers near the kelp, as also are Butterfish (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>), and Trevally (<i>Longirostrum platessa</i>). <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,199</i>) [NOTES: Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury']

>  <i>Cape Maria van Diemen.</i> - Kahawai, snapper, moki, trevally, king-fish, haku or yellowtail, rock-cod </i> Ibid.</i> 24. <i>Puysegur Point</i> - Hapuku, kahawai, trumpeter, moki, pig-fish, trevally, rock-cod, perch, butter-fish, red-cod, dog-fish <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  Trevally - <i>Caranx georgianus</i>. NUMBER OF FISH RECORDED - 60. LOCALITIES - Cape Maria van Diemen, Mokokhinou, Portland Island, Puysegur Point. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/208,13. ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Araara,</i> n. <i>trevally</i> or <i>caranx georgianus</i>, a fish. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,9</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Trevally,</i> or <i>Trevalli,</i> or<i> Trevalla</i>, or <i>Travale, n.</i> an Australian fish. In various localities the name is applied to several fishes, which are most of them of the family <i>Carangidae</i>, or <i>Horse-Mackerels.</i> An Old-World name for the Horse-mackerels is <i>Cavalli</i> (Ital. <i>cavallo</i>, a little horse). ... The Maori name for the <i>Trevally</i> is <i>Awara<i> [sic, ?Arara[sic]], and in Auckland it is sometimes called the <i>Yellow-Tail</i> (q.v.). See also quotation, 1886. Gunther says, the genus <i>Teuthis</i> is readily recognised by the peculiar structure of the ventral fins, which have an outer and an inner spine and three soft rays between. <i>(1898,Austral English,478</i>)

>  `Several canoes came off to the ship, and two or three of them sold us some fish - cavallys as they are called - which occasioned my giving the Islands the same name.' -->1902 Drummond and Hutton Nature in NZ 71 The snapper, the mullet, and the trevally are essentially northern; while the trumpeter, the red cod, and the ling are just as essentially southern. <i>(1769,Cook's Journals,164,Wharton, 1893 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROT</b> The School Trevally was known to the Maoris as the `Araara' and to Europeans as the `Trevally' or `Cavalli'. <i>Ibid..</i> 169 School Trevally Usacaranx lutescens Trevally; Skipjack <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,78</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Araara,</i> n. <i>Usacaranx lutescens</i>, trevally, a fish. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,14</i>)

>  A trevally flashes bright blue, yellow and silver under the surface as dean pulls it in and slings it in the bait-bucket. <i>(Sep 7, 2002,Dominion Post,A20</i>)

>  FLOUNDERS, Flounders, Trevalli. Groper, Blue Cod, Kingfish, Oysters - Fish Luncheons and Suppers a specialty. <i>(Apr 21, 1903,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  There were several kinds of shellfish, including kutai, kokote, and huangi; of fish, tamure (snapper), takeke (guardfish), arara (travalli). <i>(Jun 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

>  The City of Cork fishing party caught upwards of 500 fish (including schnapper, trevalli, mackerel [sic] and gurnet) off Rangitoto on Saturday afternoon last. <i>(Nov 12, 1881,Observer (Auckland),140</i>)

>  We came across a school of trevalli stretching across the surface of the sea as far as the eye could see. <i>(Feb 22, 1934,Feilding Star,4</i>)

>  <i>Usacaranx lutescens</i> (Richardson). Trevally (school trevally). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/410</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>HYDE</b> If you take home a string of trevalli to your old lady, she's going to fall on your neck. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,141</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod) ... though I am not so sure about the rawaru (blue cod). Others omitted are the tarakihi, or terakihi, the moki, the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), whai (skate), and the koekohe [sic] (trumpeter). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod) ... though I am not so sure about the rawaru (blue cod). Others omitted are the tarakihi, or terakihi, the moki, the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), whai (skate), and the koekohe [sic] (trumpeter). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <i>trevally</i>: edible fish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Trevally <i>Usacaranx archeyi</i> Griffin ... Trevally<i> Usacaranx lutescens</i> (Richardson) (Syn. <i>Scomber platinoides</i> Richardson and <i>S. micans</i> Richardson) <i>(1955,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,406</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> TREVALLY (ARAARA) <i>Usacaranx lutescens</i> (Richardson) For some unknown reason small Warehou sold on the Dunedin Fish Market and in the shops of Dunedin were called Trevally and the same fish with the same markings when grown to eighteen or twenty-four inches in length were known as Warehou. Just how and when these small Warehou received the name Trevally no one seems to know. .../ [T]he old time Maori was recognised as a student of Nature and much more observant than the Pakeha, who blindly follows other and called fish by all sorts of names without ever stopping to think - as for instance when they call Seaperch the John Dory. Each fish is totally different. The misuse of common names of fish in New Zealand is most glaring but the mistake of calling the young Warehou by the name of Trevally is not made in the North Island, as both species are caught there; only odd Trevally are caught south of Cook Strait. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,234</i>)

>  <i>trevalli </i>An Australian and New Zealand edible fish; the name is Australian. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),421</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> ... around the wharf piles where barnacles and mussels clung ... flitted the little pakirikiri and maomao; and down in the ... shadows swam the trevalli. <i>(1959,Stone, The,13</i>) [NOTES: (First Adventure)]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Trevally .. .. araara .. .. <i>Caranx lutescens </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>trevally n</i>. edible fish. Also called silver bream or skipjack. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family CARANGIDAE. This family contains the well known and shapely trevally <i>Usacaranx</i>, two species, ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,221</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> TREVALLY [<i>Caranx lutescens</i>] ... In Australia it is known as silver trevally and by a wide variety of other names not commonly used in New Zealand. The Maori ara-ara is still heard on northern coasts. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,44</i>)

>  <i>trevally n.</i> fine edible fish. Also called <i>silver bream </i>or <i>skipjack. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>TREVALLY</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Caranx lutescens</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Trevally; New Zealand Pompano. (c) Other common names: Hoppers (fish in uncleaned state). (d) Principal localities where taken: [Largely confined to northern waters and south to Cook Strait]. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,48</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> TREVALLY Abundant inhabitant of our warmer waters. Occurs south to about Banks Peninsula and grows to about 30 inches (76.2cm) <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,261</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> A handsome fish of iridescent blues, greens and silver, the trevally, or ara-ara, occurs in the warmer waters to the north of New Zealand, ... The early sealers called this fish skipjack. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,81</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> <i>TREVALLY </i>(Araara) <i>Caranx georgianus </i> <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,406</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 406]

>  <i>trevally n. Austral. & N.Z</i>. any of various food and game fishes of the genus <i>Caranx</i>. [C19: prob. alteration of <i>cavally, < cavalla</i> species of tropical fish, < Sp. <i>caballa</i> horse] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1250</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 822; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 512]]

>  trevally .. [scientific name] <i>Caranx georgianus</i> .. [other name(s)] hoppers, jackfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] trevally [TS] 1348-90, 2180, 2192 trevally, school 1387 and a series of black spots on the flanks ... [lost from a cit??] TREVALLIES 1347-96 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> The trevally belongs to a large family (Carangidae) containing fishes variously known as jacks, trevallies, jack or horse mackerels, scads, and pompanos. ... <i>Trevally Caranx georgianus </i>... Known simply as trevally in New Zealand, being the only species present; called white or silver trevally in southern Australia ... Our trevally was known for a long time as <i>Caranx lutescens,</i> and distinct from though obviously related to an Australian species. In 1974 it was recognised that there was a single somewhat variable Australasian species, <i>Caranx georgianus </i>(for a while <i>Usacaranx</i>), which took this name by historical precedence. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,86</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> Most of the food fishes here are caught all the year round, but the king fish, the barracouta, and the trevally leave in the winter. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,74</i>)

>  <i>Caranx georgianus</i>, Cuvier. The trevalli is a common fish in the harbour. <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/550</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]



 
 <i>n.</i> also called araara a school fish common in North Auckland waters and widely used for bait; arara, cavalli, horse-mackerel, yellowtail; occas. warehou cf. [aka trevalli] (See [also school trevally](sa-sho.md#school-trevally))

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> TREVALLY ... <i>Other names: Caranx lutescens</i>; silver trevally (Australia); ara ara (Maori); and a wide variety of other names not generally used in New Zealand. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,230</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>TREVALLY </i>(Araara) <i>Caranx georgianus </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,215,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> The Arara of the Maoris, or the Trevally or Cavalli of the fishermen <i>(Caranx georgianus)</i>, is a highly esteemed fish ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,110</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> TREVALLI (<i>Caranx Georgianus</i>). ... Dr. Hector says: `The trevalli is the arara of the Maoris, or trevalli or cavalli of the fishermen.' <i>Ibid.</i> 99 [quoting Hector <i>op. cit</i>.] Trevalli or cavalli of the fishermen ... In Auckland it is sometimes called yellow-tail, but this name appears to be also used for the king-fish. The fish known as trevalli in the Dunedin market is a different fish, allied to the warehou. [<i>Neptonemus brama</i>] <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,99</i>)

>  Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand, we have among the constant residents on all parts of our coast the hapuku, tarakihi, trevally, moki, aua, rock-cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are confined to the south. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  Trevally, which is ranked eighth on the list, has always been popular as a smoked fish, but is also good to eat fresh. <i>(Jun 26, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

### trey-bit
 
 <i>n.</i> threepenny coin (See [also tray-bit](tot-tyr.md#tray-bit))

>  <b>HYDE</b> If you have green grape women, flour-bin women, / Amber-in-forest, wild-mint-scented women, / Trey-bit in church or drudging kit-bag women. <i>(1999,Book of Nadath, The,xxix</i>)

### Tri-Nations
 
 <i>n.</i> the annual triangular rugby union competition between Australia, New Zealand, and South Africa [also now (from 2005?) a similar annual rugby league competition]

>  With a host of players on standby for the All Blacks, Te Pou believes several will go close to making the cut when All Black coach John Mitchell reassesses his options for the Tri-Nations in July. <i>(Jun 2, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  "From what the other guys say about the build-up to the Tri-Nations, it's going to be very intense, that's where I'll notice it... There's been a lot made of what the All Blacks did last year," says Robinson, interviewed at the last training camp before the Tri-Nations opener in Sydney. <i>(Jul 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

### trial
 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of "dog trial"

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Sheep-dog trials show how much the intelligence of these dogs can be developed by training. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/493</i>)

>  There are few, if any, sheep dog owners in New Zealand who have not seen or read of "Boy," the well-known trials competitor. <i>(Jan 5, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,71</i>)

>  As the trial season is upon us again.. a word or two of advice to young competitors may not be out of place... Continual trial work on three sheep will probably ruin a young dog. <i>(May 2, 1946,NZ Farmers' Weekly,18</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to enter a dog in a dog trial competition.

>  John and huntaway Joe have only been trialing for a month this season and won three opens plus several places. <i>(Jun 13, 1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,620</i>)

>  In the fickle world of trialing sheep, that wasn't a big lead and the test could have gone either way. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 47</i>)

>  He has trialed competitively for 10 seasons and recently won an event near Wanganui with Clip. <i>(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

### trial dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [dog trials] a highly effective sheepdog.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... besides being a crack hill dog he was about the top trial dog of his day. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,53</i>)

>  You don't need a trial dog; in fact about the opposite. One of those noisy 'sooners' (sooner bite the sheep than head the mob.) <i>(Aug 24, 1972,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> Boy is the son of Stan Watson's old trial dog, Digger .. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,15</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> She was one of his good trial dogs and had just produced five pups. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,41</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> At Molesworth in those days most of our heading dogs were the `plain-working' kind. They did not `eye' the sheep or exercise any mesmerism, as is done by the present-day trial dog. -<i>Ibid.<</i> 44 He made a beautiful head and flopped on his belly about ten yards from the sheep and held them like a trial dog. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,43</i>)

### trial ground
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land on which sheep dog trials are held.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> My idea of a champion is the dog that has done it from the shingle tops to the trial ground; a dog that, with bleeding feet, after a fortnight on shingle country, will still give it an honest go at the end of a stinking hot day. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,50</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. at his very first run on a trial ground - at Moawhanga - he won both hunts with him. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,63</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> It's a 214-kilometre route that wanders in and out of valley after pleasant valley and, most important, takes you to six different trial grounds. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,221</i>)

### trial happy
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of dog trial dogs] with diminished performance, through over-confidence.

>  Trial happy - a dog that's sick of going to trials - according to its owner - and given as the reason why it's not doing very well. <i>(1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 13</i>)

>  Apart from everything else, Peg's over-riding problem in the trial season is that she tends to get "trial happy".. which makes me "trial sad." <i>(Sep 15, 1985,NZ Journal of Agriculture,48</i>)

>  .. the term "trial happy" is really another way of saying that the dog thinks it's the boss! <i>(Sep 15, 1985,NZ Journal of Agriculture,49</i>)

>  A trialist is also someone, within whom hope springs eternal. that the sheep will be all right this year, that the dog isn't going to get 'trial happy' after the third trial, and the judge will always look as kindly at your run as you do yourself. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 47</i>)

>  "TRIAL Happy"... This is what a man tags his dog when said dog does not listen. Dog is pat to "taking over" meaning dog thinks he knows what he is doing so shall do it their way, knowing full well that "BOSS" can't reprimand him. <i>(Feb 9, 2011,Wairarapa Midweek,8</i>)

### trial man/dog trial man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a keen and usually successful dog triallist.

>  A good shepherd is usually a good trial man .. I have never seen a successful trial man knock sheep about ... <i>(Sep 15, 1949,NZ Farmer,08</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Of the four dog trial men I visited, all had won a National title. <i>(1971,In the Wake of the Axe,61</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Duncan - a dog trial man - befriended publicans everywhere and one lent us toothbrushes. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,11</i>)

### trial paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced area on a farm or experimental station where field trials are conducted.

>  .. although the trial sheep were kept exclusively on the trial paddocks (plus the fourth reserve paddock of each treatment) there were occasions when further sheep had to be brought in to keep the growth under control. <i>(Mar 15, 1943,NZ Journal of Agriculture,153</i>)

### trialist
 
 <i>n.</i> one ...[who] competes in a trial; a contender for selection in a national (usually sports, and often rugby) team who competes in a trial match

>  [headline] Strong Criticism over Treatment of Rugby Trialists <i>(May 16, 1957,Evening Post,36</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a contender in dog trials

>  ... for Fred has had outstanding success as a trialist and has won four New Zealand heading championships [at the sheepdog trials]. <i>(Jun 13, 1957,NZ Farmer,36</i>)

### trialling
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) running a dog in dog trial competitions.

>  Tom's one of dog trialing's characters. <i>(Jul 1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Horse sports aside, New Zealand's two best-known rural contests are sheep shearing and dog trialling. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,7</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> .. shrug your shoulders and say, 'That's dog trialling.' <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,223</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Now that really is dog trialling. [dog trialing] <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,224</i>)

### triallist
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an entrant in a dog trial competition.

>  <b>GORDON</b> Remove any practising triallist from dogs, sheep and hills and, as long as there was someone in the vicinity with the same interest, he or she would be as happy as a pig in mud. True triallists get as much pleasure out of their colleague's successes as they do their own. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,233</i>)

### Triangle Dinks
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Dink](dig-dou.md#dink))

### tribal
 
 <i>adj.</i> (with ref. to Maori iwi)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> ... she began to play an active part in tribal affairs, and her voice was heard with respect by the elders. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,17,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]

### tribalism
 
 <i>n.</i> (with ref. to Maori iwi)

>  <b>DUFF</b> "We must put tribalism behind us." <i>(2000,Maori Heroes,120</i>)

### tribe
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori iwi

>  <b>ELDER</b> `I approve of your itinerating amongst the natives. Nothing will tend more to increase your influence amongst them than your visiting them from tribe to tribe. <i>(Jan 17, 1822,Marsden's Lieutenants,183,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1822 Jan 17 Marsden in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 183]

>  ... I do further proclaim ... that all purchases of land in any part of New Zealand which may be made ... from any of the native chiefs or tribes of these islands, ... will be considered null and void ... <i>(Aug 14, 1839,Proclamation Sir George Gipps</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Aug 14 Proclamation Sir George Gipps in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 3(c)]

>  <b>WILKES</b> It is remarkable that every tribe has a name peculiar to itself, and distinct from the district which it inhabits; ... <i>(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/306</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/306]

>  <b>HOBSON</b> On the morning of the 14th ... two tribes ... requested that their names might be withdrawn from the treaty <i>(Feb 17, 1840,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 17 Hobson Report in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 11]

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> It appears that if two tribes are at war, and the chief of most consequence in one tribe were to marry the only daughter of the corresponding `rangitera' of the other, that would not do anything towards making peace between the two tribes; ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,33,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> In the North I am told that the race is much finer and that these are a miserable remnant of tribes expelled thence. These Maoris are certainly outcasts from the North island according to the universal tradition of the place - but whether or no the other is better I cannot say. <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,43,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> ... and then my tribe, (I find I am already beginning to get Maorified,) ... <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,52,Auckland edn ed.</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> Apart from the Ngati Maniapotos, ... are other Waikato tribes ... who have been for years past the principal mover in what is termed the King Movement. <i>(1864,NZ Native Rebellion,4</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> I have known a native, a lucky finder of a new ... Hapuku ground, to receive from his tribe a considerable `utu'. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,139</i>)

>  <b>HOGG</b> They've saved the fame of the British name,/ In this queer war with the Maori tribes;/ <i>(1875,Lays and Rhymes,315</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> A Maori tribe is an exceedingly indefinite entity, and so also is `hapu' a word primarily signifying pregnancy, but commonly used to signify having descended from one ancestor of later date than the original ancestor of the tribe. <i>(1910,Reminiscences,39</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> The great migration (heke) from Hawaiki is the most famous event in Maori history, because all the tribes trace their aristocratic lineages back to the chiefs of the voyaging canoes. <i>Ibid.</i> 333 The smallest sociological unit is the biological family, which the Maoris termed <i>whanau,</i> derived from <i>whanau</i>, to give birth . ... The term <i>hapu </i>(pregnancy) was used to denote this expanded family group for it expressed the idea of birth from common ancestors ... The term <i>iwi</i> (bone) was brought into current use to include all the <i>hapu</i> descended from common ancestors and thus related to each other by a blood tie. To denote the groupings in English, the <i>iwi</i> has been termed tribe and the <i>hapu</i> a sub-tribe.[check???] <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,36</i>)

### tribespeople
 
 <i>n.</i> members of an iwi

>  He failed to fulfil the terms of the agreement reached between a previous agent, H.T. Kemp, and Ngai Tahu tribespeople at Akaroa in 1848. <i>(Aug 30, 1988,Press, The,6</i>)

>  So, at any rate, said Kupe to the assembled tribespeople when he returned to far off Tahiti and told his wonderful odyssey of ocean-travel and of new lands far to the south-west. <i>(Nov 26, 1913,Bay of Plenty Times,6</i>)

>  By the time the body of the half-caste Maori girl, Mary Elizabeth Raymond, identified as the young woman found in the Tamaki River, leaves Auckland tomorrow for Te Puke, the assembly of hundreds of mourning tribespeople will have commenced. <i>(Jun 14, 1935,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Evening closes in, and the giant ranges, rising skywards all around us, are bathed in a rich, purple golden haze as we emerge from the forest shades, and scramble up on to the open plateau of Ruatahuna - the centre of the Urewera country, and the headquarters of the isolated tribespeople of Tuhoe. <i>(Jan 1, 1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,20</i>)

### tribute
 
 <i>n.</i> (mining)

>  <b>LYON</b> As the quartz mines began to decline in output and could no longer pay a large wage sheet, it became the custom to let them on tribute, by which parties of men were given the right to mine and raise quartz from the resultant crushing, of which a proportionate share of the gold went to the company. This system might also apply to old workings of mines in actual work, but in most of these cases the tributors followed a thin but valuable lead, which might not be much thicker than a finger, but so rich as to be worth tunnelling or working as a tribute, in which the golden lead was pursued on an upward slope. It used to be rather shrewdly suspected that tributors were not `working on the blind' but had as former employees of the company marked a lead and covered the same by a piece of mine timber or rubble till a later date give them the chance to profit by their knowledge. Many of the tributors did exceedingly well; they had nothing except their labour to lose, and the company got some profit on a good lead, and so `where's the harm?' <i>(1952,Faring South,168</i>)

>  And yet in June, 1928, right in the middle of the rising tide of production, the Paparoa Coal Company was forced, for financial reasons, to lay off 85 men and close the mine. Their offer to allow the mine to be worked under tribute (which it was for several years) was bitterly opposed by the unions which claimed that the co-operative system meant the speeding of production at the expense of conditions, and that the people working in co-operative systems were little concerned about the conditions that had been built up through industrial organisation and effort over a long time. <i>(Nov 9, 1972,Press, The,5</i>)

>  After being worked for a preiod [sic] on tribute, mining operations ceased, but the right to the valuable water race was kept intact. <i>(Apr 8, 1918,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

### tributer
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining] (See [tribute](tot-tyr.md#tribute))

>  A good deal of antimony-ore occurred in this mine, and gold film or `paint' on clay selvages was frequently met with, doubtless indicating secondary enrichment. Mining at Boatman's has declined very much, and work is now limited to intermittent tributers and occasional prospectors. <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/92</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> As the quartz mines began to decline in output and could no longer pay a large wage sheet, it became the custom to let them on tribute, by which parties of men were given the right to mine and raise quartz from the resultant crushing, of which a proportionate share of the gold went to the company. This system might also apply to old workings of mines in actual work, but in most of these cases the tributors followed a thin but valuable lead, which might not be much thicker than a finger, but so rich as to be worth tunnelling or working as a tribute, in which the golden lead was pursued on an upward slope. It used to be rather shrewdly suspected that tributors were not `working on the blind' but had as former employees of the company marked a lead and covered the same by a piece of mine timber or rubble till a later date give them the chance to profit by their knowledge. Many of the tributors did exceedingly well; they had nothing except their labour to lose, and the company got some profit on a good lead, and so `where's the harm?' <i>(1952,Faring South,168</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> TRIBUTER: one who contracts to work a claim for nominal wages and a fixed share of the profits, or for a percentage of the profits only. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962 ed.</i>)

>  The pitches will be offered to enable tributers to earn 40s. a week, and they expect that this will be an inducement to many miners to abandon the precarious occupation of gold digging, which has so long suspended their works. <i>(Apr 23, 1853,Evening Post,11</i>) [NOTES: summarised in Lyttleton Times 'from Adelaide files']

>  In one period of three weeks the tributers made as much as GBP763 per man, after all expenses had been paid. <i>(May 2, 1917,Ohinemuri Gazette,2</i>)

>  The member for Thames points out that it is alleged that the price paid by the Banks is not commensurate with the rates realised in Great Britain and America and as a result mining in New Zealand is handicapped and private claim holders and tributers who are not in a position to export, are especially placed to disadvantage. <i>(Sep 10, 1920,Thames Star,1</i>)

>  Templates for Ball mill lining plates at mill sent for casting, and operations by suppliers (mine owners and tributers at Karangahake) would be expedited when date of reopening of mill was known. <i>(Jun 17, 1939,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  All who have worked underground, and have had to try the presence of copper, which often exists in the shape of a rich black oxide, very profitable to the tributer, will be aware how that rude chemist moistens the sooty substance and applies it to the flame of his candle, and how satisfied he is, if the green flame appears, that the true metal exists in his ore, and that he is not deceived by the black oxide of iron, and other more worthless substances. <i>(Nov 8, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  Tributers Wanted to work the Niagara Water Wheel Claim, Commissioner's Flat, Kanieri. <i>(Aug 7, 1866,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  The tributers of the Blue Spur Sluicing Co. have nearly completed a junction on the northern boundary of the Great Extended, and intent to make hay while the sun shines. <i>(Jan 8, 1870,Tuapeka Times,4</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> A tributer may deduct such amount from the earnings of the mine after each washing-up or retorting. <i>(1892,Gold-Miner's Guide,10</i>)

### trick
 
 <i>n.</i> a good fellow; an amusing or alert child [ca1940 Havelock Be a trick and go and get us a cup of tea would you.]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>trick</i> an amusing or alert child; eg `Now isn't little Katie a trick.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)

### trick car
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Car dealers have a collection of jargon they use on the yard, so if you are thinking of taking up selling cars as a career, there are a few terms you should know. ... A 'trick car' is likely to have a sunroof, mag wheels, stereo and ornaments dripping from the rear vision mirror. <i>(Jan 22, 1986,NZ Herald,Sect. 2/1</i>)

### trickle
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  R. Mason ... we trickled out in warm sunshine ... B.G. <i>(1958,Tararua Tramper,13 (12) 4</i>)

>  The critics will no doubt note that <i>to oozle</i> and<i> to trickle</i>, which denoted much slower modes of locomotion, were comparatively little [30] used, and will not be surprised that Wellington trampers are sometimes afflicted with<i> perspidrip</i>, or describe themselves as <i>jagged. Jagged</i> (one syllable), worn out by exertion, exhausted, no doubt has some relation to the English dialect <i>on the jag</i>, a drunken spree, and the American <i>jagged</i>, tipsy, and also owes something to<i> jiggered.</i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,29</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

### trier
 
 <i>n.</i> one who perseveres in the face of difficulties; one who is always ready to give things a go; an active or vigorous worker; the coll. use orig f. racing slang (cf STARTER), of a horse that loses after fighting to win a race [cf AND a racehorse which is being ridden to win] e.g. in NZ

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The neddy was in the bag in the Cup; he was no trier. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,33</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> There are many opportunities to acquire land on easy terms, very many farmers' trading concerns which are ready to back a `trier' ... <i>(1927,Empire's Junior Partner, The,227</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Jack Wilcox was the only other `trier'. <i>(1927,Follow the Call,68</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Rangi Hohaia was always a trier. <i>(1944,Troop Target,105</i>)

### trifecta
 
 <i>n.</i> [transferred use (racing sense is US, NZ, Aust.)]

>  He was big on talkback by then, and loved telling his listeners what "the princess" (Amanda) "and the trifecta" (the kids) were up to. <i>(Nov 3, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

### trig
 
 <i>n.</i> short for trig station

>  In these colonies especially the proportion of land occupiers and owners is so large that most of us are directly or indirectly interested in `section pegs,' `trigs,' and such-like, while at Home the expression of this interest is practically limited to the impersonal `beating the bounds of the parish.' <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/492</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... a flying survey of the hill country was taking place and the `Eldest Brother' [a hill] had been selected as a trig station. The trig twinkled and aroused curiosity. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,71</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ... a flying survey of the hill country was taking place and the `Eldest Brother' [a hill] had been selected as a trig station. The trig twinkled and aroused curiosity. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,136</i>)

>  Made a plant of some 60 yds. of rope near 22 trig pole. <i>(Aug 19, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,54,1950 ed.</i>)

>  A correspondent of the New Zealand Times telegraphs as follows from Rotorua, "Natives at Tewhaiti threatened to tie up a native guide for Mr Phillips, surveyor if he persisted in proceeding with him to show the country or trigs, and Inspector Emerson has telegraphed to the Defence Minister regarding the position of affairs." <i>(May 15, 1895,Nelson Evening Mail,3</i>)

>  On re-observing these trigs he made a rather startling discovery in regard to the hill Maungahamu, about four miles south-east from the Taupo township. <i>(Jul 24, 1922,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The main trips were: (1) Cattle Range (Pukeroa, Pukekino, and Waingawa Trigs) and a descent to the Cow Saddle <i>(Jan 5, 1934,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  LETTERS RECEIVED - From "Trig. B.," Whangarei, on Free selection. <i>(Jul 14, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The line proposed by Mr Blair, CE., for the Invercargill-Toi Tois railway leaves the Bluff line near Woodend, at the angle of the road, twenty chains south of Trig. K., then runs due east for thirteen miles, then bends a little southward, and runs straight for the Education Reserve at the mouth of the Waimak Valley. <i>(Aug 20, 1881,Southland Times,3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to mark out when surveying?; ?to make a trigonometrical survey of [(cf TRIG STATION)]

>  A Trig Station erected by Captain Smith when he was trigging the valley. <i>(1861,McLean Papers,20/87</i>) [NOTES: (W. Fitzherbert)]

>  ".. Did much trigging, photographing, and sketching " <i>(Apr 3, 1883,Manawatu Times,3</i>) [NOTES: now in new verbal noun headword, archive this version]

>  King Tawhiao is in a disgraceful state of intoxication at Napier, while the Government surveyors are busy "trigging" his dominions. <i>(Mar 14, 1883,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  Land Purchases and Surveys were also gone into, and iron posts for trig. stations over country already trigonometrically surveyed are to be permitted; any new country not to be trigged until the Council authorises the same. <i>(Feb 2, 1878,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

### trig station
 
 <i>n.</i> short for trigonometrical station [?cf. occas spg trig. perhaps f. (or reinforced by) TRIG v, of dialect derivation, rather than the commonly believed trigonometrical (survey) station; (in SOED without regional label TD)]

>  [Caption] A tramper on the trig station at Pukematakeo, the highest point in the Waitakere Ranges. <i>(Oct 26, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,57</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Up near the trig station was a Taranaki gate. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,66</i>)

>  I and Cass attempted to observe angles to trig. stations from the base line. <i>(Sep 18, 1844,Torlesse Papers,103,1958 ed.</i>)

>  ... the Trig Station Number 12, near the termination of the Bridle-path, ... <i>(Nov 29, 1851,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

>  Mr Swainson informs me that the Trig Station pulled down was not the one erected by him. <i>(1861,McLean Papers,20/87</i>) [NOTES: (Fitzherbert)]

>  <b>17/425</b> The cliffs in this bay are very precipitous, being about 300 feet high, and they extend to the end of the range below the Taupiri trig. station (585 feet). <i>(1884,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  We reached the trig. station. <i>(1888,TrNZI,38</i>)

>  From the trig.-station on the summit we struck southwards to the coast towards Herekino. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/346</i>) [NOTES: (Cheeseman)]

>  <b>HOC KEN</b> The highest point of the Whakari range formed one of the earliest trig stations, and since then has been known as Flagstaff; ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,82</i>)

>  <b>FAIRBURN</b> The top of the highest of the two hills, which is now a Trig station. <i>(1901,Maharatanga,42</i>)

>  ... the sight of a trig station effectually dispelled the illusion. <i>(1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,410</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The trig station which stood upon the top until some years back, consisted of the bowl of a small totara tree, ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,136</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> I'm leaning against a trig station ... <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,53</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Stock and the farmer change the appearance of the countryside: the surveyor's <i>trig station</i>, the engineer's<i> stopbanks</i> along the rivers on the plain, shelterbelts of pine and macrocarpa and the work of the <i>fencer </i>change the scenery. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,171</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> A stranger immediately upon coming to New Zealand spots the `Trig' stations and asks what they are. <i>(Apr 20, 1874,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1874 Apr 20 J.G.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 178]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Next morning, when the surveyor was taking a final sight to check his overnight work he noticed two Maori girls ... going along a track which passed between him and the trig station. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,50,1935 ed.</i>)

>  We New Zealanders see Trig Stations everywhere... We seldom consider what they represent, nor the extraordinary physical effort that was involved in establishing the accurate survey of our country. <i>(Dec 17, 2003,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  <b>31</b> The object of the expedition was to arrest Kerei Kaihau, Tawhiao's secretary; who some months ago destroyed the trig station in the Upper Thames district. <i>(Nov 13, 1890,Otago Witness</i>)

>  To my look of blank enquiry [they] waved their hands towards the trig station gleaming whitely on a slightly higher rise about a quarter of a mile away. <i>(Mar 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,67</i>)

>  <b>CONDON</b> At the summit, there is a trig station 1100 feet above sea-level. <i>(1992,Hurleyville,108</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from Egmont Star, March 2, 1901.]

>  From the plantation to the summit was an exhilarating climb, with the view becoming more spectacular as we got nearer the trig station. <i>(Oct 1992,North & South,36</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> The Kapakapanui trig was carried up in sections by a 'large party of natives'. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,91</i>)

>  A climb to a "trig station" was part of the programme. <i>(Mar 9, 1899,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  Many a hot summer's day was spent at the swimming hole or hiking up the hill to the trig station, there to engrave one's initials for posterity. <i>(1981,St Patrick's College, Silverstream 1931-1980,48</i>)

>  I pause and pant at a trig with stunning views... <i>(Oct 11, 2003,Dominion Post,E15</i>)

### trig triangle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  &#65279;An interesting memento of a trip to Mount Egmont has been shown to the "Hawera Star" in the shape of a couple of photographs of a group sitting under the trig triangle erected at the summit of the mountain. <i>(Apr 20, 1899,NZ Times,3</i>)

### trigger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,42</i>) [NOTES: [see scarfing]]

### triggerfish
 
 <i>n.</i> a generic term; applied specif.leatherjacket,creamfish, kiri

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> <i>Trigger Fish</i> Parika scabra <i>(Bloch and Schn.)</i> (Syn.<i> Balistes unicornu </i>Richardson, <i>B. scabrosus</i> Richardson, <i>B. scabrosus </i>Richardson, <i>Monacanthus parika</i> Taylor, and <i>M. serrasquamosus </i>Hollard) ... <i>Aleuterus monoveros (Linne) ...Trigger Fish <i>Navodon australis </i>(Donovan) (Syn. <i>Monacanthus rudis</i> Richardson) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,413</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 413]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> SMOOTH LEATHER JACKET or TRIGGER-FISH <i>Allomonacanthus convexirostris </i>(Gunther) The smooth Leather Jacket, sometimes known as Spotted Leather Jacket, like the following species, Rough Leather Jacket, belongs to the Family of Trigger-fishes, so named from the large trigger-like spine on the back. <i>Ibid.</i> 374 ROUGH LEATHER JACKET, TRIGGER-FISH or FILE-FISH (KOKIRI) <i>Parika scaber</i> (Bloch and Schneider) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,372</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> ROUGH LEATHERJACKET ... <i>Other names: Novodon convexirostris</i>; triggerfish and filefish are used, but should rightly be given to a family of tropical fishes of similar appearance to our leatherjackets; kokiri (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,293</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] triggerfish 2311-43 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> LEATHER JACKET. ... This remarkably shaped fish <i>(Monocanthus convexirostris)</i> is very common in Wellington Harbour ... The Maori name for this fish is Kiriri. In other countries members of this genus are called Trigger Fishes, from the power ... of suddenly erecting the stiff spine on the back ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,114</i>) [NOTES: also p. 121]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> LEATHER JACKET <i>(Monocanthus convexirostris)</i> . ... The Maori name for this fish is kiriri. In other countries, members of this genus are called trigger fishes, .. <i>(1866,Handbook Fishes of NZ,58</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> Our final group is the PLECTOGNATHI, a small assemblage of fishes of unusual form generally brought about by exceptional growth of the head. In the Leather jackets or Trigger fish <i>(Cantherines convexirostris)</i> the anterior dorsal fin is reduced to a couple of spines, the first of which is very large and is the characteristic `trigger'. <i>(1928,Natural History of Canterbury,202</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 202]

>  <i>Cantherines scaber</i> (Forster). Leather-jacket (trigger-fish, file-fish). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/418</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>MORELAND</b> LEATHERJACKET [<i>Navodon scaber</i>] ... The names filefish and triggerfish are sometimes used, but more properly belong to a related group of tropical fishes. The Maori name kokiri is still in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,24</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>LEATHERJACKET </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Navodon scaber</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Creamfish (fillets); Leatherjacket. (c) Other common names: Triggerfish; Filefish. (d) Principal localities where taken: Auckland, Tauranga, Tasman Bay. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,33</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> TRIGGERFISH: <i>see Leatherjacket </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,261</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Leatherjacket [title] This cheeky little thick-skinned fish, also known as creamfish, triggerfish or <i>kokiri</i> in Maori, is widespread around the rocky coast of New Zealand. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,76</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>LEATHERJACKET Parika scaber (Navodon scaber)</i> (Triggerfish, cream fish, kokiri) ... [314] They make excellent eating and are usually sold under the name of cream fish. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,313,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] leatherjacket .. [scientific name] <i>Navodon scaber</i>[other name(s)] cream fish, filefish, kokiri, triggerfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)</i>

### trigging
 
 <i>n.</i> [verbal noun]

>  ".. Did much trigging, photographing, and sketching " <i>(Apr 3, 1883,Manawatu Times,3</i>)

### trigonel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>trigonel</i> [s] <i>Trifolium ornithopodioides </i>L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,80</i>)

### trigonometrical station
 
 <i>n.</i> [see also trig station]

>  I walked with Cass over the plains fixing on trigonometrical stations, at Courtenay, ... &c. <i>Ibid.</i>Oct 27 1849, 110 On starting work found that Takiori and Turaka ... had cut down trig station No. 13M. <i>(Sep 3, 1849,Torlesse Papers,102</i>)

>  "Major Kemp, although actually a Government officer, seems," stated "The Post," "to have been acting in a very turbulent and violent manner, interfering with the surveys and demolishing trigonometrical stations, because he had a difference of opinion with some other Natives as to the manner in which the block should be surveyed," but "the whole affair appears a very trumpery one, and not of sufficient importance to create the slightest anxiety.["] <i>(Feb 22, 1930,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  The petroleum indications occurring are described under the following heading:- Oil: Good surface indications of oil occur at Waitangi Hill at a point 69 chains slightly south-west from trigonometrical station 138, on a headwater branch of Te Hau o te Atua stream named Petroleum stream. <i>(Jul 15, 1910,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  From No. 21 Lower Kaikorai District, in a straight line running through the Trigonometrical Station on the Kaikorai hill until it joins Abott's Creek, then following that stream to its source; and from thence again in a straight line to the south-western corner of Section No. 155, Wakari District. <i>(Jun 10, 1854,Otago Witness,2</i>)

### triller
 
 <i>n.</i> a grey warbler

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The local name [at Paparoa, North Auckland] for this bird [the grey warbler] is the `Triller.' <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,72,1933 ed.</i>)

### trim
 
 <i>v.</i> to clear stranded logs and debris from a creek after a drive

>  <b>REED</b> ... trimming creeks after drives. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,151</i>)

>  The men set to work to fell the timber, and trimmed the logs, which they rolled together. <i>(Oct 8, 1898,Star, The,6</i>)

### trimmer
 
 <i>n.</i> one who cuts `points' off brisket, one who trims skin from carcases.

>  <b>GORDON</b> After <i>plucking, a trimmer</i> cuts<i> points </i>off the top of the brisket. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> It was obvious that men were still on the job from the carts loaded with skins that had to be hauled to the `thrower on' at one end of the paint table. The trimmers who lined the table, even the foreman who supervised the spraying of the skins, were gone. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,95</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a timber worker who clears stranded logs (See [trim v](tot-tyr.md#trim))

>  <b>REED</b> When a drive goes down it always leaves numerous logs behind on the banks, and the `trimmers' have to jack and roll these into the creek bed again, ready to catch the next drive. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,258</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a waterside worker

>  Before preference to unionists was passed the pay [for coal workers in the Wellington Branch of the Waterside Workers' Federation] was per hour for shovellers 1s 6d and for top hands (i.e. winchmen, bullrope-men, trimmers, and tippers), is 3d. <i>(Jun 9, 1911,Maoriland Worker,14/12/3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> anything excellent or outstanding of its class: occas. used as an ejaculation or exclamation: <i>Trimmer, eh!</i> (possibly going out of use by the 1950s, but seems to have ben reintroduced from Aust (?mainly children's slang) by TV soap operas (e.g. the Sullivans) [1953 14-16 M A2 Thames DHS 8 TRIMMER [something good 10] -->1960 17C M B6 St Bede's, Chch 8 TRIMMER]

>  A trimmer, wasn't it. [sc. a story] <i>(1937,Tomorrow,3/310</i>) [NOTES: (Sargeson)]

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  <b>BRUNO</b> Have you read Desert Daze - it's a trimmer. <i>(1948,Maleesh George</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Jess, I'm proud of you. You're a trimmer.' <i>(1965,Outcasts,105</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Don't call him that, that's a useful thing - You're a trimmer, you are - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,162</i>)

>  <i>trimmer ... NZ & Austral. colloq. </i>someone or something excellent or impressive <i>(a real little trimmer). </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,824</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>you're a trimmer!</i> a compliment or a curse; from English word `trimmer', a person who literally thrashes, meaning performs vigorously. Shepard example: `The key is stuck in the keyhole. You think you've jiggered it. Along comes your fix-it cobber and all's well. Gee, you say. You're a trimmer.' Shepard example two: `What did you move that for? I had them in the right order. Gawd, you're a trimmer.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,128</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a grade of beef from fat ox, heifer or cow.

>  <b>BARTON</b> Trimmer ox, heifer or cow. Carcasses of this grade carry more fat than permitted in the GAQ grade. <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,72</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person who grooms stud sheep for sale.

>  <b>DAVIS</b> I became an expert sheep trimmer. Having always shown some artistic ability, the art of sheep trimming came easily .. the tippy wool would be trimmed off and any hair on the britch would be neatly tidied up. <i>(1994,Lab Coats to Gumboots,158</i>)

### trimming
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [trim v](tot-tyr.md#trim))

>  <b>REED</b> After that came the `trimming' - jacking stranded logs into the bed of the creek, and clearing away debris which might impede the next drive. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,214</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> The operation of jacking the logs stranded on the banks back into the stream-bed was known as `trimming' <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,41</i>)

### trimmings
 
 <i>n.</i> esp of meals at hash-houses or pie-carts, with the usual extras (sc. fried onions, tomatoes, etc with grills (chips were not considered trimmings but a concomitant of the grill); peas and mashed potatoes with meat pies) [ca1945 Wellington A pie with trimmings is one with peas and mashed potatoes, and perhaps sauce. ca1950 Wellington hash-house Do you want steak and trimmings (sc eggs, onions)]



 
 <i>n.</i> pieces of wool `trimmed' from the fleeces by fleecepickers (cf PIECES)

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> Big Mick the Speewah ringer, must make skin and trimmings fly/ This season if he means to keep ahead;/ <i>Ibid.</i> 34 `The Bloke That Ran across a Snag' (also in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 30) `What's the blooming use of talking! - Ain't that sheep as good as most?/ Trimmings! What's the good of trimmings?/ Mighty lot of wool you've lost!/ Second cuts? Well, that ain't my fault, you've his wrinkled hide to thank;/ ... <i>(1897,Squatter's Dream</i>) [NOTES: 1897 McKee Wright Station Ballads `Shearing' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 33]

>  <b>BURDON</b> The sheep they shore were not so well woolled on the belly and carried less wool on the points than they do now, so that fewer short blows were required to take off the trimmings; but the shears they used were one inch and a half shorter in the blade and, being joined by a single instead of a double bow, required a wrist of iron to drive them continuously throughout a summer's day. A piece of raw hide known as a knocker is now used to prevent the shears clashing when closed, but before this was introduced the clack and snap of steel meeting steel was a noise inseparable from any busy shearing shed. <i>(1938,High Country,84</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Broom in hand, the fleecie would dash back down the board to sweep up the `locks' and `trimmings'. <i>(1949,High Country Days,51</i>)

>  <b>BOW</b> Fine-wool sheep, expecially Merinos, are much slower than crossbred sheep. Wrinkles, dense wool, trimmings, all have a bearing on shearing. <i>Ibid.</i> 9 <i>(1955,Wool Away!,3</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Reaching the end of the walk, and things just going fair, he returned and again said to the tall Moree Myall, `You are still cutting too often - go easy,' but the light limbed Australian continued to strip the `Basil' from the unfortunate stud ewe he was shearing. Standing by, the Moree native engaged in taking off the trimmings around the udder, then deliberately swept the glistening steel of his razor shears across the teats of the stud ewe, and deliberately shore them off level with the belly line. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,49</i>)

### trip
 
 <i>n.</i> collocation this trip, this time; for this occasion [ca1953 of a shout in a pub: `How many in, this trip?' [??to the bar, so omit]]

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> He was well-known/ By the police on terms familiar,/ Whose `In Again?' in a cheerful tone/ Perchance had jarred a temper sillier/ .../And `How much this trip, Boss?' would say/ The disappointed swagger. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,29</i>)

### trip dam
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WHEELER</b> I ask Bert about transporting [kauri] logs. `The [Kauri Timber] Company bullocked the kauri [79] logs to the streams, and the logs were floated out from trip dams on the Pungapunga.' <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,78</i>)

### trip west
 
 <i>phr.</i> death

>  Would far rather hop over the parapet and gallop across the paddock than crouch in the bottom of a trench waiting for a trip west. <i>(Aug 30, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/1 : 9</i>)

### trip-me-up
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>trip-me-up</i> [=]<i> carex lucida</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,80</i>)

### tripe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tripe</i> human intestines in phrs <i>I'll tear your tripes out</i> an exaggerated threat of a thrashing, usually to badly behaved juvenile; <i>don't bust your tripe </i>don't overdo it. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)

>  It is a serious matter to me, and I trust to you to help me all you can, and whatever expense you are put to I will settle for you. Now, don't forget, old boy, as this b- thing is worrying my tripe out. <i>(Jul 20, 1907,NZ Truth,109/5/5</i>)

### tripehound
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheepdog

>  <i>Tripe-hound</i>.- Slang for sheep dog. This was common on South Canterbury stations in the 'nineties, and I always thought it a New Zealand word until I came across it in an English novel, the other day, `Early Closing,' I think. In the novel the words was applied to a spaniel! (C.) <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,41</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Tripe-hound.</i> 4. A sheep dog: New Zealand and Australian farmers': C. 20. B., 1941, 1942. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1208</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Among words for a sheepdog is <i>tripe-hound</i>, which I have heard used by an elderly man as an extreme pejorative for people, but it can hardly have had pejorative tone as a station word. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,146</i>)

>  <i>tripehound n. (Sl.)</i> outback name for dog. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tripehound</i> sheepdog. NZA <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] [S.I.] a sheepdog.

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Tripe-hound. Slang for sheep-dog. This was common on South Canterbury stations in the [eighteen] nineties and I always thought that it is a New Zealand word until I came across it in an English novel the other day. Early Closing, I think. In the novel the word was applied to a spaniel! <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,406</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> tripehound: sheepdog. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Peter Newton called them 'tripehounds', Ian Sinclair 'meat-converters' and WV McIntyre an 'economic necessity.' To the rest of us they are sheepdogs. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,87</i>)

>  .. hot-chopper (sheep worrier), tripehound (one of the least obscene forms of abuse) ... <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### triple A
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a top quality fleece, AAA grade.

>  Good quality wool fleece: AAA. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### triple-fleecer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep that has missed at least two shearings.

>  <b>HALL</b> But the ones below are triple fleecers and really difficult; even though they may look docile, they're pretty close to being wild. One of the triple fleecers .... finally makes it to the shearer. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year, A,86</i>)

### triplefin
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish (See [threepenny](ten-tot.md#threepenny))

>  Aropund the holes, numerous triplefins watch us, hoping for something edible to be destroyed. <i>(Jul 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E 17</i>)

>  Darting about over these seaweeds are spotties, the occasional wide-bodied parore and leatherjackets, while finger-sized triplefins guard their territories from the top of the cannonball-smooth rocks. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 52/20</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Triplefins or three-fin blennies</i> Family Tripterygiidae. Three dorsal fins, with the first often partly joined to the second. About 20 species in New Zealand, ranging from estuaries through tide pools to shallow bays; often abundant. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,135</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BLUE-EYED TRIPLEFIN Tripterygion bucknilli </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,279,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>COMMON TRIPLEFIN Tripterygion capito </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,280,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>LONG-FINNED TRIPLEFIN Gilloblennius decemdigitatus </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,278,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>SPECTACLED TRIPLEFIN Gilloblennius tripennnis</i> ... Spectacled triplefins are usually pale orange-red ... with a whitish snout, a distinctive black saddle across they eyes ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,278</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>VARIABLE TRIPLEFIN Tripterygion varium</i> ... These fish are found all round New Zealand and in most localities they are one of the commonest of the blenny-like fishes. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,279,1984 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [Appears to be used in all C'wealth countries. Although <i>quiz night</i> still dominates in the UK.]

### trog
 
 <i>n.</i> a natural rock shelter (?fancifully f. troglodyte) [ca1949 R. Mason Used by trampers and mountaineers: mainly South Island]

>  For shelter, hillmen may seek a <i>trog</i>, a large overhanging boulder or bluff giving shelter like a cave. These are most likely to be found in the south-west of the South Island, so presumably the term originated with those who frequent those parts. <i>Troglodyte,</i> a race or tribe dwelling in caves, suggests the origin. The word has been known to trampers for eight or nine years. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  They cooed out, and while he skinned the spiker they found a possie in a bit of a trog and boiled up. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

### troll
 
 <i>n.</i> a (reputedly or apparently loose) woman (?f. trollop and <i>moll</i>) =prostitute, a trollop spg occas. <i>trol, trull</i>, indicating both NZ pron of <i>u + l,</i> and an uncertainty of derivation cf also TRULL [?move trull cits here] [ca1938 Havelock schoolchildren (?used innocent of derivation) prob. connected with the troll under the bridge in Big Billygoat Gruff]

>  <b>McGill</b> trol female; short for trollop, but less particular or offensive <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> The room at the pub, the whisky ..., the terrible hangovers ..., the terrible trulls. <i>(1946,For the Rest of Our Lives,344</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `You're a swine, Julien Ware,' he heard her say. ... `For Christ's sake, Stella, shut up!' he shouted. `You sound like a bloody troll.' <i>(1952,Julian Ware,222</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Say, have you seen that trol in the blue mocker. The dark one with the long ear-rings. She looked the right sort. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,205</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> `A pro. A troll. A prostitute.' <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,67</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIP</b> `You alone?' she asked. `Who did you think I'd be with?' `Don't know. Some trull.' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,55</i>)

### trolley
 
 <i>n.</i> [type of cart, aka trolly]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> TROLLEY: Bush or mine flat truck <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,189</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 189]

>  <b>MALFROY</b> Trollies .. Usually four-wheeled vehicles with a single bolster; run in pairs on tram-lines - one at each end of the log to be hauled. <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,39</i>)

>  <b>MALFROY</b> But soon I was beyond the gate again, viewing with bold interest the railway line and the platelayer's trolley, the piles of fish plates, bolts, and the metal junk. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,39</i>)

>  The small corrugated iron shed on the far side of the line ... was used by surfacemen for the storage of tools, and occasionally a trolley. <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,47,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Grenfell `Old Tolly')]

>  It appears that the deceased was driving a horse and trolly laden with timber to Dawson's mill, between 11 and 12 o'clock yesterday morning, when by some means or other he was thrown off and the wheels of the trolly passed over him lengthways. <i>(Dec 12, 1882,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  From the evidence it appeared that the two men had been coming down the sawmill tramway on separate trollies, the complainant accompanied by his wife. <i>(Feb 14, 1885,Southland Times,3</i>)

>  The first witness called was Margaret McLellan, mother of deceased, who deposed that her daughter was in the habit of riding on the timber trollies when going to school. <i>(Oct 7, 1904,Bush Advocate,2</i>)

>  On Monday afternoon, at about 1.35, witness was sitting in his hut writing when he heard the trolleys coming to the mill. <i>(Jan 29, 1913,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  The actual cost of the clay itself would be about 2d a yard at the price paid, GBP40 per acre, assuming that the land was entirely valueless afterwards, but the Board believed that the land could be sold eventually at a price which would considerably reduce the cost of the clay. Using trolleys and tramways had saved hundreds of pounds. <i>(Nov 26, 1891,Star, The,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> a four-wheeled cart; a boys' go-cart, push-cart, or billy-cart [aka trolly; ca1920 R. Mason Auckland and Pukekohe R. Mason notes that when the <i>soap-box derby</i> was introduced from the US after WW2, the name quickly changed to <i>trolley derby</i>.]

>  <b>LEE</b> I stole a box. Soon I had a trolley. I stole boards from the timber yard and built a wooden hut for my gang in the backyard. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,146</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> In Summer - Cat's Cradles (Newtown [18]90, Dunedin [19]09); ... Trolleys on summer nights (Wellington ([19]30); Knucklebones (Newtown [18]90, Dunedin ([19]09); ... <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,1/342</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Then he slowed, seeing suddenly the neat homes ..., two boys racketing a trolley, ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,119,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Paua Gatherers)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He like to be called Tank, a name which derived from a trolley he had made, shaped like a racing car, in which he pelted down Reservoir Hill. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,231</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> I once went to Charleston ... in company with the Gold Escort, and it was a picture to see those fine troopers turn out, `armed to the teeth', as the saying is. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,134</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> I once went to Charleston ... in company with the Gold Escort, and it was a picture to see those fine troopers turn out, `armed to the teeth', as the saying is. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,134</i>)

>  Today's silver scooter anbd roller blade addicts would doubtlessly scorn the wheels of the thirties. Those were the years of the trolley.... Trolleys were home made... I dragged my trolley up College Hill, rushed home and emptied my moneybox... [19] As a trolley rider, I found dresses unsuitable. <i>(Dec 2002,NZ Memories,No. 39/18</i>)

>  On the afternoon of the 7th inst. the two boys had a trolly each with four wheels, and were racing down Currie street. <i>(Mar 11, 1893,Otago Daily Times,3</i>)

>  The police said that this practice on the part of boys, in running trollies down the steep footpaths, was becoming a source of great complaint. <i>(Jul 26, 1898,Star, The,3</i>)

### trolley racing
 
 <i>n.</i>

### trolleyed
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk

>  The title is from a country and western poster Regan stole while "getting trolleyed" in a bar called Wild at Heart on a tour of Germany. <i>(Nov 22, 2000,Capital Times,7</i>)

>  He's too trolleyed and can't think straight. <i>(Feb 9, 2000,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  They're shocked - <i>shocked</i> - at their ability to get "trolleyed", but, hey, what could be better than unwinding after work in a bar where you can sometimes spot Larry Ellison, Graeme Hart or Sam Neill... <i>(Nov 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Knowing little about this person except his first name and his e-mail address, we took great delight in watching strangers emerge from immigration and customs, wheeling disobedient trolleys (and in some cases looking seriously trolleyed themselves), or carrying suitcases and far too many shopping bags, wondering if this was him.. <i>(Mar 1, 2001,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Because I can see this film could be funny ... if you were trolleyed. <i>(Sep 15, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F2</i>)

>  I was drunk. I was trolleyed. <i>(Jan 21, 2003,TV2</i>) [NOTES: <i>Shortland Street</i>]

### trolly-line
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka trolly line, trolley line, trolley-line] (See [tramline](tot-tyr.md#tramline))

>  <b>WILSON</b> He led the way along the mill tramline - wooden rails laid on sleepers and ballasted with red sawdust. The bush had been hacked and smashed to build the trolly-line. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,23</i>)

>  A large gang of navies were at work, constructing a temporary trolly-line in preparation for the more serious undertaking of repairing the permanent way. <i>(May 7, 1883,Star, The,4</i>)

>  After a short rest we organise another exploring party, more ferns are obtained, another trolley-line, ending in an overturned truck, is discovered as well as a babbling tributary of Coal Creek, then all hands assemble picturesquely on the bridge to be photographed. <i>(Mar 31, 1913,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  Beehre was leading a horse which was drawing a truck along a trolley-line, when he slipped and fell, and the truck passed over both legs, one of which was practically severed. <i>(Jun 16, 1922,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

>  Across the stage run a number of invisible trolley-lines. If the "curtains" were "up" during the interval, the cells on the one side and the gaoler's house on the other might be seen changing places by means of those rails. <i>(Jan 6, 1934,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  No horse ever foaled is fit to draw three tons, except on a trolly line after having been given a good start. <i>(Jun 18, 1904,New Zealand Free Lance,6</i>)

>  When completed the wharf, in round figures, will be about 300 feet long, and will have a 40ft by 20ft head, where cargo will be handled. A trolly line will be laid down on the structure for the purpose of running goods up and down. <i>(Jan 19, 1892,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### trollyman
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka trolly-man, trolly man, trolleyman, trolley-man, trolley man]

>  <b>MALFROY</b> The <i>trollyman</i> will have charge of the [mill] trollies ... <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,28</i>)

>  He was a Sawmill Trolleyman immediately prior to his death and resided at Rakopi, Nelson Province. <i>(Jul 23, 1921,NZ Truth,11</i>)

>  Employed as a loco. trolleyman at Ogilvie's mill at Kahikatea, Burr occasionally absented himself from home, and when he did so a neighbor, Mrs. Johnstone, was accustomed to attending to various matters about Burr's place. <i>(Nov 27, 1930,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  An American, named William Fisher, a trollyman at Mr Murdoch's sawmill, Grove Bush, met with a painful accident on Saturday. <i>(Sep 14, 1874,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  He was a trollyman, and was found on the tramway, having been run over, but could give no account of the accident, and died shortly after being found. <i>(Dec 16, 1882,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  WANTED - a Trolleyman with two horses. Apply at once to Timpany Bros., Kaikatea Sawmill, Fortrose. <i>(Oct 15, 1890,Southland Times,3</i>)

>  When the mill was full-handed they had two trollymen, and delays were caused, which were very inconvenient. <i>(Oct 4, 1905,Otago Witness,32</i>)

>  WANTED, good Timber Trolleyman must be handy with horses, for Bush Sawmill. <i>(Apr 15, 1914,Evening Post,1</i>)

 <i>abbr.</i> Te Runanga o Ngai Tahu, the Ngai Tahu tribal council [ORIGIN: Maori, acronym]

He says a corporate face like Tront is essential to supporting the constituent parts - the 18 runanga groupings that send a representative to the boardroom table. <i>(Apr 21, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

### trooper
 
 <i>n.</i> an armed, mounted constable

>  <b>PYKE</b> The divil a one, sire, barrin' the folk going up and down the road, and the trooper ... that brought the orders. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/viii/98,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> Armed troopers were guarding the coach and its load of gold, and the four Waitahuna bank robbers were lying handcuffed on the bank. <i>(1937,Thomas,36</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The gold escort from Switzers to Invercargill also passed through Gore for a long time after this date, and usually consisted of a banker with the gold and two armed troopers or mounted police. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,18,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <i>trooper n.</i> mounted policeman. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>VEITCH</b> The first Gold Escort left Clyde for Dunedin on 3 October 1862, with 6031 oz. gold securely strapped on packhorses, under the supervision of Major Bracken and three troopers. <i>(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,19</i>)

>  Forage for 11 horses, for Commandant, Non-commissioned Officers, and Troopers, 2s. 6d. per diem ... <i>(Oct 30, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  Mr. Hardy calls for the owner of the cradle; no answer; the cradle contains a load, which Mr. H. proceeds very diligently to wash, and, with his trooper, he manages to get enough gold to pay three or four licenses. <i>(Jan 2, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

### troopie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a truck used to transport troops

 <i>n.</i>

### tropic bird
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> [In Atlantic] Boobies (<i>pelecanus piscator</i>); men of war birds (<i>p. aquilus</i>); tropic birds (<i>phaeton aethereus</i>). <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/31fn</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> There were large flocks of gulls, and of the small species of black and white tropic birds which frequent the coasts of New Zealand. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,71,1952 ed.</i>)

>  TROPIC BIRD (<i>Phaeton rubricauda</i>, Bod.). <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/223</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The most valued kinds [of feathers] were tail-feathers of the<i> huia</i> ... and the long red tail-feathers of the <i>amokura</i> (Tropic bird: Phaeton rubricauda). <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,243</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Nevertheless the Maori bartered ... feathers of many hues - mainly those of the heron, huia, tropic bird (<i>amokura</i>), ... and the kaka. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,13</i>)

>  <i>tropic bird n. </i>one of two Australian varieties of sea-bird; also called <i>bos'n birds. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>WHIM-WHAM</b> Some People I have met find Lots/ Of Entertainment at the Trots; <i>(1942,Verses 1941-1942,30</i>) [NOTES: found under tropic bird headword - copied to 'trots']

>  It is the <i>Tachypetes Aquilus</i>, vulgarly called the Frigate, or Man-of-war Bird. .. Sloane, who saw these and tropic birds when he came into latitude 13deg. lOmin. says "The man-of-war bird seems very large, bigger than a kite, and black ; they fly like kites, very high, and often appear immovable over the water to wait for and catch small fish appearing on the surface ; they are sharp winged and their tail is forked. <i>(Mar 9, 1861,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  At most, a little lower, a snowy wanderer sails across with lightsome grace. It is the tropic bird... Looking at him near, we see he has no feet. At least, they are quite short, and webbed, so that he cannot walk or perch. With a formidable beak, he lacks the talons of a true sea eagle. <i>(Sep 9, 1887,Otago Witness,32</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> RED-TAILED TROPIC BIRD <i>Phaethon rubricauda. Other names</i>: Amokura, Strawtail. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,59</i>)
