### troppo
 
 <i>adj.</i> slightly crazy; eccentric esp. go troppo [?ca 1920 -->ca1946 H.S. Gajadhar, Wellington]

>  Instead of calmly laying out his past convictions, and letting his subsequent success in the world account for his longstanding reformation, he went troppo. <i>(Aug 12, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  To call a wine Chateau Troppo would be to invite a lingering death, or at best claim an unwanted niche like becoming the official drink of the Legion of Frontiersmen. <i>(Nov 22, 2000,Capital Times,15</i>)

>  The other great tragedy is that the liquor's cut which means that the old geezer.. - the one who went troppo after a night on the whiskey - will become terribly popular because of his home brew kit. <i>(Dec 16, 2000,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  the heater going troppo <i>(Apr 10, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  I got out before I went troppo. <i>(Oct 16, 2004,Ephemera</i>) [NOTES: w-ditwrath]

>  <b>DAVIS</b> But you know what Blue's like, goes a bit troppo when he sights game. <i>(1964,Watersiders,133</i>)

>  <i>troppo</i> ... adj. (Army sl.) <i>shortening of tropical madness; of soldiers, demented, eccentric, unbalanced, especially in phrases, going troppo, gone troppo. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> These jokers were victims of what you might call despair, a wartime tropical malaise. We said they'd gone troppo. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,184</i>)

>  States wanted to know what he was up to. He seemed happy enough with Barnier's explanation that he wanted to see more of Fiji and was going on a drive. As it was pitch black outside, Harman concluded Garnier had gone troppo and didn't pursue the matter. <i>(Aug 17, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),C2</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>troppo</i> displaying mental unsteadiness, often in phr. <i>gone troppo</i>; Anzac word for WWII Pacific theatre servicemen too long in the tropics and badly in need of repatriation. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  Swimmers are invited to go troppo at H20 Xtream in Upper Hutt this summer. <i>(Dec 13, 2001,Dominion,34</i>)

>  Other trees that make style statements include the palms, very trendy right now for the latest "troppo" look... The troppo style is mainly for gardens with mild winters, but several palms can rough it in colder gardens. <i>(Apr 28, 2000,Dominion,16</i>)

>  Terry's been seriously troppo for a few years. <i>(Jan 5, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  Their casualties were mostly what were called "troppo" cases, who simply cracked up, got malaria or dengue fever, or had infections that could not be treated in the tropics. <i>(May 13, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  Among those who stayed more or less at home,.. Jim Bolger's gone troppo... <i>(Feb 13, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### trot
 
 <i>n.</i> (in two-up) a succession of heads, esp. in throw a trot, do a trot

>  The boys [were] having a bit of a spin up, and he hopped into the ring, and did a trot for 700 francs. <i>(Sep 5, 1917,2NZEF Times,28</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> Sometimes a man would succeed daringly, doubling up and breaking the ring with a long run of heads, `throwing a trot'. <i>(1937,Civilian into Soldier,99</i>)

>  <b>BRUNO</b> Some ... who had ... struck a trot of `heads' at two-up. <i>(1944,Desert Daze,20 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Trot.</i> 4. A succession of heads thrown at two-up: Australians' and New Zealanders': late C. 19-20. I.e. <i>trot</i> = a run. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,911</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> . [re game of two-up; also trot up]

>  They had been `trotted' in usual way to 4s 6d or 5s. `Trotted' meant puffed up by fictitious bids. <i>(Aug 24, 1912,NZ Truth,374/7/2</i>)

>  Do you know he was bidding for her that day? - No. Didn't you `trot' him up when he was bidding for your younger brother and your mother? - No, I didn't. <i>(Oct 9, 1909,NZ Truth,224/6/1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> a common NZ variant of OED 4d. <i>trot out</i> (a woman), to walk with as a lover (1888)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I'm trotting a sheila. <i>(1946,That Summer,33</i>) [NOTES: (`Pair of Socks')]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> And past the Y.W.C.A. where I trotted out a girl once for a few weeks until I found out she had a steady down in Oamaru. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,190</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> trot (a sheila): take out regularly, court. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,304</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary to 1964 Sargeson Collected Stories 1935-1963 304]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Me trotting a sheila, dressmaker from Rintoul Street at the time. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,278</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to present false bids at a stock auction.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> trot: to take fictitious bids at auction. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,87</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> "You are "trotting" me, Mr Auctioneer!" <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,26</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> any run of luck [often as rough trot, cf. rough spin (see spin)]

>  "Hamish is in a trot now that he hasn't experienced before in his international career, but he's a good enough player to come out of it." <i>(Oct 25, 2005,Dominion Post,C14</i>)

>  10 In letters to a friend in Hastings Jerry Monahan states that he is having a good trot, and paper clippings accompanying the letters bear this out. Monahan beat Jamito for the light weight championship of the Orient, and another he beat was Young Pelky. <i>(Jan 17, 1925,NZ Truth,10</i>)

>  THERE is one way to make the money go and that is to stick to a jockey who is having a bad trot. <i>(Oct 30, 1944,NZ Truth,13</i>)

>  Foxchase is experiencing a bad "trot," which went a further stage at Riccarton on Saturday. <i>(Aug 9, 1944,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>trot </iluck, usually in `bad trot' or `good trot', mostly sport use; probably from two-up game, where a trot is a sequence of heads; eg `It's ages since I had a decent trot.' - <i>on the trot</i> a sequence, often a winning one, more usually picking a succession of horse race winners nowadays. - <i>do a trot</i> have a good run of luck. <i>trot</i> a woman, perhaps desired, as used by Gordon Slatter; probably a lightening of English word `trot', whore; c.1925. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)</i>

### trot up
 
 <i>v.</i> (See [also trotting (up)](../A#also-trotting-(up)))

>  The practice of "trotting up" stock on auction sales, that is, bidding by the owner to raise the price, is by no means uncommon. <i>(May 2, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

### trots
 
 <i>n.</i> harness racing; trotting races [aka the trots]

>  <b>McGEE</b> IRISH sits down on the form by his clothes and begins fiddling with his transistor, trying to get the trots, during the ensuing. <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,38</i>)

>  They come here, stick to the tote, the bar and the TV sets, then go home and say, "I had a great night at the trots!' <i>(Feb 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  [Hdg] Trots back in harness. "It's great, bloody great," declared the godfather of New Zealand trotting as he watched the return of harness racing to Hutt Park Raceway yesterday. <i>(Mar 3, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Horse-racing, another popular pastime (including trotting races, or <i>the trots</i>) provides two colloquialisms which need explanation. The exclamation `They're off, Mr Cutts!', announcing that any contest or afair has begun, contains the name of a one-time popular starter at the Auckland races. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>trots</i>: races <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <i>(Jun 16, 1959,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14/4</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> I can't stand races either. Call it a sport? ... Stick to the trots, that's what I say. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,99</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>trots</i>: (A) MONTEZUMA'S REVENGE. (B) That pursuit in which horses pull small carts containing individuals around in circles in front of a large number of other people who have come to see them go round in circles and who place bets on which horse, cart and drier will go round fastest. HARNESS RACING. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,106</i>)

>  <i>trot</i> ... (in pl.) <i>NZ & Austral</i>. race-meeting of trotting and pacing races. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,826</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> `I'll go up North to Auckland ... save up money and go to England, where the poms are. ... My grandad was one of them and he was a real beaut. ... He was a boozer and loved the trots, went every Saturday.' <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,54,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>trots</i> race meeting for (horse) trotters. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)

>  <b>WHIM-WHAM</b> Some People I have met find Lots/ Of Entertainment at the Trots <i>(1942,Verses 1941-1942,30</i>)

>  Taken altogether, the number of entries shows a great improvement on last year, especially in the Flying Handicap, the Cup, and the Trots. <i>(Nov 21, 1884,Star, The,3</i>)

>  Tonight on his way home he had stopped in at the T.A.B. and put two bob on 'Bright Star' for the trots at Forbury the next day. <i>(Sep 1961,Te Ao Hou,36: 46</i>)

>  The Club's trots are held on a picturesque course at Sockburn, formerly devoted to coursing. <i>(Feb 9, 1909,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tahuna Trots. <i>(May 10, 1900,Otago Witness,38</i>)

>  Nominations for the Open Hurdle Race, Springston Cup, Farmers' Plate and Trots, to be run at the Springston Race Meeting, close to-morrow at 9 p m. at the Springston Hotel. <i>(May 11, 1891,Star, The,4</i>)

>  Grand Light, winner of the President's Handicap at Timaru Trots. <i>(Jan 16, 1930,NZ Truth,13</i>)

>  The final day of the Metropolitan trots attracted another huge attendance. <i>(Nov 15, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>COSTELLO & FINNEGAN</b> Clarkson called both trots and gallops in Canterbury and also did the calls at Trentham during the 1950s. <i>(1988,Tapestry of Turf,501</i>)

>  [Hdg] Auckland Trots <i>(Dec 26, 1924,Christchurch Star,2</i>)

>  Those who want the reality of racing should try the night trots. <i>(Nov 8, 1992,Sunday Times (NZ),22</i>)

### trotter
 
 <i>n.</i> a harness horse

>  Mr. J. Westerman.. probably owns more trotters than he could number off-hand. <i>(May 9, 1934,NZ Truth,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> see cit.

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Trotters</i>.- Applied to the part of the leg left on sheep skins (up to the knee), not to the foot alone. I do not know whether this is the ordinary English usage. .br;(C.) <i>(Dec 23, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

### trotter-man
 
 <i>n.</i> one who cuts the trotters or feet off animals

>  <b>GORDON</b> The <i>trotter-man</i> cuts off the trotters and throws them down a hole. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

### trotting (up)
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the presenting of false bids at stock auctions.

>  The Timaru A & P Association have set up a committee to communicate with other associations and obtain information with a view of recommending that steps be taken for the prevention of "trotting" or false bids at auction sales of stock. The practice is said to be very rife in that district. <i>(Jun 23, 1898,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  A & P Conference vii Resolution 46 which had been proposed by Timaru. 'That in the opinion of this Conference bogus bidding or 'trotting' at auction sales is detrimental to owners of stock and land.' Mr Roberts said that trotting was hard to detect. (Bogus bidding, known as white bonnetting in Scotland.) <i>(1900,NZ Farmer,Supplement.</i>)

>  <b>BARTON</b> False bidding and 'trotting up' of the beast by a friend is a very clever trick - which rarely comes off. <i>(1971,Sheepfarming Annual 1971,206</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Did I hear someone use that terrible expression 'Trotting?" or (false bidding) to you uninitiated lot. I prefer a more legalistic description like "Judicious intervention to expedite the realisation of vendor's expectations." <i>(1990,Matawhero, Elsewhere and Other Things,38</i>)

### trou
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) short for trousers (See [down-trou](dou-dyn.md#down-trou))

>  <b>Geary</b> If you saw muck on his trou one week, bet your bottom dollar the next it'd still be there. <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,122</i>)

### trouble
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  FAULT.- A fissure, accompanied by a displacement of the strata, same as a slide, heave, hitch, throw, and trouble. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,54</i>)

### trough
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to put sheep through a footbath or trough.

>  <b>HALL</b> In the morning we drafted a few sheep and troughed a few rams for footrot. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year, A,51</i>)

### trough shell
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The men dug into the sand and brought up a large shell-fish, Mactra ovata, the trough shell, which had one leg of the gull tight between its valves. <i>(Aug 17, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

### trough-feed
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to feed farm animals by trough.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> If pasture is short, then supplementary feeding is essential, even if it means imitating the methods of ram breeders who trough-feed concentrates. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,36</i>)

### troughed
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of pastoral land] served with troughs for stock watersupply.

>  This property is one of the best of its type. The total area is spray irrigated by two Rotorainers. Very well troughed (at least 3 per paddock.) <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Press, The,64</i>)

### trousers
 
 <i>n.</i> a boyfriend [1953 15-16 M A29 Thames DHS 28 TROUSERS [a boyfriend 6]]

### trousseau
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Several months ago I started trousseauing. <i>(Apr 2, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,72</i>)

### trout
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kokopu](kiw-kwi.md#kokopu))

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Trout, n.</i> The English Trout has been naturalised in Australia. In Tasmania, the name of <i>Trout</i>, or <i>Mountain-Trout</i>, is also given to species of the genus Galaxias. See <i>Salmon</i>. <i>(1898,Austral English,480</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a person

>  The winner.. will earn.. a bottle of Plymouth gin, courtesy of some nice PR trouts who seem to think that advertising their client's product in <i>Page 94</i> is a good idea. Heaven knows why. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  It struck me that I had met perhaps the biggest f---wit this side of Australia, but a few days later some trout from the <i>New Zealand Herald</i> came to my door looking to write something about the spill. <i>(Jan 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  Who needs to turn on the television to watch tipsy, surgically challenged trouts falling up stairs when you live in Auckland? <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),79</i>)

>  And the repugnant ideas of Nationakl Party trout Katherine Rich, who last week advocated food stamps instead of welfare payments. <i>(Jun 14, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish; trout applied to native species before the introduction of exotic. [kokopu General and unspecified. -->1903 TrNZI 35/314 The upokororo appears to inhabit clear running streams ..., and I believe that the large fish locally called `trout', which were sometimes cast up on the beaches of the great inland lakes of Otago in the early days before trout were introduced, also belong to this species.] (See [kokopu](kiw-kwi.md#kokopu))

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... he came to a fine lake of fresh water ... he observed no other inhabitant in it than a small species of fish (esox), without scales, resembling a little trout; its colour was brown, and mottled with yellowish spots ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/159</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Silver eels attaining a goodly size are found in every stream; a sort of grayling is taken in a few of the clearest rivers; and the spotted Kokopu, called trout, ... abounds in every brook. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/122</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The kokopu, a name corrupted in the south of the South Island to cock-a-bully (<i>Galaxias kokopu</i>), was sometimes popularly called trout; <i>(1922,Naturalisation of Animals and Plants,187</i>)

>  ... generally so-called in Westland ... [get cit.] <i>(1899,TrNZI,31/89</i>) [NOTES: (Clarke)]

>  [British and European true sea trout member of Salmon family (salmon trout) has never been seen in NZ waters] What are commonly referred to as sea trout or sea-run trout in NZ are brown trout that have taken to feeding in salt water for greater or lesser periods, entering the estuaries of rivers periodically, also running up farther to spawn. These fish acquire a coating of silvery scales, which can be rubbed off, exposing rhe brown trout markings underneath .... No rainbow trout has ever been recorded as having been taken from salt water ... <i>(Apr 2, 1964,Dominion,9</i>) [NOTES: [Letter from Ered M. Banks, Wanganui]]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] trout, sea 1413, 1444 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>) [NOTES: archive this when system allows retrieval]

>  Although some delay was caused in stripping operations, owing to the lateness of the run of trout up the Tongariro River, both the Turangi and Ngongotaha hatcheries have been working at capacity for some time. <i>(Aug 11, 1937,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  The visitors waited until they saw a trout rise before they dropped their flies in front of it, whereas New Zealand anglers "prospected" the water, dropping their flies in all likely runs or spots. <i>(Dec 14, 1945,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The chief Tukorehu caught three splendid salmon trout in his eel-weir. <i>(Jul 16, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,23</i>) [NOTES: salmon trout is universal]

>  Trout fishing seems to be as successful as usual. <i>(Oct 8, 1881,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  TAUPO TROUT .. SMOKED TROUT 1s per lb .. FRESH TROUT may be ordered for delivery on THURSDAY. FRESH TROUT 1s per lb. <i>(Dec 6, 1913,Evening Post,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  By thumbing the spool as it spun, I added drag to the line, and the fish responded by rising to the surface, leaping with that glorious flash of pink and silver that only a rainbow trout in prime condition can show. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Dominion Post,E18</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [rainbow trout - not NZ specific]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> in New Zealand, the fresh-water brown trout when found in tidal waters.

### trout farm
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> About half a mile from us ... lies the Opawa Trout Farm, most interesting feature of the place.[check corr. location???] <i>(1891,In the Land of the Tui,179</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Mar 29 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 179]

 <i>n.</i> a roasting piece

 <i>n.</i> <i>Anacharis canadensis, (Elodea canadensis)</i> [ca1927 August R. Mason (Christchurch)]

 <i>n.</i> [limited citations found in internet search Jan 2006, for: NZ, Ireland, Austral.]

>  [advert]..<b>Trowel hand</b> Experienced finisher required for our Hastings factory... <i>(Feb 2, 2005,Hawke's Bay Today,27</i>)

### truant officer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If after the service of these notices the contravation [sic] still continues, reports are again to be made, so that the School Committees or Truant Officer may institute proceedings. <i>(Sep 5, 1896,Ellesmere Guardian,3</i>)

>  About two years ago attendance was made compulsory by the Town Committee and a truant officer appointed, but it did not work well, and matters were allowed to go on in their old way. <i>(May 14, 1885,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  One delegate said she knew of two children who had passed the sixth standard at 12 years, and on inquiries she found that the truant officer had no control over them because they had their school leaving certificates. <i>(May 13, 1943,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  According to the report of the truant officer, a boy in the Western Addition started for school the other day, and before he was four blocks from home he lamed a dog, lost his geography, scared a horse, broke his slate, and had three fights. <i>(Feb 3, 1879,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Augusta, the Truant Officer, Mr Forrest and John were all getting weary of the schools war, ... <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,26,1970 ed.</i>)

>  Letter from the Chairman Arahura School Committee with reference to the appointment of truant officers to carry out the provisions of the compulsory clauses of the Act. <i>(May 6, 1878,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  EDUCATION BOARD. MONTHLY MEETING. .. The Secretary of the Board was appointed truant officer in connection with the Fern Flat School. <i>(Mar 23, 1910,Colonist, The,2</i>)

>  Superintendent Thompson offers to place an intelligent officer at the disposal of the Committee as truant officer, and if necessary to make a house to house canvass in plain clothes. <i>(Oct 2, 1880,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

### truck
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  An Italian has trucked in a West Coast mine for twenty years; he is still alone ... ropeboys just starting can feel cocky pride in shouting: `Good day, you --- rotten Skypoo bastard!' <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,206</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> I saw two waggon teams approaching - one was a timber truck with eight horses, the other a six-horse waggon. ... I put my swag on the truck and minded his drag for him. He ... trucked me as far as Palmerston ... <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,20</i>) [NOTES: [In 1867]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The head salesman.. had supplied Marama with so much taonga and "truck". <i>(Dec 1912,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual : 30</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> [In 1867] I saw two waggon teams approaching - one was a timber truck with eight horses, the other a six-horse waggon. ... I put my swag on the truck and minded his drag for him. He ... trucked me as far as Palmerston ... <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,20</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The encroachment of<i> truck</i> on the domain of <i>lorry</i> is noticeable in New Zealand, too, especially since the Second World War. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,124</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to report fish as having been caught in one fisheries area when they were caught in another

>  The manager of the serious offences unit, Mike Green, said the ministry wanted to send a clear warning to fishermen that if they trucked they would be caught and punished. <i>(Jan 18, 2005,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

### truck iron
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>Z.W. WELLS</b> Tools hid in the bush under the first pine log west of my stream: Auger, truck irons, axe, mattock, doghook, hoe, Tools. <i>(May 4, 1863,Diary,51</i>)

### trucker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This has necessitated the erection of a large new drum, break, and wire rope, 900 yards long, to work the inclines with greater economy and with less labor, otherwise the contracting truckers could not do their work at the same prices per ton as at present. <i>(May 20, 1872,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  It is explained that truckers are mostly boys (?) which accounts for small wages of 5s and 6s a day. <i>(Apr 16, 1896,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  Then there is the wheeler or trucker. Some wheelers push the trucks with their hands, others with their heads. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,IV/613</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> Why do you think we pay you truckers threepence a box?' the other asked. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,27</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> There was a bad flood in the mine, ... The miners at the face laboured mightily still; they were on a bonus. But the shiftmen, responsible for maintaining `sets' of timber in the mine, grew steadily more shiftless about their work while the truckers ... sat uneasily between the two extremes in alternate bursts of laziness and zeal. <i>(1980,All My Lives,61</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> As you dug your way in the rails followed, enabling the truckers to push empty tubs right up to where you were working. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,128</i>)

>  Yesterday two truckers at the James mine, who allegedly "knocked off" early on Wednesday, were stood down for the day. <i>(Nov 3, 1939,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  The manager of the mine, Mr. F. Lockington, stated this morning that the Wallsend mine was better than any he had been in as far as trucking conditions were concerned, while the ratio of truckers to miners was unusually high. <i>(Feb 26, 1945,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  WANTED, Twenty experienced Miners and Truckers. <i>(Jun 16, 1868,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  PERMANENT EMPLOYMENT for TWELVE GOOD MINERS at the above Mine. Also, Truckers wanted. <i>(Feb 22, 1882,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  While Mr. Newlands was there the truckers came in with naked lights. <i>(Oct 8, 1912,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  A trucker, named George P. Jack, was killed in the Westport Coal Company's Ironbridge mine this afternoon through being caught by the coal tubs on the haulage road. <i>(Jul 5, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,5</i>)

### truckie
 
 <i>n.</i> one who drives heavy trucks for a living; a truckdriver

>  While the forestry industry is currently in a bit of a trough, the 'wall of wood' is going to have to come out of the ground sooner or later and when it does we will need more truckies... <i>(Apr 4, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

>  WCC's support for petrol tax leaves truckies aghast (headline). <i>(Nov 13, 1991,Evening Post</i>)

>  Forcing drivers into illegal spaces might have filled Wellington City Council coffers - tow truckies had a field day - but does force work? <i>(Jan 18, 2000,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Truckies are the cowboys and cowgirls of the main highways. <i>(Jul 24, 2000,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Truckies called in to help run down vehicle terrorists. <i>(Jan 26, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>) [NOTES: Original in Taupo Weekender 22/22/01]

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> `I hope our truckie hasn't any ideas about sitting down to lunch.' <i>(1960,Tangahano,23</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> I thought the truckie supplied the gang as part of the service ... <i>(1969,Green River,16</i>)

>  Warehouse drugs under truckie's bed [headline] <i>(Dec 22, 1982,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  The truckies insist theirs is a responsible job. <i>(Apr 7, 1983,Dominion,7</i>)

>  While travelling north on the Desert Road, a reader was interested in the stencilled sign on the back of a large truck: If Dolly Parton were taxed like a Kiwi truckie, she would be flat-busted. <i>(Aug 19, 1984,Sunday News,2</i>)

>  <i>truckie n.</i> (also <i>trucky) NZ & Austral. colloq</i>. truck-driver. [TRUCK 1] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,826</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1257]]



 
 <i>n.</i> [logging / saw milling]

>  WANTED, Wood Cutters, Bush Truckie. <i>(Feb 11, 1921,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  BUSHMAN and Ropeman, for Akatarawa Mill, single men preferred; also Truckie, single or married. <i>(Aug 19, 1933,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  WANTED, a good Bush Truckie. <i>(Aug 19, 1915,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  WANTED at once, crosscutter, ropeman, and truckie; good wages. <i>(Aug 27, 1907,Bush Advocate,1</i>)

### trucking
 
 <i>n.</i> catching fish in one management area and claiming they were caught somewhere else

>  Or it may catch fish in one management area and claim they were caught somewhere else - a practice known as "trucking" - to throw competitors off the scent. <i>(May 11, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,3</i>)

### trucking dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a specially trained sheep and cattle dog kept by a truck driver of livestock [for "working" stock while loading and unloading.]

>  A fresh dog on tired sheep always has a good effect, but combined with the propelling powers of the best trucking dog of his day in Addington yards, the results were an eye-opener to this squatter and his men. <i>(Feb 1, 1930,NZ Farmer,172</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> trucking dog: a specialist dog kept by the driver of a lorry which carts livestock, and used whilst loading and unloading his crate. Undoubtedly such dogs become the most agile of all backing dogs and they quickly learn just exactly how tight or loose the dividing pens should be packed. They also need to be versatile enough to work cattle. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,3</i>)

>  <i>Trucking dog</i>: Dog kept specially to help load stock into trucks. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,25</i>)

### true bill
 
 <i>phr.</i> true (dinkum), fair dinkum [ca1952 July 14 Auckland-Wellington busdriver used this as an adjective several times: e.g. This story's true bill. Here's another, true bill. In NZ 1891 [check this]]

### true dink(s)
 
 <i>exclam.</i> a shortened form of true dinkum (see dinkum, dinky die) used esp. by children to express or reinforce agreement [ca1935 Havelock schoolboys' e.g. I'll bring the comic back tomorrow, true dinks.]

>  <b>COMBS</b> True dink. <i>(1939,Harrowed Toad,47</i>)

>  <b>COMBS</b> He asked me would we get the threepence and buy some comics and I said, "No don't, Burton." True dink, I did, Mr. Bojis. <i>(1939,Harrowed Toad,47</i>)

### true lancewood
 
 <i>n.</i> Pseudopanax lineare.

>  <b>KIRK</b> true lancewood, <i> Pseudopanax lineare. </i> <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1919,NZ Plants,2nd ed.</i>)

### truesville
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>truesville</i> very true; an example of American-influenced 1960s teens adding `ville'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)

### trull
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [troll](tot-tyr.md#troll))

### Truly Rural Party
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  That honourable member was one of the great "Truly Rural" party which was to bring about an entire revolution in their political system by sweeping away the Provincial Councils altogether. <i>(1873,NZ Parliamentary Debates,14/98</i>)

### trummel
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. trammel?]

>  <b>REED</b> ... the two being held together by hardwood pegs or trummels. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,177</i>) [NOTES: 1953 D.J. McLeod quoted in Reed Story of Kauri 177]

### trump
 
 <i>n.</i> the boss; one in authority

>  The trump comes in and calls us gentlemen. <i>(Jun 1950,Landfall,14/7</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton) [also 1953 Short Stories 410]]

>  The hoops are on the last cask by 11.45, and the trump calls out all hands to load the last railway wagon. <i>(1950,Landfall,4(2) : 126</i>)

### trump of the dump
 
 <i>phr.</i> (WW1) anyone in authority

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> His offsider said `Give a man a fair go', so I gave him a bit of kidney pie and there was nothing lousy about him, but he said he had to be careful because the other fellow was the trump of the dump. ... Although, he said, he was the white-haired boy with the trump he was due for a shake-up and he had to take a tumble to himself as to how he gave the company-rations away. ... It's the offsider I'm talking about because he winked at me when the trump of the dump asked him if he had barbered the spuds for tomorrow's breakfast. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  There is a touch of irreverence in `the trump of the dump,' for O.C., and in `one-star artist';. <i>(Apr 2, 1933,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: [Slang of the N.Z.E.F.] 1938 Apr 2 McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Trump of the dump</i>, the. Anyone in authority: New Zealanders': in G.W. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,912</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>trump of the dump</i> anyone in authority; WWI. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)

### trumpet
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  [species name] Notopogon fernandezianus .. [scientific name] <i>Notopogon fernandezianus</i> .. [other name(s)] trumpet <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### trumpeter
 
 <i>n.</i> an edible sea-fish of the south of New Zealand and southern Australia.

>  Black cardinalfish, frostfish, ghost shark, ribaldo, rubyfish, sea perch, trumpeter, white warehou and yellow-eyed mullet have now been included... Other reductions to catch limits are to specific stocks of grey mullet, barracouta and oreo dory. <i>(Sep 12, 1998,Dominion,2</i>)

>  A record-breaking trumpeter has been caught off Wellington's coast. ... Back on land, Mr Lamb checked the record books and found the New Zealand record for a trumpeter stood at 38lbs (17.2kg). <i>(Aug 11, 2004,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  A large Australian fish so called from the sound it makes when taken out of the water. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),423</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> TRUMPETER ... <i>Other names: Latris lineata</i>; trumpeter (Australia); kohikohi (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,253</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Trumpeter .. .. kohikohi .. .. <i>Latris lineata</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>trumpeter n.</i> large edible fish, so called from noise made when it is removed from the water. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> TRUMPETER [<i>Latris lineata</i> ] ... The Maori name kohikohi is still in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,40</i>)

(Zoo.l.) </i>See MADO. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2033</i>)

(Zoo.l.) </i>See MADO. <i>(1898,Dominion Post,2033</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Bastard Trumpeter <i>Latridopsis forsteri</i> (Castelnau) (Syn. <i>Platystethus huttoni</i> Gunther) ... Bastard Trumpeter <i>Latridopsis acrosa </i>(Hutton) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,408</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 408]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BASTARD TRUMPETER <i>Latridopsis forsteri</i> (Castelnau) [ibid. 260] The Scientific name <i>Latris</i> means a rascal, hence the common name `bastard' ((Bastard Trumpeter). <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,259</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> MOKI [<i>Latridopsis cliaris</i>] ... Known as bastard trumpeter in Australia; only the Maori name is used in New Zealand. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,40</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> [The] trumpeter, <i>Latris lineata</i> ... ,is dark olive-green to brown striped with light yellow-lime; its silvery sides gleam with yellow. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,222</i>)

>  <i>trumpeter n</i>. large edible fish, so called from noise made when removed from the water. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>TRUMPETER </i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Latris lineata</i> . (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Striped Trumpeter. (c) Other common names: None recorded. (d) Principal localities where taken: Napier southward; Cook Strait; Foveaux Strait. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,49</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> TRUMPETER <i>Latris lineata</i> : Deep brown in colour on sides and back, with a yellowish belly; the brown is streaked with three, wide, whitish lines from the which the description `lineata' derives. The Maori name `Kohikohi', means `to gather here and there' and perhaps refers to the fact that a fisherman is unlikely to catch more than three or four at one time. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,265</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>COMMON TRUMPETER </i>(Tasmanian trumpeter) <i>Latris lineata</i> ...This species is common round Stewart Island and in the south of the [check if correct location/if cit. complete???] <i>(1982,Collins Guide,248,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <i>trumpeter</i> ... NZ food-fish that makes grunting noise as it is taken from the water. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,826</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1257-1258, sense 4]]

>  [species name] trumpeter .. [scientific name] <i>Latris lineata</i> .. [other name(s)] striped trumpeter <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Trumpeter Latri lineata </i>Also occurs around Australia ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,106</i>)

>  17. <i>Taiaroa Head. </i>- Trumpeter, pig-fish, frost-fish, rock-cod, maori chief ...18. <i>Cape Saunders</i>. - Trumpeter, Maori chief ..24. <i>Puysegur Point </i>- Hapuku, kahawai, trumpeter, moki, pig-fish, trevally, rock-cod, perch, butter-fish, red-cod, dog-fish <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,24/204</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Porae, Latris hecateia, trumpeter</i>, a fish. [1957/292 Latris lineata ] <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,139</i>)

>  Moki are very plentiful, but we only get a few trumpeter now and then. Of course, the cod and groper are plentiful, also butter-fish and barracouta. <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/53</i>) [NOTES: (Henry)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Trumpeter, n.</i> (1) A fish of Tasmanian, New Zealand, and Australian waters, but chiefly of Hobart <i>Latris hecateia</i>, Richards., family <i>Cirrhitidae</i>, much esteemed as a food-fish, and weighing sometimes 50 or 60 1bs. The name is probably for the noise made by the fish when taken out of the water. The name was formerly given to a different fish in Western Australia. See also <i>Bastard-Trumpeter, Morwong,</i> and <i>Paper-fish. ... </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,480</i>)

>  <i>Trump&#769;.et-er</i> (tr&ubreve.mp&#769;.&ebreve.t-&etilde.r), <i>n. Add. Bastard trumpeter (Zoo..l)</i>, in Sydney, the fish <i>Latris ciliaris (called moki</i> in New Zealand); in Victoria and Tasmania, <i>Latris Forsteri</i>; in Melbourne, the morwong. - <i>Trumpeter perch. (Zoo.l.) </i>See MADO. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2033</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Latris lineata</i> (Forster). TRUMPETER; KOHIKOHI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,39,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The Trumpeter is rare, but its close relative the Moki (<i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i>) is secured in good numbers near the kelp, as also are Butterfish (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>), and Trevally (<i>Longirostrum platessa</i>). <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,199</i>)

>  <i>Latris lineata</i>, Trumpeter. <i>(1928,TrNZI,60/381</i>) [NOTES: (Griffin)]

>  The fish which, at Port Nicholson, is known by the name of habooka, but which is called groper to the southward, makes its appearance about the middle of November. This, with the cod and trumpeter, remain on the coast the greater part of the summer. <i>(Jun 1, 1844,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,51</i>)

>  Fish are not so abundant in Milford Sound as they are to the south , but we got a few trumpeter and sea perch of excellent quality, and also several small species of fish which I have not seen elsewhere on the coast. <i>(Nov 16, 1863,Southland Times,5</i>)

>  Here, too, are trumpeters striped like a zebra, black and white and blue. <i>(Jun 5, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  <b>DOUGALL</b> [They] caught [at Stewart Island] trumpeter, blue cod, and a couple of hapuka (groper) ... <i>(1888,Far South,4</i>)

>  <b>286</b> trumpeter fish - goo'ee goo'ee [?] [=kohikohi `trumpeter'] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,1986</i>) [NOTES: Also Begg 1979, p.113]

>  <b>SHAW</b> trumpeter fish <i>(1858,Gallop to the Antipodes, A,135</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> ... trumpeter, soldier fish . <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,13</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> The Trumpeter (<i>Latris hecateia</i>), which is the Tasmanian name for the same fish, and which the natives call Kohikohi, is the best flavoured of any of our fishes, ... [see 1886 Sherrin cit. below???] <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,107</i>)

>  [Maori or Settlers' Name] Kohi, Trumpeter .. [Scientific Name] <i>Latris hecateia </i> <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/326</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  (Black Stewart Island) It was no unusual event ...to land ... Moki, <i>Latris ciliaris;</i> Trumpeter, <i>Latris hecateia </i>; Blue cod or Rock cod, <i>Percis colias</i>; and Red cod,<i> Lotella bacchus. </i> <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Kohikohi, Trumpeter .. [Scientific Name] <i>Latris hecateia </i> <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SENIOR</b> Amongst the sea-fishes fit for food there are ... aua, rock cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butter-fish, and red-cod are confined to the south. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,288</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> TRUMPETER (<i>Latris hecateia</i>). ... Dr. Hector wrote last year: `The trumpeter (<i>Latris hecateia</i>), which is the Tasmanian name for this fish, and which the Natives call kohikohi, is one of the best flavoured of any of our fishes, and though abundant, is so extremely local in its habits, remaining continually on certain patches of feeding-ground, ... <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,100</i>)

>  Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand, we have among the constant residents on all parts of our coast the hapuku, tarakihi, trevally, moki, aua, rock-cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are confined to the south. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The snapper, the mullet, and the trevally are essentially northern; while the trumpeter, the red cod, and the ling are just as essentially southern. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Snapper, <i>warehou</i>, butterfish, trumpeter, <i>moki</i>, flat-fish (<i>patiki</i>), and other fine sea fish were plentiful. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,107</i>)

>  <i>Latris Lineata</i> ... Trumpeter. <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/219</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

Others omitted are the tarakihi, or terakihi, the moki, the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), whai (skate), and the koekohe [sic] (trumpeter). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <i>trumpeter:</i> edible fish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

 <i>n.</i> said to a person blowing his own trumpet [1960 18C M B11 Nelson Boys C. 17 TRUMPETER (BLOWING FROM ONE'S TRUMPET)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Bastard Trumpeter, n. a fish. See Morwong, Paper-fish, and Trumpeter. In Sydney it is <i>Latris ciliaris</i>, Forst, which is called Moki in New Zealand; in Victoria and Tasmania, <i>L. forsteri,</i> Casteln. [1883] <i>(1898,Austral English,21</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> MOKI [<i>Latridopsis cliaris</i>] ... Known as bastard trumpeter in Australia; only the Maori name is used in New Zealand. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,40</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> Five species [of trumpeter] are recorded from New Zealand but there appears to be some confusion regarding ... the bastard trumpeter <i>Latridopsis forsteri</i> and the copper moki <i>Latridopsis aerosa</i>. Though officially recognised, the bastard trumpeter appears to be identical to the young blue moki ... On the other hand the copper moki seems to be a valid species - although referred to here as <i>Latridopsis forsteri</i> <i>ibid.</i> 247 COPPER MOKI (Bastard trumpeter) <i>Latridopsis forsteri (Latridopsis aerosa)</i> ... The body is silvery with four fine zig-zag horizontal bands of pink or olive tinged pink along the back ... They are most abundant around Stewart Island and become progressively less common further north ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,246,1984 ed.</i>)

### trundle
 
 <i>v.</i> to have sexual intercourse with a woman [ca1941 Wellington Also occas. used as a noun.]

### trundler
 
 <i>n.</i> a cart for carrying groceries or one for carrying golf clubs

>  <b>McDERMOTT</b> [Golf] Carts are called <i>trundlers</i> and spikes are called <i>sprigs. </i> <i>(1976,How to Get Lost & Found in NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>trundler:</i> A two-wheeled shopping basket used by those who would rather roll their purchases home than carry them there. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,106</i>)

>  <i>trundler n. N.Z. 1.</i> a trolley for shopping or one for golf clubs. 2. a child's pushchair. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1258</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 515]]

>  [Such] can be virtually restricted to the Antipodes - <i>carbonette, five-eighth</i>, or <i>huntaway </i>(dog) - or even to New Zealand - <i>dairy</i> (the shop), <i>trundler</i> or <i>section</i> (which is <i>N.Z.</i>) in the sense `a plot of land for building' but <i>Austral.</i> and<i> N.Z. </i>in the sense `a fare stage on a bus, train etc.' <i>(Aug 2, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),100</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a trundle bed

>  <b>BIG SAVE FURNITURE</b> Bed + trundler Comes with two foam mattresses <i>(2006,Pamphlet</i>)

### trunk
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> freezing was done for the first ten years [from c1882] by the cold air machines ...[66] The carcases were hung in the chambers, and the cold air was blown through kauri ducts or trunks at a temperature of minus 40[degree sign] Fahrenheit. The carcases, being relatively warmer than the air, always lost moisture to it; this formed snow which blocked the trunks, cutting down the air circulation and giving the `snow-men', who had to crawl through the trunks, a bad time in clearing them. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,65</i>)

### trunk girdle
 
 <i>v.</i> [ref. kiwifruit growing]

>  Growers who trunk girdled last February are reminded that if girdles were correctly applied, an increase in flower numbers this spring can be expected. ... To date, vines that have been repeat trunk girdled over two consecutive years showed similar floral responses to those girdled for one year. <i>(Jun 27, 2005,Kiwiflier,7</i>) [NOTES: -]

### trunk girdling
 
 <i>n.</i> [3 hits only on Google; all NZ (ref. kiwifruit growing)]

>  Trunk girdling - Effect on Return Bloom ... Greater increases in floral response resulting from trunk girdling are likely to occur with poor winter chilling and/or cool early spring conditions which lower budbreak and flower initiation. ... Further information on trunk girdling, from HortResearch, is available on The Canopy www.zespricanopy.com. <i>(Jun 27, 2005,Kiwiflier,7</i>) [NOTES: -]

### trunkfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 115. OSTRACION FORNASINI. Bianc.? Trunk Fish. <i>(1872,Fishes,71</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Ostracion fornasini </i> Bianconi. TRUNK-FISH. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,56,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The trunk-fish (<i>Ostracion fornasini </i>) has the tubercles united into a bony box of six-sided plates for protection against enemies, while the porcupine fish (<i>Dicotylichthys</i>) has the tubercles specialised into long spines. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,202</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 202]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family OSTRACIIDAE. The box fish (<i>Ostracion ... </i>) and trunk fish (<i>Paracanthostracion</i>) have the head and body completely encased in an armoured box of welded hexagonal plates. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,233</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> TRUNKFISH: <i>see Boxfish </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,261</i>)

 <i>n.</i> treenail

 <i>n.</i>

### Trust control
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  As to the proposed State control, municipal or trust control, he showed that they were all failures. <i>(Mar 17, 1904,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  It was also decided that by trust or corporate control was meant the manufacture, importation, and sale of intoxicating liquor under the management of a public utility corporation. <i>(Mar 1, 1945,Evening Post,9</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### try
 
 <i>v.</i> to extract oil from blubber by heat (See [also try out](tot-tyr.md#try-out))

>  <b>RHODES</b> `Australien' ... commenced trying the outpart of the blubber. <i>(1836,Journal</i>)

>  <b>WEEKES</b> The blubber ... is stripped off and melted down or `tried' as they call it. <i>(1841,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Weekes Progress of the Colony in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 100]

>  <b>WEEKES</b> The Blubber ... is stripped off and melted down or `tried' [check sic ?`tired'] as they call it. <i>(1842,Journal,3/10</i>)

### try down
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Then the [mollymawk] meat was tried down over a very hot fire in whalers [sic] trypots, the brown cooked meat drained of oil, trimmed, packed in casks covered with oil and headed. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,88</i>)

### try out
 
 <i>v.</i> of a whale, to yield oil

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ</i>)

>  During that time five whales were captured, and when "tried out" gave between 15 and 20 tuns oil. <i>(Dec 6, 1877,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

>  A 36ft "right" whale was cast upon the beach about two miles north of the Totara River, on the West Coast. It will be tried out. <i>(Dec 11, 1896,Colonist, The,3</i>)

>  The whalers of Kapiti, we are given to understand, have been fortunate in the number of whales they have caught; Gillott's party having obtained 120 tuns of oil, and Haim's 50 tuns with dead ones not tried out. <i>(Sep 20, 1843,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  The Northern Company's steamer Ohinemuri called in at Whangamumu on her way from the north to Auckland on Wednesday, and brought down 41 casks of oil, the product of whales caught and tried out. <i>(Aug 2, 1900,Waimate Daily Advertiser,3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to extract oil from blubber (See [also try](tot-tyr.md#try))

>  Mr Perano, of the Te Awaite whaling station, near Picton, has been communicated with regarding the prospects of trying out the mammal. <i>(Feb 3, 1920,Colonist, The,3</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> ... the blubber is boiled out in try pots erected on deck as in a sperm whaler. From its being tried out immediately after the fish is caught the oil is much purer and is free from the rancid smell of Greenland oil. <i>(Nov 1831,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Nov Bell Letter in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 9]

>  <b>CAPT. GREENE</b> [Whalers] were under the impression that ... they would have no further labour ... than to fasten to whales alongside, cut in, try out, stow away without intermission, until their cargoes would be completed. <i>(1836,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1836 Capt. Greene Report in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 154]

>  <b>STRAUBEL</b> Trying out oil and stowing down. <i>(1836,Rhodes Whaling Journal,14,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> there is a large building containing about 6 large coppers with furnaces beneath which the blubber is boiled or technically speaking tryed out. ... there are three large poles erected in the sea forming a triangle which are called shears on which a platform is made and to this the whales are towed, and here cut up ... <i>(Apr 1831,Journal,15</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> [Tonguers] take off what little blubber the ship's crew have passed over, and also take out the tongue and try it all out. <i>(1849,Plain Truths,60</i>)

>  <b>JOLLIE</b> The last whale caught was nearly `tryed out', as it was called, or in other words, melted down into oil. <i>(1878,Reminiscences,12</i>)

>  Bob McLurg tryed out blackfish oil, a nice job for a cold day. <i>(Jul 10, 1895,Chudleigh Diary,391,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Dilapidated, rickety little huts dotted about, huge whale-bones strewn everywhere, savage pigs and dogs feasting on the refuse which tainted the air ...; busy groups of men cutting out and trying out the blubber of some recent prize; ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,46</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> With steam vats the blubber was cut into larger pieces and tried-out very quickly. <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,30</i>)

>  On Thursday, 150 miles to the westward of the Kings, Capt. Sturley saw two whalers trying out. <i>(Jan 27, 1857,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  On being tried out, there was a yield of 250 gallons of very good oil, which is now fast selling at four shillings a gallon. <i>(Jan 31, 1862,Wellington Independent,3</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> When they got a whale they landed the blubber also and left men to try out the oil on shore, <i>(1915,Reminiscences of Earliest Canterbury,56</i>)

### try pot
 
 <i>n.</i> an iron boiler, often on legs, for trying out whale or seal blubber. [whaling and sealing; aka try pot, trypot]

>  Apart from the interesting history attached to the early settlement of Karori, interest in the early days will be reviewed by the exhibition of an old try-pot, which Mr. Cook has had in his possession for some years, originally in use on Mana Island, and since being brought to the mainland this old "pot" has been put to many uses. <i>(Mar 4, 1922,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  At that remote period (1856) the fair city of Timaru consisted of two houses, some sheds and half a dozen abandoned whalers' "try pots" on the beach. <i>(Oct 9, 1890,Otago Witness,31</i>)

>  the Try Pots finished Putting up. <i>(Feb 22, 1838,Piraki Log,66,(ed. Anson 1911) ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The proprietor of the store was disposing of a few iron try-pots, in barter for hogs and potatoes, to some natives. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/69</i>)

>  <b>MONRO</b> Waikawa is totally uninhabited, but the traces of its former occupation by a whaling party are to be seen in old try-pots and oil-casks and bones of whales. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 250]

>  We brought home a 5 cwt. trypot and a load of manure. <i>(Apr 11, 1887,Chudleigh Diary,353,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> The shore equipment [of a whaling station] consisted principally of `trying out' gear - two large try-pots of about 200 gallons capacity, in which the blubber was rendered down; ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,75</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRIE</b> <i>Try-pot</i>: the huge pots in which the oil was tried out <i>(1906,Treasury of NZ Verse,290</i>)

>  Two witch's pots (small edition of whaler's try-pot) and a camp oven swinging above fire. Titis sizzling in their own kato fat (fat from insides). Piro fat, the fine fat from the intestines, is more precious. <i>(Apr 20, 1952,Diary</i>) [NOTES: Stewart Isd muttonbirders') 1952 Apr 20 diary in 1982 Sansom In the Grip of an Island 154]

>  <b>MURRY</b> The Try pot and steam were as they were left [by the sealers]. [author's MS fn] An Iron Boiler of 84 gallons. <i>(1795,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1795 Murry's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/523]

>  <b>BELL</b> If the fishing is to be carried on by a shore party, the try pots and huts are erected on the beach ... The boats are sent out at daylight every morning, and when ... they kill a fish it is towed ashore and flinched and boiled upon the beach. <i>(Nov 1831,Letter,9</i>)

>  A path from the beach leads past several try pots - used for boiling whale blubber into oil - reminders of the island's whaling station days. <i>(Dec 10, 2001,Dominion,17</i>)

>  - Eliza, 42 tons, W. Ellis, for the East Coast, with 8 bags sugar, 1 case wine, 1/8 cask sherry, ton salt, 4 kegs paint, 5 packages nails, 1 package saddlery, 1 plough, 11 oil casks, 1 package merchandize. [sic] 1 try pot, 2 coils cordage. <i>(Oct 29, 1851,New Zealander, The,27</i>)

>  8 dozen Try Pots, six boilers. <i>(Nov 16, 1869,Hawke's Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  The Isabella will sail direct for Porirua, where try-pots will be shipped; then she will make for Queen Charlotte Sounds, where a whale-boat will be obtained, and then the vessel will stand away on her whaling cruise. <i>(May 10, 1875,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> I can remember seeing the bricks on which the try-pots had been supported. <i>(1915,Reminiscences of Earliest Canterbury,56</i>)

### try-hard
 
 <i>n.</i> an excessively driven person

>  Excessive zeal - as in the contemptuous label of being a "try hard", the term we stick on anyone who seems too good or too worthy to be true. <i>(Jul 31, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### try-works
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The try-works, or large iron vats for boiling out the oil, are also cleaned, repaired or renewed as circumstances may require. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,I/328</i>)

>  Try-works, Tanks, and Gear, &c., at Teawiti. <i>(Mar 2, 1867,Wellington Independent,1</i>)

>  All his search for Sullivan was in vain; and in coming aft again, his attention was arrested by the absence of a large oval tub, which had been lashed up near the tryworks. <i>(Aug 4, 1870,Tuapeka Times,7</i>)

>  In fitting out the "Celestia" on her recent cruise a very large outlay was incurred in giving her a thorough overhaul - new decks, new windlass, new tryworks, and in fact every requisite for making her a thoroughly efficient whaler. <i>(Nov 24, 1880,Southland Times,4</i>)

>  After wetting down the decks as usual, the mate gave the order to turn-to at cleaning the try-works - a step which brought us all up "with a round turn," as we say. <i>(Feb 24, 1900,Star, The,2</i>)

>  It appears that during the day two large whales made their appearance inside the Heads, and were spouting and rolling about just opposite the tryworks, when they were noticed by some of the old settlers, who immediately launched Mr James Jackson's whaleboat - which was all in readiness for the season - and gave pursuit. <i>(May 20, 1895,Marlborough Express,3</i>)

### trying house
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Trying houses. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/37 There was much stench from whale-oil, but this was disregarded, so great was the interest I felt in the whole process. After I had inspected the trying-houses, in cleaning which a native of Australia was occupied, of whose intelligence and quickness his master spoke very highly, I went through the village. <i>(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/136</i>)

### trying out
 
 <i>n.</i> [(whaling)]

>  <b>25</b> 2 finished trying out; people employed cleaning ship and other necessary jobs. <i>(Jan 27, 1836,Piraki Log,ed. Anson 1911</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Large fires glared through the darkness from the neighbouring beach, lighted for the <i>trying out</i> of the blubber of a large whale, which had been brought in that morning; a confused sound of voices reached our ears, and proved to us that even in this remote corner of the world it was the custom to celebrate any happy event with profuse libations. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/36</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> <i>Trying-out</i> - The cooking or boiling out of the oil from the blubber. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,228</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> The shore equipment [of a whaling station] consisted principally of `trying out' gear - two large try-pots of about 200 gallons capacity, in which the blubber was rendered down; ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,75</i>)

>  <b>MONRO</b> Close to where we landed, an enormous whale's head, stripped of its blubber, was anchored, ... and on a projecting ledge the process of trying out was going on ... diffusing a most grateful odour of train oil. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 247]

>  The master reports having fallen in with several whalers, principally American, to the south, all engaged in cutting or trying out, and boats out, with a large number of sperm whales about; did not speak [to] any of them. <i>(Apr 13, 1859,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The monster is towed in ... to be cut up for the `trying pans'. <i>(1857,Southern Districts of NZ,316</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [try out](tot-tyr.md#try-out))

### trying-pot
 
 <i>n.</i> = trypot

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The blubber is cut off in square pieces by means of a sharp spade; it is then carried to the shore, and immediately put into the trying-pots. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/51</i>)

>  The old whaling "trying pot" at Akaroa aroused interest locally, with the result that a similar one has been brought to light at New Plymouth, where it has lain for many years unknown to most people. <i>(Feb 1, 1912,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  THE GLAMOR OF LONDON. .. She is the crucible of the world, the trying pot to which men all unwilling must yet carry their metal to be assayed. <i>(Feb 15, 1905,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

### TSO
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Telecommunications Service Obligation - a new name for Kiwi share.

>  Competing telephone companoies now have to contribute to "Kiwi Share" losses, or Telecommunications Service Obligations. Rival phone companies would have to fork out $85 million to Telecom under its latest calculation of the TSO losses. <i>(Sep 24, 2002,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

### TT2
 
 <i>n.</i> an ice-block [Originally a proprietary name]

>  Even better news is that to do this [sc. evoke summer] you don't have to run barefoot on scalding asphalt, get caught in the undertow on some black-sanded Taranaki beach, let orange TT2 drip down the front of your togs, or spend hours removing the dear little prickles of onehunga weed from the soles of your feet. <i>(Jan 2, 2002,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  You get four cents back from a large bottle which can buy you a TT2, a handful of banana bikes or an all-day sucker. <i>(Dec 1996,North & South,44</i>)

>  This gave me good reaon to browse through a rather charming book called <i>New Zealand! New Zealand! In Praise of Kiwiana</i>, by Stephen Barnett and Richard Wolfe .. The passage reads: "More fondly recalled is the alliterative and now deceased TT2, the classic ice-block of the fifties and sixties and a mainstay of summer holidays for New Zealand children. While its name was no more meaningful than the American borrowing of 'popsicle' that has replaced it, it was ours .. Apparently there was no TT1." I think it's safe to assume that "TT2" comes from the Tip-Top brand name. <i>(May 8, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  Confectionery as children now know it did not exist. The first frozen sweets were perhaps not inexplicably known as TT2s and were manufactured in sheet metal factories out of a kind of flavoured stainless steel. <i>(Apr 13, 1997,Sunday Star Times,A2</i>)

>  For me, the rituals of the long drop were so much part of summer, inextricably woven with the juice of TT2s dribbling down our chins and arms, black sand sticking to the residue as we sprang across the beach, our jandals flip flopping against the hot sand. <i>(Aug 2005,NZ Memories,55 / 51</i>)

### tuakana
 
 <i>n.</i> an older brother or sister; a senior (compare TEINA). [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  'You do the first karanga,' Hera whispered. 'Why?' 'Coz you're my tuakana.' 'No you do the first karanga.' 'Why?' 'Because I said so, I'm your tuakana.' <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,180</i>)

>  <b>Buck</b> To sum up briefly, a male is tuakana to all his younger brothers and teina to all his older brothers. ... If there are any daughters in the family, they follow the same principle, being tuakana to all their younger sisters and teina to their older sisters. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,340</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> His closeness to Tiwha - a tuakana in a related line - was the one precious thing remaining from his youth; ... <i>(1969,Green River,14</i>)

>  <b>Hone Kouka</b> Contemporary Maori theatre in comparison is of a tender age, though like its esteemed tuakana, it holds our history, politics and traditions in its hands. <i>(1999,Ta Matou Mangai (Our Own Voice),11</i>) [NOTES: [figurative usage]]

### tuakura
 
 <i>n.</i> a tree-fern

>  Should there be no cabbage-leaves to be had, they use the leaves of the tuakura (<i>Dicksonia squamosa</i>[sic]), a fern which grows luxuriantly, in large quantities, in damp places. <i>(Nov 21, 1863,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  ".. Tuakura or katote (the names of other species) would have fitted the place well." <i>(May 26, 1930,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tuakura, <i>Lindsay. Dicksonia squarrosa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  The Tuakura (<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>) and the Ponga (<i>Cyathea dealbata</i>) are the two principal varieties of fern-tree. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/203</i>)

>  The kaponga or silver fern is almost as lovely. A peculiarity of another kind, the katote, is that its fronds ... all incline sharply upwards ... Different again is the more common fern-tree, the tuakura (dicksonia squarrosa) ... <i>(Mar 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

>  Wheki, or Tuakura Dicksonia squarrosa. <i>(Oct 1, 1896,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  The ferns, which attain the size of trees, are the splendid long-fronded mamaku with its black trunk, the mauku and tuakura, and the smaller ones are the wheki, the maru, and the taiwhaniwha [sic], the heruheru [todeamedia), and that queen of the New Zealand fern tribe, the tete or todea superba, commonly known as the "Prince of Wales' Feather." <i>(Jun 4, 1910,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The ferns, which attain the size of trees, are the splendid long-fronded mamaku with its black trunk, the mauku and tuakura, and the smaller ones are the wheki, the maru, and the taiwhaniwha [sic], the heruheru [todeamedia), and that queen of the New Zealand fern tribe, the tete or todea superba, commonly known as the "Prince of Wales' Feather." <i>(Jun 4, 1910,Evening Post,10</i>)

### tuangi
 
 <i>n.</i> a cockle.

>  Heat oil in a saucepan and add tuangi to batter and drop small spoonfuls into hot oil. ... Then, to make corn and bacon sauce, sweat bacon and chilli in pan with butter, add corn, tuangi stock and cream and reduce by half. <i>(Dec 12, 2005,NZ Herald,B4</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The rich marine life of the New Zealand shoreline provided the Maori with a vast array of shellfish: mussels, paua, pipi, toheroa, tuatua, tuangi, oysters, scallops, kina (sea eggs) and pupu (catseyes). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,12</i>)

>  T|ua t|ara; A species of lizard; <i>(1820,Grammar and Vocabulary of Language of NZ,218</i>)

>  T|ua t|ara; A species of lizard; <i>(1820,Grammar and Vocabulary of Language of NZ,218</i>)

 <i>abbr.</i> Telecommunication Users Association of New Zealand
