### tuarenga
 
 <i>n.</i> a young fish

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> In some other localities these young [smelt, <i>Retropinna</i>] are called tuarenga. The half-grown fish is termed tikihemi and the adult paraki. Certain races of fish have a broad silvery band running along the side; hence the term `silvery.' It is to be regretted that in Canterbury the term smelt is still used to apply to the minnow, <i>Galaxias attenuatus. .</i> <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,54</i>)

### tuarua
 
 <i>n.</i> ?? [ORIGIN: Maori, Ma. = second, deputy, runner-up (P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 272)]

>  Loved Nan of all her mokopuna and tuarua. <i>(May 10, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,14</i>)

### tuatara
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) the only surviving representative of a group of reptiles which has otherwise been extinct for 100 million years, now found only on a few islands off New Zealand. [Maori] [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>tuatara</b> ancient reptile(<i>spenodon punctatus</i>)] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 273 (no macron).]

>  We boast about the ancient lineage of the tuatara, but what of the Coelacanth? <i>(Dec 5, 1939,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Their reward is seeing the return of native animals to the island, including kakariki, North Island robin, tuatara, giant weta, and forest geckos. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 1</i>)

>  Stephens Island, where the queer creatures have their sanctuary, is something like 50 miles west of Plimmerton and there is no ferry service for tuataras. <i>(May 4, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Williams 447 <i>Tuatara .. 1. Sphenodon punctatus,</i> a reptile like a large lizard.: spg <i>Ruatara</i> [a2,a3-acute]? f. hearing <i>Duatara</i> (cf early n. Auckland Chief Duaterra), then with <i>d</i> translated as<i> r. </i> <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,218</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> We pulled to Karewa, a small island 8 miles from Tauranga. Upon the island are Ruatara, a species of lizard about a foot in length, which are regarded by the natives as Atua. [ed. fn] Or tuatara [Sphenodon punctatus], a small prosaurian reptile ... <i>(Mar 26, 1833,Journal,301,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> .. the only Animal a native of the Island is a Lizard (Dueterra) [tuatara]. <i>Ibid.</i> 63 The natives call him `Dueterra' the Lizard from being harmless. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),30,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> On the edge of the bank I caught a small lizard, or tuatara [a's-acute], beautifully striated with bright green lines. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/229 The Ruatara [a2,a3-acute] or lizard, is accounted a very virulent deity. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/221</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Should the patient recover, the doctor takes credit to himself that he has ejected his godship, and will not hesitate to assert that he discovered the rascal leaving the mouth, nostrils, or ears, of the sick man in the shape of a lizard, <i>(ruatara</i>[a2-acute]) or some such animal. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/264</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 6. <i>Hatteria punctata.</i> Gray .. Gigantic Lizard, Cook's Voy., 3, I. 153., or Guana. - <i>Polack,</i> N.Z. i. 317 .. `I have been apprized of the existence of a large lizard, which the natives called <i>Tuatera</i>, or <i>Narara,</i> with a general name, and of which they were much afraid ..' <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/204</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/204-205]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The creature spoken of in the traditions of the New Zealanders is frequently called Tuatara, a name also common to the iguana of the country, derived from the circumstance of its back being armed with a row of spines. It is also sometimes called <i>Taniwha</i>, a word of indefinite signification, equivalent to the English monster ... <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,75</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Captain Cook mentions a gigantic lizard in New Zealand, which the natives call `tuatera.' It is the <i>Hatteria punctata </i>of Gray, and is now nearly extinct ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,73</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Another lighthouse island, Stephen Island .. is .. the home of the <i>tuatara</i> reptile (<i>Sphenodon punctatus</i>) in great numbers. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 9</i>)

>  <i>tuatara</i>: reptile. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  The Maori name of a large lizard of ancient type now confined to certain islands off the New Zealand coast. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),424</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> tuatara .. tuakeke, tuatara, tuatete .. <i>Sphenodon punctatus. </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>tuatara n.</i> the largest New Zealand reptile, a lizard [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>tuatara .. n.</i> largest N.Z. reptile, lizard [Maori] <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>tuatara n</i>. a lizard-like reptile occurring on certain islands near New Zealand [C19: < Maori, < <i>tua</i> back +<i> tara</i> spine] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1259</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 828]]

>  <i>On the</i> TUATARA (<i>Haateria punctata,</i> Gray);<i> or Great Fringed Lizard of New Zealand</i> .. I have been enabled to peruse a minute description of the Tuatara, or Fringed Lizard. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/17</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Two sorts of lizards are to be met with, the <i>Tuatara</i> and the <i>Kakariki</i> - .. the latter eight inches long. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,498</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> This is the tuatara of the Maoris and <i>Sphenodon</i> of naturalists. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/218</i>)

>  We took away .. a number of tuataras, hoping that they would breed in captivity. <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/28</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tuatara n.</i> the Maori name of a New Zealand lizard, or reptile, <i>Hatteria punctata</i>, Gray; called also <i>Sphenodon punctatum. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,480</i>)

>  The tuatara lives in the same burrows as are used by the oii, the titi and the toanui. All three of the petrels are included in the term mutton-birds. <i>(Sep 11, 1922,Quick March,15</i>)

>  In the <i>Gazette</i> of 17th May last, (1843) No. 246, you did me the honour to insert a notice on a reptile known to the aborigines under the name of the Tuatara - a name evidently derived from two Native words <i>Tua</i>, an abbreviation for Atua, the Supreme Being; and <i>Tara</i>, rough. <i>(Jan 17, 1844,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a sycophant

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>Tuatara,</i> a species of lizard, had a similar but ephemeral use in a limited circle some years ago, when it was applied to anyone toadying to a superior. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,93</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a member of the Tararua Tramping Club -

>  [He had a nephew ... who was termed `Tua Tara', from a horrid sort of lizard that the natives abhor. ] <i>(1863,English as it is spoken in NZ,47</i>) [NOTES: [check wording/delete 1 of these 2 cits]2]

### tuatara lizard
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  It has hitherto been thought that the tuatara lizard, although admittedly ugly enough for anything, was quite harmless. <i>(Apr 19, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  A tuatara lizard.. was discovered the other day on the rocks close to the water at Plimmerton. <i>(May 4, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

### tuatarium
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Initially notorious as a bird smuggler, he targeted the Southland Museum and Art Gallery tuatarium in 1991 and stole two. <i>(Apr 17, 2003,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

### tuatini
 
 <i>n.</i> species of shark [[Tuatini also in 1855 Taylor Te Ika A Maui ?190]]

>  The tiger shark caught off Ward Island has been identified as the species Galeocerdo Rayneri known to the Maoris as mango tuatini. <i>(Feb 17, 1937,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I went to see the fish which is called the Tatera its skin is rough like the shark ... 8 feet long ... 6 gills [sketch with caption] The Tatera or ururoa or Tuatini. 8 ft. long. <i>(Nov 28, 1844,Journal,3/41</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Tuatini</i> is a species of shark, often taken ten feet long, it is very savage. The teeth were set in rows, and formerly used as knives for cutting up human bodies for the oven. <i>Ibid.</i> 626 Fam. <i>Cestracionidae</i>, represented by the <i>Tuatini,</i> whose teeth are set in blocks of half-a-dozen each, beginning with the largest and decreasing in size to the least,* it is probable that the <i>Cestracion Philippi</i> is also a visitor of the New Zealand seas. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,412</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The chief cutting tool was a knife ... It was made of wood and had inserted ... the teeth of the Blue-shark (<i>Tuatini</i>: Carcharias brachyurus). <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,313</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The chief cutting tool was a knife ... It was made of wood and had inserted ... the teeth of the Blue-shark (<i>Tuatini</i>: Carcharias brachyurus). <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,313</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> SEVEN-GILLED SHARK (TUATINI) <i>Notorynchus cepedianus</i> (Peron) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,62</i>)

>  <b>BRAMBLEY</b> Among the various species [of shark] we have landed, the seven-gilled, or tuatira, [sic, but check] and the white-pointer have one similarity. ... The seven-gillers we catch all the year. <i>(1966,Sea-cockies of the Manukau,44</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SEVENGILL SHARK ... <i>Other names: Notorhyncus cepedianus</i>; tuatini (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,175</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> SEVEN-GILLED SHARK <i>Notorynchus cepedianus:</i> Not regarded as harmful to man in New Zealand, but any shark of ten feet (3.04m) could well be. Its Maori name, `Tuatini', comes from the strangely shaped teeth, with which weapons were made. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,220</i>)

>  He accordingly journeyed to the river, and having provided himself with the tooth of a big fish called tuatini, which he notched like a saw, he made a kind of a box, into which he got, and therein drifted down the river to Potiki. <i>(May 12, 1892,Otago Witness,33</i>)

### tuatu
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Tuatu -a shark <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/389</i>)

### tuatua
 
 <i>n.</i> one of two species of edible New Zealand bivalve, Paphies subtriangulata or <i> P. donacina </i>, similar to but smaller than the toheroa. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> Millers Flat.. sometimes has amazing drifts and heaps of live tuatua on it. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,63</i>)

>  Surf clams are species similar to the common tuatua. <i>(Oct 1991,Cuisine,29/11</i>)

>  Smaller tuatuas, are still fair game though, so if you're keen, ask locals where you might find a bed. <i>(Nov 1999,AA Directions,30</i>)

>  <b>Gee, Maurice</b> Each wave carried thousands of tiny tuatua up the sand. <i>(2005,Blindsight,134</i>)

>  Mix in enough egg to make a batter of medium consistency, then stir in the tuatuas. <i>(Sep 1999,Cuisine,No. 76 : 56</i>)

>  A real attempt is made to present a Northland cuisine, with offerings such as fresh tuatua soup, garlic-crumbed mussels and oysters from the farms near Mangonui. <i>(Aug 1991,Cuisine,87</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> A tuatua pfeller is merely low and knavish, but if you were a tuarekareka pfeller, you became allasame rotten fish stinking washed up on the beach by the tide. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,122</i>) [NOTES: 1989 edition]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Tuatua .. .. tuatua .. .. <i>Amphidesma subtriangulatum </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Local clams includes toheroa (<i>Amphidesma ventricosum</i>), tuatua (the heavier, more triangular <i>A. subtriangulatum</i>), and the common white pipi (<i>A</i>.--or <i>Paphies--australis</i>). Clams live under sand, <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,79</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> We used to call them pipis, but I think that at this place they were really called tua-tuas. <i>(1973,NZ Jack,71</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The rich marine life of the New Zealand shoreline provided the Maori with a vast array of shellfish: mussels, paua, pipi, toheroa, tuatua, tuangi, oysters, scallops, kina (sea eggs) and pupu (catseyes). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,12</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> These picnics were always fun when young and not so young gathered <i>tuatua</i>, cooked them on the beach and then ate them with home-made bread and butter. <i>(1982,Myrtle and Sophia,17</i>)

>  <b>EDMOND</b> knocked himself unconscious (Jesus leans over him -/ slides out the fat, tuatua wallet -/ `For the poor' - he smiles). <i>(Jun 1986,Landfall,158/191</i>)

>  tuatua .. [scientific name] <i>Paphies subtriangulata</i> .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <i>tuatua n.</i> edible NZ mollusc (<i>tuatua pancakes</i>). [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,828</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Tuatua Paphies subtriangulata </i>and <i>P. donacina </i>... There are two New Zealand species commonly known as tuatua (sometimes less accurately as pipi). In general, <i>P. subtriangulata</i> occurs around the North Island and <i>P. donacina</i> around the South Island and southern North Island ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,160</i>)

>  And mid-40, dreadlockless and with clothes as cool as a scorched tua tua... <i>(Jan 7, 1993,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Tuatua Fritters (makes 35 cocktail serves, or six entree or lunch serves)Find a beach near you which has tutaua, check for low tide and take some friends collecting. Alternatively, your fish supplier should be able to procure tuatua meat, possibly already chopped. <i>(Aug 23, 2003,Dominion Post,F5</i>)

>  "We've been doing it for 12 years. We think we've just about got it right." I do like a good tuatua fritter but, no, the other bloke at my table, snaffled that. I had the scallops. <i>(Aug 17, 2005,NZ Herald - Viva,17</i>)

### tuawhenua
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He is a frightful sufferer from the Maori disease known as tuwhenua, a kind of leprosy. <i>(May 31, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,16</i>)

### tub
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> As you dug your way in the rails followed, enabling the truckers to push empty tubs right up to where you were working. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,128</i>)

### tub-oarsman
 
 <i>n.</i> [tub oarsman]

>  .. the whale is sounding, and the line is running through the 'chocks' or groove in the head of the boat, with the rapidity of lightning, and as it passes round the loggerhead it ignites from theheat produced by friction, but the tub-oarsman is continually dashing water upon it in the line tub. <i>(Aug 15, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The common men have nothing to do but ply their oars according to orders, except one, called the <i>tub-oarsman</i> who sits next to the tub containing the whale-line, and has to see that no entanglement takes place. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/318</i>)

### tubbo
 
 <i>n.</i> a tubby person

>  If you think I'm going down to the gymn [sic] at Parliament, forget it. I'm not going to sit there next to some opposition member looking like a bloody tubbo. <i>(Jun 30, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### tube mill
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

>  If we were going to do a tube rescue we would strip down to the boardies. <i>(Jan 14, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D4</i>)

### TUC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Trade Union Council

>  A rival organisaton was set up and named the Trade Union Council (TUC). <i>(Feb 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

### tuck in
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] to keep a tidy periphery of a mob of sheep.

>  <b>WHYTE</b> It is usually unnecessary for a dog to come round too far tucking in the corners when he has only three sheep to deal with - although with a larger flock of wild sheep on a steep hillside turning in the corners becomes an urgent necessity. A full tucking-in turn is necessary. <i>(1927,Sheep-Dog, The,52</i>)

### tucker
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) stock feed. (See [milking tucker](mar-moa.md#milking-tucker); [dog tucker](dig-dou.md#dog-tucker); [hard tucker](h-hea.md#hard-tucker))

>  The cows have exhibited an unfailing ability to consume every whisp of tucker they're offered, and then make it clear they's like more. <i>(Sep 11, 1961,NZ Dairy Exporter,73</i>)

>  He likes the black. It's a small cow which is ideal on the hill, requiring minimum tucker for maintenance. <i>(Sep 10, 1970,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

>  At this stage the lamb is still getting most of its tucker from Mum so is not dirtying pastures. <i>(Jun 11, 1981,NZ Farmer,39</i>)

>  Much of the rest of the district finishes the winter with plenty of tucker both in the paddocks and in the barns. <i>(Aug 23, 1984,NZ Farmer,74</i>)

>  Canterbury beekeepers now have their own bee tucker orchard for growing willows. We have someone somewhere working on growing carobs .. which could provide winter tucker in our drought areas. <i>(Aug 1985,Growing Today,46</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Haylage is better, more succulent tucker, too. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year, A,106</i>)

>  "We're constantly moving sheep," Hamish says, "trying to bank up tucker ahead of them all the time." <i>(Dec 14, 1988,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  It's not the sort of tucker to fatten lambs on, but somehow it does seem to clean them up. <i>(Nov 2000,Growing Today,65</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Out on the flat, big hairy ears shot up and some horses whinnied. Tucker! Again Piers whistled, and horses streamed by him ... <i>(2001,Riding Country,79</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [station staff] to eat.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the cowboy was tuckering at the cookshop on his own. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,87</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> When they were out at camp the cowboy tuckered at the house. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,25</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> As it happened he wanted a cowboy and against his better judgment he gave me the job - $2 a week, sleep in the whare with the men, tucker with the married couple. <i>(1978,Five Hundred Horses,7</i>)

>  In reply to His Worship defendant said he did not know who his brother Willie was "tuckering" with at the time referred to, but it was not with him. <i>(Oct 5, 1889,Te Aroha News,2</i>)

>  The bosses like them[,] they tucker themselves and they put the men to sleep in any sort of a stable or pig stye. <i>(Jan 19, 1912,Maoriland Worker,14</i>)

>  Wanted to know - whether young B. is tuckering himself now, as he is so often seen at the baker's shop. <i>(May 4, 1889,Observer (Auckland),17</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> When they were out at camp the cowboy tuckered at the house. <i>Ibid.</i> 130 They tuckered at the cookshop and while they were there the boys lived like kings. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,25</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) food for farm or station personnel [now in more general use.]

>  In such cases they have to wade up nearly to their chins in some parts in snow water, so as to evade their being "jammed["], probably for a week, without a "tucker." <i>(Oct 17, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  The foreign tucker went down a treat at the postwar smoko, with members "wiping the platter clean". <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> .. The blankets did duty as a carpet bag, holding ... and tucker, the latter comprising almost anything within the provinces of food. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,23</i>)

>  .. there have been hundreds of men travelling about the country in search of work, who, as the saying is, are not worth their 'tucker.' <i>(Feb 21, 1880,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Only a pound a week and tucker ... <i>(1899,Colonial Couplets,28</i>)

>  After tucker the men sat round the eating-house discussing the phenomenon. <i>(May 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,86</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> There were no huts out on the tops and they carried everything they required on their backs - tent, bedding and tucker. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,71</i>)

>  The cafe was opened last year for those who like some tucker with their tipple. <i>(Sep 24, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,Farming Today Supplement 23</i>)

>  ... it might make people sick at the thought but it's great bloke tucker. <i>(Dec 9, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D6</i>)

>  Native tucker: Chef Charles Royal has developed a wide range of native New Zealand ingredients. Now we can try horopito pepper in the form of a rub or horopito and piripiri sauce. <i>(Jan 2002,Cuisine,70</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> During this time he worked for his tucker on a bullock team, as a goad-boy. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,26</i>)

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> It is very hard work humping your blankets and tucker. <i>(May 12, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,II : 111</i>) [NOTES: 1960 edition]

>  <b>FORBES</b> A peer's son who is earning his 'tucker' as a station cook in New Zealand. <i>(Oct 1883,Contemporary Review,606</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> If they had obtained no wages for the first six months or so, they would have obtained their 'tucker' free. <i>(1911,Maoriland Bush,275</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Later Ned got the tucker cooking. It was stew and spud, like most nights. <i>(1972,Strangers and Journeys,43</i>)

>  One week might be right enough, but if they would only "butt in and get out into the farmers' country" they were bound to get enough work to live, if it was only grubbing gorse, but they would not do that while they got tucker for nothing. <i>(Aug 3, 1926,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  People would turn up on the steamer with no money and no idea. He'd kit them out with gear and tucker on the condition they'd sell the gum to him. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,22</i>)

>  <b>DAVIES</b> There would be no hope of "tucker" - a bad look-out, as we were down to the last "split pea". <i>(1884,Poems & Other Literary Remains,262</i>)

>  I can state that our "tucker" is of the best, both in quality and quantity. <i>(Sep 29, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/3 : 60</i>)

>  As for "tucker" being dear, it is all false. <i>(May 3, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  We halted to refresh ourselves from the supply of 'tucker' in our swags. <i>(Feb 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> They were short of tucker as birds were scarce, and were glad to reach a roadman's camp at the Haast, where they fed like kings for four days. <i>(1958,Great Days in NZ Mountaineering,146</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to provide food for

>  <b>NEWTON</b> One of his peculiarities was that he never liked to dine in the cookhouse; he always preferred to tucker himself and in true Maori fashion he practically lived off the land. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,63</i>)

>  The men unloading the cargo ex the Tyser steamer Marere at No.16, Jervois-quay wharf, are being accomodated and "tuckered" on that vessel. <i>(Nov 17, 1913,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  All hands are "tuckered" on the premises, a much better system than "so much and tucker yourself," the odds in favour being glaringly apparent at dinner-time, when the cooking of " spuds,'' etc., eating thereof, and travelling to and fro have to be accomplished by a bachelor in the short space of one hour. <i>(Jan 6, 1892,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  The men "tucker" themselves, in pairs .. "Tuckering" small parties is necessarily extravagant, particularly where men are unskilled in culinary arts. <i>(Aug 14, 1908,Bay of Plenty Times,4</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> All shearing work in New Zealand ... is so much and found. In ... some sheds, the Maoris tucker themselves. <i>(1913,Country Work,15</i>)

>  To quote the words of a visitor whom I overheard make the remark, "They couldn't have been better tuckered." <i>(Apr 11, 1879,Clutha Leader,6</i>)

>  All hands are "tuckered" on the premises, a much better system than "so much and tucker yourself," the odds in favour being glaringly apparent at dinner-time, when the cooking of " spuds,'' etc., eating thereof, and travelling to and fro have to be accomplished by a bachelor in the short space of one hour. <i>(Jan 6, 1892,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  The men "tucker" themselves, in pairs .. "Tuckering" small parties is necessarily extravagant, particularly where men are unskilled in culinary arts. <i>(Aug 14, 1908,Bay of Plenty Times,4</i>)

>  Ample provision appeared to be made for "tuckering" the passengers. <i>(Apr 3, 1879,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  She claimed GBP10 for damages and 17s 6d a day each for tuckering and boarding the team. <i>(Jun 10, 1922,NZ Truth,5</i>)

### tucker allowance
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an amount of meat each musterer is permitted for his team of dogs.

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Each man was allowed a maximum of five dogs; they received a tucker allowance of one-quarter of a sheep every second day. <i>(1993,Station Country I,51</i>)

### tucker bag
 
 <i>n.</i> a container for food, especially as carried by a swagger, outdoor worker, etc. (See [also tucker box](tot-tyr.md#tucker-box))

>  <b>ELKINGTON</b> Each swagman carries his "billy" (a tin can for boiling tea) and a "tucker-bag," in which he keeps his lunch. <i>(1906,Adrift in NZ,127</i>)

>  That night, either by accident or design, Jim left his tucker bag on the job when he went back to camp. Next morning the tucker bag was still there but the half-loaf of bread and the remains of a roast pigeon it contained had gone. <i>(Sep 1962,Te Ao Hou,40: 18</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> The squatter may growl at the prices of wool,/ And the farmer complain of the weather./ But though money be tight, and though times may be dull,/ It's little we [sundowners] care if our tucker-bag's full -/ <i>Ibid.</i> 51 [spg tucker-bag] It was midday when he roused himself, opened his tucker-bag, and made a fair meal of what he found there. He did not boil his billy, ... but contented himself with a drink from a small stream that ran in a gully near by. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,15</i>)

>  <b>AYTON</b> Cooking meat and vegetables. Making new tucker bag. Destroyed one tom kitten. <i>(Apr 18, 1900,Diary,27,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> So the packman, W, Sansom, dealt out a pound to each man to carry in his tucker bag. <i>(1907,Diary</i>) [NOTES: c1907-1914 Mowat diary in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 103]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1933,Press, The</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Right at the top they stopped to eat their lunch. ... But before sitting down Mr Anderson took off the tucker-bag he had carried on his back - and there were sandwiches, wholemeal scones, slices of cold mutton, bananas and fancy cakes. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,119,1974 ed. Rhodes ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Bundling the bird up in his tucker bag he finished his beat ... and gave it to the [packie].... <i>(1952,High Country Journey,180</i>)

>  <b>HOGAN</b> There's tucker in the tucker bag/ The world is good to me./ <i>(1962,Billy-Can Ballads,12</i>)

>  <i>tucker bag n.</i> bag for carrying food. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> A tucker bag with a bottle of oatmeal water was soon prepared and the two set off. <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,69</i>)

>  <i>tucker bag n</i>. bag for carrying food. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> I had a good horse, saddle and bridle, and also a ... tucker bag and tea billy as I headed for Tarndale [station]. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,37</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> The quart billy clipped on to the shoulder strap of the tucker bag ... <i>(1969,Molesworth,101</i>)

>  <b>AGNEW</b> I handed [the swagger] a couple of thick-sliced mutton sandwiches from the tucker bag and we munched away in silence, aware of the tumbling of a nearby brook, . <i>(1974,Loner, The,103</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> ... Ewan [a schoolboy] got the sandwiches from the tuckerbag. <i>(1978,Elver, The,64</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> There is a black billy pot by my camp fire,/ Tucker bag and blankets beside./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,51</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> [To our British teacher] a paddock was now a field, our tucker or crib bags (made out of sugar bags) became satchels or lunch bags and our oilskin coats became macs. We preferred to retain our bush language. <i>Ibid.</i> 61 First we [bushmen] would put our `tucker bag' in a dry place, then sharpen our axes. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,38</i>)

>  The fire would not burn, and the four sat round a few smouldering embers waiting for the billy to boil. At length the water bubbled, and a rush was made for the tucker bag. <i>(May 29, 1877,Bruce Herald,5</i>)

>  On Monday they get up at five o'clock, when it is dark: and when they had been mustered on July 6, Eastwood asked to be allowed to go to his cell for his tucker bag, which he had forgotten. <i>(Jul 14, 1885,Star, The,3</i>)

>  The bag is a calico bag, commonly called by diggers a tucker bag. <i>(May 6, 1865,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  Another way was for the men below working in collusion with the trucker, to put the specimens in a "tucker" bag put in the bottom of a truck loaded with mullock. <i>(Apr 23, 1910,Evening Post,10</i>)

### tucker bell
 
 <i>n.</i> a dinner bell; a bell rung to summon hands to meals

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He waited for the tucker bell at a nearby station. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,103</i>)

### tucker bill
 
 <i>n.</i> a food bill; a grocery bill (urban use of the word is often humorous rather than slangy) [ca1960 Wellington The tucker bill's twenty pounds this month.]

>  ... their own tucker-bill. <i>(1905,NZ Truth,13/4/5</i>)

### tucker box
 
 <i>n.</i> the box holding musterers' tucker when camped away from the homestead



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a musterer's food box.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They were also to collect the tucker box and billy from the cookshop, and have the billy swinging when the mob was yarded. <i>(1949,High Country Days,19</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> the 'rousie' had the billy boiling so .. they collected the tucker box and sought a prominence, where they could get a full view of the flat. <i>(1949,High Country Days,22</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Early next morning he left the station, riding a big, black horse called Nigger and leading a packhorse with a big tucker box hung on each side and Willie's swag across the top. <i>(1954,On the Hill,26</i>)

>  I then saw him on the top of the waggon, when he was in the act of taking the tarpaulin off the top of the tucker box. <i>(Oct 15, 1874,Clutha Leader,5</i>)

>  This sensitively constructed, family story evokes dry, Central Otago rabbiting, but with a tucker box - a loaf of bread, cheese, hardboiled eggs, a leg of cold mutton, apples and a fruit cake - of an amplitude outside the scope of present-day poor families. <i>(Feb 15, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  The carrier inevitably produced the proverbial tucker box. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  A fire is lit in an oil drum, the billy is dipped over the side of the boat and filled, the `tucker box' is produced and its contents spread, ... and luncheon is served. <i>(Mar 5, 1891,Otago Witness,28</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Mar 5 Otago Witness 28 in 1987 Hill Richard Henry of Resolution Island 88]

>  <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They were also to collect tucker-box and billy from the cook-shop. <i>(1949,High Country Days,19</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Tucker boxes from home were a source of gratification and each had many pals when these well-filled parcels arrived, and were safely put away in their respective lockers. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,35</i>) [NOTES: (boarding school)]

>  <i>tucker box n</i>. tin or wooden box for carrying food. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>tucker box n</i> tin or wooden box for carrying food. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> In the [mustering] hut were two bunks, a tucker box, an old frying pan, various billies and a teapot. <i>(1969,Wild Irishman,107</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> Ivan [the cook] had his `tucker boxes' packed, the horses loaded with all our gear,... <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,42</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Once in the tucker-box he had him a ball,/ And ate all the grub ... <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,26</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Sometimes we came across one [swagger] boiling his billy in a roadman's clearing ... and eating cold meat and bread from his tucker box <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,113</i>)

>  ".. Men once again got some welcome variety into their 'tucker boxes.'" <i>(Aug 10, 1943,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  (N.B. - Always take a dog when you go camping out; it helps to discourage the rats a little. They are inquisitive little critters, and have a great fancy for the tucker box.) <i>(Dec 23, 1887,Otago Witness,20</i>)

### tucker boy
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a young male worker who takes food and drinks to rural workers. (See [billy boy](b-bla.md#billy-boy))

>  <b>EVANS</b> Once when I was nine, and had just arrived in the shearing shed with the men's morning tea, Bobbie Rhodes of Bluecliffs Station called in and he said 'Are you the tucker boy?' A girl aged nine, I never forgot the insult. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,87</i>)

### tucker claim
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> `The Jeweller's Shop' supported its owner generously and allowed him leisure and relaxation which his hard work entitled him to and exciting week-ends in the city that no `tucker' claim would have given him. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,97,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> <i>CLAIMS</i>Worthless claims or ground were called schicers or duffers. Those yielding up to [pounds]3 per week per man were known as `tucker' claims; up to [pounds]8 per week were known as `wages' claims; above [pounds]8 were known as `pilers'; the richest of all were known as `Home-ward-bounders.' <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold</i>)

>  .. and many claims, which would command a special report in the mining intelligence of the local papers in those districts, are abandoned or talked of as mere "tucker" claims in the Tokitika or Grey districts. <i>(Jan 2, 1866,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  Circumstances of this kind go to prove that the industrious prospector is sure of finding something worth his trouble, and at all events has a much better chance of a rise than the steady worker in a mere tucker claim. <i>(Sep 4, 1869,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  To my question to one party relative to the payable nature of the ground or otherwise, I received the following impromptu reply, "Not much good, only tucker claim, very poor," &c., &c. <i>(Feb 4, 1887,Otago Witness,22</i>)

>  One or two claims in the gully contunue to pay the shareholders fair wages, while others are what the diggers call "tucker" claims. <i>(May 22, 1873,Tuapeka Times,5</i>)

>  At the Wilson there are three or four payable alluvial claims on the terraces, while others are only tucker claims, the more so as water has been scarce for the last two months. <i>(Feb 21, 1895,Otago Witness,17</i>)

### tucker diggings
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  After a few remarks about the West Coast being worked out, except as a tucker diggings, and about the good a new rush would do, the visitor goes on his way, and Bill resigns himself without a sigh to the prospect of being the sole inhabitant of the gully for the nest twelvemonths. <i>(Jan 17, 1887,Star, The,1</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> `The field', wrote a Waimea digger, `is what is usually termed `tucker' diggings ... there is gold everywhere, but it has been scattered with a sparing hand.' <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,107,1962 ed.</i>)

>  Provisions are very dear, and men have to swag them over five miles, through a bad track, all crags and hills - and the returns from mining in the Bullock Creek, and the gullies of it are so small as to give it (the Haast) the name of a tucker diggings. <i>(Dec 4, 1873,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  Even at the best of times the gullies were only tucker diggings, and for every ton of alluvial there were ten tons of stones to be shifted. <i>(Jul 15, 1903,Otago Witness,86</i>)

>  Let the <i>Invercargill Times </i> publish as a stern indisputable fact - th[a]t the present Switzer's is barely a tucker diggings. <i>(Sep 11, 1863,Otago Witness,5</i>)

### tucker fucker
 
 <i>n.</i> an army cook [ca1963 Oct 4 G.L. Gabites (Wellington) Heard `tucker-fucker' from a Wellington Taxidriver.]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tucker fucker</i> army cook. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)

### tucker ground
 
 <i>n.</i> OED: 6. [f. TUCK n.1 6 or v.1 10.] The daily supply of food of a gold-digger or station-hand; rations, meals; also, food generally, victuals: = TUCK n.1 6b. to earn or make one's tucker, to earn merely enough to pay for one's keep. Austral. and N.Z. slang.



 
 <i>n.</i> auriferous ground on which merely bare tucker can be made (cf wages ground; payable) [aka tucker-ground]

>  Tucker Flat..has been looked upon as containing worse than 'tucker' ground. <i>(1870,AJHR,40 : 4</i>)

>  Tucker Flat ... has been looked upon as containing worse than `tucker' ground. <i>(1870,AJHR,D40/4</i>) [NOTES: (Report Goldfields)]

>  [In c1865] the diggings were only considered `tucker-ground'. <i>(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,125</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> [The Scandinavian field] was called in those days `Flash Tucker Ground,' and paid about [pounds]20 per week. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,80</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> <i>(1947,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> <i>Tucker ground:</i> Poor ground, just rich enough to allow the miner to buy the necessities of life. <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,56</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> It was a matter of nice judgement. The digger recognized four classes of payable ground: `tucker ground' kept him in food with nothing to spare; a `wages claim' gave him a pound or two above living expenses; a `riser' was a rich claim; and a `piler' or `homeward bounder' the richest of all. Probably most of the `pilers' were beach-claims, where the leads were shallow and rich, the outlay small and provisions cheap because of easy access. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,244,1962 ed.</i>)

>  The whole country has been prospected ... and ascertained to afford only very ordinary `tucker ground'. <i>(1865,Evening Post,1/46/2</i>)

>  The whole country has been prospected for months, if not years, and ascertained to afford only very ordinary "tucker-ground." <i>(Mar 27, 1865,Otago Daily Times,4</i>)

>  This is abundantly proved by the testimonials which have been received from time to time, which in some instances afford striking evidence of the changes wrought in the productiveness of the soil by the application of the latest thing in plough, drill, or harrow, causing areas that were formerly, in digger parlance, only "flash tucker" ground, to yield a harvest equal to the most fertile. <i>(Jan 20, 1892,Southland Times,3</i>)

### tucker house
 
 <i>n.</i> a hotel considered from the point of view of its table

>  <b>HISLOP</b> [Stanley's Hotel at Macraes Flat, Central Otago] was one of the best tucker houses on the road, and with bacon and eggs for breakfast, the hungry C.T. who was usually found stopping there would have a full ashet to help himself from, placed before him. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,16</i>)

### tucker money
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WHEELER</b> He looked over the half-completed sketch, ... then asked, `What's the dough like?' Sensing competition, I had the presence of mind to assure him that `If you're lucky you just about make tucker money.' <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,98</i>)

### tucker stand
 
 <i>n.</i> a food depot

>  <b>EASTWOOD</b> Sometime prior to 1880, a horse track ran from the Grey River up Ford Creek and over Hughes Hill, to a "tucker stand" or food depot at Coal Creek. <i>(1982,Brief History of Gold Mining, A,56</i>)

### tucker trip
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Basically, I was working for food and a bunk - they're what we call 'tucker trips' so I'm real keen to get some big fish now the bluefin season is on. <i>(May 15, 2004,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

### tucker-cart
 
 <i>n.</i> A conveyance carrying food for an outdoor working group.

>  <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. / 17</i>)

### tucker-time
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Another pioneer said ... [that] a meal was often alluded to as `tucker-time', and there would sound forth what the hungry men regarded as the welcome call of `tucker's ready', or perhaps `the billy's boiling', or some cry of similar import. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

>  <b>FARRELL</b> They're back in a minute, creepy creepy, tails tucked to bony bellies waiting for tucker-time. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,270</i>)

>  [Caption] Tucker-time; Specials at Dinner at Mount Cook Barracks, Wellington. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Auckland Weekly News,53</i>)

### tuckered out
 
 <i>adj.</i> exhausted [Jim writes: NSOD tags this as <i>NAmer</i>, Collins as <i>chiefly US & Canadian</i>. In NZPOD and used in NZ for many years.]

>  Just spent two hours chainsawing wood so I'm real tuckered out." <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> Yeah, you wait till I'm too tuckered out before you attack ay. <i>(1974,Whanau,130</i>)

>  The wind had dropped, so it took till Tuesday afternoon to row with those heavy seas to a sheltered bay off the cape, intenting to settle in for the night "<i>all being pretty well tuckered out.</i>" <i>(Feb 2001,NZ Memories,35</i>)

### tuckering
 
 <i>n.</i> the provision of food (See [tucker n. & v](../T#tucker-n.-&-v))

>  Then another practical difficulty is the extravagance of the cost of "tuckering." <i>(Aug 18, 1908,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  I have often before now referred to the system of threshing in this district, and especially regarding the paying and "tuckering" of the men, as it is called. <i>(Apr 23, 1891,Otago Witness,17</i>)

### tuckerless
 
 <i>adj.</i> without food

>  <b>HARPER</b> We were left almost `tuckerless' on Christmas day. <i>(1946,Memories of Mountains,162</i>)

>  From her previous state the day before, jobless, moneyless, referenceless, tuckerless - any other - less you like to make things look dar-r-r-k and gloomy - it was quite a step to her present state of affluence; any ordinary girl might be pardoned for indulging in a little weep at her prospects, but not Connie. <i>(Aug 6, 1921,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  There was nothing for it - no returning, and the ark was tuckerless, - so into this dug-out Maori canoe we were - 20 all told - constrained to enter, bag and baggage. <i>(Dec 21, 1904,Otago Witness,90</i>)

### tuere
 
 <i>n.</i> hagfish, blind eel

>  Cleaning the fish in the shore water for the tuere, the blind eel, to smell and come nosing in. <i>(Jan 10, 2000,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Mr. E.R. Waite.. gives tuere as their [sc. blind eels'] Maori name, but Mr. Sutherland was told that kapo is the proper Maori name. <i>(Mar 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  Tuere to the Maoris, hagfish to the [English].. and plain blind eels to you and me. <i>(Aug 1962,Sea Spray,79</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Fam. <i>Cyclostomi</i> has the<i> Tuere. (Heptatrema domberyii,)</i> found in Dusky Bay, and also in Cook's Straits, it is about two feet long, has several small feelers attached to the head, and a broad flat tail, the color [sic] is dark brown, and the body of uniform thickness; it is known as the mud eel and is remarkable for its power of emitting slime to such an extent as completely to envelop it. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,627</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Heptatretus cirrhatus </i>(Bloch and Schneider). HAGFISH; TUERE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,6,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> <i>Heptatretus cirrhatus </i>(Bloch and Schneider). Hagfish (slime-eel, hag, slimy, borer). <i>Tuere. </i> <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/399</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod) ... Others omitted are the tarakihi, or terakihi, the moki, the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), whai (skate), and the koekohe [sic] (trumpeter). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> HAGFISH (TUERE) <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus </i> (Bloch and Schneider) The Hagfish, or Blind-eel as the fishermen call it, is one of the most unpleasant-looking creatures of the sea. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,51</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> HAGFISH ... <i>Other names: Heptatretus cirrhatus </i>; blind eel; tuere, napia (Maori) <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,297</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> HAGFISH [<i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i>] ... Hagfish and blind eel are widely used alternative names, while the Maori tuere and napia are still in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,30</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Tuere, Tuare</i>, n. <i>1. Heptatretus cirrhatus, blind eel. </i> <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,448</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> Some of the whalers at Fishing Bay, especially the Maoris, enjoyed sea foods and found turi (blind eels or hagfish) a particular delicacy after they had been properly treated. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,111</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> Blind eels like tuere and piharau - very unusual taste. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,39</i>)

### Tuesday blues
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The main drawbacks [to Ecstasy] are the come downs or "Tuesday blues" and he says the drug is only unsafe if you don't know what you are taking. <i>(Apr 5, 2003,Dominion Post,F1</i>)

### tuft-grass
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](go-gra.md#grass),[tuft-grass](tot-tyr.md#tuft-grass))

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tug</i> to masturbate. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)

### tufted penguin
 
 <i>n.</i> [variously <i>Eudyptes antipodum</i>, <i>Eudyptes crestatus</i> / <i>Eudyptes crestatus filholi</i>, uncertain taxonomy; a rockhopper penguin]

>  This is the list of eggs Dr Fulton wants to order to make a complete collection for the museum ... Rock Hopper, Tufted Penguin ... <i>(Sep 20, 1905,Otago Witness,88</i>)

>  Somewhat similar in general appearance save that the crest consists of elongated drooping feathers is the Tufted Penguin, which never breeds on the mainland but frequents the Snares, Antipodes, Auckland, Campbell, and Macquarrie [sic] Islands, and is even found within the Antarctic Circle, as is recorded by Scott, Shackleton, and Mawson. <i>(Apr 14, 1920,Grey River Argus,5</i>)

### tug chain
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> TUG CHAIN: Short stout chain used on horse working in shafts. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,189</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 189]

### tug merchant
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> Talk about jellies on springs ... Bloody tug merchant <i>(1985,Shuriken,28</i>)

### tuhituhi
 
 <i>n.</i> writing [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  In the course you will learn korero (speaking), whakarongo (listening), tuhituhi (writing), panui (presentation), and language structure in a safe and comfortable whanau environment. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

### Tuhoe
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori of the Urewera

>  Urewera Country is the high, forested and remote region of the North Island, extending from the eastern edge of the Whakatane district in the eastern Bay of Plenty down into Hawke's Bay and including Lake Waikaremoana... It was the home of the Nga Potiki, or Tuhoe people, also known as 'The Children of the Mist'. <i>(2000,Bateman New Zealand Encyclopedia,656</i>)

>  We have a really distinctive Tuhoe monotone. <i>(Jun 1998,Mana,35</i>)

>  The Urewera are the home of the Tuhoe tribe, the people of the mist. <i>(Nov 19, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  [The Urewera] is home to the Tuhoe, "the children of the mist", of all the Maori tribes the least touched by Eueopean influences. <i>(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  <i>Tuhoe n.</i> large Maori tribe found in the Urewera country inland from the Bay of Plenty, sometimes called the Children of the Mist. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,829</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  By walking the original Maori war trails, we have found a wilderness of waterways, mountains, centuries-old trees, rare forest birds, ancient Tuhoe pa and also the long-hidden battlesites of the protagonists... [30] Ngati Kahungunu, with some attached Pakeha soldiers and civilian scouts, at last accomplished what no previous government force had: overrunning and defeating the lake-dwelling Tuhoe tribes in a number of waterborne operations... <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  The world is full of mist and rain again. You'd expect mist - the Tuhoe, the Children of the Mist, are descended from a cloud maiden - but we could do without the rain. <i>(Oct 11, 2003,Dominion Post,E15</i>)

### Tuhoeland
 
 <i>n.</i> a name given by Elsdon Best to the Urewera and other neighbouring areas of ancestral Tuhoe lands

>  <b>BEST</b> The bush which still covers the greater part of Tuhoeland is what an European bushman would term `light bush'. A sawmiller would not describe it as a `timber bush'. There is a good patch of timber at Te Whaiti, but elsewhere the timber trees ... are scattered, or in small clumps. <i>(1925,Tuhoe: The Children of the Mist,9</i>)

>  <b>FIRTH</b> He [a Tuhoe elder] knew the old Native names of every tree, shrub, plant, or fern in the forests of Tuhoeland. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,43</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> But while it may not be possible to claim for Tuhoeland a monopoly of fairy-folk, it is true that nowhere in Maoriland are there such varieties of legends ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,58</i>)

>  'Twas then the bloodstained garments of the slaughtered twain were found, / And the darkling woods of Tuhoeland rang to the trumpet's sound. <i>(May 22, 1909,Star, The,2</i>)

>  "Tuhoe Land" was an article dealing with personal reminiscences in the Uriwera [sic] Country. <i>(Sep 1, 1896,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  A dangerous cliff has for many years formed a bug-bear to travellers who have essayed the overland journey through Tuhoeland, but a track has been opened up which will enable this obstacle to be safely negotiated. <i>(Feb 10, 1914,Poverty Bay Herald,9</i>)

### tuhua
 
 <i>n.</i> obsidian

>  Parts of the shore are composed entirely of jet black obsidian, or "volcanic glass," termed by the Maoris Tuhua. <i>(Feb 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

### Tuhuan
 
 <i>adj.</i> Geol. Classification

>  Adams also completed a new, tentatively Tuhuan, Rb-Sr isochron for certain Dusky Sound granites. <i>(Oct 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,58/57</i>)

>  Janet Gabites and Chris Adams (INS) presented up-to-date compilations of K-Ar and Rb-Sr age data for the late Tuhuan (U. Devonian-Carboniferous) phase. <i>(Nov 1978,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,46/35</i>)

### tuhuhi
 
 <i>n.</i> a tree

>  <b>McKILLOP</b> The tanekaha tree yields a black or brown dye, and the tuhuhi a blue-black dye from the wood and bark, as does the mako; the whakou also yields a handsome blue. <i>(1849,Reminiscences,162</i>)

### tui
 
 <i>n.</i> a glossy black native honeyeater with a tuft of white feathers at its throat, an excellent mimic. <i>Prosthemadra novaeseelandiae</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, aka New Zealand blackbird, koko, mocking-bird, parson-bird, poe] (See [parson-bird](p-per.md#parson-bird))

>  Tui work their way round the suburbs drawing nectar from seasonal flowers such as pohutukawa and flax (and, yes, introduced trees such as eucalypts and Indian coral). <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,26</i>)

>  Bird enthusiasts are in for a treat with a good chance of seeing .. tui, bellbirds and kaka in the regenerating forests ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> [Maori glossary] <i>tui </i>Bird (<i>Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae</i>) <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,302</i>)

>  <i>tui n</i> -s [Maori] : a predominantly glossy black New Zealand honey eater (<i>Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae</i>) with white markings on throat, neck, and wings that is a notable mimic and often kept as a cage bird - called also <i>parson bird </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2461</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> TUI <i>Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae Other name</i>: Parson Bird. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,221</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The vocabulary of New Zealand English has been influenced by .. the adoption of Maori words and phrases .. Examples of the native words now in general use are the names of birds, fish, plants, and trees: <i>kea, tui, hapuku, tarakihi, koromiko, raupo</i>, and <i>kauri, hapuku</i>. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <b>HAYNES</b> The bird has received the appellation of the `parson bird' (the Maori name of Tui is perhaps more generally used). <i>(1865,Ramble in the NZ Bush, A,13</i>)

>  Prosthemadera Novae Zelandiae. Tui. Very abundant. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  PROSTHEMADERA NOVAE ZELANDIAE, Gml. Tui. Parson-bird .. in the eyes of the omnivorous settler, it possesses the higher merit of furnishing an article of food, and no weak sentimental feeling saves it from the camp-oven. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/55</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> 11. PROSTHEMADERA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gray. Parson Bird. Tui. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,5</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PROSTHEMADRA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (TUI OR PARSON-BIRD.) .. <i>Native names</i>. Tui and Koko; the young bird distinguished as Pi-tui or Pikari. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,87,1888 2nd edn 1/94 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> No sign of human life that morning; the shrill cry of the curlew on the beach and the full rich carol of the tui or parson-bird from the brushwood skirting the shore fell faintly upon the ear. <i>(1881,Poenamo,2/5/99</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/305</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> [Stewart Island birds include] the fern-bird (<i>Bowdleria fulva</i>), the bell-bird (<i>Anthornis melanura</i>), South Island Robin (<i>Miro Albifrons</i>), Tui (<i>Prosthemadera novae zealandiae</i>), 'Tui,' Parson-bird or Poe-bird of Capt. Cook Melliphagidae; Prosthemadera Novae Zealandiae. <i>(1940,Rakiura,323</i>)

>  <i>tui n.</i> small New Zealand bird, with remarkable imitative powers; the mockingbird, or parson-bird. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>tui .. n</i>. small N.Z. bird, with remarkable imitative powers; mocking-bird, parson-bird. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>266.a. Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae novaeseelandiae</i> (Gmelin, 1788) <i>Tui </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,70,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tui n.</i> a New Zealand songbird with white feathers at the throat. Also called: <i>parson bird.</i> [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1260</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 829; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 516]]

>  <i>Tui Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae</i> (GMELIN, 788) .. <i>Chatham Island Tui P.n. chathamensis</i> HARTET, 1928 .. OTHER NAME: <i>Parson bird. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,291</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> The tui-tui in his coat of metallic black ... <i>(1875,Cobb's Box,1875,5 ed.</i>)

>  11. <i>Prosthemadera Novae Zealandiae</i>, Gml. Parson-bird, Poe-bird, Tui, Koko. - Often builds under the shelter of <i>Rubus</i> twines, which many of us have learnt to fear or respect as bush-lawyer .. More than once I have found it near the top of a matipo (<i>Myrsine</i>) ... <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,276</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  I noticed the following:- Pigeon, tui, korimako, kaka, ruru, piwakawaka, toutouwai, kingfisher, duck and teal in the lakes, and pukeko in the swamp, and various sea birds. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  He has had a rough time of it surveying a block on the ranges up the Wai-o-hine in the Wairarapa [sic] .. Once [sic] .. they had nothing to eat but a tui and a more-pork .. They were stewing the two birds when a kuku-pa (bush pigeon) flew up and perched .. Bob shot it .. This is not exactly what the Britishers call `sport' - but there is more of nature about it than 30 gents in pink and tops .. waiting for a wretched little fox who, when he is killed, is not equal for culinary purposes to a more-pork. Once coming up the Waikato in 1851 your Uncle James got a pot-shot at a lot of parera (grey duck) ... <i>(Mar 22, 1894,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/597,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori name for the New Zealand bird, Prosthemadera novae-zelandiae</i>, Gray; called the <i>Parson-bird </i>(q.v.), and earlier the <i>Poe</i> (q.v.). Another name is the <i>koko</i>, and the young bird is distinguished as <i>Pi-tui,</i> or <i>Pikari</i>. It is also called the <i>Mocking-bird. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,481</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> The Tui, or `Parson Bird,' as he is irreverently called, because he has a tuft of white feathers on his throat which are supposed to resemble a clergyman's bands, is shining black and metallic blue. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,70</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> - ferns, tuis and we saw the sheep sheds - smell and sound - 12 Maoris - <i>Ibid.</i> 51 Here we drive in and ask for a paddock [sic no stop] Past the shearing shed - past the homestead to a beautiful place with a little patch of bush - tuis - magpies - cattle ... <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,40</i>) [NOTES: 1978 Gordon]

>  TUI. - New Zealand bird with sweet song. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> And then at the first beam of sun the birds began. Big cheeky birds, starlings and mynahs, whistled on the lawns, the little birds, the goldfinches and linnets and fantails flicked from bough to bough. A lovely kingfisher perched on the paddock fence preening his rich beauty, and a tui sang his three notes and laughed and sang them again. <i>(1920,Stories,233,1984 Alpers ed.</i>)

>  Prominently ... is to be observed ... the tuii ... <i>(1883,TrNZI,15/317</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso)]

>  PROSTHEMADERA NOVAE-ZEALANDIAE, <i>Gml.</i> - Tui. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/125</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <i>Prosthemadera novae-zealandiae</i>. - Tui. Both Islands; also, Auckland and Chatham Islands. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/112</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  So with the New Zealand birds: the Mocker, the Tui, the warbler, the Tomtit, the Robin, the Brown Creeper, the Canary, the White-eye .. are all known to feed the Cuckoo bantlings ... <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/116</i>)

>  <i>Tu&#769;.i </i>(tu&#769;.&emacr.), <i>n.</i> [Maori.]<i> (Zoo.l.)</i> The parson bird. See under PARSON, in the Dictionary. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2033</i>)

>  (Black Stewart Island) The following is a list of the birds shot .. <i>Hieracidea Novae Zealandiae</i>, the sparrow hawk; <i>Prosthemadera Novae Zealandiae</i>, tui or parson bird. <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>)

>  (Duppa) ... ptui, or mocking bird. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/1/221</i>)

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> .. the most harmonious is the toi or tue, to which we gave the nickname of parson, from its being a beautiful glossy black, and having a small patch of snow-white feathers on the neck .. Some call this the mocking-bird, as it imitates the voice of almost every other bird ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,28</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> <i>6. Prosthemadera Novae Seelandiae. Strickl. .. </i>Poe, or Toi of the natives of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>Forst</i>. Toni of the natives. <i>Less</i>. Tui of the natives.<i> Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/187</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> The natives added to our store by catching a parrot, five Tuis, and a Weka (a species of rail), living themselves upon fern root ... <i>(Nov 15, 1843,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Nov 15 Selwyn Journal in 1845 NZ Part II (Church in the Colonies VII) 49]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Tuis or mocking-birds. <i>(1841,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/45</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> After leaving the village we crossed to another bay, E-Taua. This bay also is spacious and pleasant. Kingfishers, oystercatchers, tuis [fn Anthochoera concinnata, Vig. and Horsfield.], and cormorants [fn Pelecanus pica, Forst.], enlivened the trees on its shores, and many birds were singing in the forest. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/118 .. the warbling of the tui [fn1 Anthochaera concinnata, Vig. and Horsf.], whose note resembles that of our thrush or blackbird ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/59</i>)

>  [spg toe] Henry went in the bush: shot two Birds and two toes. <i>(Feb 27, 1844,Piraki Log,150,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

>  Rail and pukeka were caught by snares set in their sphagnum moss runs .. Tui and mako (bell birds) were killed by the birder hutching beneath a cone of fern fronds ... <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 /362</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 362]

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> The lapping of the sea mingled with the bell notes of the moko moko and tui and .. that of the burning bush wood in the boiling of the `billy'. <i>Ibid.</i> 89 The liquid notes of tuis and moko-mokos rang out in unison, and kaw-kaws and [90] parakeets made splashes of colour as the sun caught their iridescent wings. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,70</i>)

>  <i>Tui</i>.- The old name <i>Parson bird</i> had gone out of general use before 1880. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  You have done some mad things Where the glad tui sings His song, but you were always wild ... <i>(Jul 25, 1919,Hawke's Bay Herald,7</i>)

>  The Tui, or parson-bird, preaches extempore from the bush. <i>(Nov 10, 1888,Observer (Auckland),4</i>)

>  The tuis at this period of the year appear to be out of voice - their notes being of a very bass and guttural description. <i>(Oct 26, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

>  No bullocky ever loved a tui, for the simple reason that these birds are great imitators, and they would often lead these bullockies astray. The bullocky would follow what he thought was the sound of one of his bells, and instead, find a tui up a tree. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,161</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> The tuis were going mad with delight; cheerful birds dressed in sober black, with two white feathers at the throat, earning them the name of the 'parson-bird'. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,00</i>)

>  There is a definite pecking order among the birds, with the larger tui holding the "top gun" position, followed by bellbird and then hihi, which are the social underdogs of the trio. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/89</i>)

>  <b>WADMAN</b> Only the tui, oblivious in the honey-flower, blows a crystal bubble into the air and anchors it with a rusty chain. <i>(1947,Yearbook of Arts in NZ,3/148</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|ui, s. A bird so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,218</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> [The dawn chorus ceased] and with the exception of the tooi, and some other birds whose songs are almost uninterrupted, the woods are comparatively silent for the remainder of the day. <i>(1823,Journal,196,1957 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EARLE</b> Tooee or New Zealand Blackbird. <i>(1832,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,174</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> .. and also the Toi [tui] bird or Parson .. With two bands under the Chin of white Feathers .. called Parson birds. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),34,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> [spg zooe] The <i>Pigeon</i> and <i>Zooe</i> are perhaps the handsomest - the former is really a magnificent bird - almost as large as a barn-door fowl, and equally invites the attention of the Ornothologist [sic] and the epicurean - the latter has two feathery [35] wattles pendent from its ear, and some few of its notes are not unlike those of the wattle bird of Van Diemen's Land. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,34,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Tui </i>- This remarkable bird, from the versatility of its talents for imitation, has, by some, been called `the Mocking Bird'; and, from its pecu-[53] liar plumage, has by others been denominated the Parson Bird'. <i>(1835,Some Account of NZ,52</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the birds that distinguished themselves in this woodland concert, were the tui, or mocking-bird, the korimaku [a-acute], kohapiroa, and tiaki [a-acute]. The little restless piwakawaka [a2,a4-acute], a kind of thrush, who is incessantly hopping from twig to twig, put forth its single note. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/296 The <i>tui</i>, or mocking-bird, is best known to the stranger in the country. The natives vend these birds, in wicker cages, to their transient visitors : it is called tui, from the resemblance of its note to that sound. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/108</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The Tui bird's note is very sweet it reminds me of a German clock. <i>(Apr 13, 1839,Journal,2/100</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> The Tui is a curious greenish black bird with a little ruff of feathers under his chin like a clergymans [sic] bands. <i>(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,222,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> Just as I had shot the 2nd Toughie (or Toey Toey as it is also spelt) spied a Barque beating in. <i>(Oct 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,248,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Tui or mocking-bird. <i>(1845,Advance NZ,1/80</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The graceful <i>tui</i>, or parson-bird (<i>Prosthemadera Novae Selandicae</i>) sported amongst the yellow blossoms of the <i>kowai</i> ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/29</i>)

>  The tui, or parson-bird, or mocking-bird, as it is indifferently called ... <i>(1851,Canterbury Papers,11/310</i>)

>  <b>GODLEY</b> Across the path .. comes the bush, full of birds singing all day, tuis and bell-birds and New Zealand robins ... <i>(Sep 8, 1851,Letters from Canterbury, NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Sep 8 C. Godley ?Letters from Canterbury in 1947 Reed Story of Canterbury 100]

>  <b>MACKIE</b> The only native bird we find commonly kept in cages is the `tue', Parson bird. <i>(Apr 4, 1853,Traveller under Concern,96,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The tui or parson-bird is a starling, and has a small tuft of white cravat-like feathers growing from its throat. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,9/137</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Then we crept softly up .. to have a good look at the Tui, or Parson-bird, most respectable and clerical-looking in its glossy black suit, .. and white wattles of very slender feathers .. curled coquettishly at each side of his throat, exactly like bands. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,93,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tui</i>, a bird (Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae), the most characteristic of the smaller Maori birds, kept by the Maori as a pet and by them taught to speak, it being an excellent mimic, and so by the settlers called the mocking-bird, shortened to mockie, and called parson-bird because of the two white curled tufts of feathers at its throat. Then, [check parson-bird inclusion - not in Journ Polyn Soc ref???] because some folk thought mockie was short for makomako, a name of the bell-bird (korimako) in some parts, some thought it was the korimako which was the mimic, and hence both birds have been called the mocking-birds. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/147</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>tui</i>: singing forest bird. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I thought I had had enough excitement .. but Ratapu persuaded me to go on with my <i>tui</i>-catching. The hawk had scared the <i>tuis</i> away for a time ... <i>(1948,Taina,138</i>)

>  The Maori name of a handsome New Zealand bird. (Pronounce .us too-ee See parson-bird.) <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),424</i>)

### Tui
 
 <i>n.</i> a brand of beer, originally produced at Mangatainoka [ORIGIN: Maori, propr.]

>  Mr Young says: "Pass the Ecstasy/latte/chardonnay/Tui". <i>(Jun 21, 2002,Evening Post</i>)

>  Tui has been brewing on its present site since 1889 when Henry Wagstaff began brewing "quality brews to beer drinkers around the colony"... "It's this bottle of Tui," said the visitor. "It's cloudy". <i>(Sep 27, 1999,Manawatu Evening Standard,Suppl. 4</i>)

>  You score a try under the Tui goalpost every Saturday and when you're old enough you can stand at the Tui bar and drink. <i>(Feb 29, 2004,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  "It's not cool to go around the corner and find a six-pack of used Tuis," he said. <i>(Oct 31, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  "A handle of Tui's, please, I gasped, and by Christ it tasted superb sitting there beneath an electric fan, my face already cooled down after splashing myself with cold water in the bathroom basin. <i>(Mar 18, 2000,Dominion,94</i>)

>  The truth is, I was down at the pub with everyone else on Saturday, shaking my head in disbelief and looking for some solace in the bottom of a Tui handle after we were robbed of the Bledisloe. <i>(Aug 14, 2000,Salient,No. 17 : 42</i>)

>  Norman Dowling produced a large bottle of liquid "Tui." <i>(1938,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,VII/57</i>)

>  And even when we do all sit together, differences in gender rhetoric keep us talking past each other like a lineup of bill boards for Tui beer. <i>(Oct 11, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a woman soldier in WWII [[check whether refers to NZWWSA, or NZWRAC, or NZWAAC] ?sc member of the NZWRAC: NZ Woman's Royal Army Corps]]

>  This incident occurred before the NZWWSA in the Middle East became known as WAAC's.] <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East,14</i>)

>  ... Lady Freyberg was `mother' to the New Zealand girls, a special relationship she maintains, Miss Neeley said. It was her idea, she said, to settle on `Tuis' as a nickname for the women. `The New Zealand men were always known as Kiwis, so they wanted a slang term for us'. <i>(Oct 24, 1972,Press, The,6/3</i>)

>  <b>hend</b> ... Lady Freyberg's personal maid ... was a `Tui' in the war. <i>(1978,Soldier Country</i>)

>  <b>LATHAM</b> On October 29th the unit was welcomed at Tewfik by Brigadier Falconer and Mrs B.C.Freyberg (who christened them the `Tuis'. Was the reasoning that the General had his Kiwis so the girls naturally became the Tuis?) <i>Ibid.</i> 37 [caption] <i>First draft of 30 WWSA (called `Tuis') on steps of Parliament House, September 1941. (Hats! - also note WWSA badges on lapel.) <i>(1986,WAAC Story, The,21</i>)

>  "Tuis" is the name by which the girls are likely to become known. <i>(Oct 20, 1941,Top of the Bill,I/17 : 17</i>)

>  [Hdg] A "Tui" Wedding... On Friday, June 19, a very quiet wedding took place between Corporal Joyce Gilmour, N.Z.W.W.S.A... and Lieutenant Kenneth Webb, R.N. <i>(Jun 29, 1942,NZEF Times,II/53 : 5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a member of a WWI NZ pierrot party; "The Tuis", the concert party

>  <b>WOLFE</b> Another bunch of theatrical New Zealanders who sometimes swelled the ranks of the Kiwis were the Tuis, whose costume was ornithologically correct with white frothy ruffles and pompoms. <i>(1991,Kiwi,34</i>)

>  <b>DOWNES</b> The concert party was enlarged by members of the small free-lance entertainment troupe of the 4th Brigade, the Tuis. <i>(1979,Top of the Bill,54</i>)

>  The existing pierrot troupes `The Kiwis', `Tuis' and others have reached a high standard of excellence. <i>(Aug 2, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,10</i>)

>  A few evenings ago we were treated to a very meritorious and musical entertainment by "The Tuis" Pierrots (mobile). <i>(Jul 5, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/47 : 248</i>)

>  The existing pierrot troupes, "The Kiwis", "Tuis" and others, have reached such a high standard of excellence that it makes it doubly hard for a new troupe to create an impression. <i>(Aug 2, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/49 : 10</i>)</i>

### tui fever
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  They call it tui fever, the time around now when tui feast on the late autumn nectars of the bush... Tui released into the sanctuary are already being attributed to the rise in sightings of this native in the Capital's backyards. <i>(Jun 5, 2003,Contact (Wgtn),7</i>)

### tui grass
 
 <i>n.</i> toetoe grass [(misrep. of toe (toi))]

>  <b>CAIRD</b> Tui grass ... a very graceful plant, closely resembling a grass that is grown in the gardens, called, I think, Spanish grass. <i>(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,30</i>)

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> ... a cluster of tui-grass. <i>(1889,Stories of NZ Life,55</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> ... silver plumes of `tui' grass. <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,80</i>)

### tukauki
 
 <i>n.</i> a plant, <i>Libertia ixioides</i>

>  <i>tu[u]ka[a]uki,</i> a plant (Libertia Ixioides), handsome smallish flower with three round white petals and pale-yellow centre: at its best it forms a semi-globe of white blossom in most unlikely-seeming places. [brackets in name/hyphen in semi-globe???] <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

 <i>n.</i> a form of bird snare

### tuki-tuki
 
 <i>n.</i> a pestle

### Tukidale
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred sheep, bred specifically for carpet wool production. [ORIGIN: blend, toponymic origin: Tukituki, Hawke's Bay]

>  <b>BLACK & DYSON</b> The Tukidale descended from a hairy Romney ram lamb ("Dreamy") adopted as a pet in 1966 by Mr M V Coop of Tuki Tuki, east of Havelock North. <i>(1978,Carpetmakers: Specialty Carpet Wool breeds of NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>BLACK & DYSON</b> The fleece weight of the Cheviot and Down breeds does not match that of the Drysdales. Tukidales, Carpetmasters, and coarse Perendales. <i>(1978,Carpetmakers: Specialty Carpet Wool breeds of NZ,12</i>)

>  <b>WARMAN</b> It [the Drysdale] grew into something called 'a carpet on the hoof' and its success has inspired the development of two similar breeds - the intriguingly named Tukidales and Carpetmasters. <i>(1991,What Sheep is that?,28</i>)

>  <b>WONZ</b> Tukidales and Carpetmasters, other strains developed since Drysdales, have similar genes and wool characteristics. <i>(1994,NZ Sheep and Their Wool,19</i>)

>  The Tukidale breed originated in the early 1970's [sic] when mutation in a Romney flock produced a very coarse coated ram which passed on the characteristics to his progeny. <i>(1993,Wool Technology and Sheep Breeding,383</i>)

 <i>v.</i> to kill
