### Tukugate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Will Mr Sturt be asked to stand aside while the Serious Fraud Office investigates complaints into the Tukugate scandal? <i>(Mar 13, 1997,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Isn't it a fact that Tukugate could result in the collapse of this Government and a general election? <i>(Mar 14, 1997,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Parliamentary insiders report that one of the "old school" they effect to despise.. had to be called in to prevent a macho boilover in the inner circle during Tukugate discussions. <i>(Mar 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  He has said these phrases so often now during Tukugate that it begs for the parroting. <i>(May 3, 1997,Dominion,25</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tukugate sets the Govt morally adrift... With a failure to resolve Tukugate when it first came up earlier this year, the crisis has grown sticker, deeper and more unmanageable in the repetition. <i>(May 4, 1997,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  This breaks the unofficial rule, that the Opposition leader's gaze should never fall lower than the third-ranked minister unless the pickings are rich - such as Tukugate or the Tourism Board. <i>(Sep 4, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  "It follows on from Tukugate and the attempted annihilation of a Maori MP..." <i>(Mar 15, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

### tukura
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a variety of farmed native flax, Phormium tenax. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The Tukura which used to grow on the Motoa Estate was much sought after by the Maori. <i>(Aug 22, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,1</i>)

### tukus
 
 <i>n.</i> underpants [From MP Tukuroirangi Morgan whose expenditure claims on the Maori TV station included very expensive underpants]

>  He shows them his "Tuku" undies, silk boxer shorts emblazoned with cannabis leaves. <i>(Nov 5, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Wellington meanswear retailer reports the best line (to date) from a shopper: "I need a pair of tuks. Large." <i>(Feb 22, 1997,Evening Post,64</i>)

>  I keep telling myself not to panic, not to get my tukus in a knot over the catastrophe. <i>(Feb 21, 1997,Independent (Auckland),13</i>)

>  Having followed the shenanigans of Tuku Morgan, I thought I'd write to you with the suggestion that from now on men's underwear should simply be called "tukus". <i>(Mar 10, 1997,Dominion,10</i>)

>  We have a new word in the language. On Wednesday I heard some Lower Hutt schoolboys discussing the birthday of one of their number. When he was asked what sort of presents he'd got, the birthday boy recited a list, and about half-way through he said, "and I got some new tukus"... I look forward to the next edition of the Oxford New Zealand Dictionary, for the inevitable entry:tuku n. pl (Colloq.) 1. underpants, worn by males, usu. decorative. [abbrev. of Tukuroirangi, Maori]. 2. v. intr. (sl). 1. offer to sell an interview on a matter of importance when a Member of Parliament. <i>(Jun 7, 1997,Dominion,20</i>)

### tukutuku
 
 <i>n.</i> the ornamental lattice-work panels in the walls of a Maori house. [Maori]

>  <b>ALEXANDER</b> Seeing that poupou and tukutuku are the traditional means of decorating, I have taken it that both must be employed. <i>(1951,Story of Te Aute, The,205</i>)

>  Tukutuku panels adorned the walls and kowhai covered the exposed beams overhead. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,64</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 266 Tukutuku/poupou = forms of wall decoration <i>(1983,Bone People, The,467</i>)

>  <i>tukutuku n</i>. ornamental lattice-work of toi-toi reeds interlaced with flax etc., used as wall panelling in Maori whares. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,829</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  The gate across the road into the Nga Hau e Wha Christchurch National marae is chained and double- padlocked. Behind the palisade fence the weeds are knee high. The whare nui is boarded up ... None of its carvings and tukutuku are in place and the carvings are still tapu. The whare wananga beside it, meant to house the kohanga reo, lies uncompleted, torn building paper flapping. <i>(Mar 5, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The panels of this lattice-work (<i>tukutuku</i>) had elaborate patterns, and the horizontal laths were gaily painted. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,272</i>)

>  It is nearly time now to devote some attention at the school to tukutuku. The idea is to reintroduce the kakaho reeds at the back, instead of milled laths. <i>(Jun 11, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/88</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  The arts and crafts section comprised tukutuku, ... plaiting (rourou [fn A small basket used for cooked food.], rough kits and floor mats), taniko work and making poi balls. <i>(Mar 28, 1936,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/212</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <i>tukutuku</i>, ornamental lattice-work of toetoe-reeds (kakaho) cross-laced in various patterns with narrow strips of flax and pingao: a panel of tukutuku was placed between every pair of poupou on the whare walls. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> Between the beams, the wall was covered with lattice panels, which Weno told me were <i>tuku-tuku</i> work - geometric figures of white on a dark background, like very coarse cross-stitch. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,30</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/321-322, 2/127-128</i>)

>  <b>187</b> The tuhu tuhu or kowhaiwhai (coloured scroll work) and the tukutuku (decorative reed panels) are admirable examples of Maori art ... <i>(Aug 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>)

>  <i>(Nov 20, 1958,Listener (New Zealand Listener),825/2</i>)

>  <i>tukutuku n </i>-s [Maori] : decorative Maori latticework usu. of flax or kiekie stems and in the form of panels that are used esp. between the carved posts of communal buildings (as a meetinghouse) <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2461</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> It [sc. a banner] was about two foot square ... An edging of<i> tukutuku </i>the pattern enclosed trade symbols of motorcycles ... <i>(1980,Murder and Chips,66</i>)

>  I became inspired then to write a play where, as the tukutuku panel purapurawhetu (a basic star design) is being woven, its story unfolds around the weaver. <i>(Mar 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  The interior of the house has some very pretty panel lattice work, the tukutuku of the Maori house-builder. <i>(Nov 6, 1911,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Robinson's ambiguous "The What is and the What is not" plays with the binary codes of computer technology, suggesting the tukutuku of a whare, as well as the flash-card patterning of British artist Bridget Riley. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> a curse, particularly one directed towards a notable person. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  .. and it is not uncommon to hear a chief complain that he has been shot at, when on explanation it appears that he has only been thus shot in effigy. This form of insult is called a "tapatapa," or "tukutuku." <i>(Mar 31, 1852,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  Other peculiar uses of names were the <i> tapatapa </i> and <i> tukutuku </i>. To <i>tapa</i> anything was to give the name of a person to it, the effect of which was to put it, figuratively, into the keeping of the person named, after which any other person taking a liberty with it would be regarded as an insult to the person whose name had been so used. <i>(1892,Proceedings RSNZ,25/397</i>)

>  <b>Taylor</b> Another way of obtaining revenge, was by cursing. To call any object by the name of a chief, and then strike or insult it, was regarded as a curse; this was tapatapa, or tukutuku. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,94</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  JC always intended that the mezzanine wall should be furnished with tukutuku panels, but Emma hasn't got around to doing that just yet. <i>(May 10, 2003,Dominion Post,F14</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> ... an old church/ Where the tukutuku work/ Plaited by women's fingers, has split and dried,/ <i>Ibid.</i> 400 And I knew for the first time the meaning of/ The yellow woven tukutuku panels,/ <i>(1979,Collected Poems,374,1966 ed.</i>)

### tukutuku-work
 
 <i>n.</i> [tukutuku work]

>  Arundo is toetoe-kakaho, the latter word referring to the valued culms. Some people object to the reed tukutuku-work as being flimsy and perishable: in reality it is as durable as galvanized-iron and far more handome and artistic: the interior of a good Maori house is a thing of beauty. <i>(Sep 9, 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,3/381, 397</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> ... an old church/ Where the tukutuku work/ Plaited by women's fingers, has split and dried,/ Ibid. 400 And I knew for the first time the meaning of/ The yellow woven tukutuku panels,/ <i>(1979,Collected Poems,374,1966 ed.</i>)

>  And lastly, the tukutuku work which fills in the spaces between the carved pillars around the walls of the house is brought only two-thirds of the way up instead of up to the cornice. <i>(Jun 2, 1924,Evening Post,8</i>)

 <i>n.</i> inherited land [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  We've got Maori selling tukuwhenua now, inherited land from the grandparents, now . . . . <i>(Sep 6, 2005,NZ Herald,A13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [empty headword, no cits] (See [tumatakuru](tot-tyr.md#tumatakuru))

### tumatakahuki
 
 <i>n.</i> tukutuku pattern [ORIGIN: Maori, (from kiwianatown.co.nz) Tukutuku are intricately woven panels often found lining the walls of the Wharenui (meeting house). These panels express ideas from Maori mythology, philosophy and history. The patterns displayed here are : Tumatakahuki (pattern either side) double/triple binding which ensures strength. Poutama (steps) has religious and educational connotations relating to levels of attainment. Roimata Toroa (albatross tears) denoting misadventure particularly to crops. Purapura Whetu (myriad of stars) relates to population and the peopling of a region.]

>  Vertical lines reference tumatakahuki, a procedure which binds the horizontal tukutuku rods in place - an analogy for the kind of frameworks required to maintain the Maori collective. <i>(Nov 23, 2005,NZ Herald,B5</i>)

### tumatakuru
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Discaria toumatou</i>, matagouri (q.v. for anglicisations), =(wild) Irishman, a thorny shrub



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a prickly native shrub Discaria toumatou [commonly corrupted from the Maori tumatakuru to matagouri.] [tumatagora, tumatikura, tomatagaru, toumatakuru, tomatagorra, tumatagowry] (See [matagouri](mar-moa.md#matagouri))

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Much of the country bears fine grass but is overrun with a shrub called tomatagaru by the natives or Wild Irishman by the colonists. <i>(1857,BLANK ENTRY,9</i>) [NOTES: in Hamilton (1952) History of Northern Southland]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> .. one may there travel for days in succession without ever seeing anything larger than a bush of tumatakuru (discaria toumatou) a straggling prickling, almost leafless shrub, attaining sometimes a height of ten to fourteen feet, known to the colonists as 'wild Irishman.' <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,70</i>)

>  There's even pressure to propagate the frightful thorny tumatakuru, or matagouri, a pest in anyone's language. <i>(Aug 15, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Slow walk over the plain from the Tumatakuru Bush which made the men feel their way very cautiously. <i>(Jan 1844,Journal,70</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> They [sc. sandals] no doubt owed their invention to the necessity of protecting the feet from ... the sharp prickles of the small shrub `tumatakuru' (<i>discaria toumatou, Raoul</i>), which is very common on the plains, ... <i>(Jan 13, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,210</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 13 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 210]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We passed over a grassy plain filled with the Tumatikura, the taramea, and a beautiful flower ... a variety of herb centaury ... <i>(Mar 13, 1845,Journal,3/93</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> The country ... is much overrun with a scrub called Tomataguru [H.-J. matagouri] by the natives, or `wild Irishman' by the colonists ... <i>(1971,Mr Surveyor Thomson,59</i>) [NOTES: 1850s in 1971 Hall-Jones Mr Surveyor Thomson 59 (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> TUMATAKURU .us (discaria toumatou, Raoul),;a dwarf thorn, common in the plains of the Middle Island. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,314</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> Writing of the Waimea Plain in 1856: - `The ground was well grassed, but the speargrass was too plentiful. The pasturage was very good. There was a good deal of scrub in the bends of the Mataura known as `wire plant' (Olearia virgata). There were also manuka and koromiko. Maori-heads, a kind of grass tree about three feet high, abounded in the swamps, and in some we saw toitoi grass and raupo. We did not pass any cabbage trees to-day ... we proceeded slowly through tomatakuru scrub, speargrass, Spaniard, snowgrass, and other native grasses ... we camped in a bend with good feed at the Elbow and a swamp on one side. Maori cabbage abounded ...' <i>(1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 18]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Discaria toumatou</i>, Raoul. Tumatakuru, Wild Irishman.--A bush or small tree with spreading branches; if properly trained would form a handsome hedge that would be stronger than whitehorn. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,131</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> As the only `trees' to be found on the Tuapeka goldfields were Te-matau Kauri (better known as `Wild Irishmen') and Tutu bushes, of not very exuberant growth, the law in this respect went ahead of the facts. <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,61,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> A shovelful of earth from under a tomatagorra bush ... <i>(1888,Wakatipians,82</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tumata-kuru, n.</i> Maori name for plant better known as <i>Wild Irishman</i> (q.v.), <i>Discaria toumatou,</i> Raoul. `A thorny plant, very difficult to handle.' (Vincent Pyke.) <i>Tumatagowry</i>, or <i>Matagory </i>(q.v.), is the Southern corruption of contractors, labourers, and others. <i>(1898,Austral English,482</i>)

>  <i>Tu&#769;.ma&#769;.tu-ku&#769;.ru</i> (tu&#769;.ma.&#769;.tu-ku&#769;.ru),<i> n</i>. [Maori.] [Written also <i>tomatakuru.] (Bot.) </i>A New Zealand tree (<i>Discaria toumatou</i>) of the order <i>Rhamneae</i>; - called also <i>matagory</i> and <i>wild Irishman.</i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2033</i>)

>  I remember, in 1867, visiting a runholder who had a small lawn of English grass ... which ... was covered with toumatukuru bushes, which had been collected from the run. <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/268</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate)]

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> ... tumatakuru, a small thorny scrub, one of the few deciduous plants of New Zealand, which Europeans nick-named `Wild Irishman', ... speargrass, or `Spaniard', as the stronger variety was called, ... <i>(1906,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1906 W.H.S.Roberts in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 4]

>  <b>CHE</b> <i>D[iscaria] Toumatou, Raoul. ... Tumatukuru.</i> ... Can only be distinguished from the Australian and Tasmanian <i>D. australis</i> by the absence of petals. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,101</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Discaria toumatou (The Wild Irishman). ... </i>Maori name <i>Tumatakuru. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,239,1940, p. 252 ed.</i>)

>  SPINY BUSH, WILD IRISHMAN. <i>(1847,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  SPINY BUSH, WILD IRISHMAN. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> spg: in error <i>Discaria ... Toumatu</i> [sic] ... Wild Irishman. MATAGOWRI, TUMATA-KURI [sic] ... <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,54</i>)

>  <i>tumatakuru</i> also <i>tumatukuru n</i> -s [Maori<i> tumatakuru</i>] : a New Zealand shrub or tree (<i>Discaria toumatou</i>) of the family Rhamnaceae having stout sharp spines used by the Maoris for tattooing - called also <i>Irishman, matagory, wild Irishman </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,2461</i>)

>  <b>MEAD</b> ... crossing first a grassy plain filled with tumatikuru ..., taramea, ... and a pretty cream-coloured star flower, ... <i>(1966,Richard Taylor,69</i>)

>  [TUMATA KURU (SIC)] <i>tumatakuru n.</i> New Zealand spiny shrub, with durable wood, called also Wild Irishman (q.v.). <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  In our shaded valleys are the beautiful tree ferns, on the dry plains the tussock and tumatakuru. <i>(Jul 15, 1933,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Between the long deltas of screes the thickets of tumatagora grew, a shrub as grey as old bones. <i>(1957,One-a-pecker, Two-a-pecker,45</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> There was a good deal of tomataguru on the ridges. <i>(1890,History of Oamaru & North Otago,13</i>)

>  July 12 and 13 - Mr. M., Tom, and J.F. pulling down Goi on Roughridge'; July 17 - Went for firewood and brought home a load of Korokio'; `August 2 - Snow. Tom and J.F. collecting Tomatagora up the Gully'; ... <i>(1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 91]

>  Drays loaded with tomatagora and flour started for Moa Cottage. <i>(Mar 5, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [Hocken TS]]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tumatakuru, <i>Col[enso]. Discaria Toumatou. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> In the Middle Island ... a great proportion of the land ... is one grand plain of shingle scarcely covered with soil, and only supporting grass ... much inferior to that of the North Island, filled also with spiny bushes of the Tumatakura, and that singular family of plants, the Acifolia Taramea, commonly called Spaniards; ... <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,202</i>)

>  The plains are covered with grasses, the roots of which are gathered into tufts or tussocks, intermixed with the Bayonet grass or `Wild Spaniard' (<i>Aciphylla Colensoi</i>), Toumatakuru (<i>Discaria Toumatou</i>), and a great variety of the New Zealand brooms (<i>Carmichaelia</i>), ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  The spines of the Tumatakuru, or New Zealand Thorn, <i>(Discaria Toumatou,) were sometime [sic] used for tattooing ... </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/37</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> NOTE. - As I have no desire to mystify readers unacquainted with New Zealand flora, I append a brief description of the shrubs mentioned:- <i>Kowhai</i> - A papilonaceous flowering tree, of the <i>acacia</i> tribe. [17] <i>Bush-lawyer</i> - Native name, Tataramoa - A disagreeable bramble, very tenacious of grasp. <i>Koromiko</i> - A white flowering <i>Veronica. Tutu</i> - A berry-bearing, glossy-leaved plant, deadly to man and to all animals - except goats. <i>Tumata-kuru</i> - Better known as `Wild Irishman' - a thorny plant, very difficult to handle. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iii/16-17,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Amid these the native flax flourishes ..., and even the cantankerous Tumatukuru attains considerable magnitude. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/iii/81,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <i>tumatakuru</i>. See <i>matagouri. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <i>tumatakuru n. NZ</i> = MATAGOURI, the thorns of which were used by the Maoris in tatooing. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,829</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### Tumatauenga
 
 <i>n.</i> (also as Tu elliptical) the Maori god of war. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> 'I bring you three boys under the tapu of Tumatauenga, the God of War. Fight for the honour of your tribe!' <i>(2000,Uncle's Story, The,42</i>)

### tumatikuru
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tumatakuru](tot-tyr.md#tumatakuru))

### tumble-weed
 
 <i>n.</i> the dried flower-heads of <i>Scirpus frondosus,</i> pingao or nassella tussock [tumble weed, tumbleweed; definition not exclusive, some general use for seed heads that tumble along the ground in the wind.]

>  Thus, a horse with caution, and a faculty for seeing everything he passes, having been startled by a piece of paper, or a tumble weed being blown under him or against his legs, may never get over shying at these particular objects. <i>(Aug 1, 1885,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF & CALDER</b> Nassella Tussock's ... seed heads break off when mature and are blown along the ground by strong winds. It is sometimes referred to as `tumble-weed' on this account. <i>(1918,Pasture Plants & Pastures of NZ,48</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> The globular, spiked flower-head [of the pingao] drops when the seed is mature and trundles along the beach in the wind ... The Pakeha name of such spherical flower-heads is `tumble-weed'. [Marginal pencil note on this copy: `spinifex'.] <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,274</i>)

>  <i>pingao</i>, sea-shore plant (Scirpus frondosus), tumble-weed, after the stiff-spined globular seed-heads: the leaves, drying a bright-orange much used for plaiting ornamental girdles and for tukutuku[spg/hyphen???] (lattice-work) lacing in wall-panels of whares. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]



 
 <i>n.</i> the dried heads of nassella tussock

### tumbler
 
 <i>n.</i> a kind of shell or mortar bomb [?or merely a shell that `tumbles' end over end in flight]

>  <b>PILLING</b> Jerry opened out on Jock's Joy with his minnies, rum jars, tumblers etc. - another bombardment! <i>(Feb 1, 1917,Anzac Memory,121,1933 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the end of the dredge ladder round which the belt holding the buckets revolves, or changes direction

>  <b>KITTO</b> Before letting down the ladder you should examine the bottom tumbler and hangers to see they [sic] are all right. <i>Ibid.</i> 20 GETTING BUCKETS ON THE BOTTOM TUMBLER. <i>(1900,Practical Dredgeman's Manual,16</i>)

### tumbler sweep
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAWKE & SCOTT</b> The tumbler sweep consisted of a number of teeth, perhaps up to a dozen, according to whether it was a one horse or a two horse sweep... It had two curved handles and often a board to sweep the hay off when the handles were released by the driver who walked [behind. When] the handles were released the sweep turned over completely and just carried on for the next load. <i>(1985,Early Farm Machinery in NZ,89</i>)

### tumbler-rake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SINCLAIR & HARREX</b> The tumbler-rake.,.raked the cut hay until it had caught up a good quantity. Then the rake was raised to leave the hay in what was called a windrow. <i>(1978,Looking Back,92</i>)

### tumbleweed amaranth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>tumbleweed amaranth</i> [s]<i> Amaranthus albus</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,80</i>)

### tumblewheel
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hubbed wheel on six legs for feeding out and maintaining electric fence wire at the appropriate height. (invented 1980s by Taranaki farmer, Sandy Chesswas.).

>  <b>RENNIE</b> This 'tumble wheel' system, designed to make moving the wire easy, was invented by a Taranaki hill country farmer. <i>(1988,NZ Agriculture,21</i>)

>  Each Tumblewheel can be easily assembled or dismantled for storage. <i>(1996,Gallagher Power Fence Manual,34</i>)

>  It is easy to 'walk' the tumblewheel up a paddock. It [a tumblewheel] looks like something from outer space, with six legs being attached to a central core through which the tape runs. If you are just having to control cattle, the single wire tumblewheel system is ideal. [tumble wheel] <i>(2000,Lifestyle Farmer,Autumn 66</i>)

### tumblewheel fence
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a type of electric fence where wire is supported on a spiked metal or plastic wheel structure, enabling easy movement.

>  To erest a tumblewheel fence, run out the Polywire across the pasture and tension, preferably with a reel at each end. <i>(1996,Gallagher Power Fence Manual,34</i>)

### tumeke
 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) awesome, cool [ORIGIN: Maori, The English version of the word 'Tumeke' would be 'Choice' or 'Awesome' so if you would like a choice web name presence then you need this www.Tumeke.com (from TradeMe)]

>  Tumeke! We've got one signed copy of Akon's CD - 'Trouble' for you to win. <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,16,Summer ed.</i>)

>  They say that the best moves are hard to learn but look tumeke. <i>(2002,Te Mana  Taiohi,4,Summer ed.</i>)

>  Everything about Taiohi is just tu meke!!! <i>(2003,Te Mana  Taiohi,7,Autumn ed.</i>)

>  A lot of emotions were visible and the ahua among us was tumeke. <i>(Oct 2006,Pu Kaea,4</i>)

>  And we profile Turanga Merito .. in Australia's biggest theatre production, The Lion King - tumeke! <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,1,Autumn ed.</i>)

>  (heading) Tumeke Tuis! <i>(2008,Mana,85/78</i>)

### tumingi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tumingi, Middle Island, <i>Lyall. Leucopogon fasciculatus. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

### tumuaki
 
 <i>n.</i> chairperson; head; CEO [ORIGIN: Maori, *1984 VUW Used in Accountancy Dept for `Chairperson' [? for Professor Whata Winiata]]

>  Te Runanga O Ngati Ruanui Annual General Meeting [agenda item] Tumuaki Report <i>(Oct 26, 2005,NZ Herald,D6</i>)

>  The Tumuaki role is the most senior Maori Health position in the provider arm. <i>(Feb 26, 2006,Sunday Star Times,D13</i>)

>  [advertisement] <b> Chief Executive/Tumuaki Te Whare Wananga o Awanuiarangi - Whakatane Tertiary Education Sector - Leadership Role <i>(Mar 11, 2006,Dominion Post,H6</i>)

>  I am also informed by the gentleman I formerly alluded to, and who has indeed great experience in what is called "native affairs," that members of parliament, <i>Tumuaki</i>; or, as my friend the old settler says, the word translates "top knots," have, by established usage, the right to kill anyone they are able, on any pretence or none, as they choose, and that this old custom from immemorial usage has all the force of law, and that it would be both impolitic and dangerous to interfere with it ... <i>(Jun 27, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)</b>

### tuna
 
 <i>n.</i> tunny

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>ALBACORE</i> (see also Tuna) (a) Scientific name: <i>Thunnus alaunga</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name: Albacore. (c) Other common names: Tuna, Tunny, long-finned albacore. (d) Principal localities where taken: Bay of Plenty, East Cape to Gisborne, North Taranaki Bight. <i>Ibid.</i> 49 <i>TUNA </i>(3 species excluding Albacore) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Katsuwonus pelamis</i> (ii) <i>Thunnus maccoyi</i> (iii) <i>Thunnus albacares.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Skipjack Tuna (ii) Southern Bluefin Tuna (iii) Yellowfin Tuna. (c) Other common names: (i) Skipjack; Striped Bonito (ii) Bluefin Tuna; Southern Bluefin; Southern Tuna (iii) Yellowfin All sometimes referred to as Tunny. (d) Principal localities where taken: (i) A summewr visitor to New Zealand ... (ii) Southern Bluefin Tuna is a large species of temperate Australasian seas and migrates south to Foveaux Strait in summer months. (iii) A widely distributed tropical species which ranges into northern waters ... in summer months. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,9</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> STRIPED BONITO [<i>Katsuwonus pelamis</i>] ... Striped tunny or tuna, skipjack, bonito, and sometimes skipper, are alternative names in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,52</i>)

>  <b>266</b> TUNAS ... Tuna were generally known as `tunny' until the discovery of a potentially big export market to America, where tuna is rated a luxury fish. The name as then changed to conform with that used in America. Since then Southern Bluefin tuna, Yellowfin tuna and Striped, or Skipjack, tuna have been developed into important export fishes. In New Zealand the Albacore has become an important commercial fish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,WILSON</i>)

>  [species name] tuna .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] unspecified - see albacore, skipjack, southern bluefin, yellowfin, bigeye, frigate, slender and butterfly tuna. <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] tuna, tunny 2048-2113 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Anguilla</i> spp.; a freshwater eel, aka silverbelly [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Nor is the wily "tuna" beaten when merely stunned. <i>(Dec 26, 1928,NZ Free Lance,42</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|una; An eel. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,219</i>)

>  large eels - too'na [tuna] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,113,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: see also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 285]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Eight Tunas or eels. <i>(1840,Journal,2/2/288</i>)

>  Stopped at `Tommy's' [a Maori]. Kikied the `tiners' and the fish. Slept on a Maori mat, wrapped in a blanket: 4 in a bed-place - heads and tails. <i>(Aug 15, 1842,Piraki Log,143,(ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [Note Anson p166 gives <i>tunas</i>: but perhaps <i>tivers</i> `potatoes']]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Otaki] my boys are feasting on a large tuna they caught as they came along. ... They have found a native eel basket which they have set, ... [Oct 3] ... they went to the hinaki and found it almost full they took about 2 doz. <i>(Oct 2, 1844,Journal,3/26</i>)

>  We were 6 in party, ourselves, young Stark & 3 Maoris with `pikau' burdens. ... After tiring of [singing] they set off with burning brand & tomahawk to catch crawfish & tuna, the eels of the country, in a little brook near our encampment. [spg tomahawk/tomehawk???] <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/79,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> New Zealand has no large fresh-water fish. The only exception is the<i> tuna</i>, or eel; there are many varieties of it, and almost all the other fresh-water fish partake more or less of a similar character. The number of names given to this fish, and the careful way they have noticed every little difference between one and another, clearly shows the great value the natives put upon it. <i>Ibid.</i> 498 The principal ones are the<i> tuna</i>, eel, the <i>pipiharau</i>, lamprey, the <i>kokopu,</i> and <i>inanga ... </i> <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,413</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Tuna - the generic name for eels, of which the Maoris classify several varieties. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  <b>HAY</b> In the bays and tidal rivers are the mango or sharks - the most highly-prized food-fish - the tamure or schnapper, the whapuka or rock-cod, the kahawai or mackerel, the porahi or herring, the kanae or mullet, the patiki or sole, and many others. On the shores are oysters, mussels, cockles, mutton-fish, crabs, and other shell-fish in profuse abundance. In the fresh-water creeks are eels (tuna), lampreys (pipiharau), and whitebait (inanga). <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  Mabel and I called on Wi Te Tahuhu ... and saw some 2000 tuna drying. ... I think there will be quite 20,000 eels in all for Te Whiti. Piripiri and Pene took a large contract from me fencing. They gave their word as Te Whiti men but would not sign an agreement. <i>(Feb 12, 1885,Chudleigh Diary,332,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The following is a list of the fishes which are chiefly met with in the market: hapuku, kahawai, red-snapper, snapper, tarakihi, moki, trumpeter, frostfish ... gurnard, mullet, sea-mullet, ... flounder or patiki, ... grayling, smelt, kokopu, sardine, sprat, eel (tuna), black-eel, conger-eel [168], and silver-eel. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Old grandfather eel, old king tuna, you are dead meat now! <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,138</i>)

>  We are following in a long line. Like a tuna winding its way back up river from the sea, looking for a nice spot in a creek. <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,69</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tuna</i>. See <i>Eel. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,482</i>)

>  ANGUILLA AUSTRALIS Richardson. <i>Common Eel; Tuna.</i> ... Distribution: South Island and southern portion of North Island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/119</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Anguilla aucklandii</i> Richardson. EEL; TUNA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,17,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Anguilla australis:</i> [European Name] Short-finned eel [Maori Name] Tuna. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <i>Anguilla aucklandii</i> Richardson. Long-finned eel. <i>Tuna. </i> <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/403</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <i>tuna</i>, a fish (Anguilla sp.), eel, of which there are only two species in Newzealand, though there are about 110 supposed varieties named by the Maori, and even Pakehas think there are several varieties going by size, shape, and colour, but these variations are due to age and sex. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/147</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> SHORT-FINNED EEL (<i>Anguilla australis schmidtii</i>). These are the common freshwater eels called Tuna by the Maoris. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,64</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND EELS Long-finned Eel (Tuna): <i>Anguilla dieffenbachii </i>Gray, Short-finned Eel (Tuna Heke): <i>Anguilla australis schmidtii</i> Phillipps. <i>Ibid. </i> 125 The Short-finned Eel varies from dark brown to very light brown on the back, with silvery sides and belly, and is known as the Silvery-eel or Silver-belly. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,123</i>)

>  The Maori name for an eel. (Pronounce .us too|-na). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),434</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>tuna</i>: eel. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,195</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> LONGFINNED EEL [<i>Anguilla dieffenbachi</i>] ... Mud-eel and black eel are alternative names, while a variety of Maori names are in use. Tuna is widely used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,30</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> New Zealand eels, known to the Maori as <i>tuna</i>, are esteemed for their succulence and keeping qualities by the many countries which import them. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,71</i>)

>  <i>tuna n.</i> NZ freshwater eel. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,829</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> The tables themselves are loaded: hangi food, kaimoana, trout, tuna, titi, puha, karengo. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,21</i>)

### tuna heke
 
 <i>n.</i> a migrating eel [ORIGIN: Maori, aka tuna hao]

>  They found good populations of longfin eels (tuna), freshwater crayfish (koura), and - most surpassingly - torrentfish (papanoko), whose closest relative is the marine blue cod. <i>(Jun 2005,National Centre for Aquatic Biodiversity,No. 12/2</i>) [NOTES: National Centre for Aquatic Biodiversity and Biosecurity Update]

>  When the migrating eels (<i>tuna heke</i>) come down in the March floods,... <i>(1926,TrNZI,58/639</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Anguilla australis</i> Richardson. SHORT-FINNED EEL; TUNA HEKE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,17,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND EELS Long-finned Eel (Tuna): <i>Anguilla dieffenbachii </i> Gray, Short-finned Eel (Tuna Heke): <i>Anguilla australis schmidtii</i> Phillipps. <i>Ibid.</i> 125 The Short-finned Eel varies from dark brown to very light brown on the back, with silvery sides and belly, and is known as the Silvery-eel or Silver-belly. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,123</i>)

### tuna kuwharuwharu
 
 <i>n.</i> long fin eel [ORIGIN: Maori, aka long fin eel, longfin eel, long finned eel.]

>  Today the eel population in the Wairarapa is under stress and none more so than the largest species - the majestic Tuna Kuwharuwharu. Tuna Kuwharuwharu are one of New Zealand's most ancient inhabitatns but have decreased in number so rapidly that they are now nearing endangered status. So it's extremely special to see a large number of female tuna kuwharuwharu being fed at Pukaha Mount Bruce which occurs daily at 1.30pm. <i>(Feb 2011,Wairarapa News,supplement: pukaha Mount Bruce p. 8</i>)

>  <b>Joseph Potangaroa</b> Tuna kuwharuwharu are one of New Zealand's most ancient inhabitants but have decreased in number so rapidly that it is now nearing endangered status. <i>(Feb 2010,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.rangitane.iwi.nz/education/attachments/169_tuna_vweb.pdf]

(d) Principal localities where taken: Bay of Plenty, East Cape to Gisborne, North Taranaki Bight. <i>Ibid.</i> 49 TUNA (3 species excluding Albacore) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Katsuwonus pelamis</i> (ii) <i>Thunnus maccoyi</i> (iii) <i>Thunnus albacares</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Skipjack Tuna (ii) Southern Bluefin Tuna (iii) Yellowfin Tuna. (d) Principal localities where taken: (i) A summer visitor to New Zealand ... (ii) Southern Bluefin Tuna is a large species of temperate Australasian seas and migrates south to Foveaux Strait in summer months. (iii) A widely distributed tropical species which ranges into northern waters ... in summer months. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,9</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> TUNAS ... Tuna were generally known as `tunny' until the discovery of a potentially big export market to America, where tuna is rated a luxury fish. The name as then changed to conform with that used in America. Since then Southern Bluefin tuna, Yellowfin tuna and Striped, or Skipjack, tuna have been developed into important export fishes. In New Zealand the Albacore has become an important commercial fish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,266</i>)

>  [species name] albacore .. [scientific name] <i>Thunnus alalunga </i>.. [other name(s)] tuna albacore, longfinned tuna, tunny <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] tuna, tunny 2048-2113 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>GREY</b> The boatmen told of small tunny taken off Cape Brett. <i>(1926,Angler's Eldorado,27</i>)

>  <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i> Richardson. The `butterfly-fish', as it is called when young, has been taken in Otago Harbour, and is now known to be the young of the scaled tunny, described as <i>Lepidothynnus huttoni</i> Gunther. <i>(1913,TrRSNZ,45/230</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BUTTERFLY-FISH or SCALED TUNNY <i>Gasterochisma melampus</i>Richardson <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,301</i>)

### tunutunu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> For preparing small meals in a fowlers' camp or fishermen's bivouac, the open hearth was used and the fish or birds might be grilled on sticks leaning over the glowing embers. Grilling was known as <i>tunutunu</i> or</i> <i> rorerore. </i> <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,1</i>)

### tuokura
 
 <i>n.</i> the fern <i>Dicksonia lanata</i>

>  At two metres, Dicksonia lanata, or Tuokura, is the baby of the Dicksonias. It has a slim, sometimes prostrate trunk, which can be branched, and the fronds are markedly lighter yellow-green compared with other tree ferns. <i>(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

### tuoro
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The largest eels are called <i>rauhine</i>; they were originally fed and regarded as inferior gods. There is an eel found in marshy ground called a<i> tuoro,</i> which is said to have a large head, and to attack men. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,414</i>)

### tup crutch
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) carry out a pre-tupping shearing of ewes' rear ends. (See [bullseye](bri-byo.md#bullseye),[buttonhole](bri-byo.md#buttonhole),[ring-crutch](red-roa.md#ring-crutch))

>  March and April : ewes tup crutched, rams out, lambs drenched. <i>(Oct 2002,Wairarapa Times-Age,Supp. 11</i>)

### tupak grass
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](go-gra.md#grass))

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tupak Grass of Otago. <i>Carex apressa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

### tupakahi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The masses of tupakihi, the clematis,.. the groups of puriri, and clumps of nikau, composed a sylvan landscape of great beauty. <i>(Dec 3, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

### tupake
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tupakihi](tot-tyr.md#tupakihi))

### tupaki
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tupakihi](tot-tyr.md#tupakihi))

### tupakihi
 
 <i>n.</i> [wine from the plant] (See [also tupakihi (juice): see tupakihi wine](tot-tyr.md#tupakihi):-see-tupakihi-wine),[tutu wine](tot-tyr.md#tutu-wine))

>  Otene & his people came down to see us, bringing large stores of tupakihi which I quaffed with pleasure*, also mango ... Rogan fastidious about smell sent them to a distance to eat it. [fn.] The old women whose peculiar duty it is to squeeze the tupakihi berries never wash their hands by any chance. The only approach to it is in squeezing the tupakihi juice ... <i>(Dec 30, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/673,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  Went on to Kapoai ... had dinner - well supplied with pipis but alas no tupakihi ... saw another noteworthy sight ...[sic] a young karoro just out of the nest, running along in a curious human way all by himself. <i>(Jan 21, 1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/680</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]



 
 <i>n.</i> tutu [ORIGIN: Maori]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a native plant, tutu of the Coriaria spp. [plant]

>  In some districts, where tupakihi familiarly called 'toot' is plentiful, the young growth of this poisonous plant springing up after a burn may account for some deaths. <i>(Aug 1888,NZ Farmer,296</i>)

>  In January the berries of the kotukutuku, poroporo, kaiwiria, puriri, mangaio, and tupakihi, constitute its ordinary bill of fare. <i>(Jul 2, 1927,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tupakihi, n.</i> i.q. <i>Tutu</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,482</i>)

>  spg tupaki ... the plants around were ... makomako ..., tupaki <i>Coriaria ruscifolia</i>), and pukapuka. <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/146</i>)

>  The name `tutu' is applied to three distinct species of the ... natural order<i> Coriariae: Coriaria ruscifolia</i>, L. (<i>C. sarmentosa</i>, Forst., <i>C. arborea</i> and <i>C. tutu</i>, Lindsay, tutu, pohou, and tupakihi of the Maori), is commonly known as the tree-toot ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/347</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> In case ... of poisoning by having swallowed the seeds of the <i>tutu</i> or <i>tupakihi</i> plant (Coriaria ruscifolia) the smoke remedy was resorted to ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,18</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Coriaria ruscifolia, Linn.</i>] <i>Tutu; Tupakihi. </i>... The poisonous principle appears to be a glucoside, to which the name `tutin' has been applied. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,106</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Coriaria sarmentosa (The Shrubby Tutu) </i>... Maori name: <i>Tupakihi</i> or <i>Tutu</i> (usually pronounced <i>Toot<i/>). <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,226,1940, p. 239 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The land was now overgrown with firn [sic] and <i>tupakihi</i> bushes. <i>(Oct 31, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,339,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> ... (the fruit of the Kohutuhutu, <i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>) ... the juice of the Tupakihi (<i>Coriaria sarmentosa</i>), a berry similar in taste to that of the elder, whose leaves, branches and seed, are highly poisonous; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),111</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD & WARD</b> Tutu or Tuparki ... <i>(1837,British Colonies,324</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> On emerging from Pamaki [a's-acute], we ascended another plain of some miles in circumference, covered with fern and bush, above which the tupakihi [a-acute], or native elder-berry, occasionally shewed its bending stem, yielding to the weight of the fruit, which hung in purple clusters. ... The natives, on perceiving me make use of this berry, warned me not to swallow any of the seeds, as the doing so would make me <i>ourangi</i>, drunk, or mad. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/134 The flexible tu pahihi [prob ?typo see 288] (elder berry) with its pensile clusters of ripe fruit ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/288 The <i>korai, koutu utu, miro,</i> and <i>putuhutu</i>, are wild forest fruits, prized only by the elder natives. The <i>tu pakihi</i> (Coriaria sarmentosa), or elder-berry, is a very pleasing fruit. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/110</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEW</b> Tupakihi, (Coriaria) a shrub indicating the richest soil. <i>(1840,Reports and Essays,12</i>)

>  <b>WADE</b> The Tupakihi ... produces ... berries, the expressed unfermented juice of which is called tutu. <i>(1841,Journey in NZ,57</i>)

>  <b>MAUNSELL</b> <i>tutu</i> is the tupakihi of the Ngapuhi. <i>(1842,Grammar of the NZ Language</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Its carved slopes and ... are softened with fern and flax and <i>tupakihi</i> bushes. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,163</i>)

>  ... tupake that bane of New Zealand may be found here. <i>(Apr 16, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,351,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> From the <i>Tupaki</i> berry the natives make a wine, which in taste is exactly similar to that of the elder. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,45</i>)

>  `About one-half of the district ... is covered with fern and various shrubs, chiefly the Tupaki, ... <i>(1843,Report of Auckland Agricultural Society,20</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 'They sometimes press out the juice from the drupes of the tupakihi (Coriaria sarmentosa), which is called tutu, and which they drink unfermented. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/50</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> This was the season when the fruit of the `tutu' or `tupakihi', as it is called in the North Island, was ripe. <i>Ibid.</i> Jan 9 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 195-196 These plains are called `pakihi,' a word used in the North Island to signify dried up. So that `tupakihi,' the name by which the `tutu' is there known, appears to be a contraction of `tutupakihi,' or `tutu' of the [196] plains, which are well called `pakihi,' being for the most part arid. <i>((8/0/1851),Southern Districts of NZ,191</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 8 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 191]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Tupakihi Tree [Tutu], troublesome, yet [a] useful one, abounds all are the Island, ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,107,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [fn] tupakihi, called tutu in the south [i.e. in the Wellington district]. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Coriaria <i>ruscifolia</i>, Linn. ... Nat. name, `Tupa-kihi', <i>Colenso</i>. `Tutu' and `Puhou' of Southern Island, <i>Lyall. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 45</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tupa-kihi, <i>Col[enso]. Coriaria ruscifolia. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> The too-well-known `Toot' of the Otago farmer; the `Wine-berry shrub' of the early settlers of the Bay of Islands, (according to Dr. Bennett [`Wanderings in Australia'] and the `Treasury of Botany'); the `Tutu,' `Tupakihi,' and `Puhou' of the North Island Maoris; the `Taweku' of the Waikatos; the `Tupake' [a-macron, e-acute] of Coromandel district; the `Tua-tutu' of Otago, (Hooker). ... [`Tutu'] appears also to be a generic or general [term] ... <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,85</i>)

>  Tupakihi. Coriaria ruscifolia, Linn. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/40</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... we walked along the native footpath, [105] the high fern and <i>tupakihi </i>proclaiming the richness of the soil. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/6/104-5</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> [tupaki = berry] ... Then a store/ Of jellies ... pressed/ ... [from]/ Rich clusters of <i>tupaki,</i> luscious sweet;/ <i>Ibid.</i> 1/302 [tupaki] Tutu or Tupaki .....<i> O</i>. Coriariae; <i>G</i>. Coriaria; <i>S</i>. C. ruscifolia. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/184</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Coriaria rusifolia,</i> Linn. Tupakihi, Tree Tutu.--A perennial shrub 10-18 fee t high; trunk 6-8 inches in diameter. The seeds and leaves contain a poisonous alkaloid. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,131</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Tupkakihi [Maori name] .. Tutu [Settlers' name] .. Coriaria ruscifolia ..Shrub <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,178</i>)

>  CATTLE POISONING BY TUPAKIHI. .. Tupakihi does not generally affect them unless they either drink after eating, or it rains, or in close damp weather, or they eat much of it on an empty belly. <i>(Nov 4, 1874,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Coriaria Ruscifolia. Or as it is generally called, Tutu or Tupakihi, affects cattle as well as sheep, and is even much more dangerous to them. <i>(Apr 3, 1890,Otago Witness,39</i>)

>  These tracks twist in and out all over this lonely country, through the high fern and the glossy green tupakihi with its black clusters of elderberry-like fruit, and the thickets of manuka that in springtime are showered with delicate white blossom till they look as if a snowfall had powdered the face of the country, and diffuse an aromatic fragrance that is the most insistent of the grateful odours of the bush. <i>(Jul 9, 1910,Evening Post,10</i>)

### tupakihi wine
 
 <i>n.</i> = tutu wine

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> Roberts and myself bathed in the river, made Tupake* wine, ... [fn]*(<i>NOTE</i>) A blue berry with poisonous seeds - whose juice however is wholesome, and delicious in flavour. <i>(1864,Memoranda,75</i>)

### tupapaku
 
 <i>n.</i> a corpse [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>Ihimaera ed.</b> He looked so thin and pallid, his cheeks gaunt and sunken... I tried hard not to think of tupapaku - dead bodies - when I looked at him. <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,112</i>) [NOTES: (Kingi McKinnon)]

>  They were determined to take his tupapaku back home with them. <i>(1955,Mana,No. 10 : 11</i>)

### tupara
 
 <i>n.</i> a double-barrelled gun [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>BELICH</b> Men with tupara were ordered to reserve their second barrel. <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,169</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Maori corruption of `two-barrel.' Compare the aboriginal word Whilpra</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,482</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> He cleaned his `tupara' (double-barrelled gun), but never a word to me! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,152</i>)

>  Led by a chainman ... with his slasher, and Wirihana with his tupara - a bushmanlike figure was the Boss, for he cannily packed his trousers in his swag and travelled bare-legged, Maori fashion ... <i>(Apr 10, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> He ... only surrendered ... when his last home-made cartridge for his <i>tupara</i> had gone. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,117</i>)

>  The Maori word for a gun in the early days in New Zealand - `two-barrel'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),434</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>*tupara</i>: double-barrelled gun. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,195</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> TUPARA Double-barrelled shotgun. <i>Tupara</i> is Maori pronunciation of two-barrel. <i>(1967,Evil Day,382</i>)

>  <b>BELICH</b> <i>tupara</i> double-barrelled gun <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> The birds naturally prefer districts where they are not likely to be molested by the <i>pakeha</i> and his <i>tupara</i>. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,315</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> My only hope is in a handy way (to give them [sc. Maori] their due) which they have with a tupara. <i>(1959,From New Plymouth to New Plymouth,98</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from CARRINGTON 1843]

>  Bates.. demanded that the people should recover his valuable tupera (doublebarrel) from the thief. <i>(Nov 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,18</i>)

>  He was an unborn participant in the siege of Orakau, his mother.. fighting in the earthworks alongside her husband, beating off the pakeha line regiments and colonial riflemen with rifle and tupara. <i>(Sep 7, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,94</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> On arriving I was requested to fire my two Purra [tupara] as the Natives call a double barreled Gun, in honour of the (Mattie Noue) [<i>mate nui</i>] dead. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),42,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/109</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> Those Ngatiwaki are getting too many muskets - ... they have got besides two <i>tupara. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 236 <i>Tupara</i> - A double gun; an article, in the old times, valued by the natives above all other earthly riches. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(i) 14</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Little wonder that Waipeha declared `pigeons we should have', but a little hitch did crop up nevertheless, for all the <i>tuperas</i>, double-barrel guns [fn: Maori pronunciation of two-barrelled] were at Mangare, and there was only one old `brown-bess' forthcoming, and a few ball cartridges! <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/7/115</i>)

>  She shuddered when telling of the old tales of Maori rumours that floated down the Hutt of great war parties, with tupara, tomahawk and mere creeping stealthily to murder the newly arrived in their sleep... <i>(Aug 2001,NZ Memories,No. 31/47</i>) [NOTES: 10 September 1915 in NZ Memories No. 31/47]

>  It was a <i>tupara</i> (double barrel). <i>(Oct 18, 1873,Otago Witness,5</i>)

### tupara-gun
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> In fact, what with the land-trouble and too many tupara-guns, Queen Victoria's Maori nurslings were having the time of their lives; ... <i>(1938,Promenade,124</i>)

### tupare
 
 <i>n.</i> leatherwood

>  The tupare and turpentine scrub topping the low cliffs have been wind-sculpted into mushroom shapes. <i>(Jan 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> The familiar mutton-bird scrub, tupare, covers much of the ground and there is a handsome relative, also called tupare, which grows only on a few of the islands in [48] Foveaux Strait and on the Snares. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,47</i>)

>  <b>WEBSTER</b> The Maori call this shrub tupare, and for Tuhoe its significance is that it [193] grows on the sacred mountain. The leaves were much prized as pare or head wreaths ... the main route winds among boulders ... to disappear into thickets of taupare.[sic] <i>(1979,Rua,192</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Olearia colensoi</i> LEATHERWOOD ... Tuupare ...Stiffly branching shrub or small tree. Leaves leathery ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,98</i>)

>  Here, a small zone of sub-alpine scrub includes tupari and pink pine. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,65</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tupari, <<i>Hector. Olearia operina</i> and <i>O. Lyallii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  The principal are, Tupari (<i>Olearia operina</i>). <.I>Ibid.</i> 43 Tupari. Olearia operina, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/35</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  [The Snares are] almost entirely covered with scrub and trees of stunted growth, the Tupari, Akeake, and Kokomuka. Of M'Quarrie cabbage there is abundance, and of fine growth, some of the leaves measuring two feet in diameter.Patches clear of scrub are clothed with Lutaki tussock. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/176</i>) [NOTES: (Hector Outlying Islands)]

>  The traveller who visits Stewart Island in December or January will have his attention arrested by the blaze of crimson presented by the rata (<i>Metrosideros lucida</i>), often flecked with the pale racemes of the kamahi (<i>Weinmannia racemosa</i>), the deep green leaves of the puheretaiko and the tupari (<i>Senecio rotundifolius</i> and <i>Olearia colensoi</i>), and the grass-like leaves of the inaka (<i>Drachophyllum longifolium</i>), all of which form a fringe of greater or less breadth at the water's edge, and (except the puheretaiko) often expand into large masses ascending to the crests of the hills. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/216</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>O[learia] Colensoi, Hook.f</i>. ... [mainly on mountains] STEWART ISLAND: Abundant from sea-level ... <i>Tupari. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,282</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The TUPARI (<i>O[learia] colensoi</i>) is an allied plant forming extensive areas of subalpine and coastal scrub ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,208,1929 ed.</i>)

### tupari
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tupare](tot-tyr.md#tupare))

>  Tupari (<i>Blechnum procerum</i>) is usually quite as abundant as Paesia <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/119B</i>)

### tuparki
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tupakihi](tot-tyr.md#tupakihi))

### tupeia
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The staminate flowers of Tupeia [Tupeia spp.] [itself] are found in panicles, in which the individual blossoms are not only more numerous, but larger in size than those on the pistillate panicles. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,145,1940 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tupeia</i> [s] <i>Tupeia antarctica</i> (Forst.f.) Cham. & Schl. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,80</i>)

### tupeka
 
 <i>n.</i> tobacco

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> ... gave Waharoa the box ... and a pound of <i>tupeka</i> from Titore. <i>(Mar 11, 1835,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,421,1961 ed.</i>)

### tupenu
 
 <i>n.</i> a wrap-around long skirt similar to a lava-lava, worn by Tongan men on formal occasions [ORIGIN: Tongan]

>  They break all the "fobby" stereotypes of what Pacific people in this country are supposed to be like. They don't wear mumus or tupenu to their ankles. <i>(2005,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.victoria.ac.nz/modernletters/bnzp/2005/mila-note]

### tupoupou
 
 <i>n.</i> a porpoise

>  ... the most important [Chatham Island] edible sea-birds: AS WRITTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLISH hopo ho[macron]p' albatross hakoakoa ha[macron]ko[macron]k' mutton bird taiko tchai[macron]k' pied shear water tupoupou tchu[macron]poup' pied blue billie reoreo ro[macron]ro[macron] Mother Carys chicken turiwati tchu[macron]riwat' dottrel [sic] kuaka ku[macron]k' snipe <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 358</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358]



 
 <i>n.</i> a bird, the pied blue billy [only as porpoise/dolphin in Williams, Ryan (TD)]

### tupping block
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The 5,000 ewes were all out on their tupping blocks. <i>(1966,Boot, The,47</i>)

### tupping country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that is suitable for use in mating ewes and rams.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The Riverdale flats, opposite Grasmere, include some extensive areas of swamp and sweet, healthy, matagouri flats and although they lie on the shady side of the river, are wonderful lambing and tupping country. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,138</i>)

### tupping crayon
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a chalk or raddle used to indicate the occurrence of tupping in sheep.

>  WOOLPRO Glossary of Wool Terms 4 Brand - wool on the sheep's back, marked with a temporary colouring agent eg tupping crayon or raddle. <i>(2001</i>)

### tupping paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock where rams are released to join ewes for the tupping season.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Granted that it had been possible to plan a good range of tupping paddocks, the smaller the ewe mobs and consequently the fewer the rams needed in each paddock, the better for at least the important first three weeks of mating. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,49</i>)

### tupping weight
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a weight of ewes at the time of conception.

>  <b>MACMILLAN</b> The tupping weight of ewes affects their lambing percentage. Ewes weighing 45 kg liveweight at tupping may drop 80% lambs whereas if they weigh 60 kg at tupping they should drop 120% or more. <i>(1980,Land Development in the Chatham Islands,13</i>)

### tuppler
 
 <i>n.</i> a large metal wheel which revolves and feeds coal into a chute

>  Emptying the boxes is the `tuppler', a large metal wheel which revolves and feeds the coal into a chute. <i>(1971,NAC Airline Review,58/21</i>)

### tupuku
 
 <i>n.</i> butterfish

>  The hiwihiwi (<i>Chironemus fergussoni</i>), tupuku, ngutere, tangahangaha, and koeaea (<i>Coridodax pullus</i> Forster) (butterfish), are amongst those caught <i>(1926,TrNZI,58/646</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

### tupuna
 
 <i>n.</i> a grandparent, ancestor [ORIGIN: Maori, -] (See [also tipuna](ten-tot.md#tipuna))

>  He thought he'd offended the tupuna, that they were coming back to get him. <i>(Aug 14, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  Rita will be buried with her tupunas at Waimahana Bay Cemetery, Northland <i>(Dec 13, 2005,NZ Herald,C7</i>)

>  Third, our tupuna never envisaged the Treaty ending on a particular date and we have no right to abandon their dream in such a cavalier fashion. <i>(Sep 15, 2005,NZ Herald,A18</i>)

>  Any person identified who descends from Tupuna from either Ngati Koata, Ngati Rarua, Ngati Tama and Te Atiawa who were living in the northern South Island between 1820 and 1840 are invited to attend. <i>(Oct 1, 2005,Dominion Post,C13</i>)

>  At this they exclaimed in greater wonder and admiration, for the Maoris are staunch ancestor worshippers, and if a man's "tupuna" (as they call ancestors) have not been gentle folk, he is secretly despised by the Natives of New Zealand. <i>(Sep 28, 1929,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  This 'tapu' was imposed in consequence of the European traveller of the name of Bidwill having gone to the top without permission, which had caused great vexation, as the mountain it held in traditional veneration, and is much dreaded by the Natives, being, as they tell you, the 'backbone of their tupuna,' or great ancestor, and having a white head, like their present chieftain. <i>(Feb 15, 1932,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <i>English New Zeland South-sea Islands</i> ... ... ... Grand Father Toubouna Toubouna ... ... ... [Number] 2 Rua Rua. <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/287,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> `I asked his permission to ascend Tonga Riro ... But he steadily refused, saying, `I would do anything else to show you my love and friendship, but you must not ascend my tepuna, or ancestor.'' <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/113</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Our guide pointed out a singular shaped stone ... [and] informed me [it] was one of their Tupuna. <i>(May 28, 1849,Journal,6/103</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> On a small island in this lake (Rotoiti) there is a fine turkey cock who is called by the name of a tupuna. When anyone goes past the spot and calls out to him he answers. I think he is viewed as an object of reverence. <i>(Mar 13, 1851,Journal,7/78</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> `Tupuna, to stand, to spring; an ancestor; hence Tu-pu, to grow.' <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,202</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> [Ngaruawahia] has been surveyed ... and a large Town laid out in one acre allotments, ... the streets being named after Maori tupunas (ancestors) and living chiefs. <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,15</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> `One evening a smart, handsome lad came to tell me his <i>tupuna</i> was dying ... The tribe were ke poto or assembled to the last man about the dying chief.' <i>Ibid.</i> 214 ... a smart handsome lad, of about twelve years of age, came to tell me that his <i>tupuna</i> was dying, ... and had sent for me to see him before he died. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,196</i>)

>  ... the genealogical stick by which `King Tawhiao' traces back his ancestry to the earliest of <i>tupunas ... </i> <i>(Jan 1884,NZ Journal of Science,58</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Maori word, meaning ancestor, progenitor, male or female. Often used in the Land Courts in the question: `Who are your tupuna?' <i>(1898,Austral English,482</i>)

>  <i>tupuna n.</i> an ancestor; a progenitor, male or female. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

>  ANCESTOR. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <i>tupuna n.</i> Maori word for ancestor, progenitor [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>tupuna n.</i>. Maori word for ancestor, progenitor [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  Often people are whakama about gout or they blame themselves or their tupuna for it. <i>(2009,Mana,87/43</i>)

>  This stream is called Rangoriri, after an old native tupuna, who fell in and was scalded to death. <i>(Mar 19, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  It was while he was working at the radio station that Mr Flavell discovered he needed to practise what he preached, and began using his tupuna (ancestral) name -- Te Ururoa... "We are encouraging people to reclaim their tupuna names for the next generation... <i>(Sep 26, 2005,NZ Herald,A6</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  Each of the representatives of the human figure bears the name of some tupuna, or ancestor, and the whole is actually a carved history. <i>(Apr 6, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### tupuru
 
 <i>n.</i> sandals

>  Herries Beattie describes a different method: "This tussock was placed in the paraerae (sandal) and the foot inserted. Then the tussock was brought up the leg and tied above the ankle. This protection was called puru and the sandals were then known as tupuru." Patiti was also regarded as "a fine thing to put in the paraerae to keep the feet warm and if one was wading it was warmer with patiti around the feet than without it." <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40</i>)

### tuputupu
 
 <i>n.</i> a mangrove

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Manawa and Tuputupu,</i> two varieties of the well-known mangrove, cover the mud banks of the rivers and creeks of the country in which they flourish. <i>(1983,New Zealand  (Polack),2/398</i>)

### turangawaewae
 
 <i>n.</i> the ability, conferred by belonging to a home-turf, to stand on one's own two feet; a strong homeland-based identity. [Maori turanga[u-macron] a standing + waewae foot]

>  Why do they do it? Because Springer gave them a place to be, their very own turangawaewae (spiritual home). <i>(Jun 1, 1999,Dominion,11</i>)

>  I remember being taken to Ruapuke, our turangawaewae, for the first time with my parents. <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 68</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> It is my turangawaewae-ngakau, the standing-place of my heart. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,64</i>)

>  The possum and I were not going to pass the night in the same room... This was his turangawaewae, the place where he stood. <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 60/72</i>)

>  For the French, and almost all European winegrowers for that matter, wine is about the land, about a piece of dirt that is turangawaewae. And you can no more separate the land from the wine than you can call a Greek a Turk, or vice versa, which is why Europe is riddled with those convoluted wine regulations that read like a dialogue for a frustrated Homer Simpson - DO, DOG, AoC, VDQS, QmP, QbA! What all these mean is turangawaewae, the place that wine calls home, and there is only one. <i>(Aug 21, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  <i>Kalik</i>, the final book in Jack Lasenby's "Travellers Quartet", is a satisfying end to the story of Ish and his quest for turangawaewae. <i>((9/0/2002),Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  <i>Kalik</i>, the final book in Jack Lasenby's "Travellers Quartet", is a satisfying end to the story of Ish and his quest for turangawaewae. <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  Happy because he's bought some land in Rotorua, his turangawaewae after 20 years in Devonport. <i>(Sep 19, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),21</i>)

>  "You gotta honour your Pommie whakapapa, for sure, but home isn't [England], eh. Your turangawaewae is in the Pacific, where there's sand and kauri and gorse. That's where your family is, eh. Your real whanau. You can't change that, the sand's already on your feet." <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  The site - the Observatory Reserve at Kelburn - is highly significant: it is a taonga, a turangawaewae, to be treasured and preserved - none but a fool or a knave would willingly surrender it! <i>(Nov 1993,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,102/66</i>)

>  I feel like a Wellington girl. I jokingly call it my turangawaewae - my spiritual home. <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Dominion Post TV Week,3</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> These acres were his [a Maori farmer's] [13] turangawaewae; the only heirloom of consequence his family ever had - <i>(1969,Green River,12</i>)

>  <i>(Feb 17, 1982,Dominion,9</i>) [NOTES: see Incorporations]

>  Although economic factors have helped to scatter the Maori people from their homelands, the majority of them see themselves as belonging to a tribe whose turangawaewae (place for the feet to stand) and whose ahi-ka (burning fire) are located at specific regions. <i>(1984,Te Maori,31</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Turangawaewae - this term refers to the ability to stand on one's own two feet because one belongs (see papakainga) to a piece of land which reflects one's history - one's identity. It combines therefore, the feeling of belonging and the idea of the place itself. If one loses one's papakainga, one has no turangawaewae. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,23</i>)

>  I put my $16,000 into a 36-perch piece of land behind Hekerua Bay ... It is my <i>turangawaewae</i> and I'll pass it on to my successors. <i>(May 1987,Metro,70</i>) [NOTES: Roger `Auckland Life']

>  <b>Maclean</b> Each group [i.e. tramping club] had its own turangawaewae in the hills and its own chiefly figures. <i>(1994,Tararua Story,167</i>) [NOTES: check quote]

### turawera
 
 <i>n.</i> a plant

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Turawera, <i>Col[enso]. Pteris tremula. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

### turbot
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>HECTOR</b> Occasionally a large flat fish, said to resemble the Patiki, has been brought to market and sold as Turbot, but, from native information, it is probable that several distinct species yet remain to be discovered by the Pakeha fishermen. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,177</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> Occasionally a large flat fish, said to resemble the Patiki, has been brought to market and sold as Turbot, but, from native information, it is probable that several distinct species yet remain to be discovered by the Pakeha fishermen. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,177</i>)

>  `Turbot - a fish not uncommon in the Dunedin market, where it goes by the name of `lemon-sole.'' <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/215</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> TURBOT (<i>Ammotretis Guntheri</i>). ... [28] In the <i>Transactions of the New Zealand Institute</i> vol. viii., p.215, Captain Hutton writes thus of the <i>Ammotretis rostratus:</i> `A fish not uncommon in the Dunedin market, where it goes by the name of `lemon sole,' agrees so well with Dr. Gunther's description of <i>A. rostratus</i>, from Tasmania, that I have no hesitation in considering it that species.' <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,26</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>The name is given to a New Zealand fish, called also Lemon-Sole</i> (q.v.) or <i>Yellow-belly </i>(q.v.), <i>Ammotretis guntheri. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,482</i>)

>  [Flatfish submitted for opinion included] (1.) The flounder, or patiki, wrongly named `plaice,' about one-tenth adult size. (2) The turbot sole, wrongly [559] named `brill,' about one-fortieth its adult size: ... (3.) The lemon sole, wrongly named `flounder,' ... (4.) The New Zealand sole ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/558</i>)

>  Brill - Turbot - <i>Ammotretis guntheri.</i> <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/197</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  Referring to the liberation of turbot, ... <i>(1919,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,2/279</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  AMMOTRETIS NUDIPINNIS Waite. <i>Turbot.</i> A rare fish in each of the four chief towns. I have been informed that it is more common around Invercargill and Stewart Island. ... Distribution: South Island coasts; examples have been taken as far north as Whangarei. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/122</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> These two are the Turbot <i>(Colistium nudipinnis)</i> with broadly blotched upper surface, and the Brill <i>(C. guntheri)</i> (Pl. II. fig. 30) which is marked with spots forming lines along the body. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,202</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 202]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Colistium nudipinnis </i>Waite. TURBOT. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,27,1971 ed.</i>)

>  "Waiter," he called, "fetch me a nice piece of boiled turbot." <i>(May 27, 1939,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  <i>Colistium (Ammotretis) nudipinnis</i>: Turbot. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/407</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> Thirteen species of flat-fishes are known from New Zealand waters, including a large Turbot, <i>Colistium nudipinnis</i>, which is comparatively rare, and the Megrim, <i>Caulopsetta scapha</i>, which is not popular as a food fish as it is usually very thin. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,67</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Turbot <i>Colistium nudipinnis</i>(Waite) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,404</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 404]

>  <b>WILSON</b> FLATFISH ... Inhabitants of sandy or muddy, shallow flats the flatfish are also occasionally taken in deep water. This is specially so of the fairly rare Turbot which grows to a considerable size and inhabits the deeper, southern waters. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,71</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> These much sought-after but relatively rare flatfish [flounder and sole] are generally found on sandy bottoms of shallow inshore areas. There are eight commercial species in New Zealand : the sand flounder, yellowbelly flounder, greenback flounder, black flounder, lemon sole, common sole, brill and turbot. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,73</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>TURBOT Colistium nudipinnis </i>... The turbot is the largest of the New Zealand flounders and individuals over 80cm in length have been recorded ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,311,1984 ed.</i>)

>  Closely related to the brill is the turbot, the largest New Zealand flatfish. It has a reddish-brown back with darker brown splotches and is found mainly on the west coasts of both the North and South Islands, although only about 20 tonnes is trawled annually... If it is any consolation, the turbot of northern waters is a slightly different species to ours, having its eyes on the other side of the head. <i>(Dec 8, 1982,Evening Post,37</i>)

>  [species name] turbot .. [scientific name] <i>Colistium nudipinnis</i>.. [other name(s)] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Turbot Colistium nudipinnis</i> Restricted to New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,145</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] turbot 2240-2, 2253, 2292-3, 2309 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  ON sale by the undersigned:- Salmon trouts, Fresh haddocks entire, Boiled turbot ... <i>(Sep 25, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,1</i>)

>  TO ARRIVE, Per Wellington, Coromandel, and Roman Empire .. Turbot, Sardines, 1/4's and 1/2's ... <i>(Jan 27, 1882,Wanganui Chronicle,1</i>)

>  Fresh Salmon, Herrings [sic], Mackerel, Haddock, Skate, Trout, Turbot, Mockturtle, Hotch-potch, packed in tins, which would be found to make most acceptable dishes at the Diggings. <i>(Apr 18, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  FECUNDITY OF FISH. - The Trout, weighing 1lb., carries 1,000 eggs .. the turbot, weighing 8lbs., contains 385,200 ... <i>(Mar 16, 1865,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [New Zealand turbot]

>  <i>Ammotretis nudipinnis </i>Waite. (<i>Ammotretis rostratus</i> Hutton.) This fine fish is named `New Zealand turbot' by Waite ... <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/232</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

 <i>n.</i> a male homosexual
