### turehu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The surviving Turehu became mountain dwellers, and the last known traces proved them to be mere phantoms with human voices. <i>(Sep 1, 1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,911</i>)

>  Tom.. says turehu can be found in these parts. "Turehu?" I say. "Goblins", he whispers. <i>(Aug 9, 1997,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  When the mist comes low on our maunga [mountain] it means the Turehu and Patupaiarehu, spiritual beings, have come to teach us our whakapapa and the practice of the taiaha. <i>(Sep 27, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  They were here from at least 3000BC, an enlightened and peaceable peo0ple who built cities and temples and tended the land before being wiped out by the incoming Maori - though their memory remains in tales of the Turehu (fair-skinned people) and patupaiarehe (bush fairies). <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  They were here from at least 3000BC, an enlightened and peaceable people who built cities and temples and tended the land before being wiped out by the incoming Maori - though their memory remains in tales of the Turehu (fair-skinned people) and patupaiarehe (bush fairies). <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  Fairies or forest elves are known as "heketoro" and "turehu." <i>(Oct 25, 1933,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  If there should be any claims they should be by turehu, the white-skinned people who were the original pre-Maori inhabitants of New Zealand, the white tangata whenua who lived here at least 500 years or more before Maori ever arrived. <i>(Jun 16, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Intelligent Maoris.. believe.. that on its upper slopes there lived a race of little red men, Turehu, parties of whom went down at night to fish in some of the bays near Port Jackson. <i>(Jun 15, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  In those good old times the interior was occupied, they say, by Turehu or Patupaiarehe, a race short in stature and of fair skin. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/32</i>)

>  <i>turehu</i>: fairy people. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>turehu:</i> fairy-folk. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,195</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ` ... Then the fairy won't forget to come.' `The turehu won't take to sugar,' ... `It's not turehu,' ... `It's patupaiareke where we come from. And they take to sugar....' <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,116</i>)

>  <b>GILDERDALE</b> turehu fairy, ghost <i>(1982,Sea Change,242</i>)

>  Sometimes Maori women have Turehu lovers whom they meet in the bush unknown to their people. <i>(Dec 20, 1894,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  The dread of Patupaiarehe (or Turehu, as they were sometimes called) no doubt hindered the natives from ascending the mountain, but it was surprising that the natural indomitable curiosity of some colonists did not urge them to the summit. <i>(Sep 1, 1888,Te Aroha News,3</i>)

### turepo
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Paratrophis banksii</i>, milk-tree

>  Portion of a batten for a whare, Kaho, made of Turepo, Hoheria populnea ribbon wood. <i>(Sep 22, 1874,Star, The,2</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> Occasionally a large flat fish, said to resemble the Patiki, has been brought to market and sold as Turbot, but, from native information, it is probable that several distinct species yet remain to be discovered by the Pakeha fishermen. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,177</i>) [NOTES: moved to turbot headword - wrongly in turepo]

>  [Native name] Turepo .. [Settlers' name] Milk-tree .. [Family] Articeae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  ... the `turepo' of the Maoris and the `milk-tree' of the settlers. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/498</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> ... we lived on the tender bleached ends of cabbage tree leaves, and the inner bark of the turipa tree, which is sweet and not unpalatable but not very satisfying. <i>(1898,Reminiscences and Experiences,21</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[aratrophis] heterophylla, Bl.Mus.</i> ... NORTH ISLAND: ... <i>Turepo; Milk-tree</i>. ... Abounding in milky sap, which is said to be palatable. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,632</i>)

>  In <i>Paratrophis Banksii</i> there is an example of a large-leaf form of the turepo, ot `milkwood' of the bushman. ... Growing in close proximity ... is <i>Sideroxylon costatum,</i> known to the Bay of Plenty Natives as `orewa', but given theless euphonious name of `tawaapu' by writers on the New Zealand flora. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/186</i>) [NOTES: (Sladden)]

>  ... a small tree ... about 15 feet high, with smooth cineraceous bark, solitary crimson fruit ... On cutting its bark, a profusion of thick, viscid milky juice gushed out. [fn] Presumably <i>Paratrophis heterophylla,</i> Turepo, milk-tree. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,37</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> THE MILK-TREE (TUREPO) The turepo (<i>Paratrophis microphylla</i>) trunk will slowly exude a creamy sap if it is cut or bruised. The sap was used as a substitute for milk by early colonists. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,84</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Those pioneers who were without dairy cattle made a kind of `milk' from the creamy sap of the turepo or milk tree (<i>Paratrophis microphylla</i>). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,20</i>)

>  TUREPO (Milk Tree)/ <i>Paratrophis microphylla</i> / Tree reaching 12m. Bark whitish grey with noticeable breathing pores. <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,14</i>)

>  For example, there have been no podocarps, rimu or matai or smaller species such as the intriguing Streblus heterophyllus, or Turepo, known to European settlers as the milk tree, because of the sweet milky sap they used in their tea. <i>(Aug 10, 2002,Dominion Post,F14</i>)

### turf
 
 <i>v.</i> throw out or away; discard

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He'd just got a letter from his girl friend. She was marrying a Marine. So you boys elected him to the Turf Club. Another good boy turfed by a girl back here. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,100</i>)

>  <i>to turf (something) out</i>: to throw it away <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  I'll take all the rubbish ...and .... I'll follow the old bitch back and turf it all over her floor <i>(Jun 1982,Landfall,142, 145</i>)

### turi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tuere](tot-tyr.md#tuere))

### turipa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [turepo](tot-tyr.md#turepo))

### turiwata
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tuturiwhatu](tot-tyr.md#tuturiwhatu))

### turiwati/turiwhati
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tuturiwhatu](tot-tyr.md#tuturiwhatu))

>  <b>Vol. IX No. 5 p. 358</b> ... the most important [Chatham Island] edible sea-birds: AS WRITTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLISH hopo ho[macron]p' albatross hakoakoa ha[macron]ko[macron]k' mutton bird taiko tchai[macron]k' pied shear water tupoupou tchu[macron]poup' pied blue billie reoreo ro[macron]ro[macron] Mother Carys chicken turiwati tchu[macron]riwat' dottrel [sic] kuaka ku[macron]k' snipe <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs</i>) [NOTES: >1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Thinornis ... novae zealandiae </i>... The shore plover is found on the Chathams and has no near relative left in New Zealand. ... Shore Plover. PURIWATA, KUKURUATU. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,79</i>)

### Turkey
 
 <i>n.</i> a Turkish soldier

>  <b>BAILEY & ROTH</b> Old Abdul under cover was as cunning as a rat;/ As yet we'd done no shootin' - saw nothin' to shoot at,/ Till a Turkey popped his head up; that head he ne'er withdrew,/ For a rifle pinged... <i>(1967,Shanties,115</i>) [NOTES: [ca1915-1918] in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 115]

### turkey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Button-pushers, or `turkeys' as they are known in CB jargon, hold down the buttons on their CB sets and play music or use abusive language. Their actions prevent other CB operators from using the frequency. <i>(Jul 20, 1987,Evening Post,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fine whetstone used by blade shearers.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> turkey: a fine whet-stone used by blade shearers, so named from the country whence it used to be imported. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,87</i>)

### turkey drive
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an annual muster of wild turkeys before Christmas.

>  <b>KEENE</b> The exciting time came with the turkey drives each year, just before the December steamer arrived. <i>(1978,Mimiwhangata Station,39</i>)

### turkey off
 
 <i>v.</i> to depart quickly, decamp

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Well, I was so stiff I nearly turkeyed off from the line, but I decided to wait. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Turkey off.</i> To decamp: New Zealanders': C. 20. <i>(1935,Dictionary of Slang,917</i>)

>  <b>RAWLINSON</b> Everyone thought he'd just turkeyed off. <i>(1938,Music in the Listening Place,15</i>)

>  18 % `to turkey off' (to depart). <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>turkey off</i> absent without leave; WWI. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)

>  The cold of recent nights/Has led me to believe/That winter, ere it turkeys off,/Has something up its sleeve <i>(1900,Star, The</i>) [NOTES: Year is incorrect.]

### turkeys in gumboots
 
 <i>phr.</i> a reference to a celebrated TV spoof Country Calendar item

>  It was a big week for the turkeys-in-gumboots school of local television drama. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),77</i>)

### Turkish delight
 
 <i>n.</i> shellfire

>  <b>PILLING</b> We passed the French batteries serving out `Turkish delight' ... <i>(May 6, 1915,Anzac Memory,37,1933 ed.</i>)

### turn
 
 <i>n.</i> [AND: fuss]

>  `He takes girls off the streets and lives on them.' `I never go past without her giving me a turn about something or other,' the witness explained to the Bench. Once Mrs. Blood had said something particularly atrocious about him,... <i>(May 9, 1908,NZ Truth,151/6/1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [re sheep dog behaviour]

>  <i>Turn</i>.- When a dog heads or slews sheep, shepherds speak of his having done a good (etc) t. (C.) <i>(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] to slew sheep.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> turn: a dog turns his sheep when he slews them. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,87</i>)

### turn a seven
 
 <i>phr.</i> to turn a somersault

>  In the Hack Hurdles, Kimberley, of whom something was expected, turned a seven at the first hurdle Charlie Lett fortunately escaping without serious injury. <i>(May 23, 1890,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  At the first hurdle in the straight Commonwealth came a cropper, and at the second Katerina turned a seven. <i>(May 12, 1905,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  While working last week Prince Child turned a seven and his rider took away a pain in the shoulder just to remember the incident. <i>(Nov 19, 1921,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  No sooner had the competitors been dispatched on their long journey than Sir Donald and Hurakia set out to force the pace, while the supporters of Awahou had the mortification of seeing their fancy turn a seven at the second obstacle. <i>(Aug 18, 1915,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Even the best horsemen were not proof against their horses putting a foot in a grass-hidden hole and `turning a seven'. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,157</i>)

### turn custardy
 
 <i>phr.</i> variant of turn to custard q.v.

>  It's when National tries to get clever that things turn custardy. <i>(Oct 14, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

### turn dog on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to inform on f. dog `informer' 1848

>  It was a very contemptible thing, added the beak for Machray to `turn dog' on his mates. <i>(Apr 4, 1908,NZ Truth,146/5/3</i>)

>  Certainly it made Green see red, and turn dog on his old pal, Joe Winter, who had a much warmer time of it than his name would suggest. <i>(May 22, 1909,NZ Truth,205/7/2</i>)

>  Amongst other witnesses were George Muldon and his wife. The latter said that ... Shears was saying her husband had beaten him (Shears) for 18[pounds], and he would make it warm for Muldon if he went into the witness-box. ... In addition to inflaming the woman's mind, Constable Maher magnanimously offered to see what he could do to secure the husband work as compensation for loss of employment through turning `dog' on Shears. <i>(Mar 11, 1911,NZ Truth,298/7/1</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>phr.</i> to turn sour on

>  Latest political small talk is that Masterton's member, A.W. Hogg, the ill-fated ex-Minister, is turning dog on it. Next election will, it is said, see him wooing an Auckland electorate in the interests of Labor [sic]. <i>(Mar 5, 1910,NZ Truth,245/1/8</i>)

>  <b>MUNDY</b> ... Fred struggled along not doing so well, complaining that he had no help, and that the rest of the family had turned dog on him. <i>(1953,Days That Are No More,25</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> The bottle of `the red' he had tossed off in such high spirits had turned dog on him. He was on the verge of nausea. . <i>(1974,Predicament,207,1981 ed.</i>)

### turn in
 
 <i>v.</i> to give up esp. turn it in, give up a job

>  <b>HYDE</b> Timothy ... began to scribble. Birkett called, `Hey, what're you making, Sonny Boy?' `Writing to my girl.' `Christ. Fancy turning in a smoke for a bint. What's her name?' <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,129,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> turning it in: giving up the job. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,304</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary to 1964 Sargeson Collected Stories 1935-1963 304]



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to add stock to another group e.g. rams to ewes.

>  <b>PERRY</b> The ewes should be dagged and the tails trimmed before turning the rams in. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,73</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to add stock to a paddock or yard.

>  Customarily, broken-mouthed ewes are turned in to eat off the tops and the roots are then harrowed out in breaks <i>(May 20, 1933,NZ Journal of Agriculture,300</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HAFIELD</b> When the paddock has been fed off, dry stock are turned in, or the mower used, to clean up any roughage. <i>(1961,Agriculture,305</i>)

### turn it on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to provide liquor at a party etc.

>  The leader of the ruffians, when interviewed by "The Week's" Special Inquisitor, said that the whole trouble was due to shock, caused by the local hotel-keeper "turning it on." <i>(Sep 5, 1930,Star, The,5</i>)

>  AFTER "turning it on" for his men, Frederick Williamson, a well-known Christchurch builder, stayed in town for tea, and later had a few more spots, with the result that when he set out in his car he was in that state in which his driving was a danger both to himself and to other users of the road. <i>(Jul 31, 1930,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>turn it on</i> give a party or buy drinks; eg `What do you say we turn it on for the boys tonight?' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)

### turn it up
 
 <i>v.</i> to offer or allow sexual intercourse

>  <b>Gee</b> I was not seeking anything impossible, just more than 'leg over', something more than 'turning it up'. <i>(2009,Access Road,54</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>turn it up</i> make oneself, usually a female, available for sex; eg `Hey, Mort, reckon that sheila over there'd turn it up?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)

>  She said he tried to rape her behind the hedge and then in her flat. He said she told him she sometimes `turns it up' and he thought she was an easy mark. <i>(Oct 16, 1973,NZ Truth,18</i>)

### turn Nagasaki
 
 <i>phr.</i> become nasty

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>turn Nagasaki</i> become nasty as when atom bomb struck Nagasaki; eg `No need to turn Nagasaki on me, mate. I'm on your side.' Underworld. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)

### turn off
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to produce stock [usually for sale].

>  <b>NEWTON</b> In Hugh Ensor's time the station shore over 20,000 and turned off some fine lines of sheep. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,87</i>)

### turn on
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to move stock on to a crop or paddock.

>  Would you please let me know if pigs turned on lucerne will require any other food, and if so, what kind and how much? <i>(Oct 20, 1926,NZ Journal of Agriculture,287</i>)

### turn rusty
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Should he be foolish enough to do so, and gets [sic] the sack for doing it, he will get scant sympathy from his mates, but, should he on the other hand turn rusty and refuse to come up to expectations, they will say (to put it in polite language) that his mother and father were never united in the holy bonds of matrimony. <i>(Jan 7, 1911,NZ Truth,289/4/6</i>)

### turn sheep over
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to tip sheep on to their tails [ for inspection of udders or scrotum or to trim their hooves.]

>  <b>GORDON</b> Turned them all over on the board here [in the shearing shed, with hoof clippers] Of the 2,000 ewes we had, 1700 were infected the first time we turned them over. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,125</i>)

### turn tail
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] to 'spin' or back off and turn around when confronted by sheep. (See [spinning](spa-sys.md#spinning))

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Any pup that is inclined to turn tail will be sure to give up the practice provided that the trainer does not choose turns that are too difficult at first. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,4</i>)

>  A dog that under pressure from sheep and backs off a little and turns right around, is said to turn tail. Turning tail shows first a lack of confidence in a young dog, and in an older animal, a lack of strength. If a youngster is let away with it, 'turning tail' will just become a habit. <i>(1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 13</i>)

### turn to custard
 
 <i>phr.</i> turn out badly, go pear-shaped

>  "You can't take you [<i>sic</i>] eye off the ball for a minute. If you do it will turn to custard on you," he said. <i>(Feb 22, 2000,Evening Post,28</i>)

>  British Prime Minister Tony Blair's best-laid plans for the re-establishment of the London mayoralty are turning to custard, thanks to veteran leftie Ken Livingstone. <i>(Mar 13, 2000,Dominion,5</i>) [NOTES: This is the preamble to an article from AAP. It is not clear whether it came with the original article or was attached in NZ.]

>  <b>HUGHES</b> Expecting final confirmation of her releases from the clutches of the ordnance corps, she was stunned to see it all turn to custard. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,147</i>)

>  New Zealand had a shot at victory yesterday... However, it all turned to custard inside the hour. <i>(Mar 28, 2000,Evening Post,40</i>)

>  The 1970s. The decade when it all started to turn to custard. Britain decided its trading future lay with Europe, meaning New Zealand had to wean itself off the reassuring teat of the Mother Country and the economy reeled under the impact of Arab-instigated oil price shocks. <i>(Nov 30, 1999,Evening Post,Looking Back : 3</i>)

>  Retrace your steps before darkness falls and everything turns to custard - or arm yourself with a fancy torch. <i>(Aug 1996,Hot Rides,No. 8</i>)

>  Last year, New Zealand First's Tuariki John Delamere objected to her, a woman, being allowed to sit in the front row - although you'll notice he shuts his mouth now that his coalition leader, for whom he'll depend for a job if NZ First turns to custard, is a woman. <i>(Feb 13, 1998,Russell Brown's Hard News</i>)

>  The bottom line is that if the savings are worthwhile then have a go, and if it all turns to custard afterwards then go back to your local camera shop, let them laugh at you and say we told you so for a few minutes and then get it fixed. <i>(1997,Photo & Video International (NZ),Easter</i>)

>  Noel Bigwood is the man who gets involved when things turn to custard. <i>(Dec 29, 2005,Horowhenua-Kapiti Chronicle,1</i>)

>  Human nature being what it is, says Alison Laurie, senior lecturer at Victoria University's Women's Studies faculty, when it [sc. a relationship] all turns to custard "most people want to get the goodies for themselves." <i>(Feb 8, 2000,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Rather than run the risk to our egos of continually electing political teams that give us a five minute thrill but impoverish us in the process, why not appoint an expert to oversee them and then if it turns to custard we can blame him. <i>(Jul 19, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Best references to custard:... "It all turned to custard" - just about everyone interviewed on a local news show. <i>(Jan 2, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),69</i>)

>  Even positive(ish) measures, such as building new prisons, turn to custard. <i>(May 15, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  "Rigoletto has hate. Envy. Love. Revenge. Passion. Jealousy... I think it's a wonderfully profound piece of music." And what's more, says this fan. Just hearing it turns her to custard. <i>(Feb 19, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  <i>I</i> vote. <i>You</i> get elected. <i>You</i> (plural) govern. <i>They</i> (junior coalition partner) muscle in and govern, too. <i>We</i> wait for it to turn to custard. <i>It</i> custardises. <i>(Dec 18, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  You or I could go over and try to transplant the Team New Zealand ethic into a Swiss team led by one powerful individual and the whole thing might turn to custard. <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

>  After the Force deal turned to custard, Wood hinted other deal-makers were waiting in the wings. <i>(May 21, 2000,Sunday Star Times,E3</i>)

>  Although my brain has turned to custard and it's a rare occasion I'm able to complete a task or sentence in one attempt, it's these skills that allow me to know exactly how smart my kids really are. <i>(Jul 5, 2000,Contact (Wgtn),9</i>)

>  I wait with much interest to see the form and substance of underneath the froth that the Dairy Board is trying to get us to drink. Right now it all looks like it could turn to custard rather than be the top of a good cappuccino . <i>(1999,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.pandaspot.co.nz]

>  With that freedom, of course, comes responsibility, above all, responsibility for your actions, and not demanding that someone else take care of you should everything turn to custard. <i>(2000,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.freeradical.co.nz]

>  To make the most of the new law, parties will need to consider these issues when entering into contracts, and select from the menu of available rules. A little foresight may be amply repaid should commerce later turn to custard. <i>(Aug 7, 1997,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.chapmantripp.co.nz]

>  If the situation calms down, and air service to/from Fiji continues, we will proceed to Savusavu. If it continues to turn to custard in Fiji, we will divert to the Vava'u Group in Tonga and hang out there and play wait and see with Fiji. Don't feel bad for us, as Vava'u is not too bad of a place to "wait and see." <i>(May 30, 2000,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: Dave & Jaja Martin www.setsail.com]

>  When things started to turn to custard in the second half, a strong leader would have gathered his troops together to demand a monopoly of possession. Just like Buck Shelford would have done. <i>(Aug 25, 1999,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.scrum.com/super12/]

>  Lionel can't please both son and wife-to-be. Frank decides the clinic's fate. Caroline's suspicions turn to custard <i>(Jan 1999,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.shortlandst.com/current-jan1999]

>  27-01-99 On nights (1st years start nights after 6 weeks) you're it!!! (Including O&G/Paeds). Great experience but things can turn to custard. <i>(Jan 27, 1999,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.nzrda.org.nz/newsletter]

>  Last year, New Zealand First's John Delamere objected to her, a woman, being allowed to sit in the front row - although you'll notice he shuts his mouth now that his coalition leader, for whom he'll depend for a job if NZ First turns to custard, is a woman. <i>(Feb 13, 1998,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: Russell Brown's "Hard News" at www.nz.com/NZ/News/Hard News]

>  Tuck in : we're talking chilled Grand Marnier sabayon in a chocolate cup with seasonal fruit... The colours were a play of light and shadow, and I liked the way the sabayon turned to custard. <i>(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

### turn under
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to cultivate vegetation-covered land.

>  Using his Ferguson to the utmost he managed to turn the gorse under and apply lime and phosphate. <i>(Jun 10, 1958,NZ Dairy Exporter,4</i>)

### turn up
 
 <i>v.</i> lay off, stop (it), to give up, to give away

>  Pug Bob Turner has announced that he has turned up the game, and accordingly the match with Bob Byrant [sic] in Sydney is off. <i>(Nov 3, 1907,NZ Truth,128/3/1</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>turn it up!</i> give over; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Hey, turn it up and stop all those gloomy forecasts or you will find that I can go very hostile, and it's time we all took a tumble to ourselves and discussed something pleasant, say a little party. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

### turned on, be
 
 <i>v.</i> to smoke marijuana

>  Two of the youths suggested that they be `turned on' - smoke marijuana. ... Whitehead went to Patterson's home to rejoin the boys and girls, but requests to again be `turned on' were refused, he said. <i>(Jul 16, 1963,NZ Truth,38</i>)

### turner
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep or cattle dog that is not eager to work.

>  Turner - A 'Sunday passenger' or a 'tripehound' who does nothing but turn good tucker into something far less desirable. <i>(1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 13</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> turner: a dog. Why? Because he turns good food into sh--. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,104</i>)

>  <b>SWAN</b> Any dog, of any description, may be a cat cruncher, a general turner,....a dog may be a scrapper. <i>(1995,Chasin' Bacon,82</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Another of that ilk [lazy dog] would be the 'turner' which simply turns good food into something far less desirable. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,87</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a member of a harvesting-mill team who sets sheaves to be fed into the mill.

>  <b>ANGUS</b> Each mill employed ten or thirteen men, three breaking down the stack, then a 'turner' who set the sheaves the right way for the 'feeder,' the man who had the skilled task of cutting the binding and letting the sheaves slide down his arm into the mill at an even rate. <i>(1981,Aspiring Settlers,49</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> turner: he deposits each sheaf to the hand of the bandcutter, or the stacker - who very often works on hands and knees anyway. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,87</i>)

>  <b>KERSE</b> The usual gang for stack threshing [c1900] was 12 men. The boss kept steam up and attended to the mill adjustments and lubrication, three men worked in the bag hole, the bag-rammer, the bag-sower, and the bag-carrier; two men were on top of the mill, a band cutter and a man to turn the sheaves to the band cutter: two men were on the stack forking to the `turner', and then there was the chaffy, two men on the straw stack, a builder and a straw `walloper' and last of all, the water joey. Threshing out of the stock required an even larger gang, usually about three more men. <i>(1984,Knapdale Run, The,148</i>)

### turning tail
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the spinning or veering of a sheepdog when confronted by a sheep.

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Turning tail: This is the fault of a dog not turning in towards his sheep head-on. Instead he turns outwards from them ... <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,72</i>)

>  Turning tail shows first a lack of confidence in a young dog, and in an older animal, a lack of strength. If a youngster is let away with it, 'turning tail' will just become a habit. <i>(1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> of a sheepdog, turning away from the sheep in a complete circle; failing to `face up' to the sheep all the time (See [turn tail](tot-tyr.md#turn-tail))

>  <i>Turning tail:</i> Fault in a dog whereby it turns away from the sheep in a complete circle. It fails to `face up' to the sheep all the time. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,25</i>)

### turnip
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to turn lambs or sheep on to a paddock of turnips for fattening.

>  When it is necessary to turnip the sheep, the most economic method is to fence the turnips off into breaks, or small paddocks ... <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,44</i>)

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> .. necessary to get the ewe lambs down to the lowest country and turnip them for the winter. <i>(1969,Molesworth,112</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Alan recalls the trainload of 5,000 - 61 trucks - arriving at Ealing to be 'turnipped' on their place. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,35</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The hoggets should be wintered ... - if they can be fed on turnips ... so much the better. If they are turniped, then the shepherd must watch them carefully when they are once more put back on the tussocks, for they will usually fret for a time, ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,93</i>)

>  Above cattle have been turnipped since June. <i>(Sep 26, 1919,Bay of Plenty Times,1</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. they turniped their hoggets .. Furthermore, the paddocking and turniping of sheep inclined to make them more difficult to muster. <i>(1949,High Country Days,182</i>)

>  Store Cattle. - The business doing is small, and prices remain about the same as last week's quotations, only a few small lots changing hands for turnipping purposes at up to GBP5. <i>(Jun 10, 1887,Bruce Herald,2</i>)

### turnip break
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a section of a crop of turnips.

>  <b>STACEY</b> Some of the boys, who had been shifting a turnip break further across the flat, had seen the dray flying through the snow. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,123</i>)

### turnip fly
 
 <i>n.</i> [transferred use]

>  There are twenty-eight native species belonging to the genus <i>Odontria</i>, but all observers agree that the principal grass grub is <i>Odontria zealandica</i>. In the larval stage this is known as `grass grub,' `white grub,' or `curl grub,' and the adult stage is known as `grass grub beetle,' `brown beetle.' or `turnip fly.' <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/306A</i>)

>  Turnip-fly (<i>Odontria zealandica</i>): What is commonly referred to as `turnip-fly' is in reality the adult beetle <i>Odontria zealandica</i>, the common grass-grub beetle. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,24/129A</i>)

>  The turnip fly, haltica nemorum, is a colcopterous insect, having four wings, two shorter than the others; and is one of the most destructive species, as they feed on plants, when the insects are in their larvae, and in their perfect state also. <i>(Jun 18, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  The Hessian fly is reported to be doing damage in some parts of the Geraldine district, and the turnip fly is also in evidence. <i>(Jan 14, 1913,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Turnips also have come away well, and though the turnip fly is keeping them back in places the larger part of the crops are doing well. <i>(Jan 6, 1925,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  Every farmer knows the necessity for rapid growth in the early stages of the turnip plant, which is so subject to the "turnip fly" and other pests. <i>(Oct 20, 1909,Otago Witness,8</i>)

### turnip topper
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a weaned lamb that requires priming before slaughter.

>  Small store lambs, or turnip toppers, are in demand, making $25 to $33 ... <i>(Jun 15, 1994,NZ Farmer,51</i>)

### turnip weed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>turnip weed [s] Rapistrum rugosum</i> (L.) All. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,81</i>)

### turnip-scuffler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARPER</b> Before the days of turnip-scufflers seven or eight Chinese thinned and weeded the sweedes [sspg???] and turnips. <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,26</i>)

### turnipping
 
 <i>n.</i> [grazing of stock on turnips]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. they turniped their hoggets .. Furthermore, the paddocking and turniping of sheep inclined to make them more difficult to muster. <i>(1949,High Country Days,182</i>)

>  To add GBP12 10s a week to the cost of 500 sheep is no light matter, but owners who pay it consider they are lucky to find "turnipping" within reasonable access, since in some paddocks the roots are not worth eating. <i>(Sep 24, 1935,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  Forward cattle, suitable for turnipping, sold well. <i>(Sep 2, 1896,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  The demand for store wethers in good condition for turnipping is very keen. <i>(Apr 23, 1902,Otago Witness,44</i>)

### turnout
 
 <i>n.</i> mode of transport, such as a bike or side-car; as recorded in Frank S. Anthony's Me and Gus.

>  <i>Turnout</i>.- Horse complete with saddle and bridle, and often including breastplate and crupper. In great use among station hands, especially when dealing or swapping. (C.) <i>(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  I have the best turn-out in town now with my rubber tyres and newly-painted cart. <i>(May 20, 1924,Otago Witness,24</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a `show' (esp. a party), esp. in collocation A GOOD TURN-OUT [-->ca1949 Wellington She was a good turn-out last night. Plenty of beer.]

>  <b>McGILL</b> She was a good turn-out last night. Plenty of beer. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,115</i>)

### turnsies and fairsies
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Throw in the dairy cooperative philosophy of "turnsies and fairsies" and you have a sludgy mix that doesn't rise when the commercial heat goes on. [Andrea Fox] <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Dominion,13</i>)

### turnstone
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 67. STREPSILAS INTERPRES. L. Turnstone <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,26</i>)

>  68. <i>Strepsilas interpres</i>, L. Turnstone. - This cosmopolitan wader was, I believe, first observed between Ellesmere and Ninety-mile beach, Nov. 1870. <i>(1885,NZ Journal of Science,511</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>Strepsilas interpres</i> (Turnstone) Occurs among the rough beds of boulders on the Ahuna River, but never at any time numerous. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/220</i>)

>  The turnstone (<i>Arenaria interpres</i>) also breeds in the Northern Hemisphere ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,233/255</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> TURNSTONE <i>Arenaria interpres </i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,138</i>)

>  <i>171. Arenaria interpres</i> (Linnaeus, 1758) <i>Turnstone</i> Breeding holarctic region, migrating to southern hemisphere, including New Zealand ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,49,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Turnstone Arenaria interpres</i> (LINNAEUS, 1758) <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,201</i>)

### turnstyle shed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rounded design of milking shed.

>  The partnership milks 450 Jersey-Friesian cross cows through a 60-bail turnstyle shed built four years ago. Six years ago they milked about 240 cows through a 22-bail turnstyle shed. <i>(Mar 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,42</i>)

>  Farm buildings include a 50-bail turnstile milking shed ... [turnstile] <i>(Nov 5, 2002,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  There have been moves towards new turnstyle sheds in the Bay of Plenty... During the tour the group inspected 16-bail, 22-bail and 28-bail turnstyles. <i>(May 1972,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

### turntable pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a raced and rounded pen that allows sheep to be easily manoeuvred into a pot dip.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> The sloping surface which gives a slide in from the side of a bath, the tipping pen in which the floor moves from the horizontal to a steep angle, and the turntable pen, all have the common purpose of getting the sheep into the bath with a minimum of effort and with little risk of injury to the sheep. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,86</i>)

### turoa
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of red clover (Trifolium pratense).

>  <b>LEVY</b> For primary forest burn: Turoa or Hamua red clover. <i>(1970,Grasslands of NZ,183</i>)

>  Turoa - late-flowering, summer-active, diploid cultivar derived from the old Montgomery red clover. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,6</i>)

### turp
 
 <i>v.</i> to put turpentine on the arsehole anus of esp. a dog or cat as a presumed salutary punishment, or as a warning to stay off one's property [ca1940 Havelock]

### turpentine
 
 <i>n.</i> various shrubs or trees with aromatic leaves or resin (See [tarata](t-ten.md#tarata))

>  Turpentine - Pittosporum eugenioides. This is the largest of the mapau family; it sometimes attains a height of 40 feet, with a diameter of 24 inches. The bark is thin, and of a light colour; the leaves are silvery green, and the flowers pale yellow... The bark exudes a thick gum, and the juice of the leaves, which is somewhat similar, was formerly used by the Maoris as a perfume, but I fear it is too resinous for European tastes. <i>(1877,TrNZI,9 : 143</i>)

>  Turpentine (tarata) .. Pittosporum eugenioides .. Timber white and elastic; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,15</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> Hina hina abounded [near Owaka, Otago], also turpentine with its light foliage and sweetly scented flowers of palest green. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,34</i>)

>  <i>P. eugenioides</i> turpentine <i>(1877,TrNZI,9</i>)

>  It [<i>Mentha australis</i>]is called `turpentine' by the settlers. <i>(1882,TrNZI,14/383</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  50 Tarata (turpentine) slab. <i>Ibid.</i> 22 334 Turpentine slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

### turpentine bush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>DRACOPHYLLUM FILIFOLIUM</i> is one of the group of `grass trees' or needle-leaved species with few-flowered racemes ..., known generally as `inanga' in the north, `inaka' in the south, and `turpentine bush' to those who use the twigs to make a quick fire. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,114</i>)

### turpentine plant
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> The flowering koromiko (veronica) and the turpentine plant - the latter invaluable to the camper-out because it burns well even when green - are two of the most plentiful shrubs. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,2/66</i>)

### turpentine scrub
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Such scrub occurs more or less on all the high mountains; but on the drier ones - such as those of Central Otago, East Nelson, Marlborough, or the eastern portions of the Southern Alps - not forming a distinct belt, but only patches in places. Its tendency to burn is well exemplified by the local name `turpentine scrub'. <i>(1899,TrNZI,31/400</i>) [NOTES: (Cockayne)]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The verdure was eighteen inches high all over it broken here and there by rock outcrops and turpentine scrub. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,167,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> It is frequently made difficult by thick patches of hardy leatherwood and turpentine scrub (<i>Dracophyllum longifolium</i> and <i>D. uniflorum</i>) although respite is provided by ... the flowering of mountain heath. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,164</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Dracophyllum longifolium</i> INAKA ... Turpentine scrub ...Erect shrub or small tr<i>ee. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,80</i>)</i>

### turpentine shrub
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Dracophyllum uniflorum</i> turpentine-shrub <i>(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The</i>)

>  <i>turpentine shrub</i> [s] <i>Dracophyllum uniflorum</i> Hook.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,81</i>)

>  <b>MARK & ADAMS</b> <i>Dracophyllum uniflorum</i>Hook. f. TURRPENTINE SHRUB MATIPO, KARO <i>(1973,NZ Alpine Plants,114</i>)

### turpentine tree
 
 <i>n.</i> tarata [[1919 July 29] Ver.(WB). = Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W.Best, Otaki (And) <i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i> turpentine tree]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The turpentine tree is likewise very common in the neighbourhood of Port Nicholson. A considerable quantity of the gum which I obtained by tapping the trees, was very strong in flavour, and beautifully transparent. The cultivation and tending of this tree [44] might be easily learned by the natives, and the produce would, probably, prove of great importance. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),43</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i> turpentine tree <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i> turpentine tree <i>(1890,Descriptive & Priced List</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Turpentine-Tree, n.</i> The name is applied to many trees in Australia yielding a resin, but especially to the tree called <i>Tallow-Wood</i> (q.v.). . . . In New Zealand, it is also applied to the <i>Tarata.</i> See <i>Mapau. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,484</i>)

>  <b>NAIRN & SON</b> <i>Pittosporum eugenioides</i> turpentine tree <i>(1912,Catalogue (Nairn & Son)</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>turpentine tree n</i>. name for many trees yielding a resin, especially the tallow wood<i> (q.v.). </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21</i>)

>  <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i> is the `turpentine tree' of the Otago settlers, who plant it for hedges, as it bears clipping freely. <i>(1972,TrNZI,4/262</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk NZ Pittosporads)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> karo

>  <b>FEATON</b> Karo [Maori name] .. Turpentine tree [Settlers' name] .. Pittosporum crassifolium .. Tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,177</i>)

>  <i>P. crassifolium</i>, =KARO <i>(1872,TrNZI,4</i>) [NOTES: (?mainly East Coast, North Island)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> PITTOSPORUM CRASSIFOLIUM (<i>Banks & Sol</i>.) ... The `KARO'.- This species is common on the coastline throughout the Northern Island ... In common with some of the other Pittosporads, it is known to the settlers as one of the `Turpentine' trees; which are probably so called on account of the gluten, in which the seeds in the capsule are immersed, emitting a peculiar odour, when expressed. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,34</i>)

### turpentine wood
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Upon the western bank of the valley there are extensive patches - thousands of acres of it in all - of the turpentine wood, which, should it ever catch fire by any means, would make a conflagration such as petroleum only could excel. <i>(Feb 9, 1893,Otago Witness,46</i>)

>  Tent space and firewood were some what scarce [here in the Ruahine range] but dry turpentine wood or grass tree (<i>Dracophyllum filifolium</i>) provided the welcome cuppa ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,13</i>)

>  We are able to supply at Lowest Prices all Hardwoods, including Ironbark, Tallow Wood, Turpentine Wood, Blackbutt, etc., etc. <i>(Apr 4, 1914,Poverty Bay Herald,12</i>)

>  The turpentine wood was recommended as the best for resisting the teredo. <i>(Jan 28, 1905,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  Recently, while at Sydney, my attention was drawn to the so-called "turpentine" wood as a material for the construction of wharves, and other works exposed to the action of sea-water and the attacks of marine animals, which it is said to resist better than any other description of timber known in the colony. <i>(Jan 9, 1871,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Mr. Philips's amendment was agreed to, and the Secretary was instructed to get a log of the jarrah, one of the turpentine wood, &c., for the purpose of experimenting with to test their resistance to the worm here. <i>(Jan 10, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

### turutu
 
 <i>n.</i> a plant with sedge-like leaves, small flowers and large blue berries. [ORIGIN: Maori, <i>dianella intrmedia</i>; [Maori turutu[u1-macron]]]

>  An endeavour is made below to place these New Zealand native plants in the relative order of their importance, an arrangement which, in the main, is considered justified .. (11) Dianella intermedia, turutu <i>(Oct 10, 1934,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Turutu, <i>Lindsay. Dianella intermedia. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>D[ianella] intermedia, Endl. ... Turutu. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,715</i>)

>  <i>tu[u]rutu,</i> a plant (Dianella intermedia), sedge-like blades and very small flowers followed by surprisingly large beautiful blue berries which fall at once if picked. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>turutu [s] Dianella intermedia </i>Endl. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,81</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>DIANELLA NIGRA</i>. Turutu, blueberry, inkberry, or whistles thrives in lowland scrub, on clay banks, and along bush tracks. ... The reddish leaf-base leaves a yellow scar when broken ...; often it is folded into a tube so that skilful blowing can force air out in an ear-splitting whistle. Shining berries, ranging from palest grey to almost purple, or clean blue and white like old china, ripen in great numbers but they drop at a touch; ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,182</i>)

>  Of native shrubs, I noticed howhi, kowhai, ngutu-kaka, koromiko, and manuka, as well as the pretty turutu (<i>dianella intrmedia</i>) and cocksfoot grass, hawkweed, sowthistle, and wild geranium. <i>(Jul 18, 1895,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

### tusked weta
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Among other things, he fed kakapo on Maud Island, spent a fortnight alone on the tuatara sanctuary of Stephens Island, searched for tusked weta on the Alderman Islands, felled pine trees in Abel Tasman National Park, and monitored possum browse in Kahurangi National Park! <i>(Mar 2002,Thin Green Line, The,18</i>)

>  Scientists from the School of Biological Sciences.. have already investigated the rare tusked giant weta from one of the Mercury islands, used miniature radio transmitters to track giant weta in the wellington region, and discovered a new species in the southern alps. <i>(Nov 3, 1993,NewsVUW (Wellington),2(17):11</i>)

### tusker
 
 <i>n.</i> a wild pig; a large wild boar [[check OED(S)]]

>  <b>OVERTON</b> Some dogs become very cunning, and will not approach too near an old "tusker," but will just stand off and bark. <i>(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,99</i>)

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> Got a really big tusker all lined up for Bill here. ... The dogs have had him bailed three times now. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,33</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Sometimes a photograph would accompany the piece, showing a serious hunter, his broadly grinning dogs, and an expressionless tusker lying at their feet. <i>(1984,Razorback,11</i>)

>  <i>Tell Them I've Gone Pig-Hunting</i>.. is a catalogue of shot, stabbed, clubbed tuskers described with blokey energy, plus endearingly purple prose for "Nature's orchestra." <i>(Oct 26, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sp. in NZ pighunting] a wild boar. [bush pig, Irish merino, Captain Cooker DB also cross-refs to scoofler but no entry] (See [bush pig](bri-byo.md#bush-pig),[Irish merino](../I#irish-merino),[Captain Cooker](c-chi.md#captain-cooker))

>  .. from that point to the top of the mountain saddle was famous pig country, and many a good hunt for tuskers and their progeny was held there ... <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,43</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> They found a large and angry tusker, and bailing him up with the dogs, they made him angrier still. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,45</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> The boars were treated with great respect. When bailed up in a corner by dogs, a big tusker would attack men, horses and dogs ... <i>(1978,Mimiwhangata Station,60</i>)

>  <b>NGAWHIKA</b> With three ripped dogs and a 231 pound tusker to get out of a forest that was closed to hunting and heavily patrolled, the fun wasn't over for us yet. <i>(1996,Tell Them I've Gone Pighunting,108</i>)

### tussac
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tussock](tot-tyr.md#tussock))

>  A new species of grass, called tussac, the blades of which grew seven feet high, had also been discovered, which it was intended to bring over to this country for cultivation. <i>(Dec 6, 1843,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

### tussock
 
 <i>v.</i> to grub tussock or coarse herbage

>  <b>PRESTON</b> Four tramps arrived on 4 June and were put to 'tussocking' at 5s an acre. <i>(Jun 1871,Diary,7</i>) [NOTES: moved to tussocking noun]

>  Few - especially runholders and others having a practical interest in those and similar improvements, and desiring to participate in the same - but must have felt that a great desideratum in Southland was the possession of some means by which the immense benefits above referred to might be rendered possible to be shared in by others without the large outlay hitherto entailed by the necessary expenses of tussocking, ploughing, &c., required previous to sowing the English grass; which expense on a large estate, was quite possible to those possessing the extensive capital which the company in question does, but just as likely to be a serious consideration to private individuals. <i>(Oct 3, 1867,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  The Chairman presented a petition from Hugh McFetridge and others, Gladfield, requesting that certain roads be tussocked and graded immediately so as to enable them to have a more ready means of ingress and egress. <i>(May 15, 1896,Southland Times,4</i>)

>  Tenders for tussocking certain streets were opened, when that of Mr J. McBride was accepted. <i>(Nov 8, 1884,Southland Times,3</i>)

>  Tussocking Three Chain road at 4d per chain, tussocking Copeland's road at 3d per chain, J. R. Cole <i>(Aug 8, 1891,Ashburton Guardian,3</i>)

>  It was resolved to call tenders for tussocking the South Road, and a new race crossing on Gardiner's Road was ordered to be better graded and the boulders taken off. <i>(Oct 4, 1912,Ashburton Guardian,7</i>)

>  <b>MURRAY</b> [The intending farmer] will do well to `tussac' a few acres; - that is chip off with an adze the flax and coarse grass growing on the land. <i>(1866,Description Province Southland,28</i>)

>  280 chains of road had been metalled at a cost of GBP238 16s 7d, and 1420 chains had been tussocked at a cost of GBP101 19s 8d. <i>(May 6, 1905,Ashburton Guardian,3</i>)

>  It was also resolved that Mr Watson be authorised to have a length of Christie's Road tussocked. <i>(Oct 8, 1917,Ashburton Guardian,3</i>)

>  The clerk then read the correspondence, among which was a letter from Mr Joseph Hatch, in reference to the necessity of the Corporation tussocking block 17, as the long rank grass was apt to get ignited by sparks from the railway engine; and while there was a probability of the grass taking fire the houses in the locality could not be considered safe. <i>(Jan 16, 1874,Southland Times,2</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> tussock-covered

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> The lambs stopped about halfway down the spur on a big tussock face, ... <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (collective) grassland of native tussock grasses. frequent early spg also <i>tussac</i>; also<i> tussuck</i> [also nassella tussock, snowgrass includes 'the tussock', off the tussock,and 'in the tussock'; G.J.Griffiths notes: originally it meant a `tuft', `bunch', `hillock' and only later came in NZ to be transferred to the kind of vegetation that grows in that fashion; here shown Tuckett did not have `tussock' to use] (See [also nassella tussock](../A#also-nassella-tussock),[snowgrass](sho-spa.md#snowgrass))

>  Les White was a champion leg-puller of us children running wild and free in the tussock. <i>(Dec 27, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  Fat lambs made 11s 3d to 11s 11d, others 9s to 10s 9d, do off the tussock 7s 4d to 8s 6d <i>(Mar 2, 1900,Star, The,1</i>)

>  .. 29s 3d for fat wethers off the tussock <i>(Mar 14, 1916,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,5</i>)

>  No doubt plenty will be heard, at the inevitable inquiry, about mountaineering control, guiding qualifications, party discipline, food, clothes, protected matches, and the wisdom or otherwise of sticking to the tussock instead of diving deep into the jungle; but these things may stand over. <i>(Sep 4, 1931,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <i>(Jun 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jul Tuckett in Nelson Examiner in Hocken Contributions 234 (Griffiths Collection)]

>  The presence of either of these `tussocks' [<i>Danthonia flavescens, D. crassiuscula</i>] is evidence of relative dryness. <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/127</i>)

>  <b>Michael King</b> [Samuel Butler] would be remembered for, among other things, playing Bach in his sod hut homestead among the tussock and the sheep. <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,173</i>)

>  On Tuesday afternoon, says the Ashburton Mail, what appeared to be smoke was noted apparently rising from the tussock near Springburn. <i>(Aug 4, 1899,Marlborough Express,4</i>)

>  The natural grasses are always found to grow in bunches known by the name of tussocks and these tussocks vary in colour and dimensions with the quality of the soil and the nature of climate. <i>(1856,Report</i>) [NOTES: c1856 J.T. Thomson Report quoted in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 19]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> We kept one [horse] on the tether - tethered to a tussock of grass by a peculiar kind of New Zealand knot - and let the others loose; ... <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,48</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> How shall I describe everything - the little townlet, ... the scattered wooden boxes of houses, with ragged roods of scrubby ground between them - tussocks of brown grass - the huge wide-leaved flax ... the healthy clear-complexioned men, shaggy-bearded, rowdy-hatted, and independent ... - the stores, supplying all heterogeneous commodities - <i>Ibid.</i> 29 It is volcanic, brown, and dry; ... and then a stiff, wiry, uncompromising-looking tussock of the very hardest grass; then perhaps a flax bush, or, as we should have said, a flax plant; ... <i>(Jan 27, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,3/28</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Some of the houses are built of `cob,' especially those erected in the very early days, when sawn timber was rare and valuable: this material is simply wet clay [50] with chopped tussocks stamped in. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,49</i>)

>  The plains are covered with grasses, the roots of which are gathered into tufts or tussocks, intermixed with the Bayonet grass or `Wild Spaniard' <i>(Aciphylla Colensoi)</i>, Toumatakuru<i> (Discaria Toumatou)</i>, and a great variety of the New Zealand brooms <i>(Carmichaelia), </i>... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> The American ... pulled out a lump of unmistakeable `honey dew', transferred a goodly-sized plug to his left cheek, chewed away at it ..., aimed a well-directed shot at an unoffending tussac ... <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/i/9,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>HART</b> Armed with tussocks, having a fairly good quantity of earth attached, the ... warriors [sc. boys] would open fire on the enemy. <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,31</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> Some of the lads made their bricks in what they called a pug ring, formed by driving the horses round in a circle as with the old-time chaffcutter, but Cecil made his on what they called a straight. A strip of ground about 30 yards long by four or five wide was scooped free of top soil, which was dumped on the garden nearby. It was then ploughed about ten inches deep, or according to the number of bricks required, and very well watered, which was usually done with a shovel from a tank on a sledge for even distribution, and left to soak all night. A good layer of chopped-up tussocks was then evenly spread over the top. After more watering a six-horse team was driven up and down the strip until the pug was well mixed. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,116</i>) [NOTES: [c1910, Otago]]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> There were no bridges and no proper roads but there were sheep tracks through the tussocks. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,18,1956 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [pl = tussock bush]]

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> .. , I went off for some distance and sat with my dogs in the tussocks, where the sheep could not see me. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,28</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869] [pl use] .]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> They each get behind a tussock and hang on by their teeth, and when the storm gets going good and proper they gradually get swept away until that's all that's left of them. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,69</i>) [NOTES: [pre-1900]]

>  Sheep grazed among the tussocks. <i>(1962,Landfall Country,164</i>) [NOTES: Courage `An Evening for a Fish']

>  Stiffer soils.. such as those which produce the large coarse tussock popularly called snow grass, are not good producers of the clovers. <i>(Oct 30, 1890,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  Surounded by jaw-dropping vistas of tawny tussock, glowing tarns and cloud-wreathed peaks, he gets down on his knees to examine a myriad of plants no bigger than a fingernail. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/92</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Astelia banksii (aka fragrans); tree tussock

>  <b>DAWSON</b> <i>Neomyrtus pedunculata</i> may be prominent in the subcanopy and large tussocks of <i>Astelia fragrans</i> may be abundant on the forest floor. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,120</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> any grass which grows in clumps (`tussocks') rather than as a mat, esp. <i>Poa</i> and <i>Arundo</i> spp., and esp. in the high country [pl. often tussocks]

>  <i>Tussock</i>.- Up-country people only recognise the small <i>blue t. (Poa colensoi) </i>and the common yellow, usually called white t. (<i>Poa caespitosa </i>) but sometimes <i>brown </i>. and sometimes <i>yellow </i> or silver. On the other hand, some men call the blue one <i>silver </i> (rare). <i>White t.</i> includes at least two varieties, a poa and a fescue (Festuca [novae-zealandiae]). The poa is a sign of good country and the fescue is not; but five shepherds out of six can't tell them part. The dreariness of the brown tussock plains seems to have been the first thing that struck every newcomer to Canterbury. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) `In the country far distant from town, my boy,/ All desolate, drear and alone,/ Some people exist among tussocks and sheep;/ And Brittan looks after his own.' --C.RH., p. 4(2) <i>(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>MURRAY</b> Short and succulent grass is found to be growing in greater or less profusion between the tufts of coarser and more conspicuous `tussac'. <i>(1866,Description Province Southland,8</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> <i>(1869,Station Life in NZ,1874</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> ... `tussac' or `snow grass' ... <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The hoggets should be wintered ... - if they can be fed on turnips ... so much the better. If they are turniped, then the shepherd must watch them carefully when they are once more put back on the tussocks, for they will usually fret for a time, ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> <i>Tussock </i>- Tufted, harsh grasses that characterise the montane belt. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,363,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tussock</i> A clump of grass. A grass that grows in clumps in many of the drier parts of New Zealand, especially in the South Island. The parts where it grows are called tussock country. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),426</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The tussocks referred to are the SNOW TUSSOCK. <i>Danthonia flavescens</i> the RED TUSSOCK (<i>D rigida</i>), and in smaller amount (<i>D.cunninhamii</i>); and the `low tussocks' are chiefly the SILVER TUSSOCK (<i>Poa caespitosa</i>) and the FESCUE TUSSOCK (<i>Festuca novae-zelandiae</i>) or more rarely Poa colensoi, the BLUE TUSSOCK. The name `tussock' is applied to those tufted grasses which hold water in the decaying leaves at the base. Tall tussock is dominant in subalpine regions and low tussock in montane and lowland country, though in Southland the red tussock reaches sea level and dominates much of the landscape. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,129</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The term <i>tussock grass</i> has been formed from the English word <i>tussock</i> `a clump' (of grass) for a kind of grass which grew in clumps. The name ... was soon shortened to <i>tussock</i>, not merely a reversal of the initial process, since it is quite possible to talk of `a clump of tussock' in New Zealand. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,52</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Tussock .. .. wi .. .. <i>Poa caespitosa </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> the `sward' of tussock(s) which can be eaten, as ??distinct from b. the general TUSSOCK-GRASSLAND, the geographical phenomenon itself [?? can this distinction be carried by the evidence??]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> [tussac] The town sadly need improvement too; ... swamp and tussac demanded cat-like agility in traversing many a street. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,160</i>)

>  <i>Poa caespitosa </i> <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Examination of the Higher Waimarino District]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The land towards the hills was covered with tussock and scrub - white tussock chiefly, but with some blue. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,84</i>)

>  <i> tussock n</i>. native grass which once covered much open country in N.Z.; hence <i>v. </i>tussock tether,<i> tie horse to a clump of </i>tussock and <i>tussocker n</i>. tramp or sundowner. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21</i>)

>  <b>MARK & ADAMS</b> Some of the smaller tussock (<i>Poa colensoi</i>), alpine fescue tussock (<i>Festuca matthewsii</i>) and bristle tussock (<i>Notodanthonia asetifolia</i>), have become aggressive in this way. Bristle tussock now now dominates in parts of Marlborough and North Canterbury while the other two are more important in inland areas of South Canterbury and Otago. <i>(1973,NZ Alpine Plants,22</i>)

### tussock basin
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high-altitude depression, where stock graze tussock.

>  <b>FARMER</b> The three of us carried on and camped that night in a beautiful tussock basin right back in the headwaters. <i>(1994,Best Job Ever, The,223</i>)

### tussock bench
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high mountain or hill terrace, covered in tussock.

>  <b>AXBEY</b> It was a fairly typical head basin, with a small lake and tussock benches dropping away to secondary growth ... <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,43</i>)

### tussock block
 
 <i>n.</i> a large tussock grazing area of a high country station.

>  Some runholders maintain that, providing a tussock block sunny and free from snow is available, it is better to send to the plains two-tooths and older ewes, which have a better resistance to parasites, and keep the hoggets on the sunny blocks of the run. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,103</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Wethers at Glencoe station being driven back to their tussock blocks after shearing. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year, A,24</i>)

### tussock burn
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an annual burning of coarse tussock to allow for fresh growth.

>  Even the stack fires or the tussock or scrub burns which must have consumed millions of night flying beetles and moths had become unfashionable ... <i>(Oct 15, 1962,NZ Journal of Agriculture,294</i>)

### tussock bush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As they were going up the Blowhard, Chum noticed sheep skulls, with very few other bones nearby, behind almost every tussock bush. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,69</i>) [NOTES: [pre-1900]]

### tussock country
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) land where tufted tussock grasses grow [principally in S.I. high country].

>  On poor tussock country..the use of danthonia invariably increases the carrying-capacity. <i>(Mar 20, 1918,NZ Journal of Agriculture,135</i>)

>  The maintenance or improvement of Otago's large area of unploughable tussock country has for many years been the subject of much consideration and endeavour by farmers and others interested. <i>(Sep 16, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,183</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> The station consists basically of tussock country sweeping down from the crest of the Garvie mountains. <i>(1971,Historic Sheep Stations SI II,16</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS & TURNER</b> .. it was difficulty enough by daylight covering this tussock country ... <i>(1983,NZ High Country Four Seasons,26</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> George Stuart had added another large station Mangaohone to his contract sheds, and as it adjoined Otupae he now had three sheds in the high rugged tussock country. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,51</i>)

>  The original sheep, landed prior to the arrival of the Maria, and before any attempt had been made to improve the country, suffered severely, the low "tussock" country of Southland affording but scant natural feed for sheep, more especially for those newly imported of a long-woolled breed, and unacclimatised. <i>(Aug 31, 1867,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  Throughout these districts the increase of lambs this year will be considerably below the average. .. This unfortunate state of affairs arises from two causes. The first is due to the wholesale system carried on of burning the tussock country. <i>(Dec 10, 1874,Clutha Leader,5</i>)

>  The above comprises a first-class block of tussock country, well surface sown; fences all in good repair and water in every block. <i>(Nov 23, 1945,Ellesmere Guardian,1</i>)

>  Widely dispersed throughout the tussock country recently visited by the author were small patches of ground ... upon which no tussocks were growing. <i>(1941,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,22/22A</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> 'This was originally all tussock country, some of it up to the knees of men on horseback.' <i>(2007,Drybread,265</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Beneath me, stretching back into the legend haunted ranges of the Urewera country, was a tussock clothed valley with patches of scrub and bush dotted along the silvery stream that wended its way through it. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,185</i>)

>  We turned down past a swampy flat surrounded by dreary, monotonous, barren tussock country. <i>(Jan 22, 1924,Otago Witness,69</i>)

>  Tussock country. Agricultural and pastoral land. <i>(Mar 11, 1899,Mataura Ensign,4</i>)

>  It is an interesting and creditable publication, and that it meets a want is shown by the letters written by farmers who ask for advice upon such subjects as the burning of tussock country, how to treat and combat the grass grub, and also by the correspondence written by farmers concerning their experiments in growing various grasses for stock food. <i>(Sep 15, 1910,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

### tussock field
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Below Ivory Lake Hut, after weaving through some bluffs, I reach the tussock fields of the Waitaha, a remote West Coast valley. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/36</i>)

### tussock grass
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A conspicuous feature of the New Zealand landscape from summer to autumn, the flowers and seed heads of native tussock grasses wave like creamy-white pennants in the breeze. <i>(Aug 27, 1999,Dominion,14</i>)

>  <b>TUWHARE</b> standing sentinel to your bleak / loneliness the tussock grass <i>(1977,No Ordinary Sun,23</i>)

>  Tussock grasses belong to three groups; [39] the tall snow tussocks (<i>Chionochloa</i>) and the short tussocks (<i>Festuca</i> and <i>Poa</i>). <i>(May 2003,Forest & Bird,No. 308/38</i>)

>  Tussock grasses are a taonga to Ngai Tahu and in the past had many uses apart from material for leggings. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40</i>)

>  The island is clothed with a dense green mantle of vegetation formed of high tussock grass and the Kerguelen cabbage. <i>(Jul 12, 1924,Hawera & Normanby Star,15</i>)

>  They also used the ashes of burnt `tussac' grass, and a lotion prepared by boiling the leaves of the plant <i>kopakopa (Plantago major)</i>, or from the bruised leaves of the <i>kopata (Pelargonium australe). </i> <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/67</i>)

>  <i>Poa anceps,</i> Forst. - This is the common tussock-grass of the Plains and Port Hills. <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/303</i>) [NOTES: (Report on Grasses)]

>  Tussock grasses - of which New Zealand has some two dozen species - are the dominant vegetation in the first hundred metres above the treeline, often forming great sweeping fields a metre or more deep in rustling strands. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/39</i>)

>  I find that tussock grass almost covers the face of the country. <i>(Sep 25, 1890,Otago Witness,34</i>)

>  A very remarkable circumstance occurred in connection with a piece of ground on Mr Tuckey's property covered by the natural tussock grass. <i>(May 30, 1883,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  EXPORTS. Per S.S. Stormbird - 12 packages, A.E. Kitchen: 1 ton tussock grass, U.S.S. Company ... <i>(Aug 10, 1891,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  One remarkable thing about Macquarrie [sic] Island, at which the Discovery called on her voyage to New Zealand, was the fact that it was covered with a thick mantle of tussock grass, standing from five to six feet high in places. <i>(Dec 4, 1901,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Below, a close-up of a young bull sea elephant enjoying the luxury of a soft tussock grass bed on the same island. <i>(Jun 2, 1930,Evening Post,7</i>)

### tussock grassland
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Most of New Zealand's tussock grasslands are found in the South Island high country. <i>(May 2003,Forest & Bird,No. 308/ 39</i>)

>  All the tussock grasslands that you now see below the tree-line are fire affected... <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/32</i>)

>  The tussock grassland is a study almost entirely apart from the ordinary sown grasslands of New Zealand. <i>(Jun 15, 1927,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  Secondly, it is intended to produce artificially the various types of primitive vegetation in New Zealand, such as kauri forest, southern beech forest, tall and low tussock grassland, and subalpine scrubs of different kinds. <i>(Mar 10, 1932,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  <b>234</b> So let the steppes remain in U.S.S.R., the pusstas in Hungary, the veld in South Africa, the prairies in North America, the pampas in South America. Let New Zealand stick to its tussock grassland, colloquially known as `the tussock'. <i>(1946,NZ Alpine Plants,1945 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Allan)]

>  The high-country tussock grassland of Marlborough has ben occupied for pastoral purposes for almost 100 years. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,293</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> The tussock grasslands, originally some 14,000,000 acres, mainly in the South Island, have pioneered the clothing of new country, and older country too dry for forest. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,95,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Tussock (bunch grass) grasslands, generally with species of <i>Poa</i> and <i>Festuca</i> dominant. <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,24</i>)

>  The quickest and best method, moreover, had been arrived at of converting the native tussock grass land into permanent artificial pasture, carrying three times as many stock as the tussock carried before; and also of securing a rotation of crops. <i>(Jan 29, 1890,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

### tussock grubber
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person employed to dig out noxious nassella tussock [S.I.]

>  8 I worked for the North Canterbury Nassella Tussock Board in Cheviot as a tussock grubber in the late 1970s ... <i>(Mar 2002,NZ Wilderness,08</i>)

### tussock grubbing
 
 <i>n.</i> the removal by digging of tussock

>  The Clerk was instructed to have some repairs done to roads at Dromore, and to pay a small amount for tussock grubbing on a street in the Dronmore township. <i>(Dec 23, 1910,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

### tussock gully
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a narrow high-altitude valley with sides of tussock cover.

>  <b>FARMER</b> The red letter morning found us walking up a tussock gully 16 kilometres out of Rotorua towards Taupo. <i>(1994,Best Job Ever, The,18</i>)

### tussock gut
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a narrow tussock-lined chute, common in S.I. high country.

>  <b>AXBEY</b> .. they carried on up the valley, staying concealed in the bush edge, until suddenly in a tussock gut, they almost stumbled on two roa ... <i>(1991,Along the Road,144</i>)

### tussock hogget
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a young sheep grazed on tussock blocks with little attention.

>  The tussock hogget is healthy and apparently not infected with parasites to any extent. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,103</i>)

### tussock jumper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tussock jumper</i> station hand. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,116</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a musterer.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Well, what good is it [religion] to the hard-boiled rock hopper or tussock jumper? <i>(1964,Deer Shooting Days,85</i>)

>  Even the hardiest, most-seasoned tussock-jumper is reduced to 'tenderfoot' status by the end of the day. <i>(Apr 28, 1966,NZ Farmer,37</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> tussock jumper: a term usually referring to a musterer on the eastern uplands of the South Island where silver tussock grows ... <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,164</i>)

>  <i>tussock jumper</i>: Musterer. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/29</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### tussock knot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BUTLER</b> We kept one [horse] on the tether - tethered to a tussock of grass by a peculiar kind of New Zealand knot - and let the others loose; ... <i>(1860,Forest Creek MS,48,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <iTussock knot</i>.-The knot by which the leaves of a t. are doubled over so that it will hold a tether rope or dogchain safely. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...just as have tussock tether or tie, to tie a horse to a clump of tussock in such country, and tussock knot, the method of tying to prevent the horse breaking away. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,41</i>)

### tussock lamb
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Lister's lambs were about as good a quality of tussock lambs as he had seen. <i>(Sep 9, 1924,Otago Witness,15</i>)

### tussock land
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country] pastoral country on which native tussock grows. [tussock-land, tussockland]

>  <b>ROSS</b> .. that saw the lordly squatter once king of tussock land. <i>(1889,BLANK ENTRY,64</i>) [NOTES: in Woodhouse (1950) NZ Farm & Station Verse]

>  <b>FERRIS</b> .. with the deterioration of the tussock land especially, the Merino has proved his thriftiness. <i>(1940,Cavalcade of Farming 1840-1940,49</i>)

>  The successful management of a sheep run calls for .. an appreciation of the carrying capacity of tussock land to 'do' sheep well without either overstocking or understocking. <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,347</i>)

>  <b>RICKARD</b> In any study of farming in early New Zealand the men who operated the big sheep runs of the tussock lands must occupy a prominent .. place. <i>(1967,Strangers in the Wilderness,52</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> .. some of its own aims are well-planted breaks to assist pasture growth, to prevent wind erosion on tussock land, and to hold gullies against soil erosion. [tussockland] <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,321</i>)

>  Year by year, as I go my rounds along dusty roads and over bare brown plains, I see the face of nature changing more and more ; pleasant homesteads dotted here and there, and trees and fences shutting out the view, and well-tilled fields, and well-stocked pastures, and the busy hum of industry arises from places that but yesterday were silent as the grave, and the merry laughter of little children as they wend their way to school and the shout of the ploughman as he labours in the newly-broken tussock land or builds his modest dwelling on the soil that is his own at last. <i>(Oct 31, 1870,Star, The,3</i>)

>  Run. No. 1. 1325 acres 21 years lease at a yearly rental of GBP130 Miss Eliza Thompsom [sic] only one of the runs was applied for and that by the members of one family who must have thought there was something to be made out of hill-side tussock land at 2/4 acre rent. <i>(Dec 7, 1895,Oxford Observer,3</i>)

>  <b>BATEMAN</b> The tussock land abounds in the Middle Island, although the richest land was originally what are termed `flax swamps'. <i>(1881,Colonist, The,186</i>)

>  <b>VINCENT</b> It was wonderful in this bleak, hilly country, generally called `tussock land', to see what changes of colour ... there were. <i>(1885,40,000 Miles,217</i>)

>  Professor Worley also makes a great outcry as to the huts facing due north, which, he contends, will give only a view of the tussock land, and not of the mountain. <i>(Feb 7, 1928,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  A Romance of New Zealand and the Commonwealth. By Arthur H. Adams. <i>(Mar 21, 1936,Evening Post,28</i>)

>  There were thousands of hares in the tussock lands of the South Island, said Mr. Parry, and they took a heavy toll of pastures, crops, and the bark of fruit and other trees. <i>(Aug 7, 1945,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>RICKARD</b> In any study of farming in early New Zealand the men who operated the big sheep runs of the tussock lands must occupy a prominent - perhaps too prominent - place. <i>(1967,Strangers in the Wilderness,52</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> George drove us up into the foothills, which open out into unexpectedly wide rolling tussocklands, with good stands of native bush in the gullies. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,5</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> There was no bush on this wide expanse of tussockland. <i>(1985,Coldstream,xi</i>)

>  A farmer who grew a fine crop of wheat on the new tussock land at Otiake, and who had already disposed of 2000 sacks at a satisfactory price, was offered and declined 3s 5d (f.o.b.) for the remaining 1000 sacks. <i>(Nov 1, 1905,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  Investigation of tussock land, South Island GBP500. <i>(Nov 19, 1918,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

### tussock line
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. grassline](go-gra.md#grassline))

>  ... after a few hundred steep feet of the Dorset Ridge had been covered, an indifferent blaze was picked up which led through bush and scrub to the tussock line at the end of Dorset Ridge, ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,69</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the level or altitude at which most tussock will grow in place of other vegetation.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> vegetable sheep: native plants Raoulia eximia or R mamillaris, which will grow in a dense mass from about 300 metres below the tussock line up to 1000-1200 metres above that. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,88</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> .. the manuka takes over the ranges up to the tussock line. <i>(1988,Hills, The,21</i>)

### tussock madness
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) abnormal behaviour [said to be induced by loneliness.]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Like me, he had the tussock madness. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,74</i>)

### tussock paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large grazing area containing tussock grasses.

>  .. enormous boulders to be climbed, and tussock paddocks riddled with rabbit-holes ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,284</i>)

>  Each night they were driven off to a second tussock-paddock of 300 acres with no clovers or grass undergrowth. <i>(May 20, 1930,NZ Journal of Agriculture,299</i>)

### tussock park
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country conservation area growing tussock grasses and other plants.

>  Tussock park opened. The 22,000-hectare (55,000-acre) park covers the Big Ben and Torlesse ranges and has a range of flora and fauna, including tussocks, beech forests and unusual plants adapted to unstable screes and rock ridges, such as the vegetable sheep and South Island edelweiss. <i>(Nov 2, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

### tussock run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country grazing property.

>  Wanted to buy: tussock runs. <i>(Aug 10, 1972,NZ Farmer,128</i>)

### tussock station
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country pastoral property growing native tussock.
