>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> Molly worked as shepherd, drover and harvester on the tussock station running 10,000 half-Merino sheep. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,159</i>)

### tussock tether/tussock tie
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...just as have tussock tether or tie, to tie a horse to a clump of tussock in such country, and tussock knot, the method of tying to prevent the horse breaking away. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,41</i>)

### tussock track
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> John Grigg told me that on one trip he and Meikle saw a peculiar object, not far from the tussock track between Ashburton and Rakaia. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,14</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]

### tussock unit
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country pastoral property.

>  For Sale: North Canterbury cattle and sheep tussock unit.. <i>(Aug 10, 1972,NZ Farmer,128</i>)

### tussock-burner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If the original tussock-burners could revisit this area they would be terribly shocked to see the change. <i>(Oct 7, 1924,Otago Witness,31</i>)

### tussock-clad
 
 <i>adj.</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] clothed with tussock. [tussock clad]

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> [shute] A narrow, newly eroded and steep-sided gut cutting through the tussock-clad hillside. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,72</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> In mid-afternoon sun, the detail on the tussock-clad hills is lost - it's just a warm blur of tan and gold, sheep and dog country <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,222</i>)

>  It is about 30 years ago since the present member for Wakanui first set foot upon the shores of New Zealand, but it was not on the yellow level tussock-clad plain where he has since made his home. <i>(Jul 28, 1884,Star, The,2</i>)

>  What a contrast it is to turn from the tussock-clad, weed-producing vacant sections of the Government to the Lower Wairuna settlement. <i>(Jan 28, 1903,Otago Witness,31</i>)

>  A number of the "Pilgrims" are said to have wept when, from the top of the Port Hills, they caught their first sight of the dreary tussock-clad country; but they and their descendants have since had ample cause for rejoicing. <i>(Dec 16, 1911,Ashburton Guardian,6</i>)

>  Sixty years ago and more, study Methodist pioneers wended their way across the swamp or tussock-clad plains in search of land on which to settle and make homes for themselves and their families. <i>(Aug 6, 1929,Ellesmere Guardian,3</i>)

>  The usual mist was encountered along the tops, but frequently it lifted temporarily and the sun shone through to disclose beautiful vistas of bush and tussock-clad valleys looking charming in lovely shades of green and brown, and occasional glimpses of the more distant plains of the Manawatu and the Wairarapa. <i>(Oct 26, 1934,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  <i>Charadrius bicinctus</i>, Jard. and Selb. Dottrel [sc]. - One of the earlier breeders, whether on the tussock-clad plains or on river-bed spits and flats; ... <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,62</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The edges of streams on the tussock-clad plains are often fringed with flax and bulrush, whilst, in the water itself, stand numbers of blackened stumps about two feet high, bearing on their summits drooping brushes of long, coarse, green, or tawny threads. These are termed by the colonists, nigger-heads. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,5</i>) [NOTES: [check 1907]]

>  <b>LEVY</b> The original tussock-clad country of the lower levels in the South Island has either been turned under by the plough or so burnt and grazed by sheep and rabbits as to be changed almost beyond recognition. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,272,1970 ed.</i>)

>  The hills now began to get further apart, and the valley to open out; paradise ducks were to be seen and heard, cattle began to show themselves in the river-beds and upon the tussock clad terraces. <i>(Apr 16, 1892,Oxford Observer,3</i>)

### tussock-clothed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Beneath me, stretching back into the legend haunted ranges of the Urewera country, was a tussock clothed valley with patches of scrub and bush dotted along the silvery stream that wended its way through it. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,185</i>)

### tussock-covered
 
 <i>adj.</i> [tussock covered]

>  We were not more than a score of people all told, and we lived in an out-of-the-way, half-forgotten little gully walled in by steep tussock-covered spurs, with the creek from which the township took its name running through, and the Mataura river flowing past the opening. <i>(Jul 15, 1882,Wanganui Herald,1</i>)

>  Mr. Friedlander has seen tussock-covered areas replaced by the present well-equipped borough, and he would find it difficult, were he to take stock of the borough's assets, to note one worth mentioning which he did not assist to secure for the public welfare. <i>(Nov 15, 1919,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  By the passing away, at the age of 72 years, of Mr James Cochrane, Riverside, one more link has been removed from the chain of worthy pioneers who settled in the Ashburton district and converted the land from its rough native tussock-covered condition into some of the best wheat and other grain-growing land in the Southern Hemisphere. <i>(May 10, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  The camp site is at the mouth of a long narrow valley where it debouches from between low hills on to a vast expanse of rolling tussock-covered country. <i>(Aug 17, 1940,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Sendin' all the sheep a-pantin' to the shady-sided slopes/ Of the tussock-covered foothills, ... <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,46</i>)

>  (2) The typical, originally tussock-covered plains land, the soils of which vary from light and stony, as at Burnham, to heavy silts as at Ashburton; ... <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,77</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> Long tussock-covered spurs about a mile long, reached to the top on both sides. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,40</i>)

>  When the Lark is flushed from her nest on the wide expanse of the tussock-covered plains, ... what rare instinct ... enables her to find her nest ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/45</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  ... I well remember the extraordinary clouds of moths of all kinds which rose from the ground as one walked either through the tussock-covered areas or through fields of cultivated grass. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/8</i>)

>  Most of the good land on Waikakahi was cropped, and the then owner of the station, Hon John McLean, must have made a stack of money out of letting the tussock covered sheep walk. <i>(May 31, 1899,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

### tussock-dwelling
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  As we returned across the plateau to our camp we disturbed some tussock-dwelling birds, probably pipits. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,13</i>)

### tussock-former
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a plant that provides a basis from which tussock can seed and grow.

>  Such species in the main are cocksfoot and crested dogstail, both of which are more or less tussock-formers. <i>(Sep 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,200</i>)

### tussock-jumping
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) mustering.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> tussock-jumping: mustering. Like the phrase chasing mutton, this is used in a general rather than a particular sense. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,87</i>)

### tussock-spine
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... the frost sparkled on every broad flax-blade or slender tussock-spine. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,68</i>)

### tussock-swamp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Hence, next to bush and swamp-land proper (on alluvial clays), Flax-land is regarded as the most desirable for agricultural purposes. `Flax-swamps' are frequently as common as `Tussock-swamps,' though their soil is far from being so wet or watery. Like the `Tussock-swamps,' they are frequently very `tussocky' - causing walking thereon to be extremely ... fatiguing. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,80</i>)

### tussock-tether
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [high country mustering] to tether one's horse to a tussock clump.

>  <b>WALKER</b> We'll ride to the end of that spur, 'tussock-tether' our horses on the plain and walk up till we get to the good pig gullies. <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,30</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...just as have tussock tether or tie, to tie a horse to a clump of tussock in such country, and tussock knot, the method of tying to prevent the horse breaking away. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,41</i>)

>  <i>tussock n. </i>native grass which once covered much open country in N.Z.; hence <i>v</i>. tussock tether, <i>tie horse to a clump of </i>tussock and <i>tussocker n.</i> tramp or sundowner. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21</i>)

### tussockdom
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) large pastoral tracts of native grassland.

>  <b>HOBHOUSE</b> He is now a shepherd king with no moderate slice of tussockdom under his charge. <i>(Nov 1860,Hobhouse Letters and Papers</i>)

>  <b>KENNINGTON</b> .. the lords of the tussockdom. <i>(1978,Awatere: a district and its people,40</i>)

### tussocked
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of high country] covered with predominantly native tussock.

>  <b>BETHELL</b> Bronze tussocked terraces before precipitous Great purple alps ... <i>(1936,Time and Place : poems,27</i>)

>  The zig-zag hunt is on a magnificent tussocked face that you just keep looking up and up and up. <i>(Jul 15, 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,60</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> .. early enthusiasts found the flat-bottomed gullies, steep slopes and tussocked faces necessary to test man, dog, and quite often, the sheep. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,28</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> There was a tussocked hill I examined every morning about sunrise. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,173,1949 ed.</i>)

>  Old Himi had a great deal to tell, gesticulating and pointing vehemently at times across the bay, where a tall wooded peak shot a dark blue silhouette upward against the sky, contrasting strongly with the yellow tone of the tussocked hills in the foreground. <i>(Feb 9, 1889,Observer (Auckland),7</i>)

>  He has ceased to haunt the backblocks, o'er the tussocked hills to roan [sic], / And life's sunset finds him camping in the Old Men's Home. <i>(Nov 8, 1916,Ashburton Guardian,6</i>)

>  On Tuesday, July 17, near the crude shack which for the past thirty years had been his home in the bleak desolation of tussocked hill-side and rugged creek-bed, he was brutally done to death by gunshot. <i>(Sep 13, 1928,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  The tussocked plain and virgin bush are a thing of the past. <i>(Feb 8, 1896,Southland Times,3</i>)

>  The private line trends away from Milton through grren pastures, and, opening up tussocked slopes, winds its way between them to the Tokomariro River, just beyond which lies the township (as yet in embryo) of Waronui. <i>(Sep 30, 1908,Otago Witness,12</i>)

### tussocker
 
 <i>n.</i> a sundowner

>  <b>PYKE</b> Now, a `sundowner', or `tussocker', for the terms are synonymous - is a pastoral loafer; one who loiters about till dusk, and then makes for the nearest station or hut, to beg for shelter and food. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3(10)105</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tussocker, n</i>.a New Zealand name for a <i>Sundowner</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,485</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> `Now, a `sun-downer, or 'tussocker' for the terms are synonymous - is a pastoral loafer; one who loiters about till dusk, and then makes for the nearest station or hut, to beg for shelter and food.' <i>(1889,Wild Will Enderby</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Tussocker is a New Zealand equivalent for the Australian sundowner mentioned above. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,41</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealanders have coined or adapted many expressions to meet local requirements, as illustrated by the following: ... <i>tussocker</i>: hobo <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),118</i>)

>  <i>tussocker n.</i>New Zealand a sundowner (<i>q.v</i>.). <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <i>tussock n. </i>native grass which once covered much open country in N.Z.; hence v. tussock tether, <i>tie horse to a clump of tussock</i> and<i> tussocker n</i>. tramp or sundowner. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tussocker </i>shearer or casual labourer arriving at dusk to avoid work but not the tucker; from his tussock grass hide; `sundowner' in Australia. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,116</i>)

>  There is one thing, however, that I am not quite pleased with - that is, that the Building Committee should have been entirely chosen from among the big wigs of the district, as I should have liked to see them joined by a few of my own class - that is, tussockers, ditchers, or other decent and respectable farm labourers. <i>(Jul 28, 1869,Bruce Herald,5</i>)

### tussocking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If he were to stick to his trade, tussocking, he would be doing more good to people generally and himself in particular than writing such stuff. <i>(Apr 5, 1871,Bruce Herald,7</i>)

>  Contract 110. - Formation &c., near Mr McCloskie's, and Tussocking Craig's Road. <i>(Oct 4, 1887,Mataura Ensign,5</i>)

>  <b>PRESTON</b> Four tramps arrived on 4 june and were put to 'tussocking' at 5s an acre. <i>(Jun 1871,Diary,7</i>)

>  <b>WATT</b> (caption) Early view of the south portion of the town ... The tussocks are noticeable. Their removal from street lines and sections provided work for contractors, who charged so much an acre for what was known as tussocking. <i>Ibid.</i> 64 ... among them `tussacing' - the removal of tussocks from streets and town reserves. It was some time before the town clerk came round to the spelling, `tussock' ... On February 26, 1874, the council called tenders for tussocking (so spelt) the remainder of the untussocked streets south of the Puni.--> <i>(1971,Centenary of Invercargill,8f</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Other tussocking works were left in the hands of the Clerk to let. <i>(Oct 7, 1905,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  .. I have been daily, weather and personal inclination (drouthiness?) permitting, prosecuting my ditching and tussocking contract, so far as necessary to keep hunger and thirst from the door of my rough out-of-the-world sod hut. <i>(Jun 2, 1869,Bruce Herald,6</i>)

### tussocky
 
 <i>n.</i> a swagger

>  European swaggers were sometimes known as "tussockies" because of their habit of tying the leaves of two tussocks together to form a simple shelter to sleep beneath. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land, country, pasture, property etc] clothed with native tussocks. [includes sense of being tussock-like]

>  <b>COURAGE</b> The greenish-brown tussocky hills all dewy and glistening in a glow of sunlight. <i>(1896,Lights and Shadows of Colonial Life,13</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> Over the tussocky flat and down <i>(1898,Station Ballads,14</i>)

>  Tapanui Station as a tussocky run went by the board and Brooksdale Estate came into being. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,16</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On the tussocky sides of the gullies the pleasant-tasting snowberries grew. <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,IV 5</i>)

>  <b>BECKETT</b> On those tussocky valleys and tussocky hills where the wild wind rides ... <i>(1978,Mountains of Erewhon, The,71</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> At Glenrock there's a broken wall of bluffs up from the farm, with guts separating knobs, and behind that a tussocky plateau I've only been told about. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,40</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> North Island boys wouldn't have any idea of big open tussocky alluvial flats ... <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,172</i>)

>  It is due to a microbe that may be present in mouldy hay or in rank tussocky grass, or in a rank growth of oats that has decayed foliage at the bottom. <i>(Aug 2, 1933,see notes,6</i>) [NOTES: Rodney & Otamatea Times, Waitemata & Kaipara Gazette]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> The hills around Port Lyttelton are bold ridges covered with ferns and tussocky grass. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,16,1935 ed.</i>)

>  This species [of grass] is somewhat tussocky in habit <i>(1876,TrNZI,9/497</i>) [NOTES: Kirk]

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Hence, next to bush and swamp-land proper (on alluvial clays), Flax-land is regarded as the most desirable for agricultural purposes. `Flax-swamps' are frequently as common as `Tussock-swamps,' though their soil is far from being so wet or watery. Like the `Tussock-swamps,' they are frequently very `tussocky' - causing walking thereon to be extremely ... fatiguing. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,80</i>)

>  ... we came across four wild dogs baiting a sow ... in a dry tussocky lagoon. <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/323</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> On those tussocky valleys and tussocky hills/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,84</i>)

>  ... the lonely little settlement of the Maori sheep-farming hapu was more than two thousand feet above the sea, squatting on the tussocky banks of a cold clear stream that came ... down from ... Tongariro. <i>(Jan 10, 1920,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> After an early cup of tea ... I went back to bed ... leaving them ... to mount their `neddies' for a long day's mustering in those lovely tussocky hills and gullies. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,57</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On the tussocky sides of gullies the pleasant-tasting snowberries grew in profusion, while a berry known as the wind-berry grew on a little plant similar to the snowberries and both kinds were eaten and enjoyed. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,5,1965 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> This heavy bush-clad country was a completely new experience for me, as most of my previous shooting had been done on more open tussocky country. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,42</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1969,Joy of the Worm,33</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> The air up here [in the Southern Alps] was wonderfully fresh and smelt [33] aromatically of manuka scrub patching warm tussocky earth. <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,32</i>)

>  The black tussocky sand was as dragging as syrup; ... <i>(Dec 28, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B1</i>)

>  Further along the line the country is chiefly unimproved alternate heavy flats, portions of which are swampy and peaty, and rolling tussocky downs, under which lie large tracks [sic] of limestone deposits and coal-bearing country. <i>(Nov 30, 1899,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  The chairman read the audited balance sheet and also report, which stated that during the year the ordinary work of maintenance had absorbed the sum of GBP1500, which is represented by 11,054 cubic yards of shingle placed on the roads, 900 chains of the grading roads, and 284 chains of tussocky roads. <i>(May 10, 1910,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  Having procurred information as to the road to Mr Boyd's home station, we started forward, with a vast tussocky plain spread out before us as far as the eye could reach. <i>(Oct 16, 1880,Otago Witness,17</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> having patches or plants of tussock-grass; ?infested with tussock

### tussocky country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country] pastoral land that is covered in tussock.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Whilst riding after cattle over tussocky country one was always likely to come a cropper. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,136</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Most of my previous shooting had been done on more open tussocky country. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,42</i>)

>  <b>BRITTAIN</b> Before shelter belts were planted the wind tore unchecked over the tussocky country. <i>(2000,Lake Coleridge,49</i>)

### tussuck
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tussock](tot-tyr.md#tussock))

### tutae
 
 <i>n.</i> dung; excrement (lit. and fig.); a defecation [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  dung - too'tai [tutae] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,111,1986 ed.</i>)

>  In my next reincarnation I'm coming back as a full blooded maori [check], that'll scare the tutai out of all those pakeha statiticians. <i>(Apr 3, 1974,Salient,13</i>) [NOTES: [check spg]]

>  Black tutai ... It was left by Archy, the scrubcutter. <i>(Oct 1981,Climate,32/34</i>) [NOTES: 1981 Harry Goodwin `Omahu Station' in Oct/Nov Climate 32/34]

>  [Maori girl speaks] `Howzat you fullas?' but the girls were already conferring and Charlotte was enraged. ... she was going to throw that Macky in the tutae for sure. <i>(1985,Women's Work,223</i>) [NOTES: (Grace Kepa)]

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> Ah well, tutae happens. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] dags [ from the Maori for excrement.]

>  dags: tutae. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### tutae happens
 
 <i>phr.</i> Maori version of 'shit happens'

>  'Ah well', tutae happens. <i>(2007,Best New Zealand Fiction, Vol 4, The,77</i>) [NOTES: 'I've Been Thinking About You Sister', Witi Ihimaera, 2007]

### tutae kehua / tutae whetu
 
 <i>n.</i> basket fungus [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <i>CLATHRUS CIBARIUS,</i> THE `BIRD-CAGE FUNGUS'. ... This fungus, known variously as `basket fungus,'`lattice fungus,', `Devil's purse,' <i>`tutae whetu,' `tutae kehua,'</i> belongs to the Phallaceae, a family included in the Basidiomycetes. It is found commonly throughout New Zealand during the late autumn and early spring. [Distribution.- Australia; Chile; ... New Zealand] <i>(Dec 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/247</i>) [NOTES: (Cunningham)]

### tutae koau
 
 <i>n.</i> native celery (Apium prostratum) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Here in New Zealand we also hae a native celery, tukaekoau or Apium prostratum <i>(Dec 24, 2004,Dominion Post,C9</i>)

>  I'll be looking out for native celery when I next go walking along the coast. This creeping plant, tutae koau (<i>Apium prostratum</i>) is found throughout the country, accoding to Andrew Crowe, the author of <i>A Field Guide to the Native Edible Plants of New Zealand</i>... <i>(May 29, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

### tutaekuri
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of Maori potato (See [urenika](up-uwh.md#urenika))

>  These [Maori potatoes] differ from the common spud in colour and texture: some varieties, like 'Urenika' (also known as 'Tutaekuri'), have purple flesh that retains its colour when cooked, while others have black or pink skins and creamy flesh. <i>(Feb 1, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

>  Tutaekuri: Known as urenika in the north, this looks like a cross between a yam and a potato... Popular with chefs for their bright colour. <i>(Sep 7, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),13</i>)

### tute
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [tutu](tot-tyr.md#tutu))



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tute, a wild, <i>Hector. Olearia operina. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>) [NOTES: ?=??mistaken entry]

>  Holders already suffer terribly from cattle being compelled to eat tute, a most poisonous shrub, and from which they die almost immediately after having filled themselves with it <i>(Nov 1, 1843,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

### tute bush
 
 <i>n.</i> tutu

>  .. but I should recommend the principal settlement to be placed about 10 miles inland, on the banks of the river communicating with the port, being near the centre of a fine alluvial valley, apparently surrounded with hills of moderate elevation, containing probably about 200,000 acres of grass land, mostly clear of fern, and, with the exception of some tute bushes, all ready to put the plough into, without any preparatory expense in clearing. <i>(Apr 24, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  .. but I should recommend the principal settlement to be placed about 10 miles inland, on the banks of the river communicating with the port, being near the centre of a fine alluvial valley, apparently surrounded with hills of moderate elevation, containing probably about 200,000 acres of grass land, mostly clear of fern, and, with the exception of some tute bushes, all ready to put the plough into, without any preparatory expense in clearing. <i>(Apr 24, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

### tuted
 
 <i>adj.</i> poisoned by "tutu" (See [tutued](tot-tyr.md#tutued))

>  Any variety of creature, from a maggot to an elephant, can be (and has been) "tuted," and Dr Malcom's experiments were made upon rabbits by the painless method, sometimes employed with recalcitrant suffragettes, of feeding the poison through a stomach tube. <i>(Oct 14, 1913,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,7</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> With tuted cows on farms they have a sort of a frame thing <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,67</i>)

>  They, however, eat and fattened well, and these tuted sheep, or "hermits" as they were called, were kept in a separate paddock, whence the supply of station mutton was drawn. <i>(Jun 17, 1908,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> In spite of your most frantic endeavours, you will be very liable to get some sheep tuted. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,102</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> variant of tutued

>  <b>BUTLER</b> As, then, my bullocks could not get tuted on being turned out empty, I yarded them. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,7/98</i>) [NOTES: 1860 in 1863 Butler First Year vii/98]

>  A <i>Tuted</i> sheep stood erect and had full use of its extremities. <i>(1869,TrNZI,2/401</i>) [NOTES: (Dr Monro)]

>  <b>T.C.KIERNAN</b> Two bullocks dead at Troutbecks. One `tuted', the other bogged. <i>(Jan 8, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Jan 8 8T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 123]

>  If [stock] were allowed to feed or rest there soon after landing, probably half of them would be `tuted' and lost. <i>Ibid.</i> 181, 183 We also find ... the celebrated tutu, whose poisonous properties have proved so destructive to stock in all parts of New Zealand that the word `tuted' - [183] <i>i.e., </i>poisoned by eating tutu - has become a necessary part of our vocabulary. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,120</i>)

### tutin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CONNOR</b> Hodges and White.. succeeded in isolating tutin from toxic honey... The fate of tutin in the body was investigated by Ford.. who concluded that the toxin became localised in the brain and spinal chord in a detoxified state. <i>(1977,Poisonous Plants of NZ, The,57</i>)

>  There is a considerable literature on Coriaria or tutu, and the scientific results of the study of the active principle tutin (C17 H20 O7), which was first isolated in the chemical laboratory of the Department of Agriculture in 1900. <i>(Oct 10, 1934,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  The first substantial advance in the investigation of the chemical properties of tutu was made in 1900, when Easterfield and Aston succeeded in isolating a crystalline glucoside, to which they gave the name `tutin'. <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/294</i>)

### tutsan
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FEATON</b> HYPERICUM GRAMINEUM (<i>Forst.</i>) ... The leaves of some of the British species were formerly applied to fresh wounds, which they were supposed to heal; hence the plant was called in French <i>Toutesaine</i> - corrupted into Tutsan, its common English name. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,50</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> TUTSAN (<i>Hypericum</i> and <i>rosaemum</i>), is a similar [to St. John's wort] but the less common weed. It is widely spread usually near the bush edge in both islands.] ... Tutsan is a corruption of Fr. Toutsaine- meaning heal-all. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,125,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tutsan</i> [s] <i>Hypericum androsaemum</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,81</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> TUTSAN (Hypericum andro saemum) Shrubby perennial. . ., native to Eurasia. ... Tutsan is common in some places in the North Island and the Marlborough sounds. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,155</i>)

### tutt-tuttery
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  By all means faff about with a select committee inquiry, and join in the tutt-tuttery. <i>(Oct 5, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### tutter
 
 <i>n.</i> an abusive tem of address, or an obscene word for female genitals (cf. titoa) [ca1935 Havelock children ?imitated from Maori children]

### tutu
 
 <i>n.</i> a little look around, maybe even a poke around a room; used in the phrase "have a little tutu".

>  No, it was a 37-year-old, self taught mum of four - who learned her computer skills by "having a tutu". <i>(May 2005,Kokiri Paetae,1</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [tutu plant, tutu leaves, tutu tree, etc]

>  Numbers of trout are to be seen lying dead in many of the streams and rivers throughout the Masterton district. This is owing to the trout devouring the numerous green beetles which have been poisoned with the green tutu leaves which overhang the water in many parts. <i>(Jan 12, 1895,Thames Star,3</i>)

>  The writer had no pen or ink or pencil, but contrived to write with a pointed stick dipped in some reddish mixture, supposed to have been made from the tutu plant. <i>(Feb 9, 1911,Thames Star,2</i>)

>  There is still a large field for investigation in regard to the anomalous action of tutu plant on stock in the field, and why at times it may be consumed as a regular part of the diet and at other times may act as a powerful and immediate poison; possibly there may be a very simply explanation for this. <i>(Oct 10, 1934,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  The Native Reserve there is Hau-tutu (to chop up tutu tree). <i>(Sep 26, 1903,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

>  We were preparing roosts in the highest tutu trees when the rain ceased and the stormwaters quickly subsided. <i>(Jan 4, 1929,Evening Post,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an owl

>  ...swarms of fantails tease the cats, fat kereru perform nail-biting high-wire acts and tutu hoot through the night. <i>(Jul 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E12</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a large shrub whose pulpy black berries contain poisonous seeds, and whose leaves can poison stock. [Maori]

>  "Tutu'" is easily destroyed by fire. <i>(Jul 29, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,34</i>)

>  <b>OVERTON</b> He again baled up in some tutu on the other side of the stream and was then finished off with the spear... <i>(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,22</i>)

>  The name `tutu' is applied to three distinct species of the ... natural order <i>Coriariae: Coriaria ruscifolia</i>, L. (<i>C. sarmentosa</i>, Forst., <i>C. arborea</i> and <i>C. tutu</i>, Lindsay, tutu, pohou, and tupakihi of the Maori), is commonly known as the tree-toot ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/347</i>)

>  One [sc. of the cattle] had died from eating tutu, a poisonous herb. <i>(Apr 12, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,11</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER139</b> There are no poisonous plants with the solitary exception of the Toot plant. (Fn) The Toot-plant, Tutu or Tupakihi of the Maoris.. is a small bush. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter)</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Barley grass and toot to the horizon. <i>(1998,From Top to Bottom : Reinga to Bluff,117</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Five-finger, tu-tu, koromiko and gig hung in tangled profusion all along the river-bank... <i>(1936,Follow the Call,107</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> The fern is fired; if it burns well, all the thick and matted dead stuff at the bottom.. will be consumed, leaving only the shrivelled 'Tutu'; and the cane-like fern stalks.. should be cut at once, either with a strong hook, or, better still, with a short scythe, and the 'Tutu' slashed down with a bill-hook. <i>(1978,From New Plymouth to New Plymouth,138</i>) [NOTES: Quotation from HURSTHOUSE 1849]

>  <b>MORELAND</b> Leaving the horses tied below, we began a toilsome ascent through a belt of tutu - a stout herb growing as high as our shoulders... [Note.] <i>Coriaria ruscifolia</i>. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,174</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> The ridges clothed with the snow-grass tussock, the gullies growing fern and tutu. <i>(1890,History of Oamaru & North Otago,25</i>) [NOTES: quote from 1856]

>  This has been a day of sorrow.. one of our best cows died of tut and curiosity <i>(Sep 30, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,40</i>) [NOTES: check spelling - "tut" or "tutu"?]

>  For short periods, under certain adverse conditions, bees may produce poisonous honey derived from tutu, a highly toxic native plant. <i>(Feb 28, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a small shrubby native tree of Coriaria spp., poisonous to stock.

>  <b>SEWELL</b> The sheep were all down with the tutu. <i>(Jan 25, 1856,Sewell Journal 1853-1857,212</i>) [NOTES: in McIntyre (1980) Sewell Journal 1853-1856]

>  DEANS Letter 30 in Deans (1964) Pioneers on Port Cooper Plains 100 .. we all helped him, raking and gathering tutu roots, which grew very strong there, the berries being very poisonous to people, horses, sheep and cattle. When dry the roots made capital firewood ... <i>(1857</i>)

>  Tutu is a plant which dies away in winter. <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,82</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  Perhaps some of your experienced well-informed country readers would inform me as to the best plan for dealing with cattle that have got 'staggers' from eating tutu or tupakihi. <i>(Nov 9, 1867,Weekly News,12</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> .. native trees in great numbers, and the poisonous 'toot' plant, a small shrub terribly destructive to sheep and cattle. <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,170</i>)

>  The uncultivated land was surface sown, and the grass has grown for over twenty years .. High fern, flax and tutu covered the ground ... <i>(May 12, 1888,Egmont Star,6</i>)

>  Fern and tutu hills were selected for pastoral activities. <i>(Mar 15, 1947,NZ Journal of Agriculture,244</i>)

>  On many farms tutu has now been completely eradicated. <i>(Feb 24, 1966,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

>  <b>FISK</b> Coriara arborea (tree tutu). [toot] <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,209</i>)

>  The south-west wind blew as hard on them as on us, but they had no shelter for the cattle which would find the beds of creeks and rivers, where grew the deadly native poison plant tutu. <i>(Oct 26, 1945,Ellesmere Guardian,5</i>)

>  Stock were also affected by eating tutu. <i>(May 14, 1943,Ellesmere Guardian,5</i>)

>  A farmer is credited with saying that he has seen cattle eat tutu, and suffer no ill effects from it, whilst others he had observed eating it very shortly developed symptoms of poisoning. <i>(May 6, 1911,Marlborough Express,5</i>)

>  Children Poisoned With Tutu. <i>(Aug 13, 1885,Evening Post,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Report on the Dune-areas of NZ <i>(1911,AJHR,C-13</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> ... the prevailing growth [at Taieri mouth] is flax, fern, toot, and grass. <i>(May 4, 1844,Diary,215</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 4 and 5 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 215]

>  [The bull] runs a risk from a plant called the `toot', which is greedily fed on. <i>(1848,Otago Journal,3/41</i>) [NOTES: (Rev. J. Burns)]

>  ... flax, toots etc. <i>(1850,Otago Journal,6/107</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDSON</b> [North Island] ... flax, fern and toot. <i>(1854,Summer's Excursion,114</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> The too-well-known `Toot' of the Otago farmer; the `Wine-berry shrub' of the early settlers of the Bay of Islands, (according to Dr. Bennett [`Wanderings in Australia'] and the `Treasury of Botany'); the `Tutu,' `Tupakihi,' and `Puhou' of the North Island Maoris; the `Taweku' of the Waikatos; the `Tupake' [a-macron, e-acute] of Coromandel district; the `Tua-tutu' of Otago, (Hooker). ... [`Tutu'] appears also to be a generic or general [term] ... <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,85</i>)

>  I find the opinion that there are three `kinds' of `toot' unanimous among the more observant settlers alike of Otago and Auckland. <i>(1870,TrNZI,174</i>) [NOTES: (Lindsay)]

>  His hearty salutation in its faultiness proved to be about on a par with `rummy rum', `triddy', and `toot'. The last word reminds me of a man near by who was even judged to be somewhat vain of his Maori accent and pronunciation. With one word indeed he was very particular, he could not bring himself to use that manifest corruption `toot'. With him it was ever `tutu'. He had to make rather a boggle or dodge of it when he used the colonial made verb formed on his favourite Maori noun. <i>(1891,NZ Country Journal,201</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts, Morris)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Toot, n.</i> the anglicised spelling of the Maori word <i>Tutu</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,475</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Tute</i>, correctly <i>tutu</i> (toot): A poisonous native shrub. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,14</i>)

>  <i> toot n.</i> a shrub or small tree with astringent bark, the berries of which can be made into wine but the seeds are poisonous.-toot'ed a. poisoned by toot. [Maori tutu] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

>  Tutu (-->toot, -->tute .. .. tupakihi, tutu .. .. <i>Coriaria </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>toot n</i>. slang for <i>tutu (q.v.). </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),20</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> There was a shrub like a peach tree, with red flowers. Doug called it `tute'. <i>(1985,G'day Country,150</i>)

>  <i>toot</i> var. of TUTU. <i>(1960,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,811</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 506]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> esp. <i>C. sarmentosa, C. aborea</i> [(cf tupakihi; and cf 1841 citation for reference to the juice of the plant)]

>  TUTU, Coriaria arborea, is one of seven Coriaria species endemic to New Zealand. <i>(Apr 30, 2005,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> T|utu, <i>s.</i> Fruit from the Tu p|akii. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,220</i>)

>  ... his face and hands were stained with the juice of Etootoo, a berry which when squeezed, affords a nutritious beverage; ... It being Summer time, we had abundance of the Etootoo to drink. This is a berry that grows in clusters like currants, on a plant about three feet high, in colour they are like the elder-berry; they are full of seeds, which are of a pernicious nature. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,68,1986 ed.</i>)

>  He [a Maori] was a stout tall fellow ... & his face and hands were stained with the juice of <i>Etootoo</i>, a berry which when squeezed, affords a nutritious beverage, .... <i>(1827,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 185]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> the berry ... tutu or tuparki ... <i>(1837,British Colonization of NZ,324</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Near the beach appear shrublike veronicas [fn Veronica ligustrifolia.], myrtles [fn Myrtus bullata.], fuchsias, solanum [fn Solanum laciniatum.], the karaka tree [fn Corynocarpus laevigata.], tutu [fn Coriaria sarmentosa.], and flax [fn Phormium tenax.]. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/26</i>)

>  Its current is rapid ... enclosing islands covered with a jungle of tu-tu and toi-toi. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 236]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> This was the season when the fruit of the `tutu' or `tupakihi', as it is called in the North Island, was ripe. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,191</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 8 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 191]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Rested at Okiwi; one ewe died through eating Tutu. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,25</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Wakefield Handbook for NZ 124 [1845 August] 25.]

>  - saw a cow (dead of tutu). <i>(Dec 29, 1850,Torlesse Papers,178,1958 ed.</i>)

>  ... three [cows] have died, Mr. Brittan's by falling over a cliff, Mr. Fitzgerald's and Mr. Phillips's by eating tutu. ... To beasts just out of a ship, the tutu, of which there is abundance here, is certainly fatal. <i>(Jan 11, 1851,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  I ... was occupied two hours in searching amongst the tutu and fern for the boundary posts of the section required; ... <i>(Jun 14, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  ... generally about ten per cent [of imported stock] die ... partly from the `tutu,' a poisonous plant which the stock eat greedily, and which has an effect on them not unlike Strychnine. ... After about a month this plant ceases to effect them and they thrive upon it. <i>(Jul 1, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tutu</i>, or <i>Toot, n</i>. Maori name for a shrub or small tree, <i>Coriaria ruscifolia,</i> Linn., or <i>C. sarmentosa,</i> Forst., of New Zealand, widely distributed. It bears greenish flowers, and shiny pulpy black berries. From these the Maoris make a wine resembling light claret, taking care to strain out and not to crush the seeds, which are poisonous, with an action similar to that of strychnine. It goes also by the name of <i>Wineberry-bush</i>, and the Maori name is Anglicised into <i>Toot.</i> In Maori, the final <i>u</i> is swallowed rather than pronounced. In English names derived from the Maori, a vowel after a mute letter is not sounded. It is called in the North Island <i>Tupakihi.</i> In Maori, the verb <i>tutu</i> means to be hit, wounded, or vehemently wild, and the name of the plant thus seems to be connected with the effects produced by its poison. To `eat your toot': used as a slang phrase; to become acclimatised, to settle down into colonial ways. <i>(1898,Austral English,485</i>)

>  <i>Tu&#769;.tu</i> (tu&#769;.tu; <i>colloq. t&omacr.&omacr.t</i>), n.<i> [Maori.] [Written also</i> toot.] <i>(Bot.)</i> A New Zealand shrub (<i>Coriarai ruscifolia</i>) of the order <i>Coriariae,</i> extremely poisonous to cattle. [Local, N. Z.] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2033</i>)

>  The name `tutu' is applied to three distinct species of the ... natural order <i>Coriariae: Coriaria ruscifolia</i>, L. (<i>C. sarmentosa</i>, Forst., <i>C. arborea </i>and <i>C. tutu,</i> Lindsay, tutu, pohou, and tupakihi of the Maori), is commonly known as the tree-toot ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/347</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> In case ... of poisoning by having swallowed the seeds of the <i>tutu</i> or <i>tupakihi</i> plant (Coriaria ruscifolia) the smoke remedy was resorted to ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,18</i>)

>  <i>tutu</i>: small tree with poisonous berries. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,176</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> (gloss.) tutu: often called `toot' by Europeans, native shrub poisonous to cattle. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,273,1974 ed. Rhodes ed.</i>)

>  <i>tutu</i> Prounced by Europeans as `toot'. A New Zealand plant bearing shiny black berries from which the Maoris made a kind of wine. The plant is poisonous to stock; animals affected by it are said to be `tooted'. (Pronounce .us too|-too|). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),426</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> <i>(1960,Maori Girl</i>) [NOTES: see cit taupata]

>  <i>tutu n.</i> small New Zealand shrub, with poisonous seeds, the pulpy black berries being used by the Maoris to make wine [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),627</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> TUTU A poisonous shrub. <i>Coriaria sarmentosa. </i> <i>(1967,Evil Day,382</i>)

>  <i>tutu ... n</i>. small N.Z. shrub, with poisonous seeds, the pulpy black berries being used by Maoris to make wine [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21</i>)

>  <i>tutu</i> [s] <i>Coriaria sarmentosa</i> Forst.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,81</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Coriaria arborea </i>Linds. Common name: <i>Toot</i> Maori names: <i>Tutu, tupakihi, puhou</i> ... The New Zealand species of <i>Coriaria </i>were revised by Oliver and the name<i> C. arborea </i>Lindsay was applied to the common large-leaved shrub formerly regarded as identical with the South American <i>C. ruscifolia</i> L. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,45</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> A more flavoursome delicacy was prepared by soaking the [fernroot] cakes in the juice of tutu (<i>Coriaria</i>) petals. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,5</i>)

>  <i>tutu n.</i> NZ shrub with black berries that contain poisonous seeds. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,832</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 517]]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Coriaria ruscifolia, Linn.</i>] <i>Tutu; Tupakihi.</i> ... The poisonous principle appears to be a glucoside, to which the name `tutin' has been applied. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,106</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> TUTU (<i>Coriaria ruscifolia</i> [spp.]), usually corrupted into `toot,' is a native of New Zealand, abundant in the open country throughout. It usually grows 2 or 3 feet high ... Tutu is one of the best known poisonous plants in this country, the poison occurring in the young shoots and seeds. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,120,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i> tutu n.</i> see TOOT <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,12</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> The natives were very fond of a plant called <i>tutu</i>, ... <i>(1930,NZ Memories,115</i>)

>  Pronounced `toot'. `Various species of Coriaria. A poisonous plant. There are three sizes and the smaller (like a fern) is the most dangerous. The past participle of the verb meaning to be poisoned by t., is spelt either <i>tooted</i> or<i> tuted. </i> <i>(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <i>tutu</i>, a small tree (Coriaria Ruscifolia); its young leaf-shoots are poisonous to stock, especially when hungry; the seeds and stalks poisonous to man. C. Thymofolia is tutu-heuheu and tutu-papa. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/147</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> TUTU .us (coriaria sarmentosa),;a shrub with long feeble branches, its fruit growing in racemes or clusters. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,314</i>)

>  <b>ALLOM</b> There is a great variety of native grasses [in the Wairarapa]; ... mixed with other kinds of vegetation, such as the coarse grasses, fern, <i>tohi-tohi,</i> porcupine grass, flax, <i>tutu, manuka</i>, and others. [? itals indicate native names]. <i>(1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 119]

>  <i>Coriaria sarmentosa</i> tutu <i>(Jan 1856,Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal</i>) [NOTES: (And)1856 Tancred Notes on the Natural History of ... Canterbury (Edinburgh New Philos. Journal (NS) Jan 1856)]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>) [NOTES: 1857. R.Wilkin, in a letter printed by C. Hursthouse, `New Zealand,' p. 372: [+other cits incl. tree tutu]]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The tutu not having yet begun to spring I yarded my bullocks at Main's. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,vii/97</i>) [NOTES: 1860 in 1863 Butler First Year vii/97]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Tutu (pronounced toot) is a plant which abounds upon the plains for some miles near the river-beds; ... <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ix/131</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tutu, <i>Col[enso]. Coriaria ruscifolia. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The toot-plant, tutu or tupakihi of the Maoris ... is a small bush, one of the most common and widely distributed shrubs of the islands. It produces a sort of `hoven' or narcotic effect on sheep and cattle, when too greedily eaten. It bears a fruit, which is produced in clusters, not unlike a bunch of currants, with the seed external, of a purple colour. The poisonous portion of the plant to man are the seeds and seed-stalks, while their dark purple pulp is utterly innocuous and edible. The natives express from the berries an agreeable violet juice (carefully avoiding the seed), called native wine. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),139</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  (<i>Coriaria ruscifolia</i>, var). The plant known as Tutu, on the pasture lands of Otago, is a strong robust shrub 3-6 feet high, dying down to the ground every year. <i>Ibid.</i> 40 Tutu. Coriaria ruscifolia, Linn. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/34</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> NOTE. - As I have no desire to mystify readers unacquainted with New Zealand flora, I append a brief description of the shrubs mentioned:-.. <i> Tutu </i>- A berry-bearing, glossy-leaved plant, deadly to man and to all animals - except goats. <i>Tumata-kuru</i> - Better known as `Wild Irishman' - a thorny plant, very difficult to handle. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iii/16,1889,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Tutu or Tupaki ..... <i>O</i>. Coriariae;<i> G</i>. Coriaria; <i>S</i>. C. ruscifolia. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/302</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> CORIARIA RUSCIFOLIA (<i>Linn</i>.) ... The `TUTU', or TUPAKIHI'.- <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Taweku [Maori name] .. Tutu [Settlers' name] .. Coriaria ruscifolia ..Shrub ... Tutu [Maori name] .. Tutu [Settlers' name] .. Coriaria ruscifolia .. Shrub Tupkakihi [Maori name] .. Tutu [Settlers' name] .. Coriaria ruscifolia ..Shrub <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,103</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Tutu - a wine made from the berries of the tupakihi <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/390</i>)

>  <b>WADE</b> The Tupakihi ... produces ... berries, the expressed unfermented juice of which is called tutu. <i>(1841,Journey in NZ,57</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  A combination of cooked karengo and tutu juice was fermented and used as a laxative by pre-contact Maori - though I wouldn't recommend the potion to amateur herbalists, who might find their self-healing process turns into a suicide mission, given that every other part of the tutu berry, apart from its juice, is deadly poisonous. <i>(Oct 12, 2002,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  Perhaps the most infamous of all native plants is the tutu family. Steams, leaves and seeds of these shrubs and small trees are extremely poisonous and paralysis and death may occur if eaten. It has small green flowers, purplish-black fruit enclosed by enlarged petals, and the new shoots from the base look like overgrown asparagus. <i>(Feb 3, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

>  <b>CONNOR</b> Tutu beer and tutu pie made with fruits including the seeds caused death or serious illnesses in Europeans early in New Zealand history. <i>(1977,Poisonous Plants of NZ, The,52</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Simonin gathered a handkerchief full of tutu berries, beat them up, squeezed them, and got a pint of wine, which was first-rate drink. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,402</i>) [NOTES: Quote from Journal of A.J. Barrington, March 16, 1864]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Simonin gathered a handkerchief full of tutu berries, beat them up, squeezed them, and got a pint of wine, which was first-rate drink. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,402</i>) [NOTES: Quote from Journal of A.J. Barrington, March 16, 1864]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Many cows ate the dark green leaves of tutu bush and died. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,77</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> <i>C[oriaria] arborea, (C. ruscifolia,</i> Auct. pr. p.) The `Tree Tutu;' which Dr. Hector asserts is quite distinct from the common `Herbaceous Tutu,' or `Tutu' proper. ... Tree Toot also occurs in the forests of the Te Anau and Manipori Lakes. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,84</i>)

>  Tree Tutu (<i>Coriaria ruscifolia</i>). A small shrub-tree, with a trunk 6-8 inches diameter, growing often solitary in the bush. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/34</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Coriaria rusifolia</i>, Linn. Tupakihi, Tree Tutu.--A perennial shrub 10-18 feet high; trunk 6-8 inches in diameter. The seeds and leaves contain a poisonous alkaloid. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,131</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The other New Zealand species [of<i>Coriaria</i> are <i>C. lurida, C. angustissima,</i> and <i>C. arborea</i>, the tree tutu. There are many hybrids. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,241</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> [On] the forest margin the TREE TUTU (toot) (<i>Coriaria arborea</i>) is a common small tree ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,183,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <i>tree tutu [s] Coriaria arborea</i> Lindsay <i>Ibid.</i> 81 <i>tutu, tree</i> [s] <i>Coriaria arborea</i> Lindsay <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,80</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>CORIARIA ARBOREA</i>. Tree tutu is found throughout [New Zealand]. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,64</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Coriaria arborea</i> TREE TUTU ... Shrub with stout, arching, perennial branches. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,112</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... shrubs common in the tussock zone: matagouri, native brooms, dwarf koromikos, monoao, sarmentose tutu, the grasslike spiky-leaved Spaniard, the harsh patotara, golden cottonwood, cushion-like pimeleas, and the low, matted, carpet-like growth of the scabweeds are characteristic of the low tussock country, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,35,1970 ed.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Coriaria sarmentosa (The Shrubby Tutu) </i>... Maori name <i>Tupakihi</i> or<i> Tutu</i> (usually pronounced <i>Toot</i>). <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,239</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Coriaria plumosa</i> FEATHERY TUTU ...Summer-green prostrate to ascending shoots in colonies. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,112</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> having tutu endemic esp. tutu (toot) plant

>  Tutu bush ... and other shrubs. <i>(1843,Letters from Settlers</i>) [NOTES: (H.S.Tiffen) 1842]

>  <b>ED. WARD</b> ... saw his cow lying dying of `toot' poison. <i>(Jan 6, 1851,Journal,101,1951 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> From the poisonous Tutu berries (<i>Coriaria sarmentosa</i>) a grateful and not intoxicating drink was expressed ... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/19,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The toot-plant, tutu or tupakikihi [?check] of the Maoris ... <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),139</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> By reason of its including the too-familiar and fatal <i>`Toot'</i> plant, [<i>Coriaria</i>] is ... of no secondary importance to the New Zealand colonist. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,83</i>)

>  <i>Coriaria sarmentosa</i> spg TOOT PLANT <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Coriaria sarmentosa </i>spg TOOT PLANT <i>(1908,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>78</b> I mention this cure because I have actually seen it done when droving cattle through tutu-areas. <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> One died as a small boy - eating `toot' berries on his way home from school. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,140</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Haul out the toot roots and everything near ye;/ Grub away, tug away, toil till you're weary,/ <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,4</i>) [NOTES: [1852-1860 Barr] in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 4]

### tutu around
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  I'm stuck here like a beached whale and he's off tutuing around sight-seeing! <i>(Jul 10, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),81</i>)

### tutu bed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a grove of poisonous Coriaria or tutu.

>  <b>SEWELL</b> It seems that the spot where the sheep were placed was just upon the borders of a tutu bed ... <i>(Jan 25, 1856,Sewell Journal 1853-1857,213</i>) [NOTES: in McIntyre (1980)]

### tutu beer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tutu beer and tutu pies made from the edible berries - but accidentally including seeds or other parts of the plant - have caused death and serious illness to people. <i>(Feb 26, 1985,Dominion,3</i>)

### tutu berry
 
 <i>n.</i> [tutu-berry]

>  The infant son of Samuel Rogers, Te Awamutu, has been poisoned by eating tutu berries. <i>(Sep 30, 1882,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Recently United States Consular reports contained the formulae for making ink from tutu berries. <i>(Mar 12, 1918,Thames Star,2</i>)

>  .. Mr. Freeman recalled to mind that one man died through eating a pudding he had made for himself containing tutu berries. <i>(Apr 30, 1924,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Three young Maori children were poisoned on Sunday afternoon through eating tutu berries, states a Thames correspondent. <i>(Sep 7, 1938,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  In another case two young men partook of some tutu-berries, about 4 p.m. <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/290</i>)

### tutu grove
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a closely-growing group of poisonous Coriaria or tutu shrubs.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Certainly there was no grass in it, but the tutu-groves sprouting again from their burnt stools were open. <i>(1921,Tutira,167</i>)

### tutu land
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) country upon which the poisonous Coriaria or tutu grows [ thought to be a sign of fertility.]

>  <b>PAUL</b> The tutu land will yield very good crops. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,96</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> If the soil is good, such as strong flax and grass, or tutu land, two or three successive crops of wheat may be taken ... <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,98</i>)

### tutu poison
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  TUTU POISON .. At the Otago Institute, Dr. Malcolm detailed the results of experiments with the toxic properties of the tutu which should be of immediate interest in a sheep-growing country. <i>(Jan 6, 1914,Marlborough Express,4</i>)

### tutu poisoning
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) sickness and possible death of farm stock from the eating of tutu. [1907 citation: toot-poisoning]

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> There were no tracks - just hard punching all the way, with a fair number of sheep dying from tutu poisoning. <i>(1949,Early NZ Families,5</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Since increasing the cattle numbers and top-dressed areas, the station has lost more cattle through tutu poisoning. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,160</i>)

>  <b>PULLEN</b> After that we had trouble with tutu poisoning. <i>(1996,On the Road Again,29</i>)

>  T. Venn on Tutu poisoning case ... 283. <i>(Jan 2, 1862,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Antidote for Tutu poisoning. <i>(Apr 18, 1892,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Thus Dr. Lauder Lindsay states in the <i>British Medical Review</i> (July 1865): `He seemed a fortunate farmer or runholder who had not lost more than 25 per cent. of his stock from toot-poisoning, whilst in some instances, the losses were so high as 75 per cent.' <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,228,1940, p. 241 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1865 in 1907 [1940] Laing and Blackwell Plants of NZ 228 [241]]

>  Birds are said to be unaffected by the seeds, but ... domestic fowls have been poisoned by eating the berries, the symptoms being typical of tutu-poisoning. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/346</i>)

>  Treatment of a beast suffering from tutu poisoning by pulling its tongue as far as possible out of the mouth towards the ear would be of very little avail. <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,78</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> The danger of tutu poisoning and the pleuro scare made constant observation of the cattle necessary, but all went well in this corner of the south. <i>(1937,Thomas,115</i>)

>  Do any readers know a remedy for tutu poisoning? <i>(Sep 13, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,40</i>)

>  Mr. C. H. Mills, of Havelock, formerly a resident of Wellington, states in a letter to a local paper that his brother, Mr. T. H. Mills, has found kerosene a complete antidote for tutu poisoning in cattle. <i>(Jun 2, 1881,Evening Post,2</i>)

### tutu pudding
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tutu Pudding [page] 61. <i>(Jul 15, 1897,Otago Witness,33</i>)

>  Isaac Hill seized the bucket and ran towards the river, but he tumbled over and began to imitate Goulter so exactly that we all were reminded of the tutu pudding. <i>(May 5, 1928,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  It was very evident and very fortunate that we had not put on our best clothes for that Sunday's work, and it was highly probable that none of us would ever eat any more tutu pudding. <i>(Jul 30, 1897,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

### tutu soil
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high-fertility soil that supports Coriaria or tutu.

>  <b>PAUL</b> Tutu soil is the most expensive to break up ... <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,97</i>)

### tutu wine
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  To experiment upon the flax is inexpensive and easy to accomplish, and some of our brewers who desire to make themselves famous might try both that and the manufacture of tutu wine. <i>(Jul 8, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> With the advent of 'waipiro' it no longer became necessary to make tu-tu wine. <i>(1915,Reminiscences of Earliest Canterbury,19</i>)

>  Indeed, in the very early days the tutu was known as the wine-berry shrub. This wine, however, has not always proved to be above suspicion. Canon Stack ... relates that ... after partaking of some tutu-wine he was seized with alarming symptoms. <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/292</i>)

>  <b>HUTT</b> A Canterbury Pakeha settler in the 1840s recorded that gourds were made into wine containers for fermenting, but "with the advent of 'waipiro' it no longer became necessary to make tu-tu wine". <i>(1999,Maori & Alcohol,14</i>)

>  The men thought it would be the wiser to give me a deep draught of tutu wine that would lull me to eternal sleep, but the women pleaded hard that I might be allowed to live amongst them, and the King, full of mercy, gave me his support. <i>(Sep 24, 1887,Tuapeka Times,5</i>)

### tutu-grown
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> We turned off ... where a track opened up in a tutu-grown valley between two mighty green hills <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,4</i>)

### tutu-papa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Tutupapa, <i>Col[enso]. Coriaria thymifolia. <i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  ...the fruit of the Tutu-papa (<i>Coriaria thymifolia</i>)... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a type of tutu, Coriaria plumosa. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>CROWE</b> The Maori, however, commonly consumed the juice of the petals of these two plants and according to Colenso, another small-leaved one called tutu-papa, in a variety of ways. Tutu-papa is Coriaria plumosa according to Beever. <i>(1990,Native Edible Plants of NZ,63</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### tutu-tree
 
 <i>n.</i> [contrast tree-tutu (no entry for this TD)] (See [tutu](tot-tyr.md#tutu))

>  <b>DOMETT</b> So men compared him to the <i>tutu</i>-tree,/ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/152</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> And there's the lights on the leaves of the taupata, and on the tutu trees over the creek where the cows can't get at them. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,215</i>)

>  Some were cases of cattle living constantly on the open run, but from some cause or other were penned at night, and let out in the morning to the run with empty stomachs, and having found a 'Tutu' tree they never left it until all the shoots were consumed, and in consequence in a short time delirium would be almost certain to follow, and then death, unless discovered by the owners and well dosed and bled, in which case, unless too far gone, they might be saved. <i>(Dec 27, 1859,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  There are plenty of tutu trees in Cambridge. <i>(Nov 1, 1865,Daily Southern Cross,6</i>)

>  At this point the rata begins to grow, although it is not seen to much advantage until further up the lake, and here also the tutu tree begins to attain considerable dimensions. <i>(Mar 30, 1870,Star, The,2</i>)

>  A seven-year-old boy of Mr Steven Liddle, of Kaiti, was poisoned on Saturday by eating the young shoots of the tutu tree. <i>(Nov 30, 1896,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

### tutua
 
 <i>n.</i> a worthless person, a nobody [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>MANING</b> But then certainly, the pakeha was a <i>tuatua</i>, a nobody, a fellow not worth a spike nail; <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(ii) 19</i>) [NOTES: [check spg]]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Within each tribe the population was further divided into slaves, who were mostly prisoners of war,<i> tutua</i> or commoners, and the numerous class of chiefs, <i>rangatira</i>. At the head of the tribe there was a paramount chief, the <i>ariki.</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 302 [Maori glossary] <i>tutua </i>Low-born (person) <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,20</i>)

>  Rangihaeta insisted that only the lives of the principal Europeans could compensate as sufficient utu for the loss of his wife. 'We are sure to be killed for this some day,' he told Te Rauparaha. 'The white people will take utu, let us then have some better blood than that of these tutua (common people).' <i>(Sep 1960,Te Ao Hou,32: 8-9</i>)

>  To steal a man's clothes was the act of a "tutua," a no-account fellow, but to steal his wife was the act of a chief. <i>(Mar 23, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  From Hemi te tutua. 'Hemi te tutua' is Jim the nobody. <i>(Jun 1981,Landfall,6(4) No. 31-32 : 115</i>)

### tutued
 
 <i>adj.</i> of stock, poisoned by eating tutu



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of a grazing animal] affected by eating tutu. [including tooted, tuted and tutu'd]

>  <b>KENNARD</b> On the trip we lost two bullocks drowned when crossing the Clutha and two died at Teschmaker's through getting 'tooted' ... <i>(1859,Journal,41</i>) [NOTES: in Hamilton (1952) History of Northern Southland]

>  As, then, my bullocks could not get tuted on being turned out empty, I yarded them. <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,83</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Those [animals] feeding amongst it .. are apt to be affected by it, or be, as the phrase is, 'tutued.' <i>(1974,Colonial Experiences,211</i>)

>  Two bullocks dead at Troutbecks'. One "tuted" the other bogged. <i>(Jan 8, 1879,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> If they [sheep] were allowed to feed or rest there soon after landing, probably half of them would be 'tuted' and lost. <i>(1900,BLANK ENTRY,120</i>) [NOTES: in Canterbury Old and New]

>  <b>HALL</b> We also find here and there patches of the celebrate tutu, whose poisonous properties have proved so destructive to stock in all parts of New Zealand that the word 'tuted' ie poisoned by eating tutu - has become a necessary part of our vocabulary. <i>(1900,BLANK ENTRY,181</i>) [NOTES: in Canterbury Old and New]

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Got a few [sheep] tutued coming through Aotea but did not lose any. <i>(Feb 24, 1909,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Had a bit of trouble with the sheep that we tutued yesterday. <i>(Feb 25, 1909,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Laurie & Person Bros. looked around the fences & whilst away came across a tutued steer. <i>(Jul 1, 1914,Stevens Diary</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> It came on to rain so Walker moved the sheep down the hill to shelter and got 70 of them tooted. <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,92</i>)

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> .. a driver with a 'tuted' mob had many cares on his shoulders. <i>(1949,Sheep and Sheepmen of Canterbury,31</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> With tuted cows on farms, they have a sort of a frame thing to put them in ... [tooted; tuted] <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,67</i>)

>  [Hdg] Tutued Sheep <i>(Jun 15, 1916,Lake County Press,5</i>)

>  It appears that a station manager in going his rounds.. found a good fat bullock tutued. of which he could not save the life. <i>(Apr 10, 1890,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  TREATMENT OF TUTU'D CATTLE. <i>(Jun 28, 1875,Clutha Leader,3</i>)

>  <i>Toot&#769;.ed </i> (t&omacr.&omacr.t&#769;.&ebreve.d), a. Poisoned by tutu; - said of <i>u&#769;.tued </i> cattle. <i>[Local, N. Z.] </i> [SP] Bleeding and a dose of ammonia were usually tried on <i>tutued </i>animals. </i>Ibid.</i> . 2033 <i>Tutu&#769;.ed</i> (t&omacr.&omacr.ed), a. See TOOTED. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2032</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [Sheep] feeding amongst [tutu], on being dogged or driven, are apt to be affected by it, or be, as the phrase is, `tutued'. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,211</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> To animals this shrub [tutu] was deadly poisonous, especially to cattle, and my grandfather had many `tutu-ted'. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,115</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> [1879 Kati Kati] Our cow, a recent purchase, was found next day staggering about, wheeling round and round in a most alarming fashion. Luckily, a half-caste passing by saw her, and said, `Your cow is 'tutu'd'; give me a sharp knife,' and promptly bled her by cutting her ears, when she fell down exhausted, but did not die, as many of our cows did from time to time later on, when having eaten too greedily of a poisonous native plant `tutu.' <i>(1908,My Simple Life in NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tooted</i> quasi <i>past participle</i> from <i>Toot.</i> The cattle are <i>tooted,</i> sc. poisoned by the <i>Toot.</i> [1863, 1891 Potts] <i>(1898,Austral English,475</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Tooted</i> quasi <i>past participle</i> from <i>Toot.</i> The cattle are <i>tooted,</i> sc. poisoned by the <i>Toot.</i> [1863, 1891 Potts] <i>(1898,Austral English,475</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  Tutuheuheu. Coriaria angustissima, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/40</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

### tutukiwi
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pterostylis</i>, an orchid

>  And here and there we came at other times on the curious green elfs-hood or tutukiwi, a ground-orchid; or the difficult to see grey spotted huperei, another ground-orchid, a gastrodia. <i>(Mar 20, 1934,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Pterostylis Banksii (Banks' Pterostylis)</i> ... Maori name, <i>Tutu Kiwi. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,129</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>PTEROSTYLIS ... Banksii.</i> ... The hood is upright at the bottom and then curves forward, the upper sepal often forming a long tail. ... [24] Elf's Hood. TUTU-KIWI. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,23</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Much the largest and commonest [helmet orchid] is the tutukiwi (<i>P[terostylis] bankii</i>), ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,309</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Pterostylis</i> GREENHOOD ORCHIDS ... Tutukiwi ... Greenhood are familiar Australasian orchids, but the individual species are not yet fully understood. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,290</i>)

>  One of my favourites, now in flower at Otari Wilton's Bush, is the greenhood orchid, tutukiwi (Pterostylis banksii)... It stands erect (tutu- stand erect; tutukiwi - standing kiwi) up to 30 centimetres tall, has four to six narrow leaves and a magnificent green, hood-like flower up to five centimetres high. <i>(Nov 20, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the sub-antarctic or Snares Island snipe, <i>Gallinago huegelii</i>

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SUB-ANTARCTIC SNIPE <i>Coenocorypha aucklandica Maori name</i>: Tutukiwi. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,139</i>)

### tuwhenua
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The disease known to the Taupo and East Coast tribes as "ngere-ngere," to the Ngapuhi and Northern tribes as "puhipuhi" and to the Wanganui and Western tribes as "tuwhenua," is one and the same disease, and is true leprosy. <i>(Jul 31, 1890,Otago Witness,19</i>)

### TVNZ
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Television New Zealand

### twacking
 
 <i>n.</i> violence by prison staff against inmates

>  Violence used against inmates by staff at Napier's Mangaroa Prison was common practice, according to a sociology lecturer... Staff concerned about the practice - called 'twacking' by inmates and staff - had told him of it about the middle of last year. <i>(Mar 30, 1993,Dominion,1</i>)

### twang
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is a perverse tendency to expect an accent because of his name [Lijvsebic], but the New Zealand twang is there if all-but educated out. <i>(1999,Sport,22</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Although he was ex-Hutt High, he now had an Oxford accent. He said his antipodean twang undermined his credibility with his patients. <i>(1985,Nest of Cuckoos,24</i>)

>  <b>HERZ</b> The disregard of the external evidences of culture, is shown in the speech of the people. Sad to relate, one far too often hears the younger generation talk with a twang that horrifies the ear of anyone used to good English ... A mother will request her daughter <i>`Kyte</i>' to ask the <i>`lydy'</i> whether she will<i> `tyke'</i> another piece of </i>`cyke'. </i>The vowels and diphthongs are so drawn and twisted that it is often difficult to recognise the King's English. ... This twang is worse in Australia than in New Zealand but is gaining ground here and ought to be strenuously eradicated by school-teachers, for it really does not sound nice, and robs sweet girlish lips of all their poetry. <i>(1912,New Zealand (various authors/titles),352</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> She spoke without the faintest `twang' which Creed had grown accustomed to expect, in a greater or lesser degree, amongst the back-block girls of New Zealand. ... [29] Billy [a back-country farm-boy] always said `didjer' [did you], and ... as no one had ever taught him ... that well-known and useful little formula `How now, brown cow!' in order to correct his colonial twang and get his vowel sounds rounded correctly, ... <i>(1924,Heather of the South,17, 29</i>)

>  A physical difference which is accentuating itself rapidly is to be found in what is known as the "Colonial twang," in speech. <i>(Jun 30, 1893,Star, The,2</i>)

### tweaker
 
 <i>n.</i> a heavy methamphetamine user

### tweedmen
 
 <i>n.</i>

### tweeds
 
 <i>n.</i> men's (sports) trousers [ca1935 at least He got his working tweeds caught in the fence. `I'll warm your tweeds for you.' said to a boy, `I'll give you a thrashing'.) ca1946 University students' `Down with the tweeds.']

>  <b>INGRAM</b> What a motley looking mob we were! ... Some wearing `bun' hats, some straws and others felts. Some in navy serge suits, some in grey tweeds, some even in knickerbockers ... <i>(Jan 4, 1917,Anzac Day,9,1987 ed.</i>)

>  Some drongo's beer spills on your tweeds/ As you're drinking, as you're drinking in the pubs./ <i>(1951,Moa on Lambton Quay,21</i>) [NOTES: (James Ryan)]

>  <b>REV.R.W.CONNELLY</b> As for clothing, I suggest as New Zealandisms: ... suit-coat (jacket), singlet (sometimes used in England but more commonly called Vest) tweeds and strides. <i>(Nov 26, 1957,Letter</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,75</i>) [NOTES: [see touch one's kick]]

### tweeler
 
 <i>n.</i>

### tweensitter
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>adj.</i> tiny

### twelve apostles
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the twelve-man team operating a threshing mill

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> twelve apostles: the team of twelve needed to operate a threshing mill. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,87</i>)

### Twelve Apostles
 
 <i>n.</i> see 1908 citation

>  The recent calls to the Legislative Council are giving rise to speculation as to whether the "Twelve Apostles" called in 1892 are likely to be re-appointed should the present Government be in office in October next. <i>(Jun 24, 1898,Nelson Evening Mail,3</i>)

>  The death of the Hon. Feldwick removes another of the "twelve apostles," by which name the dozen members of the Legislative Council first appointed under the seven years system in 1892 were known. <i>(Aug 8, 1908,Wanganui Herald,5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural, historical) twelve holders of large valuable Maori land holdings in Hawke's Bay, subject of a special commission in 1873

>  <b>CW RICHMOND</b> There were no small farmers. The Twelve Apostles hold the agricultural lands. <i>(Dec 11, 1879,Letter,471</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers II]

>  .. a block of the fattest land in the colony - was parcelled out amongst a syndicate of twelve gentlemen, notoriously known as the twelve apostles. <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Weekly News,8</i>)

>  'You've seen, my lad,' he pronounced, 'country that for years past has been an out-station of the Kereru run - land owned by the 'Twelve Apostles.' <i>(Jul 8, 1953,Free Lance,33</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The names of twelve owners of these properties were so much in the news at the time that they came to be known as 'The Twelve Apostles.' <i>(1958,Story of Hawke's Bay, The,171</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Thomas Tanner, who acted as chief negotiator for the purchasers (locally known as the Twelve Apostles) began with an extra-legal lease before the block went before the Native Land Court. <i>(1972,Evolution of Hawke's Bay Landed Society,54</i>)

>  <b>ROLLESTON</b> The notoriety of the defendants gave rise to their being called, variously the Twelve Apostles (actually, only seven were involved in the ownership of twelve shares) or the Forty Thieves. <i>(1980,Master, The,96</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> A rival of <i>The Hawke's Bay Herald, The Times</i> had increased its circulation prodigiously by publishing articles headed <i>Acts of the Apostles</i> and <i>Apostolic Creed</i>. Though there were 7 leases and 12 shares, not 12 apostles, the name stuck. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,12</i>) [NOTES: <i>Thomas Tanner and partners leased the Hastings Block in 1867 and purchased it in 1870, thus avoiding laws against purchasing land from the Maoris - the book is in the Beaglehole Collection</i>]

>  They were retained, for GBP300 to do all that the Heretaunga people required - I mean the "twelve apostles." <i>(1877,NZ Parliamentary Debates,XXV/316</i>)

>  I wish to illustrate what I mean by referring to the purchase of land in the Hawke's Bay District by the gentlemen who are known as "the Twelve Apostles."...Owing to the action taken by those "Apostles." the land has been largely increased in value. <i>(Sep 21, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  The Napier Telegraph has treated the Twelve Apostles of Hawke's Bay to some healthy reading for once. <i>(Nov 15, 1890,NZ Observer,1</i>)

>  Re railway gold passes, it is said ex-members have lost their [sic]; if so, call in all that are left and issue leather ones - the same as has been supplied to the twelve apostles and Sir R. Stout. <i>(Aug 14, 1893,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  He (Tiwai) overtook them in the ranges on their way to the Twelve Apostles, who are, I believe, somewhere inland. They are going for the purpose of consulting the Twelve and other Hau Hau tribes to commence hostilities in this quarter. <i>(May 3, 1867,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Had a conscientious person been there to value the land he would have assessed it as L180 an acre. This was a property among the Hawke's Bay twelve apostles' acquisitions. <i>(Feb 21, 1905,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> It was a Hastings lawyer did the fixing, to keep it out of the papers. He acts for a lot of those awesome Hawkes Bay families who came over with the Twelve Apostles. [fn] A nickname for the Founding Fathers of the town of of Hastings. <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,129</i>)

>  There is a Tory Party - or, in other words, what they call the land-grabbers, or "Twelve Apostles" in Hawke's Bay. <i>(1895,NZ Parliamentary Debates,84/182</i>)

### twelve o'clock hold
 
 <i>n.</i> sc. at the end of the LONG PULL

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> `When your dog gets the sheep `home', he must bring them right into the circle, on the far side if possible, and hold them perfectly still - yourself, the sheep, and the dog all in line: the sheep looking at the dog and the dog looking at the sheep, although the dog doesn't have to be in the ring. This is called the `twelve o'clock hold'. <i>(1970,Tat,119</i>)

### twelver
 
 <i>n.</i> a twelve-year sentence

Untold!' said that hardened space hero ... <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,103</i>)
