### twin
 
 <i>n.</i> [[??omit as an expected application of <i>twin</i>] (cf TRIPLET)]

>  <b>REED</b> A `twin', or `sister' tree, was less rare, though unusual. The two trunks were generally united near the base, but occasionally the separation occurred at a little height above ground. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,95</i>)

### twin lamb disease
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere, but recorded first in NZ] antepartum paralysis or sleepy sickness in ewes.

>  The troubles which are most likely to affect the ewes are (i) ante-partum paralysis, or as the farmer more frequently calls it, 'dopiness' 'twin lamb disease' or 'sleepy sickness' ... <i>(Sep 1, 1937,NZ Farmer,36</i>)

>  The more correct name for antepartum paralysis or sleepy sickness, is pregnancy toxaemia (toxaemia meaning poison). It is also sometimes known as twin lamb disease, from the fact that in 95 per cent of cases the ewes is found to be carrying two or more lambs. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,37</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> Twin lamb disease is another common name for this condition [sleepy sickness] as ewes carrying twins are most susceptible. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,98</i>)

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> Sleepy sickness (pregnancy toxaemia.) This disease is sometimes referred to as 'twin lamb disease' as it is more likely to develop in ewes carrying twins. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,163</i>)

>  <b>COATES</b> We called it twin lamb disease as it mostly affected older ewes carrying twins. <i>(1995,Dining With the Cats,111</i>)

### twink
 
 <i>n.</i> a white-out correction fluid

>  The [Collins English] dictionary does not, however, contain entries for <i>DPB, EFTPOS, GST, CNG, first fifteen, street kids, May holidays</i>, and it is still too early for <i>orange roughy, night shelter,</i> and <i>Rogernomics, twink</i> and <i>lotto</i> (New Zealand-style) ... <i>(Feb 27, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>) [NOTES: (G. Kennedy)]

### twinkie
 
 <i>n.</i> a silvereye

>  <i>tauhou</i>, a wandering bird (Zosterops halmaturina), blight-bird; twinkie; button-eye; wax-eye; ring-eye. Tauhou means stranger and this name was given by the Maori when the bird appeared, in 1856, in the central part of Newzealand, gradually spreading north and south till the islands were occupied. It is an Australian bird, and was blown across the Tasman sea in the year mentioned. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  It seems natural for Otago to adopt the greenie (tauhou), or waxeye, or silver-eye, or twinkie, many-names and many numbered <i>(Jul 25, 1938,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The white-eye, or twinkie, was first noticed in New Zealand about 1861. <i>(Sep 28, 1878,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  The common name for the wax-eye was, and is, "twinkie"; and "spug," - all the way from Scotland - stands for the sparrow. <i>(Nov 7, 1906,Otago Witness,90</i>)

### twinkle
 
 <i>n.</i> silvereye [perhaps erron. for twinkie q.v.]

>  Among their [sc. white-eyes'] familiar English names are, wax-eyes, blighties, winter migrants, pennyweights, and twinkles. <i>(Aug 10, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

### twinner
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe that is found, in scanning, to be carrying twins.

>  Should they be just the twinners or should they all be tagged and recorded? Most say just record the twinners and tag their lambs. <i>(Jul 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> There were not a lot of twins to be seen in the paddocks, but the flocks were big and if they went through enough sheep, they would get the few twinners they needed. <i>(1994,Lab Coats to Gumboots,1</i>)

>  Milk production will be poor, and post-lambing stress, especially among twinners, reduces their chances of bringing their lambs to the shearing board. <i>(Aug 19, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,16</i>)

### twinning
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [recorded early elsewhere but of significance in NZ for the fat lamb export industry] producing twin lambs.

>  The effect of flushing on twinning was quite large. Static and dynamic live-weight effects, as defined earlier, had about an equally important influence on twinning. <i>(Jan 1965,NZ Journal of Agriculture,59</i>)

>  .. we shall be able to increase twinning in your flock at a faster rate. <i>(Jul 1971,NZ Journal of Agriculture,102</i>)

### twinning paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area where scanned twinning ewes are placed to lamb.

>  If cover is so high as to suggest quality loss may occur in twinning paddocks after docking, twin ewes can be moved to paddocks for lambing singles ... <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Winter 23</i>)

### twinning rate
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the tendency of ewes to produce twins and the extent to which twin lambs are produced in a flock.

>  .. woolly-faced ewes .. gave fewer lambs, had a lower twinning rate, had a lower body weight, and were more difficult to get in lamb than open-faced ewes. <i>(Oct 15, 1962,NZ Journal of Agriculture,366</i>)

>  As the live weight of the ewes increase twinning rate increases at a consistent rate of about six extra lambs per 100 ewes for each 10 lb increase in live weight. <i>(Jan 1965,NZ Journal of Agriculture,57</i>)

>  The most striking result was that the flushed group..had a significantly higher twinning rate and slightly higher barrenness than the group losing weight. <i>(1965,NZ Journal of Agriculture,59</i>)

### twins
 
 <i>n.</i> a pair of twos (in a card hand)

>  Certain of the expressions are necessarily omitted from a publication of this nature. (We have omitted certain others - Ed.) TERMS MEANINGS DERIVATION / Twins Pair of 2s Two Little Ones / Triplets 3 twos Three Little Ones / Stiffy and Mo 2 Jacks A rather low pair Godivas 2 Queen[sic] (only) (Obscure) / 2 Bullets 2 Aces / Cradle Snatchers 2 Queens + 2 Jacks / Double Event 2 ` + 2 Kings / Jacob, Joseph, John 3 Jacks / Mary, Maud, Kate 3 Queens / Elingomites 3 Kings (site of Wreck) / Shrapnel 3 Aces / Three Weeks 3 7's / September 3 10's / Jacob, Joseph, John, / Jeremia or Rotarians 4 Jacks / Shrapnel or Bullets on the Roof Full hand or 2 pair on Aces / Gooseberry - 3 Kings and 2 Queens / The Worker - One Chip <i>(1928,Auckland University Carnival Book</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Auckland Students' Association Souvenir Programme of `Tilly in the City' 16]

### twinset
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) identical twin calves [used for research purposes.]

>  Research is continuing with the twinsets. <i>(Jul 1983,NZ Dairy Exporter,25</i>)

### twist
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  He said that accused asked them `if they could twist a drink.' Sub-Inspector: What is meant by twisting, eh? That which twists you, I suppose. (Laughter). Witness: The beer costs 4d a pot and three of us had a drink. No, I'm telling a big lie, I twisted 4 pots at 4d a pot, that's 1s 4d, isn't it? <i>(Nov 4, 1911,NZ Truth,332/6/2</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (underhandedly) take larger commission for car sales than agreed on with the vendor

>  One salesman.. said he alone "twisted" between 60 and 70 times.... They were running a racket, twisting... He knew about twisting, condoned it and quite possibly, perhaps probably, twisted himself. <i>(Apr 12, 1995,Evening Post,12</i>)

 <i>phr.</i>

### twister
 
 <i>n.</i> a tool to make a twitch in fencewire (See [wire-twister](../W#wire-twister))

>  <i>Twister:</i> Tool to make a twitch. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> =cockabully, (threefin) blenny

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Twister <i>Helcogramma medium</i> (Gunther) (Syn. <i>Tripterygion dorsalis</i> Clarke, and <i>Enneapterygius mortenseni</i> Rendahl) <i>(1955,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,411</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 411]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> TWISTER <i>Helcogramma medium </i>(Gunther) The name Twister was given to this small fish for two reasons, (1) from the characteristically twisted or wavy markings on each side of the body, (2) from its amazing habit in captivity of eluding other fish, `twisting' and escaping capture when a larger fish was chasing it. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,334</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> THREEFIN BLENNY ... <i>Other names: Tripterygion sp.</i>; cockabully, twister, weedfish. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,284</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> TWISTER [<i>Helcogramma medium</i>] ... Cockabully is a widely used name embracing a number of species. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,18</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> [Caption] Fig. 72: BLENNIES OR COCKABULLIES ... 3. Cockabully, <i>Forsterygion.</i> 4. Twister, <i>Helcogramma. </i> <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,228</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The Blenny goes by several other names too. In his various forms he is the Cockabully, Threepenny, Twister, and Topknot. All are slightly different in looks but come under the collective name of Blenny for easy grouping. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,28</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>TWISTER Helcogramma medium</i> A moderate sized fish with a blunter, more rounded head than most triplefins. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,282,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] twister 1920-2, 1934, 1968 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### twitch
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  It appears that he was twitching a chain with a crowbar, when it broke, smashing and tearing away his nose. <i>(Aug 1, 1907,Feilding Star,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [OED 6. vb draw tight by means of a cord (dial); check EDD] (See [also vbl n twitching](../A#also-vbl-n-twitching))

>  <b>R. GILBERD</b> The old method of tightening the chain securing the logs to trucks is called `twitching', or `taking a twitch'. First the chain is fastened leaving it quite slack. A lever is pased through the bight and used to twist the slack up on itself. When the chain is tight enough the end of the lever is tied to the chain. A `chain-rack' is now used - a giant Hayes wire-strainer. It is sometimes, but improperly, called a `twitch'. `Twitch' is probably an amalgamation of `twist' and `hitch'. <i>(1950,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Okaihau]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  twitch [] browntop: Chewings fescue: couch: creeping fog: Indian doab: onion twitch: paspalum vaginatum: poa pratensis: redtop: yarrow [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,82</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Twitch is the name applied here to several grasses, as <i>Poa pratensis, Agrostis stolonifera</i>, and <i>Agropyron repens</i>(the true couch or twitch). There is but one means of eradication--i.e., cultivation during summer and the careful cleaning of crops. The <i>Poa</i> and the <i>Agrostis</i> can be controlled with less effort than can the true couch. More than one season will be necessary to effect the eradication of this persistent weed. <i>(Jan 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,120</i>)

>  Twitch is the name applied here to several grasses, as <i>Poa pratensis, Agrostis stolonifera</i>, and <i>Agropyron repens</i>(the true couch or twitch). There is but one means of eradication--i.e., cultivation during summer and the careful cleaning of crops. The <i>Poa</i> and the <i>Agrostis</i> can be controlled with less effort than can the true couch. More than one season will be necessary to effect the eradication of this persistent weed. <i>(Jan 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,120</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> POA PRATENSIS (<i>Poa pratensis</i>) is also called twitch and Kentucky blue grass. It is a very widely spread twitch of arable land and gardens. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,42,1967 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> name given to a great number and variety of grasses and other plants; in New Zealand also called couch grass. [[write a etym note on relations of quick, quitch, couch, cooch, cwitch, twitch (see also quid.cud)]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> TWITCH ( a. <i>Agropyron repens</i>) is sometimes called couch, and [also] blue English, white, or old man, twitch or couch. It is one of the worst twitches of arable land and gardens. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,49,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF & CALDER</b> <i>(1948,Pasture Plants & Pastures of NZ,36</i>)

>  <b>McELDOWNEY</b> <i>Monday.</i> ... But Richard is also doing a big job clearing twitch. <i>(1983,Warm South,15</i>) [NOTES: (1962) 1983 McEldowney Warm South 15 [1962 December] 24]

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> I'm thinking about all the jobs I have to do around the place, such as dealing with the twitch and oxalis ... <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,5</i>)

>  <b>WILS</b> <i>Agropyron repens</i> TWITCH ... Couch <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,406</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> TWITCH ( a. <i>Agropyron repens</i>) is sometimes called couch, and [also] blue, English, white, or old man, twitches or couch. It is one of the worst twitch of arable land and gardens. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,49,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>twitch, white</i> [=]<i> couch</i> [s]<i>Ibid.</i> 85 <i>twitch, onion</i> [s] <i>Arrhenatherum elatius</i> (L.) Beauv. var. <i>bulbosum</i> (Willd.) Spenn. ... <i>twitch, onion-rooted</i> [=] <i>onion twitch</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,82</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> ?<i>A. stolonifera</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF & CALDER</b> Creeping Bent ... called Fiorin in Ireland ... is sometimes called water-twitch in New Zealand. <i>(1918,Pasture Plants & Pastures of NZ,25</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> TWITCH ( a. <i>Agropyron repens</i>) is sometimes called couch, and [also] blue, English, white, or old man, twitches or couch. It is one of the worst twitch[?es] of arable land and gardens. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,49,1967 ed.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>mountain twitch</i> [s] <i>Pyrrhanthera exigua</i> (T. Kirk) Zotov <i>Ibid.</i> 82 <i>twitch, mountain<i> [s] <i>Pyrrhanthera exigua <i>(T. Kirk) Zotov <i>(1969,Handbook for New Zealand, The</i>)

### twitch chain
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> TWITCH CHAIN: Chains holding load and tightened by short iron rod. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,189</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 189]

>  Twitch Chains. <i>(May 28, 1885,Wanganui Herald,3</i>)

>  30 Horse covers, 5 tarpaulins, reins, twitch chains, etc. <i>(Oct 12, 1907,Feilding Star,3</i>)

>  Whilst engaged in tightening the twitch chain from the top of his load of timber the chain broke, and he fell from that height on to his head and shoulder. <i>(Sep 24, 1891,Manawatu Herald,2</i>)

>  Mr Charles Bryan, a trolleyman employed at Messrs Waller and England's sawmill at Aratika met with a very severe accident on Saturday by a chain, which is attached at one end to the brake handle and the other end to the twitch chain, coming undone and catching in the packing of the gramway causing the handle to fly back with tremendous force and hit the unfortunate man on the head. <i>(Oct 25, 1911,Grey River Argus,5</i>)

### twitch stick
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a piece of wood or metal used to wind up tie wires on a fencing post.

>  One method is to tie the wires loosely and then tighten them with a 'twitch stick', a timber batten used to wind up the wires like the rubber band in a model aeroplane. <i>(Jun 1997,AgFact,196</i>)

>  For the defence Mr Raymond admitted that young Nichols cut the gums for twitch sticks, and thought they were of no value. <i>(Dec 5, 1895,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

>  In the morning J. Neale's waggon, which had contained twenty bales of wool belonging to Messrs S. Franklin and T. N. Stewart, had been swept clean of wool, although not a chain or a twitch stick had become unfastened. <i>(Jan 9, 1905,Bush Advocate,4</i>)

>  <i>Twitch stick:</i> Twister made of wood or steel. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,30</i>)

>  On the Saturday morning he found the latch prized [sic] off and the bars drawn together with a rope and a twitch stick. <i>(Jul 13, 1914,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

 <i>phr.</i> [or on bicycles]

>  A `boxer' is a gratuity to the `ringie' at two-up, a game supplying the phrase `two ladies on bicycles,' two pennies showing Britannia. <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Two ladies on bikes.</i> The figure of Britannia on the obverse of the two pennies: two-up players', esp. New Zealanders': C. 20. I.e. when both turned up tails; the `heads' betters call them the <i>two bastards on bikes.</i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,921</i>)

### two minutes' silence
 
 <i>n.</i> a (local) newspaper

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Off she went to the pictures, while the grand old gunner with the local `two minutes silence' (newspaper) and the fat cat dozed happily before the cozy kitchen stove. <i>(1978,Soldier Country,198</i>)

### two ones
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Even the metallic ring of the coins could be heard, followed by the suppressed sights of `heads' bettors as `tails' were cried while `two ones', `barred,' `heads a dollar', `tails' ditto, `pass the bat', and other equally quaint and even classic chants, were wafted on the midnight breezes. <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth,343/7/3</i>)

### two shakes of a (dead) lamb's tail
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SARGESON</b> MRS HARDY: ... They'll all be here in two Shakes of a dead lamb's tail. <i>(1964,Wrestling with the Angel,71</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Norah and I will have the lot of them done in two shakes of a dead lamb's tail, don't you worry. <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,132</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> I'll be out again in two shakes of a dead lamb's tail. <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,12</i>)

>  Northlanders have always known Auckland is full of sharp operators ready to fleece you quicker than two shakes of a lamb's tail. [Check TWO] <i>(Jan 13, 1989,Dominion,3</i>)

### two shakes of a dog's hind leg
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>two shakes of a dog's hind leg</i> give me a few seconds and I'll be with you; variant <i>two shakes of a dead lamb's tail</i> rather less basic. NZA. <i>(1998,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,116</i>)

### two up
 
 <i>n.</i> [two-up]

>  The money to be made at the game of "two-up" is not worth the risk. <i>(Apr 23, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  At the Police Court this morning Heke Martin and James Ryan were sentenced to two months' hard labour for playing unlawful games, namely spinning jinny, two up, and draw poker. <i>(Jan 25, 1921,Hawera & Normanby Star,7</i>)

>  "Must admit nothing else on except two up and crown and anchor." <i>(Mar 1, 1941,Waiapu Church Gazette,2</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Euchre and cribbage pass away many an hour, while in some camps a school of poker, nap or `two up' is sometimes started. <i>(1913,Country Work,47</i>)

### two-and-a-buck
 
 <i>n.</i> half a crown

>  Following are other local money-names... 2.6d <i>half-bull, half-caser or two-and-a-buck..</i>. <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Sydney Bulletin,(Red Page)</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from <i>Loafer</i>, Tauranga) [Bill Pearson]]

>  The crown was called a<i> bull</i>, a <i>cartwheel</i>, a <i>caser, five bob</i>, and most familiar of all a <i>dollar</i>, the latter dating from the time when the rate of exchange was four US dollars to the pound. The only instance recorded of Cockney rhyming slang term for a coin is <i>Oxford scholar,</i> the derivation from dollar being obvious. The half crown was called a <i>half-dollar</i>, a <i>half-bull, half-caser</i>, <i>two-and-a-kick,</i> and <i>two-and-a-buck</i>. In the latter instance buck stands for sixpence, though apparently this term was rarely used by itself for that coin denomination, but almost always in association with a number of shillings. The florin seems to have had fewer English nicknames, only <i>two bob</i> being recorded as being in use in New Zealand. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,15</i>)

### two-bailed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>KEENE</b> ... a two bailed cowshed by the road gave us shelter ... I took the bail peg, split it with the shears and used it to splint the broken [bicycle] bar, binding it round with twine. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,106</i>)

### two-bunker
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a small two-bedded hunters' hut erected by DOC or Forestry and used by trampers.

>  Completed in January 2008, the new two-bunker is about four hours in from the road end at Hawdon Shelter. <i>(Jun 2008,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,12</i>)

>  Soon we were at the valley floor, and 20 minutes later, around 8pm, we were at Reardon hut, a two bunker not much larger than a telephone box ... <i>(Nov 1996,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.ctc.org.nz/index.php?option=com_content&task=view&id=1392&Itemid=42]

>  Someone has dismantled a bunk, so it's now a two-bunker with plenty of floor space. <i>(2004,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.remotehuts.co.nz/pic/pic/kokatahi.html]

### two-cut
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOWEN</b> My advice to all is to concentrate on shearing to a pattern, keeping the comb on the sheep, watching yourself for `two-cut' and keeping it to a minimum. <i>Ibid.</i> 158 TWO-CUT. This happens where the staple of wool is cut above its base on the sheep, making it necessary for the handpiece to come back and trim off a short piece of wool that has been cut before. Such short pieces are of little value. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,3</i>)

 <i>v.</i> [Internet search Feb 2006: also used in N Amer, India, Austral.]

### two-spot
 
 <i>n.</i> two hundred dollars

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> he asked if he could have an ounce of skag on credit . . . I said ok, they could have it for twelve hundred. I was still scoring for a grand so it seemed like an easy two-spot for the morning. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,19</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a two-year prison sentence or term

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> They contemplate a `two-spot' (a two-year) or a `three-spot' sentence with equanimity. <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,84</i>)

### two-thirds of five-eighths of fuck all
 
 <i>phr.</i> nothing

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>two-thirds of five-eighths of fuck all </i>very little; eg `What did you make at the races?' `Two-thirds of five-eighths of fuck all.' From the end line of the limerick: <i>There was a young man from Bengal/ Who had an elliptical ball. The square root of his prick/ Time seven-eighths of his grip Was two-thirds of five-eighths of fuck all</i>. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,116</i>)

### two-tier farming
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the growing of trees in conjunction with pasture for animals. (See [agri-forestry](a-all.md#agri-forestry))

>  <b>RENNIE</b> Where trees are grown in conjunction with pasture for animals, this is called 'two-tier' farming or agri-forestry. <i>(1988,Agriculture in NZ,11</i>)

>  <b>FISK</b> ... blanket plant it [steep land] in trees, or plant it thinly and graze stock underneath. This is known as two-tier farming and experiments so far suggest you can run as many animals, and they will do as well, if there are up to 100 stems/ha. <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,44</i>)

### two-tooth
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep of 18 to 22 months old.

>  There was not a very large gathering of buyers, and the sheep on the whole were not first class quality; though there were two or three pens of good lambs and a pen of good two tooth wethers. <i>(Jul 2, 1889,Ashburton Guardian,2</i>)

>  Sheep: 700 were yarded and sold, except one pen of two-tooth ewes, which were passed in at 16s. <i>(Apr 6, 1907,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  The sheep forwarded to the laboratory were one "two-tooth" and one "four-tooth." <i>(1898,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,VI/71</i>)

>  76 Two-tooth half bred Wethers, fat and forward. <i>(May 12, 1944,Ellesmere Guardian,1</i>)

>  <b>Geary</b> Hey, did you hear Old Hobbs bought some of my two-tooths at the sale? Can you believe that? <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,160</i>)

>  The weight of these half-bred sheep at two-tooth varies ... from 56lb. to 65lb. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,124</i>)

>  Ewe lambs 32 two tooth ewes 402 four & six tooth 638. full mouthed 1322 broken mouthed 11, wedders 290 total 2695. <i>(Mar 14, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> A hill-country flockowner keeps all his ewe lambs, turning a percentage of eighteen-month-old ewes - two-tooths, as they are called - into his main flock every year ... <i>(1962,Country Occasions,51</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The boss was there working the drafting gate - two-tooths to the left-hand gate, six-tooths to the right. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,1</i>)

>  <i>two-tooth NZ & Austral.</i> sheep about one year old, with two permanent teeth. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,833</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 518]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Ages of sheep. </i>Although there are many variations, the following names are usually used: A sheep is a <i>lamb</i> until weaned. After weaning a lamb is called a <i> hogget </i> until shorn. A male lamb is a <i> ram lamb </i> until castrated, then a <i> wether </i>. A female lamb is a <i> ewe lamb </i>. After shearing, a ewe or ram lamb is usually called a <i> shorn hogget </i> until cutting the first two teeth. It is then called a <i> two-tooth </i>. Some use the term two-tooth as soon as the lamb is shorn. Next year the sheep is a <i> four-tooth, then a <i> six-tooth </i> and then an <i> eight-tooth </i> or <i> full-mouthed </i> sheep. It is full-mouthed as long as the teeth remain perfect but then becomes broken-mouthed and gummy. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  The sheep were two, four, and six-tooth ewes. <i>(Feb 7, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Dad.. crouched beneath that travesty of an old felt hat marked from many a billy's hot wire handle, and from beating and "yah-hooing" at yarded stubborn sheep or upbraiding a two-tooth. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,26</i>)

>  The above are fine-woolled two-tooth ewes, got by the late Lord Western's improved Merino rams, imported by George Willis, Esq., of Wanstead. <i>(Feb 12, 1848,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,195</i>)

>  Fat ewes, 17s 9d; light b.f. wethers, 22s 6d; small two-tooth ewes, 21s <i>(Apr 21, 1938,Evening Post,22</i>)

>  Peraney rams will be available from May next year for hogget mating and as two tooths from November next year. <i>(Sep 29, 2005,Dominion Post,c8</i>)

>  The New Zealand and Australian Land Company's Southland Leicesters are specially mentioned by the Lyttelton Times, one two-tooth ram, described as a really fine sheep, both for wool and symmetry, fetching GBP30, and a two-tooth Lincoln GBP32 10s. <i>(Mar 16, 1874,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  Competition was very keen and a total clearance resulted at the following prices: Two-tooth wethers 25s 2d to 25s 7d; 2-tooth ewes 24s 1d; 4-tooth wethers and ewes 22s 6d; mixed 2-tooths, 18s 6d. <i>(Feb 6, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  Two-tooth wethers sold at 26/-, and a nice line of wether store lambs at 23/9. <i>(Feb 12, 1929,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I.. also took out a hundred or so old ewes and a handful of cull two-tooths. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,88</i>)

>  FIFTY MERINO RAMS, comprising six-tooth. four-tooth, two-tooth sheep, and ten-month-old ram lambs, for SALE in the Wairau. <i>(Mar 23, 1850,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,13</i>)</i>

### two-up
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a gambling game popular with shearers and mustering gangs in the early 1900s [simply comprising two coins being tossed and players betting on the chances of their both falling the same way.]

>  <b>CHINN</b> There were still three days to go and the shearers filled in this time as best they could, some doing a good business in sly grog on the quiet, and playing the national game of two-up as there was no police surveillance. <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,58</i>)

### two-up king
 
 <i>n.</i>

### two-up ring
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There was a flutter in the "two-up ring" at Raurimu, on the North Island main trunk railway works, recently, just after the last pay. <i>(May 29, 1906,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  Time-serving politicians, with a purchased Press, are often their tools, while the economic world is their two-up ring, into which double-headed coins soon find a way. <i>(Nov 13, 1923,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>WESTON</b> The `two up' ring is their billiard room or bridge parlour ... <i>(1918,Three Years with the NZers,252</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> He hadn't been lucky at the two-up ring lately. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,125</i>)

### two-up school
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Euchre and cribbage pass away many an hour, while in some camps a school of poker, nap or `two up' is sometimes started. <i>(1913,Country Work,47</i>)

>  Roll of men proceeding to Crown and Anchor and Two Up schools. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,10</i>)

>  A large ... crowd assembles nightly at the Two-Up School held in the garden at the back of the A.P.M.'s billet. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,50</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> He went away and drew the shouting and the two-up schools in camp over his mind for protection. <i>Ibid.</i> 137 Five minutes later, two dozen mounted police charged the two-up schools, involving a heavy loss of stakes as the men scattered from under the horses' hoofs. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,102</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Two-up school <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,71</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Starkie drew [pounds]84 ... and at two the same day was broke, the two-up school taking the proceeds. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,71</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> During play in a two-up school in Tripoli, one of the coins was sent spinning high in the air ... <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,71</i>)

>  The two-up school goes on in the small bedroom <i>(1987,Metro Fiction,8</i>) [NOTES: (Keogh, Chiaroscuro)]

>  The "swell mobsmen" are in plenty, the "confidence trick" men, the "two up" school, the common pilferer, the adept pickpocket and the betting tout are all here, but these are not the worst, for numbers of the more desperate criminal class are in town and others are coming. <i>(Oct 29, 1900,Star, The,4</i>)

>  Still, raiding a two-up or hazard school is a popular event in the lives of some constables, for when the boys in blue make their blind bull-dog break on the ring, any loose coin that they can grab lying around is said to be theirs for keeps. <i>(Jan 8, 1921,NZ Truth,1</i>)

### two-upper
 
 <i>n.</i> a two-up player

>  The "John" Department's sudden burst of energy which resulted during last week in the rounding up of various schools of "two-uppers" and "hazardites" on the Wellington wharf, at Petone and Foxton, can be taken as one of those periodical stunts which are pulled off by the police in consequence of receiving a gentle reminder from someone higher up. <i>(Jan 8, 1921,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  Two-uppers Fined. <i>(Mar 22, 1914,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  In Wellington, some time back, "two-uppers" were wont to congregate in Kelburne [sic], in the region of the Botanical Gardens, and also at the back of the plantations on Mount Victoria, but the march of progress has compelled them to take to darker quarters. <i>(Jun 4, 1907,Evening Post,6</i>)

### tyke
 
 <i>n.</i> a (usually Irish) Catholic probably a rhyme on <i>Mike</i>; cf MICK DOOLAN [AND tyke 1902: prob. an alteration of TEAGUE, a nickname for an Irishman] [Bob Cotterall writes that the word, as referring to Catholics, was "In Auckland in common use round the 1900's, and came from the number of Yorkshire Catholics in circulation. Very common in referring to Catholic rugby teams. USE IN PRINT. Bishop Cleary was displeased on finding the sentence "...but Tykes came again" in a report of a rugby match that appeared in the SHC Magazine round about 1929, 1930, 1931 or 1932, and referred, in "<i>The Month</i>" to (I think) '..an unpleasant epithet. Anyhow, it's there, in print, round about 1929-32 in the Sacred Heart annual mag." ca1930 W.H. Orsman]

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> They'd call themselves micks, or doolans or tykes, but if you called them anything like that. they'd get maggoty. <i>Ibid.</i> 245 tyke Roman Catholic <i>(1984,Blackball 08,119</i>)

>  <i>tyke n. (Sl.)</i> anyone professing the Roman Catholic faith. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),21</i>)

>  <i>tyke n. NZ & Austral. sl. derog.</i> Roman Catholic. [Ir.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,834</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1268]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>tyke</i> Catholic; Partridge says variant of `Teague', Ulster name for Roman Catholic, originally English transcription of Irish name `Tadhg', an Irishman. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,116</i>)

>  One of the witnesses, a typical Tyke, maintained that the prisoner was "wrang in his heid." <i>(Apr 23, 1904,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

### Tylosaurus haumuriensis
 
 <i>n.</i> particular mosasaur

>  Hector's two species of mosasaur, taniwhasaurus oweni and Tylosaurus haumuriensis, are regarded as valid. <i>(Dec 1970,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,29/15</i>)

### type
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1945 Wellington Training College Are you types ready yet?]



 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. odd-bod]

>  [Mount Ruapehu] Are you a `type' or an `odd-bod'? It was several days before I fathomed this out. The cry would go up, `Ruapehu types arriving, get cracking'. The best club cups would be sorted out, an official or prominent member would greet the new-comers, show them proudly over the house and dispense tea and hospitality with a cordial air. Then I'd hear, `Odd-bods coming up the track'. A member would supervise his arrival, see that no one entered the club room with nailed boots on, and offer a cup of tea, but there was not that ostentatious display of club china, `eats' and cordiality. I found that a `type' is a member of a recognised club and therefore someone to be treated with respect. An `odd-bod' is also a tramper or mountaineer, but of no specified breed, though entitiled to shelter. The same law holds for bush and mountain; hospitality must be dispensed for folk of the right ilk, so that the billy is always on the boil. But tourists! Well, I'd just hate to be a tourist! They were treated with loftiest contempt. I suppose I come under the heading `odd-bod', vouched for by a `type' ... <i>(Jun 14, 1951,NZ Woman's Weekly,24</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [an occasional trampers' c.p. [d?] Tramper 16(1):3 [d?] ibid 16(11):4 . Mason notes: Heard occasionally in T.T.C - inference that a person is a poor type, perhaps from army?]

### typey
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of livestock] true to a perfect example of a breed.

>  While the importance of type is fully recognised, the fact remains that if a competitor can successfully raise a calf showing little or no type, it naturally follows that he or she can rear a "typey" calf just as well. <i>(Nov 21, 1927,NZ Journal of Agriculture,326</i>)

>  A typey sheep of N Rutherford's was commended. <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Farmer,1490</i>)

>  Jos. Brooks scored in a strong class of ewe hoggets with a very typey sheep. <i>(1928,NZ Farmer,1491</i>)

### typha
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... a kind of sowing through small holes with strings of the Flaxplant or <i>Arakeke [a2-macron, e1-grave]</i>e seams are caulked with the pappus of the Reed-mace or <i>Typha.</i> <i>(Jun 8, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/300</i>)

### typo
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of taipo

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Most sought after, in palm or rotting log, was the typo... Up t o four inches long in body and thickness, this insect is of terrifying appearance. The typo is the only living thing in the New Zealand bush capable of inflicting a painful, though non-venomous wound. <i>(1988,Point of Departure,7</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from c1900.]

### tyrant
 
 <i>n.</i> a real tyrant, a little tyrant, said of a naughty child, or someone when young

>  `My husband was a real tyrant as a teenager and will readily admit to committing burglaries during that time,' she said ... `He was only a mixed up kid when he committed those crimes.' `Since then, he has settled down ....' <i>(Nov 16, 1983,NZ Truth,373/9/4</i>)

### tyre kicker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr Burgess, who works as a cad technician at Meritec, is a former engineer - or "tyre-kicker". as he calls it - for the RNZAF. <i>(Mar 27, 2003,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

>  The rest are gawkers, or tyre-kickers, as real-estate agents call them. <i>(Oct 5, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

>  The corporation had deliberately not decided on a more public process to market the properties in order to eliminate the "tyre kickers" from the process. <i>(Dec 5, 2005,NZ Farmers' Weekly,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> someone looking at used cars or other goods without a firm intention to purchase; a browser. Also extended to other situations where people may show a lack of genuine commitment to an enterprise etc.

>  Car dealers have a collection of jargon they use on the yard, so if you are thinking of taking up selling cars as a career, there are a few terms you should know. ... `Sandwich munchers' and `tyre kickers' are not very popular in car yards. <i>(Jan 22, 1986,NZ Herald,Sect. 2/1</i>)

>  Letting you browse without pressure to buy, as it's possible to do for two hours at Kirkcaldie's without being treated like just another tyre kicker. <i>(Jan 27, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  Success has attracted all sorts to the wine industry, including a mess of tyre kickers and egocentrics in search of sex appeal, and at odds with the fundamental structures of premium winemaking. <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)
